# <unclear>ALPHONSO</unclear> <note>original: "ALPHONSUS"</note>

<note>This name is written in large, formal capital letters. It likely identifies the author of the manuscript or its primary owner. In the period this was produced, "Alphonsus" (the Latin form of Alphonso) was a common name for prominent scholars and theologians.</note>

<note>The cover is made of <term>limp vellum</term>—a flexible, durable parchment made from animal skin. The leather ties or "thongs" visible on the edges were used to secure the pages within the binding and to keep the book tightly closed when not in use.</note>

<summary>This is the front cover of a parchment-bound manuscript, featuring a faded inscription of the name "Alphonso" in large capital letters. It represents the external identification of the book, showing signs of heavy wear and historical use.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as the title page for a 1532 collection of Hermetic and Neoplatonic texts published in Basel. It introduces key works by Hermes Trismegistus, Iamblichus, and Proclus, which were central to Renaissance occult and philosophical thought.</meta>

# THE <term>PYMANDER</term> <note>Original: *Pymander* (or Poimandres), meaning "Shepherd of Men," a foundational text of Hermeticism where the Divine Mind reveals the secrets of the universe.</note> OF HERMES TRIS-
## MEGISTUS, ON THE POWER AND WISDOM OF GOD.

### BY THE SAME AUTHOR, THE <term>ASCLEPIUS</term> <note>A dialogue between Hermes and his disciple Asclepius, famously known for its "Lament" over the decline of Egyptian religion and its discussion on the creation of "animated statues."</note>, ON THE WILL OF GOD.

->*Small works most highly distinguished by the holiest mysteries and truly heavenly oracles.*<-

### <term>IAMBLICHUS</term> <note>A Syrian Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who emphasized "theurgy," or ritual practices intended to unite the soul with the divine.</note> ON THE MYSTERIES OF THE EGYPTIANS, CHALDEANS, AND ASSYRIANS.

### <term>PROCLUS</term> <note>One of the last great Neoplatonists (412–485 AD); his commentaries were instrumental in transmitting Greek philosophy to the medieval and Renaissance worlds.</note> ON THE PLATONIC <term>ALCIBIADES</term> <note>A commentary on Plato's dialogue "Alcibiades I," which focuses on self-knowledge and the nature of the soul.</note>, CONCERNING THE SOUL AND THE <term>DIVINE SPIRIT</term> <note>Original: *daemone*. In Neoplatonic philosophy, a "daemon" is a tutelary spirit or intermediary between gods and mortals, not a malevolent "demon."</note>.

### BY THE SAME AUTHOR, ON SACRIFICE AND MAGIC.

->*All of which have been cleansed with diligent care and finally restored to their original brilliance.*<-

# BASEL,
# 1532.

<summary>This is the title page of a 1532 compilation of essential Hermetic and Neoplatonic treatises, including works attributed to the legendary Hermes Trismegistus and the philosophers Iamblichus and Proclus. It represents the Renaissance era's deep interest in ancient mystical wisdom and the "purification" of classical texts for modern study.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Pymander, Asclepius, Iamblichus, Proclus, Hermetica, Neoplatonism, Basel, alchemy, theurgy, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory material from the title page. It is the beginning of the translator Marsilio Ficino’s famous preface, where he establishes the legendary lineage of Hermes Trismegistus to justify the importance of these texts to his patron, Cosimo de' Medici.</meta>

<sig>A 2</sig>

### MARSILIO FICINO THE FLOREN-
### tine, his Preface to the *PYMANDER* of HERMES TRISME-
### GISTUS, addressed to Cosimo de' Medici,
### Father of his Country.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'E' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or deity, surrounded by foliage and architectural elements.</image-desc>

AT the time when Moses was born, there flourished Atlas the astrologer, the brother of Prometheus the Natural Philosopher and the maternal grandfather of the elder Mercury, whose grandson was Hermes Trismegistus. <margin>Therefore he was a contemporary of Moses, as many maintain (Volume 1, Chapter 5 of the *Polygonal History* <note>Likely referring to a historical compendium or specific chronology popular in the Renaissance.</note>).</margin> Augustine writes this about him <note>Original: *Augustinus*. St. Augustine discussed Hermes in *The City of God*, acknowledging his antiquity but viewing his "prophecies" with caution.</note>, although Cicero and Lactantius <note>Church Father Lactantius was actually very fond of Hermes, seeing him as a pagan prophet who anticipated Christian truths.</note> assert that there were five Mercuries in succession, and that the fifth was he who is called Thoth <note>Original: *Theuth*</note> by the Egyptians and Trismegistus by the Greeks. They claim that this man killed Argus, ruled over the Egyptians, and handed down laws and letters to them. Furthermore, he established the characters of these letters using the shapes of animals and trees <note>A reference to Egyptian hieroglyphics, which Renaissance scholars believed were a form of symbolic, mystical language rather than a phonetic alphabet.</note>. He was held in such great veneration by men that he was counted among the number of the gods. Very many temples were constructed to his divine power. Out of a certain reverence, it was not permitted for the common people to pronounce his proper name rashly or in public. The first month of the year among the Egyptians is named after him; a town was founded by him which even now is called <term>Hermopolis</term> <note>Original Greek: *ἑρμόπολις*, meaning "City of Hermes."</note>, that is, the City of Mercury. They called him Trismegistus, that is, "Thrice-Greatest," because [he was] both a philosopher...

<summary>In this preface, Marsilio Ficino introduces Hermes Trismegistus as a historical contemporary of Moses, blending Egyptian mythology with classical history to present Hermes as a divine lawgiver and the inventor of hieroglyphs.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Hermes Trismegistus, Cosimo de' Medici, Moses, Thoth, Hieroglyphics, Hermopolis, Thrice-Greatest</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s preface to Cosimo de' Medici. Ficino is explaining the genealogy of the "Ancient Theology" (Prisca Theologia), linking Hermes Trismegistus to a lineage that culminates in Plato, thereby framing the Hermetic texts as a precursor to both Platonic philosophy and Christian revelation.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>MARSILIO FICINO</header>
<meta>catchword: nitus</meta>

...stood out as the greatest, and the greatest priest, and the greatest king. For it was the custom of the Egyptians (as Plato writes) to choose their priests from the number of philosophers, and to elect their king from the assembly of priests. Therefore, just as he surpassed all other philosophers in sharp intelligence and learning, so he was then appointed priest, excelling all other priests in the holiness of his life and the worship of the divine. Finally, having obtained royal dignity, he obscured the glory of previous kings by his administration of laws and his great deeds; so that he was deservedly called Thrice-Greatest <note>original: "ter maximus"</note>. He was the first among philosophers to turn his mind from physical and mathematical subjects to the contemplation of divine matters. He was the first to dispute most wisely concerning the majesty of God, the order of spirits <note>original: "daemonum". In this context, these are intermediary spiritual beings, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense.</note>, and the migrations of souls. He was therefore called the first author of theology. Following him, Orpheus held the second rank in this ancient theology. Aglaophemus was initiated into the sacred rites of Orpheus. Pythagoras succeeded Aglaophemus in theology, followed by Philolaus, the teacher of our divine Plato. Thus, a single lineage of ancient theology <term>Prisca Theologia: The Renaissance belief in a "primordial theology" shared by ancient sages which predated and supported Christian truths.</term>, consistent with itself in all respects, was forged from six theologians in a wonderful order, beginning with Mercury and being perfectly

<column-break/>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>
<sig>A 3</sig>
<warning>Small dark stain/burn hole in the middle of the left margin area affecting the word "phustantum".</warning>

finished by the divine Plato. Mercury wrote many books pertaining to the knowledge of divine things, in which—oh immortal God!—what secret mysteries, what stupendous oracles are revealed! He speaks not merely as a philosopher, but often as a prophet, and sings of things to come. He foresaw the ruin of the ancient religion, the rise of the new faith, the coming of Christ, the future judgment, the resurrection of the age <note>original: "resurrectionem seculi". This refers to the end of the world and the dawn of a new era.</note>, the glory of the blessed, and the punishments of sinners. For this reason, Aurelius Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD).</note> doubted whether he uttered these many truths through his skill in the stars or through the revelation of spirits. Lactantius <note>Early Christian author (c. 250 – c. 325 AD) who often cited Hermes as a pagan witness to Christian truth.</note>, however, does not hesitate to number him among the Sibyls and the prophets. Finally, out of the many books of Mercury, two are especially divine: one regarding the divine will, the other regarding the power and wisdom of God. The former is titled *Asclepius*, the latter *Pymander*. Apuleius the Platonist <note>The North African writer and philosopher (c. 124 – c. 170 AD) who was traditionally credited with the Latin translation of the *Asclepius*.</note> translated the first into Latin; the other remained among the Greeks until these times. But recently, having been brought from Macedonia into Italy, it came to us through the diligence of Leonardo of Pistoia, a learned and upright monk. I, however, being encouraged by your exhortations, decided to translate it from the Greek language into Latin; it was right...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino traces the genealogy of wisdom from Hermes Trismegistus through Orpheus and Pythagoras to Plato, arguing that Hermes was a pagan prophet who foresaw Christian truths. He also recounts how the Greek manuscript of the Pymander was recently rediscovered and brought to Italy from Macedonia.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Marsilio Ficino, Prisca Theologia, Plato, Pymander, Asclepius, Renaissance Humanism, Leonardo of Pistoia, prophecy, lineage of wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s dedication to Cosimo de' Medici. After tracing the lineage of ancient wisdom (Prisca Theologia) through Hermes Trismegistus to Plato, Ficino now explains why he is dedicating this specific translation to Cosimo and provides an overview of the book's contents, known as the Pymander.</meta>

# MARSILIO FICINO
<meta>catchword: do se</meta>

...I thought it right, fortunate Cosimo, to dedicate this little work to your name. For since I myself, aided by your resources and amply supplied with your books, have applied myself to Greek studies, it is fitting that I offer the first fruits of my Greek studies to that same person. Nor was it right to dedicate the work of such a wise philosopher, such a pious priest, and such a powerful king to anyone, unless the person to whom it is dedicated surpassed all others in piety, wisdom, power, and all other things. Now, to descend to the writings of Mercury <note>original: "Mercurij". This refers to Hermes Trismegistus, whom the Romans identified with Mercury.</note>, the title of this book is *Pymander*; for of the four persons who debate in this dialogue, the primary role is attributed to Pymander. He published the book in Egyptian letters; and, being likewise skilled in the Greek language, he communicated the mysteries of the Egyptians to the Greeks by translating them from there. The purpose of this work is to discuss the power and wisdom of God. And since their operations are twin, the first of which remains in the very nature of God, while the second extends to external things—and while the former conceives the first and eternal world, the latter gives birth to the second and temporal world—it discusses most seriously both operations and both worlds: what the power of God is, what His wisdom is, by what order they conceive internally, and by what progression they give birth externally. Furthermore, how those things which have been produced toward

<column-break/>

# PREFACE
**A 4**

each other behave, in what they agree, or in what they differ, and finally in what way they regard their author. The order of the volume is such that it is divided into fourteen small books: so that the first parts of the dialogue are given to Pymander, Trismegistus holds the second, Aesculapius the third, and Tatius <note>Tat, the son of Hermes in the Hermetic tradition.</note> occupies the fourth place. Mercury therefore intends to instruct Aesculapius and Tatius in divine matters. He who has not learned cannot teach divine things. But by human wit we cannot discover those things which are above human nature. Therefore, there is need of divine light, so that we may behold the sun itself by the light of the sun. Indeed, the light of the divine mind is never infused into the soul unless the soul itself, like the moon toward the sun, is turned completely toward the mind of God. The soul does not turn toward the mind unless it also becomes "mind" <note>Ficino is referring to the "Nous" or higher intellect.</note>. Indeed, it does not become mind before it has cast aside the deceptions of the senses and the clouds of the imagination <note>original: "phantasiæ". In Renaissance psychology, this was the faculty that processed sensory images.</note>. For this reason, our Mercury stripped away the mists of the senses and imagination and withdrew himself into the inner sanctuary <note>original: "adytum"</note> of the mind. Soon Pymander—that is, the divine mind—flows into him. From there, he contemplates the order of all things, both those existing in God and those flowing from God. Finally, he explains to other men those things which were revealed to him by the divine power.

<summary>Ficino dedicates his translation of the Pymander to Cosimo de' Medici, arguing that only a man of Cosimo's stature is worthy of Hermes Trismegistus. He outlines the Hermetic cosmology of the "two worlds" and the process of divine revelation through the silencing of the senses.</summary>

<keywords>Cosimo de' Medici, Hermes Trismegistus, Pymander, Neoplatonism, divine revelation, cosmology, Nous, imagination</keywords>

# PREFACE
**Page 8**

...This, therefore, is the title of the book, its purpose, and its order. But you, fortunate Cosimo, read happily, and live long, so that it [the book] may also live long for the benefit of your fatherland.

->JACQUES LEFÈVRE D'ÉTAPLES<-
<note>original: "Faber Stapulensis." Lefèvre was a leading French humanist and theologian who edited this edition of the Hermetic corpus.</note>
->TO THE READER.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'C' featuring a floral or foliate design.</image-desc>WE HAVE TAKEN CARE, with what diligence we could, to return this work in a corrected state from a corrupted copy, both out of love for Marsilio [Ficino], whom we venerate as a father, and because we are moved by the magnitude of the wisdom of Mercury <note>original: "Mercurij." Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage.</note>. Furthermore, for the sake of helping students, we have added a few things by way of summaries; although we freely admit that each of the books requires greater effort for its explanation, and a talent far more outstanding than our modest strengths might suggest. Farewell.

<column-break/>

# ARGUMENT OF THE FIRST DIALOGUE.
**Page 9**

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'I' featuring a bird (possibly a swan or pelican) amidst foliage.</image-desc>IN the first dialogue, Mercury treats the Mosaic mysteries <note>original: "Mosaica mysteria." This refers to the secrets of creation found in the Book of Genesis, traditionally attributed to Moses.</note>. For Moses saw darkness upon the face of the abyss, and the spirit of the Lord carried over the waters: but here [Mercury] sees a terrifying shadow migrating into a moist nature—which moist nature the word of the Lord was indeed cherishing. The former [Moses] announces all things created by the powerful word of the Lord; but the latter [Mercury] asserts that that shining word, which illuminates all things—the sprout of the mind—is the Son of God. He asserts that the Father Mind and the Word of the Mind do not differ in nature; he said it was consubstantial <note>original: "consubstantiale." A technical theological term meaning "of the same substance," used here to suggest that Hermes Trismegistus understood the nature of the Christian Trinity before the time of Christ.</note>. He saw also that there is a union of these. And if you desire more fully—and if it is right to ascribe such divine knowledge to men before the fleshly veil of the Word [the Incarnation]—he saw the Son being born from the Father, and the Spirit proceeding in a miraculous way from the Father and the Son, when he said: "The Mind, however, being God, most full of the fertility of both sexes, Life and Light, with its Word, gave birth to a second Mind, the Demiurge <note>original: "opificem." A "maker" or "craftsman" who shapes the physical world.</note>." He saw too the Mind, by its exuberant fertility, stretching itself out to external things, and forming the universe according to the pattern of its own divine Word, and distributing to each its own nature according to its kind in the air, in the water, and on the earth. Soon, man was made after His own likeness and image, to whose use the Demiurge Mind subjected all sensible things. Man soon fell from the intelligible sphere <note>original: "intelligibili sphæra." The realm of pure spirit and thought, as opposed to the physical world.</note> to the sphere of bodily and lustful things; from here he also contracted the origin of the "fuel of evils" <note>original: "fomitis malorum." A term for the inclination toward sin.</note>; and finally the knot of all things was loosened (by the will of God), and man was made subject to death along with the rest. What else is here, [but] that God [is] the Word...

<sig>A 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: uer- (word-)</meta>

<summary>Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples introduces his edited version of the Pymander, followed by a summary of the first dialogue which draws striking parallels between Hermetic creation and the Book of Genesis. The text presents Hermes Trismegistus as a "pagan prophet" who anticipated Christian doctrines like the Trinity and the Fall of Man.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Pymander, Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples, Marsilio Ficino, creation myth, Moses, Genesis, Consubstantiality, Demiurge, Fall of Man</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Argument" or summary of the first book of the Corpus Hermeticum, comparing the Hermetic creation story to the biblical account in Genesis. It then transitions into the start of the first dialogue, the Pymander, where the narrator (Hermes) experiences a divine vision.</meta>

[I heard the] holy [voice] cry out: "Sprout, grow, and propagate all seeds and my works," which is much like the Mosaic saying: "Increase and multiply, and fill the earth." <note>original: "Crescite & multiplicamini, & replete terram." A direct reference to the command given in Genesis 1:28.</note> Soon Mercury instructs us on how the way to the Mind <term>Mind: The Latin 'Mens' (equivalent to the Greek 'Nous') refers here to the Divine Intellect or the highest spark of human consciousness that connects us to God.</term> lies open, and what evil error finally leads us away from it; he shows to whom the Mind is present as a bountiful giver of goods, and from whom it is likewise absent. He explains how, just as we fall by certain steps from an intellectual and immortal nature and degenerate into decaying things, we are also restored to that prior state of a purified mind by certain opposite steps. Let Moses be the leader of the Hebrew flock by divine decree, and let Mercury be the leader of the Egyptian flock. Now, by holy instruction, he feeds his flock, living an active life; but then, praising the Father of all things with hymns and thanksgivings, he rises into the light of the contemplative life. This is the summary of the Pymander.

<column-break/>

# THE PYMANDER OF MERCURY
### TRISMEGISTUS, ON THE POWER AND WISDOM OF GOD.
### MARSILIO FICINO OF FLORENCE, TRANSLATOR.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut drop cap 'C' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or scribe, working at a desk or table with books.</image-desc>

WHILE I WAS REFLECTING on the nature of things, and raising the sharp edge of my mind to higher realms, my bodily senses were already lulled to sleep—just as usually happens to those who are weighed down by sleep due to fullness or exhaustion. Suddenly, I seemed to see someone of immense bodily size, who, calling me by name, cried out in this manner: "What is it, O Mercury <note>original: "Mercuri." Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage whom the Renaissance believed was a contemporary of Moses.</note>, that you desire to both hear and behold? What is it that you wish to learn and understand?" Then I said: "Who are you?" He replied: "I am Pymander, the Mind of divine power. See what you wish, for I will be with you everywhere." I said: "I desire to learn the nature of things and to know God." To this he replied: "Embrace me in your mind, and I will instruct you in all that you hope for."

<summary>This page concludes the introductory summary comparing Hermes to Moses and begins the Pymander, the most famous Hermetic text, where Hermes Trismegistus encounters a divine vision of the "Mind of Power."</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Pymander, Divine Mind, creation, active vs contemplative life, Marsilio Ficino, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues from the introductory "Argument" by Marsilio Ficino, which summarized the Hermetic creation story and compared it to Genesis. We are now entering the primary text of the "Pymander," where Hermes (Mercury) begins to describe his mystical vision of the creation of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

When he had said these things, he changed his form, and suddenly revealed all things. I beheld an immense vision: all things turned into light, exceedingly sweet and pleasant, which wonderfully delighted me as I gazed upon it. A little while later, a certain dreadful shadow, in an oblique revolution, glided beneath it and changed into a moist nature <note>original: "humidam naturam." In Hermetic thought, this refers to the primordial state of matter, a chaotic watery abyss similar to the "deep" in the Book of Genesis.</note>, agitated by an ineffable tumult. Then a great smoke broke out into a sound; from the sound came a voice, which I thought was the voice of light. From this voice of light, the Word <term>Word: The Latin 'Verbum' translates the Greek 'Logos,' representing the divine reason and creative utterance that brings order to chaos.</term> came forth. This Word, standing by the moist nature, cherished it. From the depths of the moist nature, a pure and light fire immediately flew out, seeking the heights. The light air, too, being the parent of spirit, took the middle region between fire and water. But earth and water lay so mixed together that the face of the earth, covered by the waters, was nowhere visible. These two were then set in motion by the spiritual Word that hovered over them, sounding in their ears.

Then Pymander said: "Do you perceive what this vision means?" I replied, "I shall come to know." Pymander then said: "I am that light: Mind, your God, older than the moist nature which flowed out from the shadow

<column-break/>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER.</header>

from the shadow. The offspring of the Mind is the shining Word, the Son of God." "What then?" I asked. "Think of it this way," he said: "that which in you sees and hears is the Word of the Lord, but the Mind is Father God. For they are not separate from each other; their union is life."

**TRISMEGISTUS:** "I give thanks to you."
**PYMANDER:** "But first, meditate upon the light and know it."

**TRISMEGISTUS:** When these things were said, I prayed to him for a long time that he might turn his Idea <note>original: "ideam." Here referring to the divine blueprint or the true form of reality.</note> toward me. When he had done so, I suddenly perceived in my mind a light existing with countless powers: an ornament without end, fire surrounded by immense force, and dominating in its own stability. I perceived these things through the word of Pymander, who spoke to me again as I was stunned with wonder: "Have you seen in your mind the first form, prevailing with infinite empire?" Pymander said such things to me. But I asked him: "From where did the elements of nature flow?"

**PYMANDER:** "From the will of God, which, having embraced the Word and looked upon the beautiful world, fashioned the rest according to that example from its own elements and vital seeds. But the Mind, God, full of the fecundity of both sexes <note>The Divine Mind is often described in Hermetic texts as being androgynous or "male-female," possessing all creative potential within itself.</note>, life and light, brought forth with its Word another Mind, the Artisan <term>Artisan: The 'Opifex' (Greek 'Demiurge'), the divine craftsman who shapes the raw material of the universe into the physical world.</term>, who is indeed the God of fire and the spirit of divinity."

<summary>In this pivotal scene, Hermes receives a vision of the universe's creation, witnessing the emergence of light, the "Word," and the elements from a primordial shadow. Pymander reveals himself as the Divine Mind and explains the relationship between God (Mind), the Word (the Son), and the Artisan who shapes the material world.</summary>
<keywords>Pymander, Hermes Trismegistus, Logos, Creation, Elements, Divine Mind, Artisan, Marsilio Ficino, Hermeticism</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the creation narrative of the universe. In the previous section, the Word (Logos) emerged from the Light to order the chaotic "moist nature." Now, the "Craftsman Mind" begins to organize the heavens and the earthly creatures, culminating in the creation of Man.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

...seven <note>The text continues from the previous page: "septem" (seven).</note> governors thereafter he fashioned, who encompass the sensible world within circles: and their arrangement is called Fate <term>Fate: Here, 'Fatum' refers to the deterministic laws of the cosmos governed by the seven planetary spheres.</term>. Then the Word of God joined together with the pure craft of nature from the elements tending downward, and it was united with the craftsman Mind. For it was of the same substance <note>original: "consubstantiale." This is a significant theological term, suggesting the Word and the Mind share the same divine essence.</note>. And the elements of nature, falling downward without reason, were left behind as mere matter. The craftsman Mind, together with the Word, containing the circles and turning them with swift speed, directed his own machine toward himself, and commanded it to revolve from a beginning without beginning to an end without end. For it always begins there where it ends. The revolution of all these, just as the Mind itself willed, fashioned animals devoid of reason from the lower elements; for it did not grant them reason. The air brought forth winged creatures, and the water produced swimming ones. Water and earth were also separated from one another in the manner that pleased the Mind. Afterward, the earth brought forth the living creatures she had within her: namely four-footed beasts, creeping things, wild animals, both fierce and domestic. But the Intellect, father of all, being life and light, procreated Man like unto himself,

<meta>catchword: [procre]ated,</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

ated, and rejoiced in him as his own son; for he was beautiful, bearing the image of his father. For God, truly delighted by his own form, granted all his works for human use. But Man, when he had observed the procreation <margin><unclear>it seems wrong to me</unclear> <note>original: "peccat mihi." This marginalia likely reflects a reader's or corrector's disagreement with the theological claim in the text.</note></margin> of all things in his father, wanted to fashion something himself. Thus, from the contemplation of the father, he fell into the sphere of generation <term>Sphere of generation: The physical world where things are born and die, as opposed to the eternal divine realm.</term>. And since he held power over all things within himself, he observed the handiwork of the seven governors. These governors, rejoicing in the contemplation of the human mind, made Man a participant in their own particular rank. After he learned their essence and looked upon his own nature, he now longed to penetrate and break through the boundary of the circles, and to grasp the force of the governor presiding over the fire. He who had all authority and power over the mortal and irrational living things of the world emerged and leaped forth through the harmony <note>original: "harmoniam." In this context, it refers to the structured arrangement of the celestial spheres.</note>, penetrating and breaking the power of the circles, and revealed Nature, which glides downward, as the beautiful form of God. When he gazed upon Nature, endowed with marvelous beauty,

<summary>This page describes the creation of the seven planetary governors (Fate) and the physical world, followed by the birth of Man, who is created in God's image and given authority over creation. Man's desire to create leads him to descend through the celestial spheres toward the material world of Nature.</summary>

<keywords>Seven Governors, Fate, Craftsman Mind, Word of God, Creation of Animals, Creation of Man, Spheres of Generation, Celestial Harmony, Nature</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the creation of Man. Following the previous page's description of the "Craftsman Mind" creating the celestial spheres (the Seven Governors) and the earthly animals, we now see the descent of the archetypal Man into the material world.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

...and all the actions of the seven governors <note>These are the planetary spirits or spheres that govern the laws of Fate.</note>, and moreover to possess the very image of God himself, he smiled with immense love, for he saw the beauty of his own human form mirrored in the water and its shadow cast upon the earth. Furthermore, seeing a form similar to himself existing within himself, as if in the water, he loved it and desired to join with it. The effect immediately followed the will, and he brought forth a form devoid of reason. But Nature, having embraced the one she was drawn to with love, completely entwined and mingled herself with him. 

Consequently, man alone among all earthly living creatures is judged to be of a double nature: mortal on account of his body, but immortal on account of the substantial <note>original: "substantialem." This refers to the true, spiritual essence of humanity that exists before the physical body.</note> man himself. For he is immortal and holds authority over all things. Yet the other living things, which are mortal, suffer being subject to Fate. Man, therefore, stood superior to the harmony <note>original: "harmonia." The celestial order or the interlocking "gears" of the planetary spheres.</note>; but having fallen into the harmony, he became endangered and was made a slave. He, fortified by the fecundity of both sexes by the one who is the source of both sexes, and made watchful by him who is ever-watchful, is held and subjected to his dominion. <term>TRISMEGISTUS.</term> After these 

<column-break/>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>
<sig>B</sig>

things, the Mind in which you yourself are the very essence of my reason. Then <term>Pymander</term> said: "This is the mystery that has remained hidden from the human race until this day. For Nature, mingling herself with man, brought forth a wonder that surpasses the admiration of all miracles. For when he himself had been imbued with the harmony of those seven by the one I described to you a little before—namely, the father and the spirit—Nature herself did not resist. On the contrary, she immediately gave birth to seven men according to the natures of the seven governors, possessing both male and female sexes and being sublime <note>original: "sublimes." Here meaning exalted or celestial in nature.</note>." 

To this I replied: "O Pymander, I have just been seized by a burning desire; I wish to hear the rest besides, so I beseech you, do not leave me here." But he said to me: "Be silent, be silent, for I have not yet finished the first discourse." <term>TRISMEGISTUS.</term> Behold, I am now silent. <term>PYMANDER.</term> The generation of these seven, as I said, was brought about in this manner. For the female was earth and the water was the element of union; she took maturity from fire and spirit from the ether. And Nature joined the bodies together to shape them into the appearance of man. Man, however, proceeded from Life and Light into Soul and Mind. Life indeed bestowed the Soul, and finally, Light gave the Mind. Such were indeed

...until the end of the cycle of first principles and kinds, all the members of the world flowed forth together into the senses. But now hear the rest of the discourse you so greatly desired. Immediately, God cried out with a holy word: "Increase, grow, and multiply, all you seeds and works of mine. Moreover, you to whom a portion of the Mind has been granted, recognize your own kind and consider your immortal nature. Know that the cause of death is the love of the body. Learn thoroughly the nature of all things." When these things were said, Providence, through Fate and the harmony <note>The "harmony" refers to the celestial order of the planetary spheres that governs physical life.</note>, fashioned the mixtures and mingled the generations, whence all things were propagated according to their own kind. <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and Pymander (the Divine Mind). The previous page concluded with the warning that those who love the body remain in darkness, while those who know themselves attain the Good. Here, the Mind explains how it assists the holy and punishes the wicked, followed by a description of the soul’s ascent through the seven planetary spheres.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
->MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS<-

I, the Mind, am present to those who are good, pious, pure, religious, and holy. My presence brings them aid, so that they immediately recognize all things and find the Father to be peaceful and gracious. Therefore, they give thanks, piously offering blessings and praising Him with solemn hymns. They willingly surrender the body to its own death and despise the allurements of the senses, for they clearly judge how lethal the flatteries of the senses truly are. Moreover, the Mind itself, acting as a gatekeeper, does not permit the incoming snares of the body to achieve their end. I continually block the entrances through which foul charms are accustomed to flow, and I extinguish all the fuel of lusts.

On the other hand, I stay far away from the ignorant, the wicked, the cowardly, the envious, the unjust, the murderous, and the impious. I leave them to the judgment of the avenging demon <note>original: "dæmonis ultoris." In Hermetic thought, this is not necessarily a "devil" in the modern sense, but a corrective spiritual force or a personification of the soul's own darker impulses that lead to suffering.</note>, who, striking with the sharpness of fire, afflicts the senses and further arms the man for the commission of crimes, so that the one guilty of a fouler fault may be subject to a sharper punishment. The demon inflames him without pause toward insatiable desires, fights against him in the darkness, examines the sinner, and in a wondrous way incites and increases the force of fire for his torment.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** You have explained everything to me diligently, O Mind, just as

<column-break/>

<page-num>21</page-num>
->PYMANDER<-
<sig>B 3</sig>

I requested. But answer me this further: what will happen after the ascension <note>The "ascension" refers to the soul's journey back to the divine source after the death of the physical body.</note>?

Pymander said: First, in the dissolution of the material body, the body itself falls into alteration. The visible form you once had disappears into the unseen. The idle habits of one's character are surrendered and released to the demon. The bodily senses, having become parts of the soul, flow back into their original sources, destined to rise again one day into their own activities. The powers of anger and desire depart into irrational nature.

Thus, the remainder of the soul returns upward through the harmony <note>The "harmony" refers to the seven planetary spheres which were believed to wrap around the Earth like layers of an onion. In this journey, the soul "gives back" the negative traits it picked up from each planet during its descent into birth.</note>. 

To the **first zone**, the soul gives back the power of growing and decreasing. 
To the **second**, the machination of evils and idle deceit. 
To the **third**, the idle deception of concupiscence <note>Concupiscence: an intense longing or lust, particularly for worldly or physical pleasures.</note>. 
To the **fourth**, imperious and insatiable ambition. 
To the **fifth**, profane arrogance and the recklessness of audacity. 
To the **sixth**, evil and idle opportunities for seeking riches. 
To the **seventh** zone, the inherent lie. 

Then, finally, stripped of the effects of the harmony, the soul returns to its desired nature, possessing its own proper power. Together with those who are already there, it praises the Father. The souls then bring themselves into the number of the Powers <note>original: "poteſtatum." This refers to a high rank of spiritual beings or divine energies.</note>, and having themselves become Powers, they enjoy God.

<summary>This page describes how the Divine Mind protects the virtuous while the wicked are tormented by their own desires. It then details the "ascent of the soul," where the spirit sheds various earthly vices as it passes through seven celestial zones to return to God.</summary>

### MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

And this is the highest good <note>Latin: "summum bonum." In this philosophy, the "highest good" is not just moral excellence, but the ultimate spiritual goal.</note> for those to whom the lot of knowledge belongs: namely, to become God. Why does anything else concern you? Why not, having embraced all things, choose to be a guide for those who are worthy of your care, so that the human race may attain divine salvation through your gift? Having spoken such things, Pymander returned into the assembly of divine powers. I, however, blessing and giving thanks to the Father of all things, rose up, now strengthened by Him and taught the order of the whole of nature, having contemplated a most clear vision. I then began to proclaim the beauty of piety and knowledge to men: "O people, earth-born men, you who have surrendered yourselves to drunkenness, sleep, and ignorance, live soberly; restrain yourselves from your excess, you who have been soothed by irrational sleep." And they, listening, gathered together with one mind. Then I spoke again: "Why, O earth-born men, do you rush headlong into death, when the power to attain immortality is by no means lacking to you? Recall yourselves, you who labor in poverty, wrapped in the darkness of ignorance. Depart from the dim light. Take hold of immortality. Flee from corruption." But some of them, mocking,

<column-break/>

<page-num>23</page-num>
### PYMANDER

departed, rushing headlong on the path of death; others, however, prostrated themselves at my feet and begged that I might instruct them. Lifting them up, therefore, I became the guide of the human race. Indeed, I showed them what the method of attaining salvation was, and I poured the words of wisdom into their ears. From this it happened that they emerged from the storms of the rains <note>This is a metaphor for the chaotic and material world that "drowns" the soul.</note>. At last, as evening approached and the rays of the sun were setting, I commanded them to give thanks to God. After they had given thanks, each one retired to his own bed. I, however, inscribed the benefit of Pymander in the inner chambers of my soul, and having obtained all that I had sought, I rested in joy. For the sleep of the body had become the sobriety of the soul; the closing of the eyes, a true vision; my silence, a fertile pregnancy of goodness; and the utterance of my speech, the birth of all good things. These things happened to me by drawing from the Mind—that is, from Pymander, the Word of divine power. Whence, inspired by the divine spirit, I became a master of the truth. Therefore, with all the strengths of my soul, I give thanks to God the Father. Holy is God, the Father of all. Holy is God, whose will is [fulfilled] by

<meta>This page continues the prayer of Hermes Trismegistus following his vision of Pymander. Having received the divine mission to guide humanity, Hermes offers a hymn of praise and concludes the first book of the work. The second section begins the summary of the next discourse involving Aesculapius.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER.</header>

...is filled with his own powers. Holy is God, who becomes known to his own followers. Holy are You, who by the word established all things. Holy are You, whose image is all of nature. Holy are You, whom nature never formed. Holy are You, stronger than all power. Holy are You, greater than all excellence. Holy are You, better than all praise. Receive these holy sacrifices of words <note>Latin: "sacrificia uerborum." This refers to the "rational sacrifice" or "offering of the intellect," a central Hermetic concept where praise and knowledge replace animal sacrifice.</note> flowing from a soul and heart devoted to You. Ineffable, to be proclaimed only by silence by one who has avoided the fallacies contrary to true knowledge. Grant [this], strengthen me, and make those who dwell in ignorance participants in this grace: my brothers by kinship, but Your sons. For indeed I provide faith to You, I bear testimony of You, and I rise into life and light. You are the Father to be worshipped. But Your Man <note>The "Man" here refers to the archetypal, divine human described earlier in the vision, who shares in God's creative nature.</note> desires to share in holiness with You, since You have granted him power and judgment over all things.

<column-break/>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>SUMMARY OF THE SECOND BOOK.</header>

The second [book] discusses how God is intelligible in a rational way to man alone; and from the center, the pole, the place, and the forms of things, it seeks God Himself (around whom, in whom, and from whom all things are moved) as stable and unmoving, and always remaining the same to Himself. It teaches that nothing in the world can be empty: but that those things which seem to be empty are filled with an even more abundant fullness, emulating the highest plenitude. Soon, it suggests to Aesculapius <note>Aesculapius is a student of Hermes, named after the Greek god of healing, though here he is treated as a historical/philosophical figure.</note> through his responses that it is easier to say what God is *not* than what He *is*. Nevertheless, it asserts that God is the absolute Good and the Father, whose office is to beget all things: which, when understood as an internal generation remaining within the very nature of God, brings forth the piety of divine religion. That the name of "the Good" belongs to God alone agrees with this Gospel truth: *Why do you call me good? No one is good except God alone.* <note>original: "Quid me uocas bonū? nemo bonus niſi ſolus deus." A reference to Matthew 19:17.</note> And what it adds regarding the extreme calamity of those departing this life without children is a veiled discourse: for do not conceive of this as regarding the body, but the birth of the soul. Consider its children and sons to be the offspring of virtues, and consider wisdom as the masculine power for contemplating God and His most divine powers. It hints at this openly when it says: *Finally, the punishment is such, that the sterile soul migrates by judgment into a certain body in which neither the nature of male nor female exists.* <note>This refers to a spiritual state of fruitlessness, where the soul loses its ability to create or participate in the divine order.</note> This is as if it were openly saying: through this body, the soul might never perform good deeds, nor ever receive anything of the good. You, therefore, must flee the sterility of the soul with all your might: and beget offspring that will one day make you blessed, and in this way imitate—as far as you are able—the fruitfulness of the Father who is always begetting, through the fruitfulness of your own mind.

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: B</meta>

<meta>This page transitions from the first book’s concluding prayer to the second book’s dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Aesculapius. The discussion shifts from mystical praise to a philosophical investigation into the nature of God, the distinction between being (essence) and the Divine, and the mechanics of celestial motion.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
->### AESCVLAPIVS. II<-
<meta>The catchword "nis" at the bottom of the page is the second half of the word "stationis" (of the station/standing still) which completes the sentence on the next page.</meta>

God and divinity itself: I speak now not of that which is begotten <note>original: "genitum"</note>, but of that which is unbegotten <note>original: "ingenitum"; in Hermetic thought, God is the uncaused cause who was not created by another.</note>. If, therefore, it is divine, it is essence <term>Essence: from the Latin 'essentia', meaning the fundamental nature or 'being' of a thing.</term>. If it is God, it is even above essence. However, this is to be understood in this way: for the first God is intelligible <note>Capable of being understood by the intellect rather than the physical senses.</note> not to himself, but to us. Indeed, the intelligible falls under the understanding of the one who perceives through the senses. Therefore, God is not at all intelligible to himself; for He is not something existing as anything other than that which He understands, but understands from Himself. Yet He is something different from us, and for that reason, He is understood by us. But if the Word <note>original: "logos"; refers to the Divine Reason or ordering principle of the universe.</note> is intelligible, it is not God. But if He is as God, He is surely not as the Word, but rather as a distinguishing act.

Furthermore, everything that is moved is moved not in a moving thing, but in something stable. That which moves also remains fixed; for it is impossible for it to be agitated together with the thing it moves.

**AESCULAPIUS.** In what way then, Trismegistus, are the things in the world changed together with those things that are moved? For you were saying that the wandering spheres <note>The planets, which appear to move independently against the background of stars.</note> are moved by the fixed sphere <note>original: "aplane sphæra"; the outermost sphere of the universe containing the fixed stars, which was believed to drive the rotation of the inner spheres.</note>.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** That is not motion, O Aesculapius, but resistance: for they do not travel in the same direction, but in a contrary manner. Truly, this opposition contains a stable reverberation of motion; for the counter-strike of the standing-still <note>original: "stationis"</note>

<column-break/>

<page-num>27</page-num>
->### PYMANDER.<-

is an agitation. And therefore, because the wandering spheres run in a contrary and clearly subordinate way to one another, they are stirred by a contrary meeting around an opposition of this kind by that which remains firm. This indeed cannot be otherwise: for the Bears <note>original: "arctos"; the constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor. Because they are circumpolar, they never "set" or dip below the horizon in the northern hemisphere.</note> themselves which you know, neither ever rise nor set, but perpetually return around the same point. Do you think they move, or rather stay still?

**AESCULAPIUS.** They move, O Trismegistus.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** With what motion, Aesculapius?

**AESCULAPIUS.** With a motion that runs forever around the same point.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** But truly, that circulation is the same point, and a motion around the same point is held fast by stability; for the stable point prevents that which is above it from wandering, even as it moves around it. Thus, that which is above it, being hindered, presses around the same point. And so this contrary agitation is firm, established perpetually by the opposition itself. 

I will place an example before your eyes from the living things on earth: just as when a man swims, while the water is surely running, the counter-strike <note>original: "repercussio"</note> of his hands and feet together causes the man to stay still, lest he be carried away by the water or submerged in it.

**AESCULAPIUS.** You have brought a very clear example into our midst, O Trismegistus.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Therefore, whatever moves, moves in a state of stability and is moved by stability. The motion of a living being...

<summary>This page features a dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and Aesculapius regarding the nature of God's existence and the mechanics of celestial motion, using the analogy of a swimmer to explain how opposing forces create stability.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his pupil Aesculapius. In the preceding section, they established that everything moved must be moved in something stable. Here, they investigate the nature of that motion and the concept of the "void," leading toward a definition of the incorporeal Mind and God.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS
<meta>catchword: also</meta>

...all [motion] (which consists of matter) is by no means caused by those things which are outside the world; rather, it moves from the internal to the external, whether by the soul, or by the spirit, or by some other incorporeal thing. For a body by no means moves a living <term>Living: 'animatum', literally 'ensouled'. In this philosophy, life is defined by the presence of an internal soul that provides the impulse for movement.</term> body, nor even the whole body together, even if it exists as inanimate. 

AESCULAPIUS: For what reason do you assert this, O Trismegistus? 

TRISMEGISTUS: Wood and stones and all other things that lack a soul are not moved by bodies, O Aesculapius. For that which is inside the body, moving the living thing itself, is certainly not that body by which both are moved: both the body of that which carries and that which is carried. Hence, even that which sleeps is alive, for the primary reason that it moves <note>The author suggests that even in sleep, internal biological and spiritual motions continue, proving the soul's constant activity.</note>. Do you not see, then, that the soul is most burdened when it supports two bodies alone? Moreover, it is clear that whatever is moved in something is also moved by something that is standing still. 

AESCULAPIUS: In that [stationary thing], certainly, those things that change must change, O Trismegistus. 

TRISMEGISTUS: You speak rightly, Aesculapius; nothing in the order of things is empty <term>Empty: 'vacuum' or the 'void'. Hermetic philosophy rejects the idea of a truly empty space.</term>. Only that which is not, which is deprived of existence, should be called empty. For nothing can be found that, while it exists, is empty. 

AESCULAPIUS: Are there not also found

<column-break/>

<page-num>29</page-num>
# THE PYMANDER
<note>original: "PYMANDER"; This refers to the 'Poimandres' or 'Shepherd of Men', the title often given to this collection of revelations.</note>

also certain empty things, Trismegistus? Such as an empty jar, an empty well, and many other similar things. 

TRISMEGISTUS: Alas, how far you wander from the truth, O Aesculapius. You think those things are empty which are spacious and, of all things, the most full. 

AESCULAPIUS: How is that, O Trismegistus? 

TRISMEGISTUS: Air is indeed a body; this body penetrates all others and, rushing through them, fills everything. For a body of this kind is not composed of other corporeal things; thus it happens that whatever you call empty is actually full of air. Therefore, those things should be called concave rather than empty. For they exist, and they are full of air and spirit <note>original: "spiritus"; here referring to the 'pneuma' or vital breath that permeates the atmosphere.</note>. 

AESCULAPIUS: This reasoning is invincible and beyond doubt, Trismegistus. Air is a body; this body flows into other things and, flowing in, fills all. But what shall we say the Place is in which everything is moved? 

TRISMEGISTUS: It is incorporeal, O Aesculapius. 

AESCULAPIUS: But what is the "incorporeal"? 

TRISMEGISTUS: It is Mind <term>Mind: 'Mens', representing the Divine Intellect or 'Nous'.</term> and Reason <term>Reason: 'Ratio' or 'Logos', the principle of order.</term> embracing itself, free from all physical weight, alien to error, unable to suffer <term>Impassibilis: 'impassible', meaning a state of being unaffected by external pain, passion, or change.</term>, intangible, standing by itself, purifying and preserving all things, whose rays are the Good, the Truth, the Primal Light, and the first form of souls. 

AESCULAPIUS: What then is God? 

TRISMEGISTUS: He is none of these things, but rather...

<summary>Hermes explains to Aesculapius that there is no such thing as a "void," as all seemingly empty spaces are filled with air and spirit. He defines the space in which the universe moves as the "incorporeal," identifying it with the divine Mind and Reason.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Aesculapius, void, vacuum, air, incorporeal, Mind, Reason, soul, motion, Pymander</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his pupil Aesculapius. Having previously discussed that nothing in the universe is truly "void" or empty, Hermes now moves to define the source of all existence—God—distinguishing the Creator from his creations like Mind, Spirit, and Light.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>
<meta>catchword: God</meta>

...yet they are the cause of all things, present indeed to all, and present even to each individual; nor does He allow anything not to exist. All things are procreated from those things which already exist, for from nothing, nothing comes <note>original: "de nihilo autem nihil prouenit". This is a fundamental principle of ancient philosophy (often associated with Parmenides), asserting that matter and being cannot be created from absolute non-existence.</note>. For those things which do not exist have no nature by which they might have the power to become, but rather a nature by which they lack that power. Conversely, those things which exist do not have a nature through which they might at some point cease to exist.

<term>AESCULAPIUS:</term> What then do you say God is?

<term>TRISMEGISTUS:</term> God is certainly not Mind <note>original: "mens". In this context, Mind refers to the cosmic intellect or consciousness.</note>, but He is indeed the cause that Mind exists; nor is He Spirit, but the cause by which Spirit stands forth; nor is He Light, but the cause by which Light exists. Therefore, it is necessary to worship God with these two titles, which belong to Him alone, and fit no other being whatsoever. For among those who are called gods besides Him—whether they be spirits <note>original: "demones", referring to intermediate supernatural beings, not necessarily evil "demons" in the modern sense.</note> or men—none can be so good as the one God. For He Himself is the "Good," and nothing else besides the Good. All other things are separated from the very nature of the Good: for both the body and the soul have no place in which they can contain the Good. For His goodness is as vast as the existence of all things, both bodily and incorporeal, both those perceived by the senses and those understood by the intellect. That is the Good; that is what God

<column-break/>

<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

is. Therefore, take care that you never call anything else good, for this would be a profane error. Nor should you call God anything else except the Good alone, for you would fall into the same impiety. Thus, while the word "Good" is spoken by everyone, what it truly is, is not understood by all. For this reason, God is not known by everyone; instead, because of ignorance, they call both certain gods and even some men "good." Yet they can never be good, nor can they even become so. All the other gods are immortal and honored with the name of "god," but God is the Good, not according to an honor bestowed upon Him, but by His very nature. For the nature of God is one: namely, the Good itself. 

Both names—God and the Good—refer to one thing, from which all kinds of things flow. For the Good provides all things and receives nothing. God bestows everything and desires nothing. Therefore, God is the Good, and the Good is God. The other title for God is "Father," for the reason that He begets all things; for it is the duty of a father to generate. Wherefore, the procreation of children is considered a most excellent and pious pursuit in life among the wise. Indeed, the greatest calamity and impiety happens to the one who 

<summary>Hermes defines God as the "Good" and the "Father," explaining that God is the source of Mind, Spirit, and Light rather than being those things himself. The text emphasizes that true goodness belongs only to the Creator, who gives all and needs nothing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his pupil Aesculapius. The previous section concluded a discussion on the nature of God as the ultimate cause of Mind, Spirit, and Light. This page opens with a warning about the spiritual consequences of failing to produce children, followed by a summary and the beginning of a new sacred discourse on the origins of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>
<meta>catchword: DISCOURSE</meta>

...dies from life without children. For this reason, he pays penalties to the spirits <note>original: "dæmonibus." In Hermetic thought, these are not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediate supernatural beings or guardians of the cosmic order.</note> after death. The punishment, finally, is of this sort: that the sterile soul is judged to migrate into a certain body that possesses the nature of neither male nor female. This body is indeed one that has been raised up and joined together by the sun. Therefore, O Aesculapius, have no dealings with a man who has begotten no children. Nevertheless, pity his misfortune, since you know what penalty awaits him after his passing. Let these and such things be said to you, O Aesculapius, from which a certain foreknowledge of the nature of all things is gathered.

### SUMMARY OF THE THIRD DISCOURSE.

*The third discourse recalls again how all things proceeded to their birth in their proper order from the shapeless womb of matter—which was illuminated by forms as if by holy splendors—and how the blessed minds of the heavens fulfill the work entrusted to them by the highest father. It also explains that man was born for a twin duty: specifically, for the contemplation of eternal things, and for the judgment of good and evil, and the management of human affairs through various inventions of the arts as well as through prudence and moderation. To this man, the material for contemplation and for lifting himself up to the divine is most abundantly supplied by the heavens, the earth, herbs, sprouts, and the entire harmony <note>original: "conspiratio," meaning a "breathing together" or cosmic sympathy between all parts of the universe.</note> of the world and the most beautiful order of things; for nature is like a single book full of divinity, and a mirror of divine things.*

<column-break/>

<page-num>33</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER <note>The "Pymander" or "Poimandres" is the name of the divine intellect that provides revelations to Hermes.</note></header>
<sig>C</sig>

### SACRED DISCOURSE III

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap 'G' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or deity, amidst foliage.</image-desc>

THE GLORY of all things is God, the divine, and divine nature. The principle of the universe is God: mind, nature, matter, and wisdom for the demonstration of all things; the principle, divinity, action, necessity, the end, and renewal. For there was an infinite shadow in the abyss; water, moreover, and a thin intellectual spirit existed within the chaos through divine power. Then a holy splendor flourished, which led forth the elements from the moist nature under the sand, and all the gods <note>Likely referring to the planetary governors or celestial powers.</note> chose a seminal <note>original: "feminalem." While the text reads "feminine," this is likely a typesetter's error for "seminalem" (seed-bearing), referring to the generative powers of nature.</note> nature. When all things had previously been indistinct and unarranged, the light elements soon flew forth into the high region, while the heavy things settled under the moist sand. When the elements were distinguished and balanced, being carried by a fiery spirit, the heaven shone forth in seven circles. The gods were seen in the forms of the stars with all their signs. The stars were numbered according to the gods who inhabit them. The wider circuit conspired in its course with the airy circle, carried by the divine spirit. Each of the gods fulfilled the work assigned to him by his internal power. Thus, living things were born: four-footed beasts, reptiles, aquatic creatures, and flying things all at once.

<meta>This page continues the Third Discourse, elaborating on the purpose of human life and the cyclical renewal of the natural world. It then transitions into the Fourth Discourse, which introduces the "Crater" or mixing bowl of wisdom and the concept of the Monad (the One).</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>
<meta>catchword: ARGUMENT</meta>

Likewise, every sowing that sprouts through seeds—the grass, the herbs, and the buds of flowers—contained within itself the seed of regeneration. The generations of men were created for the knowledge of divine works and to serve as a testimony to nature; they were made to rule over all things covered by the heavens, to discern what is good, to increase their kind, and to propagate their numbers. And every soul, veiled by the shadow of the flesh, was made to look up at the movement of the celestial gods, to the works of God and the progress of nature, to the signs of good things, and to the knowledge of divine power. A certain portion of the soul was granted for the judgment of good and evil, and for discovering the various crafts of good works. Nevertheless, the soul begins to live in these things and to obtain wisdom according to the portion or the lot of the courses of the circling gods <note>original: "deorum circumeuntium," referring to the planets and stars whose movements were believed to influence human fate and wisdom</note>. Furthermore, it is dissolved back into that in which great moments stood out, and the reminders of craftsmanship left upon the earth are lost in the innovation of times, and in the decay and generation of the living flesh. But when the seeds of the fruits and the works of nature and art have wasted away, old things will finally grow young again through the necessity and the renewal of the gods, and by the course of the cycle of manifold nature. For the very harmony of the world <note>original: "conspiratio mundi," suggesting a breathing together or universal sympathy of all parts of the cosmos</note> is divine, with nature itself blooming anew.

---

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER.</header>
<sig>C 2</sig>

For nature itself also exists within divinity.

### ARGUMENT OF THE FOURTH DISCOURSE.

*The fourth discourse exhorts us to take hold of wisdom, which is the wide bowl <note>original: "crater." In this context, it refers to a symbolic mixing vessel where the soul is "dipped" or purified to receive the Divine Mind.</note> of the divine mind. Whosoever's soul immerses itself in this bowl becomes a participant in knowledge and gazes upon the Mind. But truly, that the soul may lift itself up to it, it is necessary for it to transcend the heavens, to overcome the circuit of the stars and their progress, and to leave the choirs of the blessed far below. Then it tends toward the one and only God—the insuperable and boundless infinite Good. When the soul beholds this Good, it despises all physical bodies and considers this life (which we consume here) to be misery, being intent only on the desire for the incorporeal Good. Yet no one is able to immerse themselves in the divine bowl who does not hate their own body, and who has not loved God himself rather than themselves; and who, neglecting mortal things, has clung to divine things alone, is present for them alone, meditates on them alone, and burns for them alone. Soon, by the analogy of unity and numbers, it lifts us up to contemplate the true Monad <note>original: "monadem," meaning the "Unit" or "The One," the fundamental source of all numbers and existence</note> and the true numbers of nature.*

### THE MIXING BOWL, OR THE MONAD. IV.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'V' depicting a figure kneeling or sitting amidst foliage, possibly a classical or biblical scene.</image-desc>The Creator <note>original: "opifex," the craftsman or demiurge of the universe</note> fashioned the entire world by the Word <note>original: "verbo," equivalent to the Greek "Logos," the divine reason or creative speech</note>, not with hands. Think of Him in this way: as always present, doing all things, the one God, establishing all things by His will. For that is His body: not tangible, not visible, not measured, not distant, nor like any other thing. For He is neither fire, nor water, nor

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the purpose of humanity as observers and rulers of nature before introducing the "Crater" of wisdom—a spiritual vessel in which the soul must be immersed to achieve divine knowledge.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Discourse of Mercury Trismegistus, titled "The Mixing Bowl or the Monad." <page-num>36</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

air, nor even spirit, but rather all these things depend upon him. The Good is such that it belongs to him alone. He also wished to adorn the earth with a certain decoration of his divine body. Therefore, he sent down man, a mortal animal derived from an immortal living being. And indeed, the world is filled with the living creatures of a living world through intellect and reason. For man was made a contemplator of the divine work; and truly, while he wondered at it, he recognized its author. 

Indeed, O Tat, God imparted Speech <note>original: "Sermonem," referring to the Greek "Logos," meaning both speech and the outward expression of reason</note> to every individual human, but Mind <note>original: "mentem," referring to "Nous," the higher intuitive intellect or spiritual consciousness</note> not at all—not because he was envious, for envy certainly does not come from him. Malice does not proceed from God, but rather it dwells here below with the souls of men who lack Mind.

**TAT:** Why, O father, did God not communicate Mind to all?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Because, O son, he wished to set it in the midst as a contest and a prize for souls.

**TAT:** Where has he placed it?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** When he had filled a wide mixing bowl <note>original: "craterem," a large vessel used in antiquity for mixing wine and water; here a metaphor for a font of spiritual purification</note> with it, he sent a herald, commanding him to announce such things to the souls of men: "Let whatever soul that is able dip herself into this bowl—that soul which believes that the bowl will lead her back to him who sent her down, and which knows for what purpose she was born." Whoever, therefore, heard the proclamation

<column-break/>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>THE PYMANDER</header>

and immersed themselves in Mind, they became partakers of knowledge <note>original: "cognitionis," implying "Gnosis" or deep spiritual insight</note>, and looking up toward Mind, they emerged as perfect men. But those who neglected the proclamation remained partakers of speech alone, yet devoid of Mind, ignorant of both for what reason and by whom they were begotten. 

Furthermore, the senses of these men are like those of irrational creatures; entangled in anger and lust, they in no way admire the things worthy of sight. For, enslaved to the lusts of the body, they believe man was born for the sake of these things. 

However many have been supported by God’s gift, O Tat, are considered immortal instead of mortal by the comparison of their works, encompassing with their intelligence everything on earth and in the sea, and whatever else exists above the heavens. And they raise themselves up so far that they behold the Good itself. Truly, when they perceive this, they consider the life we live here to be a kind of misery. Despising all things both corporeal and incorporeal, they are carried toward the One alone. 

This, O Tat, is the science of the Mind: the contemplation of divine things and the knowledge of God, the bowl itself being divine.

**TAT:** Indeed, O father, I desire to be washed in the liquid of this bowl.

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Unless first...

<meta>This translation continues the Fourth Discourse of the Hermetic corpus, titled "The Mixing Bowl or the Monad." On the previous page, Hermes Trismegistus described a symbolic mixing bowl (Crater) sent by God, filled with Mind (Nous), which souls must "dip" into to achieve spiritual consciousness. The current text picks up with the consequences of this spiritual choice and the necessity of rejecting the material body to achieve divine knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>

O son, unless you hate your body, you will not be able to love yourself: but as soon as you love yourself, you will immediately attain the Mind; and having finally obtained this, you will straightway lay hold of knowledge. <term>TAT.</term> In what way do you assert these things, O father? <term>TRISMEGISTUS.</term> It is impossible, O son, to attend to both at once—namely, to things mortal and things divine. For since there are only two things found in the order of reality, the body and the incorporeal—and the one is called mortal, while the other is called divine—by the election of one, we lose the other. For as often as the care for one is relaxed, the activity of the other is intensified. Therefore, the choice of the better part is honorable to the one choosing; it not only renders the man who chooses it a god, but also demonstrates piety toward God to all others. However, the choice of the inferior part destroys the man himself, and although it commits no offense against God, it errs in this one respect: just as processions <note>original: "pompæ," referring to a ceremonial or theatrical parade</note> pass through the midst, being themselves incapable of any action but hindering others, in no other way do these people wander and stray like mere processions for the sake of bodily pleasures. Since these things are so, O Tat, divine duties ought to come first, and human things should follow. God is certainly beyond blame; the cause of evils comes from us, for we prefer evil things to good ones. You see

<meta>catchword: see (from the Latin "cernis")</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>THE PYMANDER</header>

O son, that it is necessary for us to transcend celestial bodies, and the choirs of spirits <note>original: "dæmonum." In the Hermetic sense, these are not necessarily "demons" in the modern evil sense, but intermediary spirits or guardian entities that inhabit the spheres.</note>, and the circuit of the stars, and their motions, so that we may reach toward the one and only God. For He is the unsurpassable Good, infinite without end, never beginning as far as He is concerned, but having a beginning for human knowledge. Such knowledge is not His own beginning, but it provides for us the beginning of knowing Him. Therefore, let us embrace this beginning; for when this is known, we shall run through all things very swiftly. It is a difficult thing, however, for one who is leaving behind familiar and present things to turn oneself toward the higher and better things. For those things which we perceive with our eyes delight us exceedingly, while hidden things produce distrust. Certainly, manifest things are often bad; the Good is hidden from those who dwell upon things that are visible. For He has no form, nor any figure: for this reason, He is similar only to Himself, but dissimilar to all other things. Indeed, the incorporeal cannot appear to the body; such is the difference between the similar and the dissimilar, and the inferiority of the dissimilar to that which is similar. The <term>Monad</term> <note>original: "Monas," meaning Unity or the indivisible One</note>, that is, Unity, is the principle, root, and origin of all things; without a principle, there is nothing. A beginning, however, depends on nothing except itself. A beginning is not the start of a principle, but of another thing. The Monad, therefore, as the principle, contains every num-

it contains the pure number, contained by no one: and it begets every number, though it is by no means begotten. Whatever is begotten is certainly imperfect, divisible, subject to growth and decay. But to that which is perfect, none of these things happen. Indeed, that which grows increases by the power of the Monad <note>original: "monadis," referring to the mathematical and philosophical concept of "The One" or the source of all unity</note>. However, it vanishes through its own weakness when it can no longer contain the Monad. Let this image of God be inscribed for you, O Tat, according to your strength; if you consider it diligently and understand it with your internal eyes, believe me, my son, you will find the ascent to the heights. Moreover, the image itself will lead you. For the vision has a certain power: it seizes those who burn with the desire to behold it, and draws them in the same way that the stone called the magnet <note>original: "magnetes"</note> draws iron.

### ARGUMENT OF THE FIFTH.

*The Fifth teaches that God is equally hidden and yet shines through every single particle of the world: that all things which present themselves to us are His images, so that the marvelous and beautiful order of things clearly declares the Ordainer. Individual things, circumscribed by their own fixed limits, declare their Lord and Author, whom the Sun—the king and prince of the stars—obeys and serves, whom he fears and reveres; and in summary, the entire structure of the world declares its Architect. Soon, he offers a more confident judgment regarding the divine internal generation: that it is the essence of God to conceive*

<column-break/>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER.</header>
<sig>C 5</sig>

*to conceive and to make all things: so that it is impossible for God to exist unless He always acts upon all things. For the Divine Word <note>original: "logos"</note> is all things in such a way that it is no thing of all, in which the corporeal and incorporeal, being and nothingness, harmonize and coincide. Then Mercury ends and terminates the dialogue with songs of divine praise.*

### THE FIFTH DIALOGUE.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'H' featuring a seated figure in a landscape with foliage.</image-desc>

I will conduct this discourse with you as well, O Tat, lest the principal name of God be lacking to you, and lest you be ignorant of that which seems hidden to most, yet is manifest. For if He were nowhere revealed, He would be nothing. Whatever offers itself to the sight is begotten: but that which is hidden is eternal. Nor is there a need for Him to appear, since indeed He never ceases to be. Indeed, He places all other things before the eyes: but He Himself remains secret, as one who enjoys eternal life. He clearly leads all things out into the light, while hiding in the innermost sanctuaries <note>original: "adytis," the most sacred, private part of a temple, here used metaphorically</note>, yet His imagination <note>original: "fantasia," referring here to the power of God to manifest Himself in mental or physical forms</note> expresses each thing. For imagination is only concerned with those things which are begotten, in which nothing exists besides generation. But that one thing is unbegotten, and incomprehensible to imagination. Yet since all things become clear through Him, He shines forth again through all and in all, and He appears especially to those to whom [He grants] knowledge.

<summary>Mercury concludes the Fourth Discourse by comparing God's attraction to a magnet and begins the Fifth Discourse, explaining that God is both hidden in His essence and manifest through the visible order of the universe.</summary>
