<meta>This page continues the opening of the Fifth Discourse, following the introductory argument which stated that it is the essence of God to conceive and bring forth all things. Here, Hermes begins to instruct his son Tat on how to perceive the invisible God through His visible creation.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

wished to communicate of Himself. You, therefore, O my son Tat, first of all, beseech the Lord and Father—the unique one, the one, and He from whom the "one" comes—with pious prayers, so that you may be worthy of His mercy. Only then will you be able to perceive so great a God, if even a single ray of His intelligence shines kindly upon your understanding. For understanding alone, being hidden, perceives things that are hidden. Therefore, if you look with the eyes of the mind, O Tat, He will be revealed to you (believe me). God, indeed, being entirely free from envy <note>original: "expers inuidiæ." This reflects the Platonic idea that a good Creator has no jealousy and therefore desires all things to be like Himself.</note>, shines everywhere through every single particle of the world, and He presents Himself as so well-known that you may not only understand Him, but even, so to speak, touch Him with your own hands. For His image is observed everywhere by our eyes; it presents itself and impresses itself upon us. But if your own inner light is hidden from you, how will you find either yourself or Him? Finally, when you wish to see God, look up at the Sun, O my son Tat; look up at the course of the moon; look up at the order of the remaining stars. Come now, who preserves the perpetual order of these things? Indeed, every order is bounded by the limits of number and place. The Sun is the most excellent of the celestial gods. The remaining heavenly beings <note>original: "coelites," referring to the stars or planetary deities</note> obey the Sun alone, as if he were their prince and king. The Sun, so great—larger than the earth and sea together—nevertheless allows the innumerable stars smaller than himself to revolve above him. Whom

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

does he fear? Whom does he revere? O son, the movements of the various stars are diverse. Who assigns the measure of movement to each?

<term>TAT</term> Arcturus <note>Here used to represent the circumpolar stars or the celestial axis that appears to turn the sky.</note>, forever turning around the same point, and pulling the whole <term>machinery of the world</term> <note>original: "mundi machinam," a common metaphor for the interconnected, functional structure of the universe.</note> with it.

<term>TRISMEGISTUS</term> Who uses this instrument? Who circumscribes the sea within its boundaries? Who fixes the weight of the earth and balances it in the center? There is certainly someone, O Tat, who is the author and lord of these things. For it is impossible to preserve place, or number, or measure without the power of an author. Order cannot arise from deformity. Moreover, deformity lacks a lord who might provide it with order. If only the power were given to you, O son, that you might fly with the aid of wings into the lofty region of the air, and having gained a place midway between heaven and earth, you might behold the solidity of the earth, the spreading of the sea, the flow of the rivers, the vastness of the air, the driven speed of the fire, the courses of the stars, and the rapid velocity of the heavens. O most happy spectacle, my son; O most blessed vision! For with one movement of the lights you would comprehend the sequence of the whole world; you would see the immobile maker set in motion, and the hidden one made clearly visible. But if you also wish to investigate God the craftsman through those fragile things which are sustained by the earth, and those which are buried in the depths...

<summary>Hermes explains to Tat that God, though invisible, is visible through the perfect order of the cosmos, such as the movements of the sun and stars. He argues that such harmony requires a divine Author and describes a mystical vision of the universe's unity.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Creator, cosmic order, Sun, Arcturus, world-machine, divine vision, providence</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section where Hermes encourages Tat to see the invisible Creator through His visible works. Having discussed the celestial bodies (the Sun and stars), he now moves to the most intimate evidence of design: the human body.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

Come now, my son, examine the craftsmanship <term>opificium: the act of making or the work produced by a craftsman</term> of the human body. From this observation, learn thoroughly who is the founder of such a beautiful image: who is the painter of the eyes? Who carved out the nostrils and ears? Who stretched the lips of the mouth? Who extended and bound the nerves? Who watered the veins? Who heaped up the solid bones? Who wrapped the flesh in a thin skin? Who separated the fingers and the joints? Who extended the foundations of the feet? Who pierced the pores and opened the passages? Who compacted and compressed the spleen? Who impressed the shape of a pyramid <note>Ancient anatomists often described the heart as having a pyramidal or conical shape.</note> upon the heart? Who stretched out the fibers of the liver? Who sculpted the pipes of the lungs? Who gave the belly its spacious capacity? Who fashioned the noble members of the body in plain sight, and who altogether hid the private parts in darkness, wishing them to lie secret from the sight of onlookers? See how many works of divine art are demonstrated in a single material, each one beautiful and rightly measured, yet differing from each other by their own specific functions. Who indeed fashioned these individual things? What kind of mother, what kind of father? Is it not that invisible God alone who has constructed <note>original: "molitus est," suggesting great effort or architectural labor</note> all things by His own will? For since no one would dare to assert that a statue or an image is made without a craftsman or a painter, will we think this wonderful construction

<column-break/>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

has stood without a founder? O blind little man! O too impious! O one overwhelmed by the deep shadows of ignorance! Take care, take care, O my son Tat, that you never deprive the work <note>original: "artificium"</note> of its craftsman <note>original: "artifice"</note>. Rather, call God by His fitting name, "Father," and consider it His own specific work to be a Father. But if you compel me to say something bolder, I will say that His essence <term>essentia: the fundamental nature or "being" of a thing</term> is this: to conceive and to make all things. Truly, just as nothing can be made without a maker, so it is impossible to think of God unless He is always doing all things—namely in heaven, the air, the earth, the sea, in the whole world, and in every single particle of the whole world, both in that which exists and also in that which does not yet exist. For there is nothing in all nature that He Himself is not. He is indeed those things which are, and even those things which are not at all. The things that are, He has brought into the light; the things that are not, He keeps hidden within Himself. This God is better than any name; He is hidden, yet again He is the most manifest of all. He is visible to the mind, and present to the eyes. He is bodiless, and yet (if I may say so) many-bodied <note>original: "multicorporeus." This suggests that while God has no single physical form, all physical bodies are expressions of His being.</note>. For there is nothing in bodies which He is not, for He alone exists as all things. Moreover, He has all names, because He is the one Father; and yet He has no name...

<summary>Hermes instructs Tat to recognize the divine "Craftsman" by contemplating the intricate design of the human body and its organs. He concludes that God is the "Father" whose very essence is to create and who exists within all things, both visible and invisible.</summary>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

<note>original: "consilium habet"</note> ...possesses His own counsel, since He is the father of all. What, then, shall praise You? Something placed above You or below You? To where shall I turn my eyes to praise You? Upward or downward? Inward or outward? There is no limit, no place around You, nor is there any other thing among all that exists. In You are all things; from You are all things. You provide everything, yet You receive nothing; indeed, You possess all things, and that which You do not have is itself nothing.

When, indeed, shall I praise You, Father? For we are unable to grasp Your time or Your moment. In what matter especially shall I sing Your praises? In those things You have created, or rather in those things You have not? Perhaps in those things You have brought into the light from the darkness, or in those which still lie hidden, buried in Your secret depths? By what means, finally, shall I sing hymns to Your divine power? In such praise, shall I remain my own master, or rather be transformed into another?

Truly, You are whatever I may be; You are whatever I may do; You are whatever I may say. For You are everything, and there is nothing besides You. Even that which is not, You are it; You are all that has been born, and all that has never been born. You are the understanding Mind <term>Mens: the higher intellect or divine consciousness</term>, the crafting Father, the God who accomplishes good, making all things good. For of Matter, the purest form is air; of air, the soul; of the soul, the mind; and of the mind, finally, God.

---

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>SUMMARY OF THE SIXTH BOOK</header>

*The sixth book contends this: that Goodness, and the nature of the Good (that is, goodness itself), is found in God alone; likewise the nature of beauty, power, and wisdom. In the world, however, there is only the name of "good." The text rebukes men blinded by error, warns them to abhor vice, and teaches the path that leads us to goodness and beauty itself: namely, piety joined with knowledge <term>cognitioni: often implying 'Gnosis' or spiritual insight</term>. Ignorant mortals, wandering from the track of piety, can never reach this path.*

### T H E  S I X T H  B O O K

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'B' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or deity, within a rectangular frame with floral motifs.</image-desc>

GOODNESS, O Aesculapius <note>Aesculapius is the Latin name for Asclepius, the Greek god of medicine. In the Hermetic tradition, he is a disciple of Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, exists in nothing at all except in the one God; rather, God Himself is always the Good itself. For this reason, it is necessary that God be the essence of all motion and knowledge. Nothing deprived of this essence exists; this essence possesses within itself a stable action, needing nothing, infinite, overflowing, and bestowing. This is the single principle of the universe, bestowing and pouring out the Good. Whenever I name "the Good," understand that which is all good things, and which is always good. This belongs to the one God, for He lacks nothing, so that He might become evil by desiring to acquire something; nor does any loss ever fall upon Him, so that He might be afflicted with grief after a loss. For He is greater than any portion...

<summary>This page concludes a hymn of praise to the omnipresent God as the source of all existence and introduces the sixth discourse, which argues that true Goodness exists only in the divine and not in the material world.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Aesculapius, Divine Goodness, Pymander, Neoplatonism, Piety, Gnosis, Creation</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced the sixth book of the Pymander, which argues that Goodness exists in God alone and not in the world. This page begins mid-argument, discussing why the divine essence is free from the disturbances of the material realm.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS</header>

...of evil: nor is there anything stronger than Him by which He might be conquered. No injury can fall upon Him, by which He might be provoked, harassed, or inflamed to anger. Nothing escapes His yoke, such that He might feel indignant or angry at being despised. Nor is anything wiser than Him, which might strike Him with a sense of rivalry. Therefore, since these things do not happen to Him, nothing remains of His nature except Good itself. 

Just as none of those things that are evil exist in this essence, so also He finds the Good in nothing else. For in all other individual things, the various traits of the world are present—in small things and in large things, and even in those which follow the "One" <note>original: "secundum unum"; likely referring to the Monad or the primary principles of unity</note>, and in that greatest and most powerful living being of all <note>referring to the Cosmos itself, viewed as a sentient organism</note>, which He certainly fills with things born of passions. 

For generation <term>generatio: the process of being born, created, or coming into existence</term> is itself a kind of passion <term>passio: in this philosophical sense, "passion" means being acted upon by external forces or undergoing change, which the ancients associated with suffering and instability</term>. Where there is passion, there is in no way the Good; and where the Good is, there is no passion at all. Where there is day, there is no night; where there is night, there is no day. 

Wherefore, the Good cannot exist in the process of being born; it remains, therefore, only in that which is unbegotten <note>original: "ingenitum"; that which has no beginning and is eternal</note>. But just as the participation in all things is granted to matter, so also the world is "good" by its participation in the Good. I say "good" only insofar as the world itself also creates all things; in that specific part, the world is good, but in all other respects it is not good. For it is capable of suffering, it is subject to movement,

<column-break/>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

and it is, moreover, the cause of all passions. Furthermore, in man, "good" is measured only by comparison to evil; that is, we call "good" among men that which is not excessively evil, or that which is the lesser evil. Thus it happens that our "good" is nothing other than the smallest portion of evil. 

From this it follows that a "good" of this kind cannot be separated from evil. For this human good is tainted by the admixture of evils. Once it is infected, it no longer remains good; and if it does not remain good, it becomes evil. Therefore, the Good is in God alone—or rather, God Himself exists as Goodness itself. 

Therefore, O Aesculapius, only the *name* of the Good exists among men, but the *nature* of the Good does not exist at all. For the material body cannot grasp it, being worn out on all sides and oppressed by depravity, labors, pains, desires, anger, deceptions, and foolish opinions and trifles. Yet consider this the worst of all, Aesculapius: that each of these things we have mentioned is actually believed by men to be the highest good. 

Evil must be fled above all—especially the luxury of the belly, which is the fuel of all evils; from this comes error, and from this, the deprivation of the Good. Indeed, I give great thanks to God, who, as I was thinking about the nature of the Good, [sent] the thought...

<summary>This passage argues that true Goodness is an exclusive attribute of God because it requires total stability and freedom from "passion" (change or suffering). It concludes that what humans call "good" is merely a lesser form of evil, as the material world is inherently tainted by corruption and physical needs.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Aesculapius, The Good, Evil, Passion, Generation, Material Body, God, Cosmos</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Book VI, emphasizing that the "Good" exists only in God and cannot be found in the material world. It concludes Book VI and provides a summary (Argumentum) for Book VII.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
# OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS
<meta>catchword: others (from "caeteris")</meta>

He infused this certain truth: that Good cannot exist in the world. For the world is a heap <note>original: "congeries"</note> of evils; but God is the Good, or rather, the Good is the overflowing fullness of God. Indeed, the excellences of good things, shining purely and most clearly around His essence, are perhaps the very essence of God. We must dare to assert, O Aesculapius <note>Aesculapius is the student or interlocutor to whom Hermes is speaking</note>, that the essence of God—if indeed God has any essence at all <note>The author suggests that God might transcend the human category of "essence" or "being"</note>—is the Good itself. 

But Beauty and the Good are not to be found in the parts of the world. For whatever things stir the senses are but idols <term>idola: in this context, phantoms or mere reflections that lack true substance</term> and certain vain shadows. But those things which escape the organs of the senses belong to Beauty and the Good; and just as the sharp sight of the eye cannot see God, so too it cannot recognize Beauty and the Good. For these are the most perfect parts of God, unique to Him, related, inseparable, and especially beloved; and just as God Himself loves these things, so they in turn love God. 

If you are able to perceive God, you will also perceive Beauty and the Good, shining for everyone and illuminated by God. For Beauty is a Good without comparison and without change. God admits no comparison and no change. Just as you have come to know God as Beauty and the Good, so indeed to all other...

---

<page-num>51</page-num>
# PYMANDER
D 2
<meta>catchword: move (from "motus")</meta>

...living beings, since they cannot be separated from God, these things are never shared. If you seek God, you are seeking Beauty itself. There is but one path that leads to it: piety joined with knowledge <term>cognitio: often used in these texts to signify *gnosis*, a direct, experiential knowledge of divine truth</term>. 

Therefore, ignorant mortals, straying from the path of piety, are not afraid to call even a man "good," though he can have no true knowledge of the Good. Instead, he is entangled and snared in evils; he judges evil to be good and uses evils in an incurable way, fearing their loss or removal. Finally, he strives with every device and power, not so that he might be free from a single evil, but rather so that his evils might increase in number and magnitude. 

Such, O Aesculapius, are the "honors" and "goods" of men, which we can neither flee nor hate; for that is the most difficult thing of all, mainly because we must use them and be sustained by them, and once deprived of them, we can in no way carry on our lives.

->THE ARGUMENT OF THE SEVENTH BOOK<-

The seventh book laments the plague of ignorance, which overturns the whole earth and corrupts the soul while it is shut within the chains of the body. It exhorts the ignorant to come to their senses and commands us to cast off the body, that mortal garment, from which...

<page-num>52</page-num>
# THE WORKS OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS

...we are snatched away into ignorance and death; and to recall us into the inner sanctuary <term>adytum: the innermost, most sacred chamber of a temple, here used metaphorically for the deepest part of the soul</term> of the mind, which alone perceives Him who is neither perceived by ears, nor seen by eyes, nor expressed by speech.

### THE SEVENTH BOOK

Where are you rushing, drunken mortals, you who have drunk deep of the unmixed wine of ignorance? Since you cannot bear it, vomit it out; live soberly, and look upward with the eyes of the mind. And if you cannot all do so, let at least those who are able do it. The plague of ignorance subverts the whole earth and corrupts the soul shut within the bonds of the body, nor does it allow her to take the path of salvation. Do not allow yourselves to be submerged in the lake of corruption and death; breathe now, return to your senses, run back to the fountain of life, and take hold of Him who will lead you into the inner sanctuary of truth. There is the shining light, mixed with no darkness. There no one wanders in a drunken delirium, but all are sober and watchful, and with the eyes of the mind they gaze more sharply upon Him who wishes to be seen. He is neither perceived by ears, nor seen by eyes, nor expressed by speech. The mind alone sees Him, the mind alone proclaims Him. But first, you must strip off the garment you wear—the cloak of ignorance, the foundation of wickedness, the bond of corruption,

<meta>catchword: the (from "lum," the end of "vinculum")</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>53</page-num>
# PYMANDER

the dark veil, the living death, the sensitive corpse, the walking tomb <note>original: "sepulchrum circunuertile," literally a "turning" or "mobile" grave</note>, and finally the domestic thief, who hates even while flattering, and envies while hating. Such is the hostile shadow with which you are covered. It drags you down to itself, lest perhaps by beholding the beauty of truth and the nearby Good, you might hate its wickedness, or sense its snares which it constantly fashions against you. This dulls and blunts the edge of the inner senses; thick matter chokes it, and intoxicates it with abominable and loathsome pleasure, so that you may never hear or see those things which ought rightly to be heard and, above all, to be gazed upon.

### ARGUMENT OF THE EIGHTH BOOK

*The Eighth [chapter] teaches first that the celestial bodies constantly maintain one and the same course, which they first received from their father. And that death and destruction first appear concerning earthly living bodies, but the name of death and the destruction of bodies is almost void: for the bodies themselves are changed, they do not perish; rather, immortal matter transforms the qualities themselves—the images and reflections of the divine ideas <note>original: "idearum idola simulacraq;"</note> (with which God wished to adorn matter). And man is an immortal animal, yet dissoluble, the bond and knot of the whole of nature, partly*...

...partly corporeal and partly incorporeal, and capable of the higher mind, he was born in the likeness of the world, so that with a sensible body <term>sensible: that which can be perceived by the physical senses</term> he might comprehend the sensible world, and with the mind he might rise to the best mind in happy contemplation.

### THE EIGHTH BOOK

**OF THE SOUL** now and the body, O son, we must speak: in what way the soul is immortal, and how great its power of action is in the joining and dissolving of the body. Death pertains to none of these. For "death" <note>original Greek: θάνατος (thanatos)</note> is a concept born of a mortal name; it is either a hollow thing, or, by the removal of the first letter, it becomes "immortal" <note>original: ἀθάνατος (athanatos). The author is using a wordplay to suggest that what we call death—*thanatos*—is actually a step toward the immortal—*athanatos*.</note>—that is, death exists in place of what is called *athanatos*, which means immortal. For death signifies destruction. But nothing of those things that are in the world perishes. For if the world is the "second god," and is moreover an immortal living being, it is impossible for any part of an immortal living being to perish. Whatever things are in the world are members of the world, especially man, who is a rational animal. First of all is the truly eternal, immortal, and unbegotten God, the author of all. Second, thereafter, created in His image, is the world, begotten by Him and by Him preserved, nourished, and gifted with immortality as if by its own father,

<meta>catchword: father (from "tre," the end of "patre")</meta>

---

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

father, living indeed always and immortal. For "ever-living" and "eternal" <note>original: semper vivens & sempiternum</note> differ from each other in this: that the "eternal" indeed was not made by another. But if it was made by itself, it was not made at some point in time, but is made always. For the eternal, insofar as it is eternal, is the whole. But that Father is eternal of Himself. However, the world was made "ever-living" and immortal by the Father. And as much of matter as was subject to the Father, the Father himself—acting upon the corporeal and gathering it into a mass—rendered it entirely spherical. He impressed quality upon this existing immortal matter, which possesses an eternal reason <note>original: rationem; here meaning a structural principle or logic</note> of matter. Full of all "ideas" <note>In the Platonic sense: the perfect forms or blueprints of things</note>, the Father introduced these qualities and hedged the world around with every quality in a sphere, as if in a circle. He wished to adorn that which comes after Him, supporting every body with immortality, lest matter—should it wish to depart from this union—be resolved again into its own deformity. For when matter was incorporeal, O son, it was deformed. This matter also, revolved around certain small qualities, possesses a nature of growing and likewise of decreasing, which men call "death." Such confusion exists among earthly living things.

<summary>This section argues that death is a misnomer for the transformation of matter, establishing a hierarchy where God is the eternal First Cause and the World is a "second god" endowed with immortality. It explains that matter was originally formless and "deformed" until the Father shaped it into a perfect sphere and filled it with the "ideas" or qualities that sustain life.</summary>

<keywords>immortality of the soul, cosmology, second god, etymology of death, matter and form, Pymander, Hermes Trismegistus, eternity vs ever-living</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of "death" as mere dissolution and transition rather than total destruction. It concludes the Eighth Book and provides the summary and opening of the Ninth Book, which shifts focus to how human behavior is influenced by either divine or demonic "seeds."</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS</header>

Indeed, the celestial bodies preserve one and the same order, which they surely received at the beginning from their father. This order is preserved by the indissoluble restoration of each. However, the restoration of the constitution of earthly bodies—the very dissolution itself—restores them into indissoluble bodies, that is, into immortal ones. And so, let it be understood that there is a deprivation of sense, but not a destruction of bodies.

Man, moreover, is the third living being <note>original: animal. In this context, it refers to any "animated" or "souled" creature, including humans and the world itself, rather than just beasts.</note>, born in the image of the world. Living according to the will of the father, he possesses not only a kinship with the "second god" <note>Hermes refers to the physical Universe or Cosmos as the "second god."</note> beyond all other earthly living things, but he also possesses an understanding of the First God. Clearly, he comprehends the second god through corporeal sense. But toward the First God, as towards an incorporeal and good Mind, he rises by means of his own mind.

**TAT:** Is that living being <note>man</note> not then destroyed?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Speak better, speak better, O son! <note>original: melius melius ominare. This is a formulaic way of saying "God forbid" or "may the omens be better," used when someone speaks of something ill-fated.</note> And furthermore, meditate on what God is, what the world is, what an immortal living being is, and what a dissoluble living being is. Know, therefore, that the world exists from God and within God; but Man consists from the world and within the world. God is the beginning, the embrace, and the constitution of all things.

### THE ARGUMENT OF THE NINTH BOOK

->The Ninth Book instructs men: although all use intellect, those who are material-minded conceive from the seeds of demons (whose presence<-

<column-break/>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>THE PYMANDER</header>
<sig>D 5</sig>

no part of the world is without) and at last, being pregnant, they give birth to adulteries, debaucheries, murders, parricides, and every kind of outrage. But essential and spiritual men conceive every ornament of virtue with God as the sower, and they bring forth every good duty of virtue. For the seeds of God are few, yet they are indeed great, beautiful, and good, and far superior to the innumerable multitude of vices. 

However, those who follow these seeds neither please the common crowd, nor does the crowd please them. Indeed, they are thought to be insane and meet with laughter, because the earth is the seat and province of wickedness. Yet the world was formed by that law so that, receiving all seeds from God, it might become a worthy instrument of divinity, and all things might proceed in the wondrous beauty of fair order toward their beginning, in which they might persevere, and at last obtain their end. This would surely happen, unless the enemy, coming in the midst of the wheat, should oversow weeds <note>original: zizania. This is a direct reference to the "tares" or weeds from the Parable of the Weeds in the Gospel of Matthew, showing how the translator or author blended Hermetic and Christian imagery.</note>. You, therefore, having become spiritual, choose the divine seeds; and now, being cautious, flee the seeds of evil demons, who watch to seize us while we are sleeping in material affection.

### THE NINTH BOOK

**YESTERDAY**, O Aesculapius, I delivered a perfect discourse; but for the present, I judge it necessary that we discuss sense briefly. Sense and motion seem to differ most in this: that motion is according to matter, while sense is according to essence.

<summary>This section explains that "death" is merely the dissolution of earthly bodies back into their immortal components and defines man's unique position between the physical Cosmos and the Divine Mind. The text then introduces the struggle between divine and demonic influences (metaphorically described as seeds) on human behavior.</summary>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of human cognition and the divine. The previous page established man as the "third living being" who possesses both physical senses to perceive the material world and a mind to perceive the First God. This section explores how "sense" and "intelligence" function together and introduces the concept of spiritual "seeds" that influence human morality.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS</header>

Yet both of these seem to return to a single point, and in human beings, they are not distinguished by reason. In other living creatures, the sense of nature is unified, but in humans, intelligence is added. Indeed, the intellect differs from intelligence in the same way that God differs from divinity. For divinity proceeds from God, while intelligence proceeds from the human. This intelligence is the sister of speech, or rather, both are instruments for one another. For speech is not uttered without intellection, nor does intellection come to light without speech. For this reason, sense and intelligence in man are joined together, acting in harmony with one another. Indeed, we can neither understand without sense, nor can we perceive through sense without intellection in any way. It is possible, however, to have intellection without [external] sense, as is the case for those who see phantasms in their dreams. It seems to me that both operations are exercised in the visions of dreams; but that the sense is then awakened from sleep into a state of vigilance. Furthermore, man is divided into soul and body. Whenever both parts of sense are in agreement with each other, then the intellection conceived in the mind is brought forth. The mind conceives all notions—good ones whenever a seed is infused by God, but contrary ones when the seeds <note>original: spermata</note> are cast by certain demons.

<column-break/>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>POIMANDRES</header>

are cast. Truly, no part of the world is destitute of the presence of demons; their entire light descends from God himself. Finally, the demon, having been infused into the human, scatters the seeds of its own operation. The mind, being sown with these seeds, then gives birth to adulteries, lusts, homicides, parricides, sacrileges, contempt for the divine, throat-cuttings, the overthrowing of cities, plagues upon men, and all other works of the demons. Clearly, the seeds of God are few, but they are great, beautiful, and good: namely, virtue, temperance, and piety. Now, <term>piety</term> <note>original: pietas; here meaning "godliness" or "devotion"</note> is the knowledge of God. He who knows God, being filled with all good things, attains divine notions—notions, I say, that are not like those of the many. For this reason, if any give themselves to this knowledge, they neither please the common crowd, nor does the crowd please them; they are eventually thought to be insane and are mocked. Sometimes they are even held in hatred, afflicted with insults, and deprived of life. For we have said that wickedness <note>original: improbitas</note> dwells here, and that the earth is its province. I say the earth, not the whole world <note>original: mundus; here meaning the entire cosmos, which Hermes views as fundamentally good</note>, as some impious people wrongly claim. Nevertheless, the man devoted to God, as soon as he has tasted the divine vision, consigns all other things to oblivion; and even those things which are considered evils by other men, [he overlooks.]

<summary>This passage argues that human intelligence and speech are interconnected tools used to process sensory and divine input. It explains that human actions are the result of "seeds" planted in the mind by either God (producing virtue) or demons (producing vice).</summary>
<keywords>intelligence, speech, dreams, demons, seeds of God, piety, knowledge of God, cosmic order</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how divine and demonic "seeds" influence the human mind. The previous page established that while humans possess both physical senses and intelligence, their moral character depends on whether they receive divine influence or demonic interference.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
# OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS

To such a person, good things happen, as he consults prudently and refers every detail to knowledge; and what is truly wonderful, he always turns even evils completely into goods. But let us now return again to the discourse on **sense**. It is a human characteristic to join sense together with **intellection** <note>Intellection (intellectio) refers to the act of understanding or the operation of the higher mind.</note>. For all men, as I mentioned above, are guided by intellection. However, one is guided according to **matter**, while another is guided according to **essence** <note>The author distinguishes between those focused on the physical world (matter) and those focused on the true, spiritual nature of being (essence).</note>. For he who is a slave to wickedness is guided according to matter, and has received the seed of understanding from **demons**, as we have said. But for those who are friends of goodness, God Himself protects their nature. For God is the author of all things, and as the one who brings them about, He makes all things similar to Himself. Yet these created good things are sterile in the practice of their operation. For the revolution of the **World** <note>The Latin 'Mundus' is used here to refer to the physical universe or Cosmos.</note>, driving generations forward, produces certain qualities—infecting some and defiling them with evil, while refining others and purifying them with good.

O **Aesculapius**, the World possesses sense and motion, though not at all similar to human sense and motion, but rather far more powerful and much simpler. For the sense and intelligence of the world is this one thing: namely, to create all things and to dissolve what has been created. It is the instrument of the **Divine Will**, constructed for this primary reason: so that, receiving seeds from God

---

<page-num>61</page-num>
# POIMANDRES

into all things, and hiding them within its safe bosom, it may bring forth all things by composing them, and take all things away again by dividing them. And like a skilled farmer, it prunes whatever has become overgrown, so that at the appointed intervals of time, it may flourish and turn green again. Nor is there anything to which the World itself does not grant life: it is at once the place of life and also its teacher. Indeed, **bodies** consist of matter in various states: some of these are made of **earth**, some of **water**, others of **air**, and many also of **fire**. All of these are certainly composite; however, some are more densely packed, while others are simpler—the former are heavy, the latter light. The speed of the World's movement induces different qualities through the variety of generations. For its frequent breathing provides bodies with quality together with an abundance of life.

God, therefore, is the father of the **Cosmos** (that is, the world); but the Cosmos is the father of those things within the Cosmos; and the Cosmos is indeed the **Son of God**. Those things within the Cosmos are situated under the dominion of the Cosmos. And it is rightly called the **Cosmos**, for it **orders** <note>original: κοσμᾶ [kosmā] — the author is drawing an etymological link between the name 'Cosmos' and the Greek verb meaning 'to order' or 'to adorn'.</note> everything through the variety of generation. This is done through the unceasing flow of life, the perpetual operation, the swiftness of necessity, the mixing of the elements, and the order of things generated. The Cosmos itself is also an **ornament** <note>The Latin 'ornatus' translates the secondary meaning of the Greek 'Cosmos'.</note>

<summary>This passage describes the Cosmos as a living, sensing entity that acts as the instrument of God's will, functioning like a cosmic farmer that creates, dissolves, and orders all life. It establishes a hierarchy where God is the Father, the Cosmos is the Son, and all material things are subject to the Cosmos's cyclical movement.</summary>
<keywords>Cosmos as Son of God, Aesculapius, intellection, four elements, divine will, cyclical creation, etymology of Cosmos, matter vs essence</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the nature of the divine mind and its relationship to the Cosmos. The previous page discussed how human intelligence is either guided by material demons or divine essence. Here, the text shifts to the "sense and understanding" of God, emphasizing that God does not merely possess these qualities but *is* all things.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
# OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS
<meta>catchword: has run,</meta>

it has been named both by necessity and by merit. The sense and understanding <note>The Latin 'intellectio' refers to the higher cognitive power of the soul, distinct from mere physical sensation.</note> of all living things flow from external sources, from that which contains the things breathed into them. The **Cosmos**, however, perpetually preserves whatever it received from God from its very origin. God is certainly not, as it seems to some, deprived of sense and mind. For those people, weighed down by their own misery, speak wrongly against Him. 

Whatever things exist, O **Aesculapius** <note>In these dialogues, Aesculapius is the student and Hermes Trismegistus is the teacher.</note>, are in God and depend upon God; some acting through the body, some moving through the essence of the soul, others giving life through the spirit, and others serving as receptacles for the dead. But we shall say more correctly that God does not *possess* such things, but rather, to express the clear truth, we shall confess that He **is** all things. He does not receive these qualities from external sources, yet He reaches out to the furthest ends of existence. 

This is the sense and understanding of God: to move all things always. Wherefore there shall never be a time in which any of the things that exist vanish into nothingness. Whenever I say "existing things," I mean the **treasure of God**. For God Himself embraces existence: outside Him is nothing, and He Himself is outside nothing. These things, O Aesculapius, will seem true to one who understands, but unbelievable to the ignorant. For to understand is itself to believe; but not to believe is, without a doubt, to be ignorant. Indeed, my discourse has run all the way to the truth—

---

<page-num>63</page-num>
# PYMANDER <note>The 'Pymander' (or Poimandres) is the opening treatise of the Corpus Hermeticum, representing the "Divine Mind" that reveals knowledge to Hermes.</note>

—has run: the mind also, being broad and led by discourse to a certain certainty, has reached the truth. Finally, comprehending all things and finding them to be in harmony with what the discourse interpreted, it believed immediately, and happily rested in that beautiful faith. Therefore, things said about divine matters are believed when they are understood; when they are not understood, they are denied. But let these things said about intelligence suffice.

### SUMMARY OF THE TENTH BOOK

The Tenth Book constantly asserts that God is absolutely all things, which individual things contract into themselves individually through participation; yet it maintains that God is the Father and the Good, by the very fact that He Himself is all things. Soon it discusses the **rapture of the soul** in silence: where things seen by the eyes of the mind are neither lawful to reveal, nor is the power to reveal them granted to our will. 

As for what he adds regarding the transformation of animals, the following discourse of the same **Mercury** <note>The text uses 'Mercurius,' the Roman name for Hermes Trismegistus.</note> declares that one should follow the allegory of the fiction, not the literal fiction of the words, when he says: *“For no body other than a human one contains a human soul, nor is it lawful for a rational soul to collapse into the body of a soul lacking reason.”* For divine law prohibits such an abominable birth. 

And a little after, he adds: *“The punishment suitable for such a nature is not of the kind you, my son, and certain others might think—to whom it seems that our soul, after it has cast off its human form, [enters] into degenerate bodies...”*

<summary>This page concludes a discourse on God's omnipresence and the necessity of faith through understanding, before transitioning into an 'Argument' (summary) of the Tenth Book, which clarifies that human souls do not reincarnate into animals.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Aesculapius, Divine Mind, Pymander, Metempsychosis, Allegory, Divine Law, Omnipresence</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 63 regarding the nature of the soul and the divine. The text concludes a point about the souls of animals (brutes) before beginning a new section titled 'The Key,' which serves as a summary of Hermetic doctrine for the disciple Tatius.</meta>

...regarding the brutes; for this is an impious error. As for the claim that the soul of a child is its own cause through self-contemplation, let those who defend the concept of a hidden mind and intelligence see to that.

### THE KEY. X

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'H' featuring a figure, possibly a deity or scholar, amidst foliage.</image-desc> I dedicated yesterday’s discourse to you, O **Aesculapius**; thus it is right to dedicate today’s to **Tatius** <note>Tatius is often portrayed as the son or primary spiritual successor of Hermes in these dialogues.</note>. This work is all the more worthwhile because the present discussion will be a brief summary of everything we have said before. **God**, the **Father**, and the **Good**, O **Tatius**, possess the same nature, or rather, the same activity. For the terms "increase" and "decrease" apply to those things that are changeable and unchangeable—that is, the human and the divine. God wills each of these to be. Elsewhere we have demonstrated the activity of divine and human things (as in others), which must be understood in this context. The will of this activity is His essence, and His essence is to will that all things exist. What are **God**, the **Father**, and the **Good**, if not the very "being" of all these things that do not yet exist? Furthermore, God, the Father, and the Good are the very existence of those things that are, to which nothing else from all things can be compared. For the **Cosmos** and the **Sun** are fathers of those things that exist according to

<column-break/>

<page-num>65</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER.</header>

participation <note>Hermetic philosophy often suggests that the Sun and the Universe act as intermediaries or "secondary gods" that participate in the Father's creative power.</note>, yet they are not the cause of life and goodness to living things. If this is the case, the Cosmos is moved by the will of the **Good**, without which it could never be nor come to be. A father is the cause of his children, both for their procreation and their nourishment, receiving the appetite for the good through the sun. For the **Good** itself is active. It cannot belong to any other than Him; for while He receives nothing, He wills all things to exist. I do not say, O **Tatius**, that He *makes* all things; for one who "makes" is, over a long period, deficient and in need. Indeed, such a one sometimes makes and sometimes ceases; he is in need of both quantity and quality—sometimes arranging things of a certain sort and size, and at other times their opposites. But God, the Father, and the Good exist by the very fact that He is all things. Such is He, then, to one who can perceive these things. For He wills to be, and He is, and He is Himself—most especially Himself. For all other things exist because of Him. Indeed, it is the property of the **Good**, O **Tatius**, to make itself known: that is the Good, O **Tatius**.

<term>TATIUS.</term> O father, you have made us partakers of a good and beautiful vision; by this, the eye of my mind has been, as it were, already purified by such a sight.

<term>TRISMEGISTUS.</term> Yet it is not like the rays of the sun, which, by their excessive brightness, damage the eyes and make them dim...

<summary>This page introduces 'The Key,' a summary of Hermetic wisdom where Hermes explains to Tatius that God, the Father, and the Good are one essence that wills all things into existence. It distinguishes between the 'making' of worldly things and the 'being' of the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Tatius, The Good, Essence, Procreation, Divine Will, The Sun, Spiritual Vision</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Key" or summary of Hermetic doctrine. The previous page ended with a discussion on how God and the Sun participate in the existence of all things. The catchword "corporis" (of the body) links the bottom of page 66 to the top of page 67.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
-># OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS<-

so too is the vision of the Good itself. For it illuminates and increases the light of the eyes all the more, according to how much one is able to receive the influx of the intelligible splendor. The divine light is faster and sharper in its penetration, filling all things with innocence and immortality. Those who are able to drink more deeply of this brilliance fall into a holy sleep <note>original: "obdormiūt"; this refers to a mystical trance or the quieting of the physical senses to allow for spiritual vision</note>. Indeed, they often rise from the body to that most beautiful sight, just as our ancestors **Uranus** and **Saturn** did.

<term>TAT</term>: I wish that we might do so as well, O father.
<term>TRIS</term>: If only it were so, O son! However, we are still too weak for such a vision. For we shall only be able to lift the eyes of the mind and behold the incorruptible and incomprehensible beauty of the Good when we have said absolutely nothing about it. For the knowledge of it is a divine silence and the focused application of all the senses. He who understands this can think of nothing else; he who gazes upon this can gaze upon nothing else; he who hears this can hear nothing else, nor can he even move the members of his own body. Truly, with all the body's senses and movements stilled, he who surveys the whole mind remains fearless; it irradiates the entire soul, draws it completely away from the body, and finally transfigures it into the very essence of God. For it is impossible, O son, for a human soul lying in the body's—

<column-break/>

<page-num>67</page-num>
-># PYMANDER<-

—dregs to take on a divine form. Nor is it permitted to behold the beauty of God unless one has first been transformed into God.

<term>TAT</term>: In what way do you say this, O father?
<term>TRIS</term>: It is the distribution of every soul, O son.
<term>TAT</term>: But how are you distributing the changes in the meantime?
<term>TRIS</term>: Have you not heard in those things we discussed generally, how all souls flow forth from the one single soul of the entire universe, as if distributed and running throughout the whole world? There are many changes for these souls: some indeed into a better and happier state, but others into the opposite. For the souls of reptiles are transformed into aquatic creatures; the souls of aquatic creatures migrate into terrestrial ones; those of the earth ascend into birds; those of the air are turned into humans; and finally, the immortal souls of humans pass into <term>daemones</term> <note>original: "dæmones"; in this context, these are not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits or guardian intelligences</note>. From there, they happily revolve into the choir of the gods. There are two choirs of gods: one of the wandering stars <note>the planets</note> and another of the non-wandering stars <note>the fixed stars</note>. And this is the supreme glory of the soul. But if a soul has fallen into a human body and remains wicked, it tastes nothing of immortality, nor does it enjoy the Good. Its journey is reversed, and it is cast down back into reptiles. This is the judgment and punishment of an evil soul. Depravity...

<summary>This page describes the mystical ascent of the soul through "divine silence" and the contemplation of God, leading to a state of deification. It also outlines the Hermetic view of metempsychosis, where souls migrate through various life forms based on their virtue or vice.</summary>

<keywords>divine light, mystical silence, transfiguration, metempsychosis, reincarnation, daemones, celestial choirs, punishment of the soul, Hermes Trismegistus, Tatius</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Key" of Hermes Trismegistus. The previous page (67) ended mid-sentence, discussing the soul trapped in the body's dregs or filth; this page completes that thought by identifying the soul's greatest evil as ignorance.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
-># OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS<-

of the soul is ignorance. A soul that is ignorant of the nature of things and of the Good, deprived of its eyes, entangles itself in the passions of the body. Such a soul is corrupted by the habit of an evil demon <note>original: "cacodæmonis"; in Hermetic thought, this refers to a state of spiritual delusion or a malevolent spirit that leads the soul toward material obsession</note>; being ignorant of itself, it serves monstrous and deformed shapes once it has cast off its physical bodies. It carries its own body around like a troublesome burden, not presiding over the body as its master, but being subjugated by its own sluggishness. On the contrary, the virtue of the soul is knowledge <note>original: "cognitio"; specifically referring to *gnosis*, or direct spiritual insight</note>: for the person who is truly learned is good, pious, and already divine.

<term>TAT</term>: Who is this person, O father?
<term>TRIS</term>: One who neither speaks nor hears much, and who, when dealing with two types of discourse—whether speaking or hearing—fights in the darkness. For God, the Father, and the Good, can neither be pronounced by the tongue nor perceived by the ears. Since these things are as they are, they contain their own senses within all things that come from Him, because they cannot exist apart from Him. However, there is a great difference between knowledge and sensation. Sensation occurs through a movement that overcomes us, while knowledge is the boundary or endpoint of science. Science <note>original: "scientia"; used here in the sense of certain, structured knowledge of the divine</note> is a gift from God, since every science uses the incorporeal mind as its instrument, and the mind in turn uses the body. For this reason, both knowledge and sensation return to bodies, whether they be intelligible or material. For it is necessary that all things consist of opposition and contrariety, and it is impossible for it to be otherwise—

<column-break/>

<page-num>69</page-num>
-># PYMANDER<-

—possible for it to be otherwise.
<term>TAT</term>: Who then is this material god?
<term>TRIS</term>: The World <note>original: "Mundus"; referring to the entire physical cosmos</note>. It is beautiful indeed, but it is not "good." For it consists of matter and is subject to passions. It is the first of those things that are "acted upon," but only the second among those things that "exist." Furthermore, it is needy by its very nature. Though it was created at a point in time, it exists forever in a state of becoming <note>original: "generatione"; the process of constant birth and change</note>; it is the birthplace of all qualities and quantities, for it is constantly in motion. Indeed, every material motion should be called "generation" or "becoming." In this way, the state of the Intelligible world <note>the realm of pure thought and spirit</note> drives material motion. Since the world is a sphere—that is, a head—there is nothing material above that head, just as there is nothing intelligible beneath the feet. All things are material, but the Mind drives the "head" in a circle—that is, a motion according to the nature of the head. Therefore, all things that are close to the skin of that head—which is surely the soul—are born immortal, as if the body were contained within the soul. Those things that possess a soul are filled by the body. But whatever is far from that "skin," where the things that participate more fully in the soul reside, is merely body. Yet the whole is a living being <note>original: "animal"; meaning a thing endowed with *anima* or soul</note>. Thus, the entire world is composed of something material and something intelligible. The world is the first living being; man is—

<sig>E 3</sig>

<vocab>Hermes Trismegistus, Pymander, Tat, soul, evil demon, knowledge, science, world, matter, becoming</vocab>

<summary>This page defines ignorance as the soul's greatest vice and knowledge (gnosis) as its virtue. It further describes the physical Cosmos (the World) as a "material god" that is beautiful but imperfect, functioning as a living sphere driven by the divine Mind.</summary>
<keywords>ignorance, gnosis, virtue, evil demon, material god, cosmos, sphere of the world, immortality, intelligible vs material</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat. Following the previous discussion on the soul's entanglement in the body, Hermes now defines the hierarchical relationship between God, the World, and Man, and explains how the soul descends into forgetfulness through the physical body.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header># OF MERCURIUS TRISMEGISTUS</header>
<meta>catchword: dus</meta>

[Man is] the second living being after the world, but the first of the rest of living beings. For whatever gift of the soul God grants to others, man also possesses. Nor is he only considered not good, but even evil, insofar as he is mortal. For the world is not good, because it is changeable; however, it is not evil, because it is immortal. Man, however, both because he is changeable and also because he is mortal, is judged to be evil. 

The soul of man is carried in this manner: the Mind <note>original: "Mens"; the highest part of the soul, or intellect</note> is in the reason, reason is in the soul, the soul is in the spirit <note>original: "spiritus"; here referring to the *pneuma*, a subtle substance that links the immaterial soul to the physical body</note>, and the spirit is in the body. The spirit, diffused through the veins, arteries, and blood, moves the living being in every part, and sustains and carries about the suspended mass of the body. Whence some, being deceived, have thought the humor of the blood to be the soul. These people were clearly unaware that first the spirit must flow into the soul, then the blood must congeal and the hollow veins and arteries must be extended, and finally the living being is dissolved; and that is the death of the body. 

All things, however, depend on one principle. The principle is from the one and the only. And the principle indeed moves, so that the principle may stand forth again. Yet the One itself remains, nor does it depart from its unity. These three, therefore, are: God the Father, the Good; and the World; and Man. God holds the world, and the world—

---

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header># PYMANDER</header>
<sig>E 4</sig>

—holds man. The world is the son of God <note>original: "Mundus dei filius"; in Hermetic thought, the cosmos is the first "offspring" or reflection of the divine</note>, and man is the offspring of the world. For God is not ignorant of man, but has care for him, and wishes to be known by him. This is the only salvation for man: the knowledge of God. This is the ascent to Olympus <note>original: "olympu[m]"; a classical metaphor used here to describe the return to the divine or celestial realm</note>. From this one thing alone the soul becomes good; not that it is good sometimes and at other times evil, but it is so by necessity.

**TAT**: How do you assert this, O Trismegistus?

**TRIS**: I assert this, I say: that the soul of a child contemplates itself, not yet having departed from its own proper nature on account of the body. For the body is not yet entirely compacted, and the soul beholds itself as beautiful in every way. For it has not yet been corrupted by the passions of the body, depending even then on the soul of the whole world. But when the body has been compacted, and it disperses and distracts the soul into its own mass, then the soul is subjected to forgetfulness, and is deprived of the vision of the beautiful and the good. 

Forgetfulness itself is wickedness; and the same happens to those who depart from the body. For when the soul runs back into itself, the spirit is contracted into the blood, and the soul into the spirit. The Mind, free from its veils <note>original: "uelaminibus"; the layers of material or subtle substances that wrap the intellect during its incarnation</note>, being divine by its own nature, having obtained a fiery body, wanders through all places, and leaves the soul to a just judgment and deserved punishment. 

**TAT**: In what way do you say this, O father?

<summary>This passage details the Hermetic hierarchy where God contains the World and the World contains Man. It explains the "descent" of the soul into a physical body, where it loses its original purity and divine "vision" through forgetfulness and the influence of material passions.</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, hierarchy of being, Mind (Mens), Spirit (Pneuma), knowledge of God (Gnosis), soul's descent, child's soul, reincarnation, divine judgment</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat. Hermes has just described the chain of existence connecting God to the physical body. He now elaborates on how the Mind, Soul, and Spirit are layered like garments to protect the divine essence from the harsh density of the material world.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>-> OF MERCURIUS TRISMEGISTUS <-</header>
<meta>catchword: For indeed</meta>

**TRIS.** The Mind <note>original: "Mens"; the highest, divine part of the intellect</note> is indeed separated from the soul, and the soul is separated from the spirit. The clothing of the Mind is the soul, and the clothing of the soul is the spirit <note>original: "spiritus"; the *pneuma* or vital breath that connects the immaterial soul to the physical body</note>. It is necessary, O son, for the listener to understand along with the speaker, to harmonize with him, and to have a hearing sharper than the voice of the one speaking. The wrapping together of these garments, O son, is completed in the earthly body: for it is entirely impossible for the Mind, naked in its own nature, to settle within an earthly mass. For the dregs of the earth are not able to receive a Mind so divine, nor can such a great power sustain a form shaped into a suffering body. 

Therefore, the Mind took on the soul as a cloak; furthermore, the soul, being divine, uses the spirit as its vehicle <note>original: "uehiculo"; a reference to the 'chariot' of the soul common in Neoplatonic and Hermetic thought</note>. The spirit runs through the whole living being. And so, as soon as the Mind is released from the earthly body, it immediately puts on its own proper cloak—that is, a body of fire—clothed in which it cannot swell into an earthly body so long as it is thus covered. For the earth does not sustain fire at all; indeed, it would all be consumed by a tiny spark. For this reason, a cold moisture has been poured around the earthly mass, as a kind of obstacle to the burning of the fire. Since the Mind is the sharpest and swiftest of all divine concepts, it encompasses the bodies of all the elements.

<column-break/>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>-> PYMANDER <-</header>
<sig>E 5</sig>

For indeed, the craftsman of the heavens himself uses fire most of all for his creation. The Maker of the whole uses all things; but the maker of man uses only those things which are around the earth. For the human mind, deprived of [divine] fire and only fit for human arrangement, cannot construct divine things. 

Certainly the human soul—not every soul, but the pious one—is blessed and divine. After such a soul has been released from the prison of the body through death, and after it has undergone every struggle of virtue and piety, it becomes either Mind or God. Now, the struggle of religious piety is this: to know God and to do harm to no one. 

The impious soul, however, remains in its own nature; it is tortured excessively by itself and seeks an earthly and human body to enter. For no body other than a human one can contain a human soul; nor is it lawful for a rational soul to fall into the body of an irrational creature <note>Hermes here rejects the idea of transmigration into animals (metempsychosis), arguing that a rational soul must remain in a human form</note>. For divine law forbids such a wicked degradation. 

**TAT.** In what way then, O father, is the soul of man tortured? Or what punishment is more severe for this soul? 

**TRIS.** Impiety, O my son Tat. For what fire has a more burning flame than impiety? What biting beast so [tears] the body...

<summary>Hermes explains the soul's "garments" of spirit and fire, and describes the post-mortem fate where pious souls achieve divinity while impious ones are tormented by their own nature.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Mind, Soul, Spirit, pneuma, four elements, fire, piety, afterlife, reincarnation, Pymander</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat. Hermes has just explained that the Mind (Mens) is protected by layers of soul and spirit to inhabit a physical body. He now transitions into the consequences for a soul that fails to align with the divine, refuting the idea of reincarnation into animals in favor of a psychological and spiritual torment overseen by the Mind itself.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>OF MERCURIUS TRISMEGISTUS</header>
<meta>catchword: thanks</meta>

...[ma]cerates, just as impiety lacerates the soul. Do you not see by how many evils the impious soul is weighed down? Alas, my son, thus the impious soul cries out: "I burn, I am consumed; I know not what to say or what to do; evils flowing in from all sides devour wretched me; I see nothing, alas, wretch that I am, nor do I hear." Such are the voices of the afflicted soul, such is the punishment fitting for its nature—not such as you, perhaps, my son, and certain others imagine, who believe that our soul, after it has cast off the human form, degenerates into the bodies of beasts <note>original: "brutorũ"; the text here explicitly rejects metempsychosis, the belief that humans can be reborn as animals</note>. For this is an impious error: indeed, the manner of its chastisement is otherwise. Truly, when the Mind becomes a daemon <note>original: "dæmon"; here meaning a guiding or punishing spirit rather than an evil entity</note>, it is commanded to take up a body of fire in the service of God; then, poured into the wicked soul, it scourges it with the whips of its sins. The wicked soul, lashed by these, turns itself toward murders, insults, reproaches, and various robberies, and finally slips into all those things through which men commit crimes. But when the Mind flows into a holy soul, it raises it to the light of wisdom. This soul afterwards never languishes in the numbness of sleep, but brings help to the human race through both words and deeds alike, and assists in all ways, always emulating its parent <note>referring to God as the Father of all</note>. Wherefore, O son, it is necessary that we, giving thanks to God

---

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

pray that we may be made sharers of a good mind. The soul indeed migrates into what is better, never into what is worse. There is also a certain communion of souls: the souls of gods communicate with the souls of men; God shows himself common to each, for he is more excellent than all, and all things are indeed weaker than he. The world is subject to God, man to the world, and the beast to man. God is over all things and around all things. The rays of God are his acts; the rays of the world are the forces of nature; the rays of man, however, are the arts and sciences. Acts are exercised through the world, and these descend into man through the natural rays of the world—nature through the elements, and men through arts and sciences. This indeed is the administration of the whole world, depending on the nature of the One, and running rightly through the one Mind, than which nothing is more powerful, nothing more divine, and finally, nothing more unified. There is a communion of men with the gods, and of gods with men. This is the "Good Spirit" <note>original: "bonus dæmon"; likely referring to the *Agathos Daimon*, a figure of wisdom in Hermetic thought</note>: the soul that is full of this is blessed; but wretched is the one that is empty. 

<term>TAT</term>. For what reason do you say this, O father? 

<term>TRIS</term>. Know, my son, that every soul has the Good itself for its Mind—for our present discourse concerns this, and not that servant of the Mind <note>the "lower" or more functional aspect of the intellect</note>, whom we mentioned above, who was sent down as a result of judgment...

<summary>Hermes explains that the "punishment" of the soul is not reincarnation into animals, but a spiritual scourging by the Mind itself. He describes a hierarchy of existence where God influences the world through His "rays," and the holy soul acts as a conduit for divine wisdom to help humanity.</summary>
<keywords>chastisement of the soul, metempsychosis, Agathos Daimon, hierarchy of being, divine rays, arts and sciences, communion of souls</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse between Hermes Trismegistus and his son Tat. In the previous section, Hermes described the Mind as a divine guide that punishes the wicked soul with the "whips of its own sins" rather than through reincarnation into animals. We join the text as he describes the final descent of a soul that has been abandoned by the Mind.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>OF MERCURIUS TRISMEGISTUS</header>
<meta>catchword: ARGUMENT—</meta>

...to the underworld <note>original: "ad inferos"</note>. The soul, when stripped of the Mind’s presence, is able neither to act nor to speak. Indeed, the Mind often dwells outside the soul; at such a time, the soul neither hears nor sees, but is similar to an unthinking beast. Such is the power of the Mind when it deserts a soul that remains entangled in the body and is dragged by it to the lower realms. Such a soul, my son, has no mind; therefore, it is not right even to call this creature a "man." For man is a divine living being <term>animal: here used in the Latin sense of 'anima' (soul), meaning any creature endowed with life, not specifically a beast</term>; he is not to be compared with the beasts of the earth, but with the celestial gods. Nay, if we must dare to speak the truth, the true man is either superior to the heavenly beings, or at least shares an equal lot with them. For whenever any of the heavenly beings descend to earth, they must leave the boundary of heaven; but man ascends into heaven and measures it. He is not ignorant of what is below or what is above, and he diligently inquires into all other things. And what is even greater: without ever leaving the earth, he is raised into heaven—so vast is the power of human nature. Therefore, we must dare to say that an earthly man is a mortal god, while a heavenly god is an immortal man. Thus, by the power of these two—namely, man and the world—all things are governed, though all are ultimately subject to the One.

---

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>ARGUMENT OF THE ELEVENTH [BOOK].</header>

*The eleventh discourse, concerning the Mind and filled with divine speech, declares that eternity comes from God, the world from eternity, time from the world, and change from time; and that both life and death depend upon change. It most pleasingly demonstrates—through both reason and analogy—that no power (neither divine, nor human, nor of the things above, nor of the things below) is comparable to the insurmountable power of God, who is the One. Since He cannot be idle, He is always performing all things. You may rightly understand this in relation to the eternal generation of the Word <note>original: "uerbi"; referring to the Greek "Logos" or Divine Reason</note>; for he says that it was necessary for the divine work to be present so that those things which are made, those which were made, and those which shall be made in the future might exist. That work is life; it is beauty; it is goodness; and finally, it is God and the life of all things. But what is this, if not what the Evangelist <note>referring to St. John the Apostle</note> sang more loudly with his trumpet when he said: "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God. This was in the beginning with God. All things were made through Him, and without Him was made nothing that was made. In Him was life" <note>A direct reference to the opening of the Gospel of John, showing how Renaissance scholars tried to harmonize Hermetic philosophy with Christianity</note>. Therefore, the life of all flows from God and is God Himself. Thus it follows that true death is not that destruction of things which is commonly imagined; rather, it is that process by which the life of living things, withdrawing itself into hiddenness, abandons the physical form. Indeed, the author uses a wonderful sense of proportion to help us comprehend Him who escapes all proportion—who, though grasped, remains incomprehensible, and who, though unknown to all, presents Himself as knowable in all things. Therefore, all you who are pious, attend here fully now, and incline your minds to the discourses of the Mind, so that you may at last know Him who fashioned all things for this very reason: so that you might perceive Him through every individual thing.*

<vocab>Hermes Trismegistus, soul, mind, divine man, eternity, the Word, God, death, life</vocab>

<summary>Hermes concludes his talk on the soul by defining man as a "mortal god" capable of spanning heaven and earth, followed by a summary of the eleventh book which links Hermetic philosophy to the Gospel of John.</summary>

<header>THE MIND. XI.</header>

->Stop putting it off for so long, O Thrice-greatest Mercury. Be silent now, and remember what has been said before. Indeed, I will not be reluctant to express my meaning to you, all the more because many and various opinions about the world and God wander among mortals.<-

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Indeed, to speak frankly, I do not yet grasp the truth of this matter. Therefore, my lord, express the pure truth yourself. For I trust that you alone can illuminate such things.

**THE MIND.** Listen to me then, my son. This is how God, Time, and the Universe relate to one another. God is Eternity; Time is Generation <note>original: "generatio" — in this context, the word refers to the process of becoming, including birth, growth, and decay</note>. God brings about Eternity; Eternity brings about the World; the World brings about Time; and Time brings about Generation. The essence, as it were, of God is the Good, the Beautiful, Blessedness, and Wisdom. The essence of Eternity is Sameness. The essence of the World is Order. The essence of Time is Change. The essence of Generation is Life and Death. The activities of God are Mind and Soul. The activities of Eternity are permanence and immortality. The activities of the World are composition and decomposition. The activities of Time are increase and decrease. And finally, the activity of Generation is quality. 

Therefore, Eternity is in God, the World is in Eternity, Time is in the World, and Generation is in Time. Eternity stands around God, the World moves within Eternity, Time is bounded within the World, and Generation

<column-break/>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

is completed within Time. Thus, God is the source of all things. Their essence is Eternity, and their matter is the World. The power of God is Eternity. The work of Eternity is the World. The World was not created at a single point in time, but has always been created by the Age <term>æuo: the Latin word for an age or eternity, representing the timeless state from which the world emerges</term>. Since the Age never ceases, the World will never cease. And since the World is embraced by Eternity, no part of the World ever perishes.

**TRISMEGISTUS.** But what is the Wisdom of God?

**THE MIND.** It is the Good, the Beautiful, Blessedness, every Virtue, and the Age. From the Age comes immortality, for the Age grants permanence to matter. For the origin of matter depends on the Age, just as the Age depends on God. Generation and Time are twin natures, existing both in heaven and on earth. In heaven, these two are surely immutable and immortal, but on earth, they are changeable and subject to corruption. 

The soul of Eternity is God. The soul of the World is Eternity. The soul of the Earth is Heaven. God is in the Mind, the Mind is in the Soul, and the Soul is in Matter. All these things exist through the Age. This entire Great Body <note>Referring to the physical universe</note>, in which all bodies are contained, is filled internally and embraced externally by a Soul full of Mind and God. This Soul provides life to all things: outwardly to this immense and perfect Living Being <term>animal: here used for the 'Cosmos,' which ancient philosophers viewed as a single, living, breathing creature</term> that is the world, and inwardly to all other creatures that...

It presents the universe as a living entity (the "World-Animal") sustained by a divine soul that permeates all matter.</summary>

exist in the world for all that lives; and above in heaven, through that which is ever the same, He sustains all things; while below on earth, He drives the process of generation <note>original: "generationem" — in this context, the word refers to the continuous cycle of birth, growth, and becoming in the material world</note>. Truly, Eternity contains the World, whether by necessity, providence, or nature. And whatever else anyone thinks, or will ever think, it is God who brings it all about. The action of God is a power that cannot be overcome, with which no one would dare compare any human or even divine power. Therefore, take care, O Mercury, that you do not consider anything below or even anything above to be equal to God, for you would wander far from the truth. For nothing is like Him, who is unique, solitary, and one. Nor should you think that the same power belongs to anyone else but God. For what is there besides Him that could lead to life, immortality, and the transformation of qualities? And what else besides these things would God achieve? For God is not idle; if He were, all things would be idle. Indeed, all things are full of God, and idleness is found in no part of the world. Truly, "idleness" is a hollow word, both regarding that which acts and that which is acted upon. It is necessary for all things to come into being, and to always be made according to the nature of each place. The agent is not present to one thing alone, but to all, and not just one thing
