...indeed around the heaven, and partly around the earth. Neither do those things on the right move to the left, nor those on the left to the right. Likewise, the upper things do not move to the lower, nor the lower back to the upper. But that all these things have been created, my dearest Mercury <note>original: "mi Mercuri" — Pymander addresses Hermes Trismegistus by his Latin name, Mercury</note>, there is no need for you to learn through my long discourse. For they are bodies, they possess a soul, and they are moved. It is impossible for these to come together into one without the power of a Joiner <note>original: "congregantis" — the one who gathers or assembles the disparate parts of the universe</note>. It is necessary, therefore, that there be someone of such a nature, and entirely one. For since there are many various movements and dissimilar bodies, yet a single order of speed is found in all of them, it is impossible for there to be two or more creators; for a single order would not be preserved among many. Furthermore, whoever among them was the weaker would surely envy the more powerful, from which a certain discord would arise. And so, if there had existed a single author of changeable living things, he would also desire to create immortal things, just as a father of immortal things would strive after mortal ones. Moreover, since there is one soul and one matter <note>original: "materia" — the raw substance of the universe</note>, to which of them would the craftsmanship chiefly belong? If it belonged to both, to which would the larger province go? Think of it this way: every living body, whether mortal or immortal, consists of matter and soul; truly all

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>*PYMANDER.*</header>
<sig>F 2</sig>

living bodies are animated <note>original: "animata" — possessing an anima or soul</note>, while non-living things are almost bare matter. Likewise, the soul, according to itself and being near to its parent, is the cause of life; and the cause of life is any of the immortals. In what way, then, do mortal living things differ from mortals, or immortal things from immortals? It is thus clear that there is one author of all these things above the others. For there is one soul, one life, and one matter. Who is that one? Who else but the unique God? To whom else does it belong to procreate living things, but to God alone? Thus, the one God has confessed that there is one world, one Sun, one Moon, and one divinity. How many do you wish God himself to be? Therefore, the One performs each thing among the many. Do you think it is a difficult and laborious thing for God to produce life, soul, immortality, and change? For you yourself can do so many things: you see, hear, smell, taste, touch, speak, walk, breathe, and understand. It is not one person in you who sees and another who hears; nor does one speak while another walks, or this one smell while that one tastes, or this one breathe while that one understands. Instead, one being provides all these things. Nor is it possible for those things to happen without the divine will of God. For just as he who ceases from these actions is no longer

<header>*THE TRISMEGISTIAN MERCURY*</header>
<meta>catchword: life is made</meta>

...living being; likewise, he who would cease from the construction of those things would not be God at all. To even suspect this is wicked <note>original: "nefarium" — implying a moral and religious transgression against the nature of the divine</note>. For if it is granted that nothing exists in the nature of things which does not possess a certain natural vigor for acting, and at the same time the execution of a certain work: how much more must it be asserted that God does not lack the power and the effect of all things? For whatever is idle is imperfect. To call God imperfect is a sin. Therefore, God makes all things. Now, O Mercury, give your whole attention to me for a short time. Devote yourself to me, and you will immediately understand the work of God. It was necessary for this work to be present, so that those things which are made, or which were made, or which will be made in the future, might exist. And that work, my sweetest Mercury, is life; it is indeed beautiful, it is good, and it is finally God. But if you ask me to place these things before your eyes through examples of works, notice what happens to you when you wish to procreate: yet this is not similar to Him, for He is not diffused by pleasure; He has no helper in His work, being effective by His own nature, and always engaged in His work by His own power. Being Himself whatever He has ever made. If He should ever withdraw His influence, life would fail, and all things would collapse into death. But since all things live, and life is one...

<column-break/>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>*PYMANDER*</header>
<sig>F 3</sig>

...the life of all things, God is one. Again, if all things are living—both those seen in heaven and those lying on the earth—and a single life of all flows from God, then that life itself is also God himself. All things are made by one Founder. Life, indeed, is the union of Mind and Soul <note>original: "unio mentis & animæ" — the 'Mind' here represents the higher intellect or spirit, while 'Soul' is the animating principle</note>, but death is not the destruction of things joined together, but the dissolution of the union of many parts. Therefore, the image of God is Eternity <note>original: "æuum" — the timeless state of being</note>, the image of Eternity is the world, the image of the world is the sun, and the image of the sun is man. But most people think that death is a kind of change, for the reason that the mass of the body dissolves and life withdraws into the hidden. Indeed, my beloved Mercury, I teach you that the world is changed for this reason: because certain of its parts constantly depart into the hidden; yet never think that it perishes. These are truly the "passions" <note>original: "passiones" — in this context, the passive transformations or states the world undergoes</note> of the world: namely, revolution and concealment. Revolution is indeed turning, and concealment is renewal. Furthermore, the world, which takes all forms, does not receive forms that are external or foreign, but rather it moves them within itself. But if this world was created having all forms <note>original: "omniformis" — containing the potential for every shape or species</note>, what kind of author does it have? He certainly cannot be formless. But if He himself also has all forms, He will be similar to the world. If, however, He had only a single form, He would be within this world.

<summary>This passage argues that God is the perpetual source of life and that "death" is merely a dissolution of parts rather than true destruction. It establishes a hierarchy of divine images linking God to Eternity, the World, the Sun, and Man.</summary>

...is inferior. What then shall we say to these things? I pray, let us not remain in uncertainty. For what is uncertain in divine matters is not yet known. Therefore, the Divine has a single **idea** <note>original: "idæam" — in Hermetic philosophy, an "idea" is the perfect, incorporeal archetype or blueprint in the mind of God</note>. The specific form of this idea, since it escapes the sight of the eyes, is incorporeal, and it expresses individual forms through physical bodies. Let no wonder at all hold you back regarding the fact that there exists a certain incorporeal idea. For it is like the idea of speech, or the strokes of letters in inscriptions. Though they seem to stand out quite a bit, they are by their nature flat and smooth.

But meditate now on what is said more boldly, and what shall be asserted even more truly. Just as a human being cannot live without life, so too God cannot live unless He produces good things. For this is the life of God, this is His action: to set all things in motion and to breathe life into all.

Indeed, some of the things we have spoken of above require a certain depth of understanding. Consider in this example what I most especially wish to signify. All things are in God, but not as if they were placed in a physical location. For a "location" is an immobile body, and those things placed within it lack motion. Truly, a thing is situated differently in a physical body than it is in the *imagination* <note>original: "phantasia" — the mental faculty that can hold images without physical volume</note>. Think of Him as containing all things; think that nothing is more spacious than an incorporeal nature, nothing more fertile, nothing

<column-break/>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>
<sig>F 4</sig>

stronger, and that He is the most spacious, the fastest, and the most powerful of all. 

Starting again from your own experience, meditate in this way: command your soul, and it will fly away faster than you can give the order. Command it, I say, to cross over to the ocean; it will be there before you have finished the command, without ever departing from where it is now. Command it again to fly into heaven; it will need no wings, and nothing will obstruct its course—not the fire of the sun, nor the vastness of the upper air, nor the rotation of the heavens, nor the bodies of the other stars. Instead, penetrating all things, it may transcend even to the highest body <note>referring to the outermost limit of the physical universe</note>. Furthermore, if you wish to pass through all the celestial spheres and investigate what lies beyond, that too will be permitted to you. 

Notice how great is the power of your soul, and how great its speed! If you can do these things, could God Himself not do them? Contemplate God in this way: as having all *thoughts* <note>original: "intelligentias" — cosmic forms of knowledge or intellects</note> within Himself, and possessing Himself as the entirely whole world. 

Unless you make yourself equal to God, you will never understand God; for like is always known by its like. Stretch yourself into a magnitude without end; emerge from the body, go beyond all time, become **eternity**, and thus, finally, you shall know Him. Assuming nothing is impossible within yourself, consider yourself

Think of yourself as immortal, able to comprehend all things—all knowledge, and likewise every art. Believe that nothing perishes through death. Grasp all these things at once: places, times, masses, qualities, and quantities. In this way, finally, you will be able to understand God. 

On the contrary, if you submerge your soul in the body and confine it, and prostrate yourself in the mire with these words: "I know nothing, nor am I even able to know; I dread the vast depths of the sea, I cannot fly to heaven, I know not what I am now, nor do I know what I shall be in the future." What business have you with God? For while you are evil and a slave to a stinking body, you cannot perceive the beautiful and the good. Indeed, the extreme of depravity is not to recognize God. But to trust and hope that one can eventually find the Good is a certain divine way, leading by a straight path to the Good, easy and accessible; it will always meet you as you proceed everywhere, whether walking,

walking, sailing, by day and by night, while speaking and while silent. For there is nothing in the nature of things that does not bear some image of divinity. 

<term>TRIS.</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage</note> Is God not invisible? 

<term>MENS.</term> <note>The Divine Mind</note> Speak more reverently, Trismegistus, for who is more radiant than He? Indeed, He fashioned all things for this reason: so that you might see Him through each individual thing. This is the goodness of God, this is His power: that He shines through all things. Nothing is invisible, even among incorporeal <note>non-physical or spiritual</note> things. The Mind is seen in the very act of understanding. God is perceived in His operation. Let these things be explained to you thus far, Trismegistus; the rest you shall now investigate for yourself, and you will not be deceived by a false image of the truth.

### ARGUMENT OF THE TWELFTH BOOK.

*The twelfth book, proceeding to discuss the lower mind—which is a certain image or vestige of the First Mind—associates it with individual souls after the exact determination of the First Mind. But in irrational creatures, this mind is nature <note>original: "natura" — here referring to animal instinct</note>; in humans, however, it is reason. Those humans who are deprived of its benefit are carried away like beasts by a burning impulse toward the fulfillment of desire. They set no limit to their lust, nor do they find an end to their evils and passions; they suffer by fate and divine decree, paying the deserved penalties for their ignorance. On the contrary, the good, over whom the Mind presides...*

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>
<meta>catchword: indeed</meta>

the image of the good and the beautiful; they receive the good things which Fate has ordained for them. Therefore, you will by no means escape Fate: for indeed, the divine law is Fate. Yet you can flee from evils and the punishments that follow as the attendants of wickedness. Mercury, full of the oracle, discusses many things: he has often heard the oracle pouring forth, and he commends two divine gifts in humans: the mind and speech. He reveals the oracles of the Good Spirit <note>original: "boni daemonis" — likely referring to Agathodaemon, a figure of divine wisdom in Hermetic thought</note>, or if you prefer, of the celestial spirit divinely breathing forth, regarding God, the Word, the world, and matter: that nothing in the world is devoid of life, nothing should be called mortal, but rather everything should be perceived as dissoluble <note>meaning things do not "die" but rather break down and change form</note>, and nothing is immobile. He teaches that God meets only man, among all dissoluble things, in various ways, and in what manner man can rise from the things of the senses to perceiving God.

### THE TWELFTH BOOK

The Mind <term>Mens: the higher, divine intellect as opposed to mere human reason</term> indeed, O Tat, is born from the very essence of God (if indeed He has an essence). This Mind, whatever its nature may be, alone understands itself purely. Therefore, the Mind is not at all divided from the essence of God, but is rather connected to it in the same way that light is connected to the body of the Sun. This Mind is God within humans, and for that reason, some among the number of men are gods, and their humanity is closest to divinity. For indeed, the Good Spirit proclaims that gods are

<column-break/>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

indeed immortal humans, while humans are mortal gods. In irrational animals, however, that Mind is simply nature. For wherever there is a soul, there also is the Mind: just as wherever there is life, there also is a soul. In living beings without the discourse of reason, the soul is life empty of the Mind. Truly, the Mind is the helper of human souls, calling them back to their own proper good. In living beings lacking reason, it works together with the nature of each; in the souls of men, it sometimes resists and fights back. For the soul, once infused into the body, is continuously corrupted by pleasure and pain. From the mixture with the body, pleasure and pain spring forth like little streams, into which the soul falls and is suffocated. To whichever souls the Mind presides, it illuminates them with its own brilliance, resisting their diseases and evils. Just as an expert physician causes pain to the body of a sick person, burning and cutting it for the sake of recovering health, in the same way, the Mind afflicts the pleasure-seeking soul to pull up the roots of pleasure. For from this comes every disease of the soul. The gravest disease of the soul is impiety. Opinion <note>original: "Opinio" — referring to false belief or a mind clouded by sensory illusions rather than truth</note> truly allures one not toward any good at all, but rather toward evil.

It features the famous Hermetic maxim that "gods are immortal humans, and humans are mortal gods."</summary>

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

But truly the mind <term>Mens: the higher, divine intellect</term>, by resisting the soul's passions, provides for the soul's well-being just as a physician provides for the health of the body. All those human souls that have by no means obtained the Mind as their guide suffer the same things as the souls of irrational beasts. The Mind abandons them to the rule of desires, toward the fulfillment of which they are carried by a certain burning impulse; and in the manner of wild beasts, they are immoderate in both their anger and their lusts. What is worse, they set no limit to their lusts, nor do they find an end to their evil passions. God has set before these people the Law as a judge and a punisher <note>original: "lictorem" — a lictor was a Roman officer who carried out the sentences of magistrates, often carrying an axe and rods</note>.

**TAT.** Here, O father, that discussion about fate <term>Fatum: the predetermined course of physical events</term>, which we left incomplete in previous sections, comes back to life. For if it is decreed that some shall be adulterers and some shall be sacrilegious, why is anyone punished for it, since they have sinned while being compelled by the necessity of fate?

**TRIS.** All things are the works of fate, my son; without it, nothing of bodily things exists. No good can happen without it, nor can any evil occur without it. It is decreed that he who commits something shameful shall suffer. He committed the act for this reason: so that he might suffer whatever he suffers after he has committed it. However, we have spoken elsewhere about fate and the punishments for crimes. Our present discourse is instead about the Mind: what the Mind is capable of,

<meta>catchword: capable of [valeat]</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

capable of, and how its instinct differs in humans and beasts. And again, how in beasts the Mind itself is not beneficent <note>meaning it does not act as a moral guide in animals</note>, but in humans it restrains the impulse of lust and extinguishes the fire of anger. From this it follows that even among humans, some exist as irrational and others as rational. All humans indeed are subject to fate, to birth <term>generatio: the process of coming into physical being</term>, and to change <term>transmutatio: the shifting of physical states or "becoming"</term>. For the beginning and end of fate are these two: namely birth and change. And all humans indeed suffer that which fate has ordained. But those rational ones, whom we said the Mind assists as a guide, do not suffer in the same way as the rest; rather, being far removed from wickedness and not being evil, they suffer the least amount of evil.

**TAT.** How do you say this again, O father?

**TRIS.** Is not an adulterer evil? Is not a murderer evil? The rational man does not suffer as an adulterer, but in the same manner as an adulterer <note>Hermes suggests that the rational man might face the same external physical fate or circumstances as a sinner, but his internal state and spiritual outcome are different</note>. He does not suffer as a murderer, but in the same manner as a murderer. It is impossible to escape the quality of change, just as it is impossible to escape the effect of birth. But it is permitted for one who possesses Mind to avoid wickedness. Wherefore, my son, I have always heard the good spirit <note>original: "bonum dæmonem" — likely Agathodaemon, a personification of divine wisdom</note> dictating—who, if he had also committed his records to writing,

<summary>Hermes explains that while all humans are subject to the physical laws of Fate, birth, and change, the Divine Mind (Mens) allows rational people to master their passions and avoid the moral stain of wickedness. He addresses the paradox of Fate and free will, suggesting that the Mind acts as a spiritual shield even when physical circumstances are difficult.</summary>
<keywords>Fate, Mind, Mens, Tat, Hermes Trismegistus, free will, virtue, vice, Agathodaemon, birth, change</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue of the Twelfth Book between Hermes Trismegistus and his son, Tat. Following the discussion on fate and the punishments of the law, Hermes begins to describe the teachings of the "Good Spirit" (Agathos Daimon) regarding the supremacy of the Mind over necessity.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

...bestows certain wondrous benefits upon the human race daily. He alone, my son, as if he were the first-born god beholding all things, poured out divine oracles upon us. Indeed, I heard him preaching thus at one time: "All things are one, especially intelligible bodies <note>original: "intelligibilia corpora" — non-physical forms or entities that can be understood by the intellect</note>. We live by power, by act, and by eternity. His mind is good, just as his soul is good." Since this is so, nothing among the intelligible things is distant from the Intelligible itself. Furthermore, the Mind, the ruler of all, and the soul of God can accomplish whatever it wishes. These were his words. But you, consider these things, and stand by with attentive ears to the discourse I shall now introduce concerning fate. If you diligently avoid contentious quibbles, you will find without doubt that the Mind, the soul of God, dominates all things—both fate and law, and all other things. Nor is anything of those things which belong to fate impossible for the Mind; and therefore, the human mind is superior to fate, yet it does not neglect those things subject to fate. And these are the best oracles of the Good Spirit <note>original: "boni dæmonis" — referring to Agathos Daimon, a legendary teacher of Hermes</note>.

**TAT.** These things have been treated truly divinely and even fittingly, O father. But I pray you declare this further to me. You said that the Mind operates in irrational beasts in the manner of nature, when [it is] cooperating with their affections...

<column-break/>

<page-num>95</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

...cooperating with their affections. But the affections <term>Affectus: the way a being is "affected" by external things; emotions or impulses</term> of rational beings, I believe, are passions <term>Passio: in this context, the state of being acted upon by an outside force or internal desire</term>. The Mind cooperates with affections. Affections are passions. Therefore, the Mind is a kind of passion, for thus it is shaped by passions. 

**TRIS.** Bravo, good son, how nobly you ask! It is right that I answer you. All incorporeal things that are within bodies are subject to being acted upon; indeed, they are properly called "passions." For everything that moves is incorporeal, while everything moved is corporeal. Incorporeal things are also moved by the Mind, and movement is a "passion." Therefore both undergo action: the one indeed dominating, the other being subject. But when it is separated from the body, it is freed from passions. Indeed, nothing anywhere is incapable of being acted upon, my son. All things are "passible" <term>Passible: from the Latin 'patibilia', meaning capable of being acted upon or feeling an external influence</term>. But "passion" and "passibility" differ mostly in this: that one acts, and the other undergoes. 

Bodies also act according to themselves: for they are either immobile or they are moved. Whichever of these it is, it is a passion. But incorporeal things are always operating, and for that reason they are "passible" [subject to action]. And do not let the names disturb you. For operation and passion are the same thing; yet there is no harm in using a more honorable term <note>Hermes suggests that while "passion" sounds like suffering, in philosophy it simply describes the mechanics of how the Mind and Body interact</note>. 

**TAT.** You have given a clear explanation of these things, O father. 

**TRIS.** Furthermore...

<summary>Hermes explains that the Divine Mind (Mens) is superior to Fate and acts as a ruler over all things. He clarifies a philosophical point that even the Mind is "passible" because in the interaction between soul and body, all movement and action involve being "acted upon" in some way.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book Twelve of the Corpus Hermeticum. Hermes Trismegistus is explaining to his son, Tat, the distinction between human beings and other animals, focusing on the divine gifts of Mind and Speech, and the hierarchical structure of the cosmos.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

Furthermore, consider, my son, that God himself has bestowed these two things upon man alone of all animals: namely, speech <note>original: "sermonem" — often used to translate the Greek "logos," meaning both speech and the rational principle</note> and mind. These are considered to be of the same value as immortality. Whoever uses these for what is right differs in no way from the immortals. Moreover, once such a person is released from the chains of the body, he will be led by both [speech and mind] into the chorus of the blessed and the gods.

**TAT.** Do not the other living creatures besides man use speech?

**TRIS.** No, my son, they use voice <note>original: "voce" — mere sound or vocalization as opposed to reasoned speech</note>. There is a very great difference between voice and speech. Speech is common to all humans, but voice is specific to each person, and even specific to each genus of animal.

**TAT.** Oh father, do the different races of men not use different speeches?

**TRIS.** They are different, O son. Yet humanity is one, and speech is also one; though it is translated from one place to another through interpretation, it is ultimately found to be one word <note>original: "verbum" — again, the Logos or universal meaning behind different languages</note>. This word is the same among the Egyptians, the Persians, and the Greeks. But you seem, O my son, to be ignorant of the power and greatness of the word. The blessed God, the Good Spirit <note>original: "dæmon bonus" — referring to Agathos Daimon, the personification of divine wisdom</note>, declared that the soul is in the body, the mind is in the soul, and the word is in the mind. Indeed, he asserted that God is the father of these. Thus, the word is the image and mind of God. And the body is indeed 

<column-break/>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>THE PYMANDER</header>

the [body] of the Idea <note>original: "idææ" — the formal pattern or archetype</note>, while the Idea is the [image] of the soul. The purest portion of matter is air; of air, the soul; of the soul, the mind; and finally, of the mind, God. God is around all things and through all things simultaneously. The mind is around the soul, the soul is around the air, and the air is around matter. 

Necessity, providence, and nature are the instruments <note>original: "organa" — the tools or functional parts</note> of the world and of the order of matter. For indeed, each of the intelligible things <note>original: "intelligibilium" — non-physical realities understood by the intellect rather than the senses</note> is an essence, and their essence is "the Same" <note>original: "ipsum idem" — a philosophical term for unchanging identity or unity</note>. But of those bodies which are in the world, each is a multitude. Composite bodies, possessing this "Same" yet passing into one another by turns, perpetually preserve the immortality of that same identity. In other bodies which are uncomposed, there is a number for each body. It is impossible for constitution, composition, or dissolution to occur without number. For units <note>original: "unitates" — ones or fundamental building blocks</note> beget and increase number, and when dissolved, they pull number back into themselves. 

Matter is indeed one. This whole world is a great god, the image of a greater God, united to Him, preserving the father’s order and will. It is the complete fullness of all life. Nor is there anything in it—throughout all eternity and the father’s restoration, whether looking at the whole or the part—that does not enjoy life. Nor

<summary>Hermes explains that humans are unique because of Mind and Speech (Logos), which provide a path to immortality. He describes a cosmic hierarchy where God permeates all things through a chain of Mind, soul, and matter.</summary>
<keywords>Logos, Mind, Agathos Daimon, hierarchy of being, immortality, speech vs voice, intelligible essence, cosmic order</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book Twelve of the Corpus Hermeticum. Hermes Trismegistus is explaining to his son, Tat, the nature of the cosmos as a living, divine entity where nothing truly "dies," but only undergoes change and dissolution. The catchword "rere" connects the end of page 98 ("parere") to the start of page 99 ("rere").</meta>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

There is nothing in the world that is void of life, nor was there ever, nor will there be. For its Father willed the world to be a living being for as long as it exists. Therefore, it is necessary that the world is a god. How, O son, can there be things lacking life within a god, within the image of the whole, and within the fullness of life? The deprivation of life is corruption, and corruption is destruction. In what way, then, can any part of that which is incorruptible be corrupted, or any part of God be destroyed?

**TAT.** Do not the animals that are in the world, which are parts of the world, perish, O father?

**TRIS.** Speak more correctly, my son, for you are mistaken in the term used. Nothing in the world dies; rather, composite bodies are dissolved <note>original: "dissoluuntur" — Hermes argues that what we call death is merely the breaking apart of a physical compound, not the end of existence</note>. This dissolution is not death, but the resolution of a mixture. Moreover, they are dissolved not so that the things which exist may perish, but so that they may be renewed <note>original: "iuuenescant" — literally "to become young again"</note>.

**TAT.** Since there is a certain operation of life, is this not a kind of motion? And therefore, is anything in the world immobile?

**TRIS.** Nothing, O son.

**TAT.** Does the earth not seem immobile to you?

**TRIS.** Not at all; it is agitated by many motions, yet it alone is also stable in a certain way. Would it not be ridiculous for the very nurse of all things, who conceives and brings forth every individual thing, to lack motion? For it is impossible for anything to give birth without motion.

<column-break/>

<page-num>99</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

It is no less ridiculous than to call that body sterile. For the name "immobile" signifies nothing other than something sterile. Consider this whole matter generally, O son. Whatever is in the world moves either by increasing or decreasing; and that which moves also lives. Yet it is not necessary that every living thing be the same. At the same time, the entire universe, existing as a whole, is indeed immobile <note>Hermes distinguishes between the movement of parts within the world and the stability of the universe as a totality</note>, but its parts are movable in every direction. Nothing, however, is subject to corruption; rather, certain false names disturb mankind. For the generation of life is not a "creation," but an unfolding of hidden life <note>original: "latentis explicatio uitæ"</note>; nor is change "death," but rather a concealment <note>original: "occultatio"</note>. 

Since these things are so, all things are immortal. Spirit is the life of matter, and Mind is the life of the soul. Everything that lives flows from the Mind; therefore, every living thing remains immortal through the Mind. But most immortal of all is man, who receives God and is conformed to the divine essence. For to him alone, out of every race of living beings, God himself joins company: by night through dreams, and by day more frequently through signs <note>original: "portenta"</note>, through all of which He foretells the future—through birds, through entrails <note>original: "intestina" — referring to the practice of reading animal organs for divination</note>, through the spirit, and through the Sibyl <note>A Sibyl was an ancient prophetess believed to speak the oracles of the gods</note>.

<summary>Hermes explains to Tat that nothing in the universe truly dies, but only undergoes dissolution and renewal, as the entire cosmos is a living, moving divine entity. He emphasizes that human beings are uniquely immortal because of their connection to the Divine Mind and their ability to receive divine prophecy.</summary>

<keywords>immortality, dissolution, motion, World Soul, divine essence, divination, Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, Pymander</keywords>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

...because of this, man is truly said to know what is, what was, and what is soon to come. But first, my son, I would wish you to notice that each kind of the remaining living creatures inhabits its own province of the world: the moist ones inhabit the water, the terrestrial ones the earth, and the winged ones the air. Man, however, makes use of all these: earth, water, air, and fire. He looks up at heaven and reaches it with his senses. But God is around all things and simultaneously through all things. For He is the act and the power of all. For it is a difficult thing, O son, to perceive God. Whenever you wish to look upon Him, observe the order of the world and the beauty of that order. Observe the necessity of those things which are perceived by the senses, and the providence <note>Latin: *providentiam* — the divine foresight and care governing the universe</note> of all things that were made before, and which are made daily. Observe matter, full of life. See such a great God walking with all the good and beautiful gods, spirits <note>original: *daemonibus* — in this context, these are intermediary divine beings, not evil "demons"</note>, and men.

**TAT.** But these, O father, are certain operations.

**TRIS.** Ah, my son, from where do these proceed if not from God Himself? Are you unaware that just as the parts of the world are heaven, water, earth, and air, in the same way the members of God are life, mortality, necessity, providence, nature, soul, mind, and the participation in all these,

<column-break/>

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

participation, the good itself. There is nothing anywhere, nor has anything been made, where God himself is not present.

**TAT.** So, He is in matter, father?

**TRIS.** Matter, my son, is separate only so far as a proper place may be assigned to it. What else do you think it is than a raw and unformed mass? <note>original: *rudem indigestamque congeriem* — a phrase famously used by the Roman poet Ovid to describe Chaos</note> Do not believe that this can exist unless it is shaped; and if it is shaped, it is certainly shaped by someone. For we have said that the operations of shaping are parts of God. By whom, then, is life given to each living creature? By whom are the things that are changeable changed? Whether you call it matter, or body, or essence, remember that these are the acts of God. The act of matter is the principle <term>ratio: the underlying reason or formative logic</term> of matter; the bodily act is the principle of bodies; the act of essence is the principle of essence. Therefore, God is all. And there is nothing in the whole that is not God himself. Thus, neither magnitude, nor place, nor quality, nor shape, nor even time exists around God, for He is the Whole. And the Whole is around all things and through all things. Cherish and adore this word, O son. For there is but one way to worship God: not to be evil.

### ARGUMENT OF THE THIRTEENTH BOOK.

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus explains that God is omnipresent, residing within all matter and governing the world through divine providence. He concludes that the only true way to worship the Divine is to refrain from evil, before introducing the mystery of spiritual regeneration.</summary>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

**[Creator]**, man is a son of God, by the will of the one God: *a mystery that is not taught, but is hidden in silence, and in silence is venerated by the holy ones.* You, Christian, read the rest: *and if by a new regeneration* <note>Latin: *regeneratione* — a spiritual rebirth or "being born again" into a divine state, a central theme in Hermetic philosophy</note> *in God you have been reformed through the Son of God—having driven away ignorance, sorrow, lust, injustice, luxury, deception, envy, fraud, anger, rashness, and malice, those twelve horrible avengers of darkness—and having attained the ten opposing powers of God’s piety, rejoice with your whole mind. Remember to give thanks to God the Reformer with all your mental strength; burn entirely with His love. Having been made entirely of spirit and reborn from the spirit, sing together the hymn of piety. Retain the purity of this regeneration, having turned yourself entirely toward God the Father and the author of regeneration, His Son. You have obtained this fruit from this thirteenth [discourse], or at least you have been moved by the kindness of your mind to think so piously.*

### ON THE COMMON DISCOURSE. XIII

**TAT.** In common discourses, O father, you treated the subject of divinity through certain riddles, and without the addition of any clear explanation, asserting that no one can be saved before regeneration. Indeed, while you were ascending the mountain, I stood by you then as a suppliant, praying that you would at some time disclose to me the method of regeneration. For that alone remained for me to learn. Then finally

---

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

you promised you would reveal it when I became a stranger to the world. Behold, I am now prepared, father; I have shaken off the deceptions of the world from my mind. You, therefore, keep your promises: explain the manner of regeneration, either openly or secretly, as you please. I do not know, O Trismegistus, from what matter or from what womb man is born [anew].

**TRIS.** O son, contemplative wisdom is in silence, and its seed is the True Good.

**TAT.** By whose sowing, father? For this I do not know.

**TRIS.** By the will of God, my son.

**TAT.** And what of the one who is born? For he is without share in intelligible essence <note>original: *intelligibilis essentie* — refers to a being that can be understood by the mind rather than the physical senses</note>. If he is otherwise, he who is born will be God, the Son of God.

**TRIS.** The All in all, consisting of all powers.

**TAT.** You speak in riddles, O father, and you do not speak with a son in the manner of a father.

**TRIS.** This kind [of knowledge], O son, is not taught; but whenever God wills, it is brought back into the memory by a certain suggestion.

**TAT.** You suggest things that are impossible and too forced; wherefore I desire to speak against these things.

**TRIS.** You are quite at variance with your father’s stock, Tat.

**TAT.** Do not begrudge me, father, for I am your legitimate son; come, I pray, teach me the method of regeneration.

**TRIS.** What shall I say, son? I have nothing to say beyond this: that

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

Indeed, I see a vision, offered to me now by the kindness of God: through it, I have been translated <note>Latin: *translatus* — carried across or transformed</note> into an immortal body, and I am no longer the man I was before. But having recently been made Mind <note>original: *mens* — in Hermetic thought, "Mind" is a divine faculty above mere human reason</note>, this mystery is not taught. See through this formed element, through which it is possible to see, how my first composite form has been neglected by me; not that I am colored, or have touch or limit, for I have been made a stranger to those things at present. Do you see me with your eyes, my son? For when you meditate intently with your body and sight, I am not seen by these eyes. 

<term>TAT</term> <note>The disciple and son of Hermes in these dialogues</note>: O father, you have stirred me into a frenzy and a mad sting of the mind; at present, I do not see myself. 

<term>TRIS</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary "Thrice-Greatest" sage</note>: I wish, dearest son, that you too had passed out of yourself while sleeping, like those who are occupied by dreams in their sleep. 

<term>TAT</term>: Tell me then, who will be the author of regeneration? 

<term>TRIS</term>: The Son of God, a man by the will of the one God. 

<term>TAT</term>: Now, O father, you have made me mute and senseless: I too, alienated from my former state of mind, behold that same mind along with its character and greatness, and in it, a falsehood. For the mortal form is changed every single day; it turns toward growth and decay according to the time, as it is false. What then is

<column-break/>

<page-num>105</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header> <note>Pymander, or Poimandres, is the "Shepherd of Men" and the divine intellect that instructs Hermes</note>

true, Trismegistus? 

<term>TRIS</term>: That which is not disturbed, not determined, not colored, not shaped, not divided, naked, clear, comprehensible by itself, unchangeable, good, and entirely incorporeal. 

<term>TAT</term>: I am truly going mad now, my father: for when I hoped to become wise through you, my senses were dulled by this kind of thought. 

<term>TRIS</term>: So it happens, son: for how will you perceive by sense that which is carried upward like fire, and downward like earth, and is moist like water, and breathing like air? It is not hard, not soft, not dense, not penetrable, but to be considered only by power and act. He who is able, let him pray that he may understand the generation which is in God. 

<term>TAT</term>: I am powerless, father. 

<term>TRIS</term>: Let this not be so, O son: return into yourself, and you will obtain it; will it, and it shall happen: purge the senses of the body, and free yourself from the irrational avengers <note>Latin: *ultoribus* — punishing forces or vices that haunt the soul</note> of matter itself. 

<term>TAT</term>: Are there avengers within me? 

<term>TRIS</term>: Not a few, O son, but many and horrible. 

<term>TAT</term>: I do not know them, father.

Hermes begins to list the twelve "avengers" or vices of the body, starting with Ignorance and Sorrow, which must be purged to achieve divine rebirth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Tat regarding spiritual rebirth. In the preceding text, Hermes began listing the twelve "avengers" or vices (such as ignorance, grief, and injustice) that hold the human soul captive in the "prison of the body." Here, he completes the list and explains how the ten divine powers (virtues) drive them out to complete the process of regeneration.</meta>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

...the eleventh is rashness, and the twelfth is malice. These are indeed twelve in number. Under these, many others are contained, which, through the prison of the body, force the man shut inside to suffer through his senses. However, these vices are absent from the one who is supported by the mercy of God. And thus the method and discourse of rebirth <note>original: *regenerationis* — the spiritual process of being "born again" into a divine state</note> is established. Henceforth keep silence, my son; praise God by being silent, and by that means divine mercy will never be absent from us. 

Rejoice then, my son, for by divine power you have been raised into the contemplation of truth. The knowledge <note>original: *cognitio* — likely translating the Greek *gnosis*</note> of God has descended into us: with this approaching, ignorance is driven out. The knowledge of joy has descended into us: with this present, sadness flees completely, and turns toward those who are prepared to receive it. I call the power that leads to joy "constancy," which is undoubtedly the sweetest of all. Let us therefore embrace it most willingly, son; for as soon as it arrives, it will drive out all softness. 

Now, I call the fourth power "continence" <note>original: *continentiam* — self-control or temperance</note>, the force that overcomes all desires. This step, O son, stands as the foundation of justice. Notice, moreover, how it has driven injustice out from created works. We have indeed been made just, my son, now that injustice is absent. I call the sixth power descending into us—

<meta>catchword: descending—</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

—descending, namely, communion against excess. As this [excess] leaves, I call upon truth: soon deception flees, and truth is present. See how the good is completed when truth is present: for envy has departed from us, since the good is innate to truth, together with life and light; nor did the avenger of wrath approach further. Instead, all the avengers were rejected by a sudden impulse. 

You have understood, son, the method of rebirth itself. When the number ten <note>original: *denario* — the "Decad," representing perfection and the divine powers</note> is present, the intellectual generation is composed, which rejects the number twelve <note>original: *duodenarium* — the "Dodecad," representing the twelve zodiacal vices</note>; and we have observed this from the generation itself. Whoever, therefore, because of the kindness of the rebirth which is according to God, lets go of bodily sense, knows himself to be composed of divine things, and having been made unwavering by divine power, rejoices with his whole mind. 

**TAT.** O father, I conceive this, not by the sight of eyes, but by the act of the mind, which operates through inner powers. I am in heaven, on earth, in water, in air; I am in animals, in trees; I am in the body, before the body, and after the body, and everywhere. But I would like you to tell me this further: how do the twelve avengers of darkness get repelled by the ten powers? What is the method, O Trismegistus?

**TRIS.** This tabernacle <note>A metaphor for the physical body or the human vessel</note>, O...

<summary>Hermes describes the spiritual mechanics of rebirth, explaining how ten divine virtues (the Decad) displace twelve bodily vices (the Dodecad). Tat experiences a mystical expansion of consciousness, feeling himself present in all elements of the cosmos simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>regeneration, Decad, Dodecad, gnosis, Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, virtues and vices, spiritual transformation, cosmic consciousness</keywords>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

<meta>The catchword "lus" on page 108 connects to "oculus" on page 109.</meta>

...my son, is established in the circle of the zodiac, which consists of that twelvefold number. These numbers <gloss>original: ij — likely referring to the numbers 12 and 10</gloss> are both one and all-forming according to the "ideas" <note>In the Platonic sense, "ideas" are the perfect, original patterns of all things.</note> of nature, guiding the wandering and circuit of man. Therefore, those avengers <note>The twelve vices previously listed.</note> are in a way separated from each other, yet in their actions they are in a certain way joined; for behold, rashness is inseparable from anger. It is with good reason, therefore, according to right logic, that they create a gap only so far as they are repelled by the ten powers—that is, by the number ten <note>original: *denario* — the Decad, representing divine perfection and the ten virtues.</note>. For the number ten, O son, is the parent of the soul. But where life and light are united, where the numbers of unity itself are born from the spirit, there unity according to reason embraces the ten, and in turn, the ten embraces unity.

**TAT.** Behold, father! I see the universe; I see myself in the Mind <note>original: *mente* — likely translating the Greek *Nous*, the divine intellect.</note>.

**TRIS.** But this is rebirth, my son: to no longer be present in a body measured by quantity. It is for this very reason that I have brought forth the mystery of rebirth, so that we may not be slanderers of the Whole <note>original: *totius* — the totality of the divine cosmos.</note>, standing against the many whom God himself wills.

**TAT.** Answer me this, father: Will this body, which is composed of power, ever be dissolved?

**TRIS.** Beware that you do not dare to say such things further! You propose impossibilities and err deeply; for the eye of the mind...

---

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>PYMANDER</header>

...will be profaned by such excessive impiety in speaking. The sensible body of nature is far removed from essential generation <note>original: *essentia ligeneratione* — likely a typo for *essentiali generatione*, meaning spiritual birth into true Being.</note>. One is indeed dissoluble, while the other is indissoluble; the former is mortal, the latter immortal. Or are you unaware that you are born both a god and a son of the One?

**TAT.** Oh, how I would wish, O father, to have heard that analogy through the hymn which you said you heard from the Powers while you yourself were in the eighth <note>original: *octonario* — the Ogdoad, or the eighth sphere beyond the seven planets, where the soul reaches the divine.</note>.

**TRIS.** Pymander <note>The "Shepherd of Men," the divine Mind who first revealed these truths to Hermes.</note> himself sang of the Eighth, O son, from which it is fitting for you to loosen the veil <note>original: *umbraculum* — literally a "shade" or "tent," referring to the material shell of the body.</note>. For that pure Pymander, the Mind of divine power, revealed no more to me than what has been written, thinking that I would investigate the rest myself. He exhorted me to honorable duties, through which all the powers within me sing in harmony.

**TAT.** I wish to hear and understand these things.

**TRIS.** Be still, my son; be still, I say. You shall now hear the harmonious song, the hymn of rebirth, which I would never disclose so easily unless I considered it to be for your benefit. This is not taught, but is hidden in silence. Do you see me, my dearest son? Observe each thing diligently, for it is fitting that you also, when praying under the open sky, turn your face toward the South as the sun sets, and toward the East as it rises. The entire nature of the world [receives] this...

<summary>Hermes explains that spiritual rebirth (regeneration) occurs when the ten divine virtues displace the twelve zodiacal vices, leading the soul to the "Eighth Sphere." He prepares to share a secret hymn of rebirth with Tat, emphasizing that such knowledge is kept in silence and requires specific ritual orientation toward the sun.</summary>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

<meta>catchword: words</meta>

Let [the earth] hear the hymn: listen, earth; listen, whirlwinds of rain; O forests, be silent! I am about to sing of the creator of all things, the All and the One. Listen, heavens; be still, winds; let the immortal circle of God hear this prayer. I sing now of the creator of all, the divider of the lands, the one who balances the heavens, who commands the sweet water to flow from the ocean in every direction for the nourishment of mankind, and who commands the fire to shine from above for the deeds of men and gods. Let us all together with one voice give thanks to him, who crosses the heavens and is the nature of all creation. This is the eye of the mind; he will gladly receive the blessing of the powers. O my powers, praise the One and the All! All the powers of my soul, sing in harmony with my will. Holy Knowledge <note>original: *Cognitio sancta* — likely translating the Greek *Gnosis*.</note>, which shines by your light: through you, I sing of the intelligible light and I exult in the joy of the mind. All powers, sing along with me. Constancy, sing with me. Let my Justice sing of the just through me. Let my Fellowship <note>original: *Communio* — referring to the spiritual community or the sense of universal connection.</note> praise the All itself. Through me, let Truth sing of the Truth. Let our Goodness, finally, sing of the Good. O life, O light, from you the blessing flows into us. I give thanks to you, Father, the energy <note>original: *actus* — signifying the active expression or operation of divine power.</note> of all powers. I give thanks to you, O God, the power of all energies. Your Word <note>original: *Verbum* — the *Logos* or divine reason.</note> praises you through me; through me the world of words

<column-break/>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>THE PIMANDER</header>

receives the sacrifice of words: these my powers cry out; they sing of the All; they fulfill your will. Your will proceeds from you and returns into you, the All; receive from everyone the sacrifice of words <note>original: *sacrificium uerborum* — this is the "rational sacrifice" (*logikē thysia*), where the seeker offers praise and prayer instead of animal blood.</note>. O Life, save all that is within me. O Light, illuminate the Whole. Spirit is God. Your Word drives me, O spirit-bearing craftsman. You alone are God. This your Man <note>Hermetic terminology for the perfected, divine human essence that has been reborn.</note> cries out through fire, through air, through water, and through earth, through spirit, and through all created things. I have found blessing from eternity, and for what I desire, I shall find rest in your will. 

**TAT.** I have come to know this hymn, father, sung by you from the impulse of your will, through which I have reached my own world. 

**TRIS.** Say the "intelligible world" <note>original: *mundum intelligibilem* — the realm of pure thought and divine ideas, perceived by the mind rather than the senses.</note>, my son. 

**TAT.** I say the "intelligible world," father. Indeed, my mind has been illuminated by your song, with the result that I myself also burn with the love of singing. 

**TRIS.** O my son Tat, do not sing divine praises without a certain purpose. 

**TAT.** I shall sing from the mind, my father. 

**TRIS.** Those things which I observe, I pour into you, O Tat, progenitor of generation <note>A paradoxical title, suggesting Tat has now become a source of spiritual life himself.</note>. 

**TAT.** I offer sacrifices of words to the God of words. O God, you are Father, you are Lord, you are Mind; receive the sacrifices of words which you require from me. By your will all things are perfected. 

**TRIS.** Offer, my son, a sacrifice pleasing to God the Father, and to the prayer...

<summary>This passage presents the "Secret Hymn" of Hermes, a poetic and liturgical celebration of the "sacrifice of words" used to worship the Creator. It marks the climax of Tat's spiritual transformation as his mind is illuminated and he begins to perceive the divine, "intelligible" world.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the dialogue on spiritual rebirth between Hermes and Tat and begins the fourteenth discourse, addressed to a different student (traditionally Ammon or Asclepius). # MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS <note>Mercury is the Latin name for the Greek god Hermes, who was identified with the Egyptian Thoth to become Hermes Trismegistus.</note>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

...be intent on your own. **TAT.** I give thanks to you, father, because you have willed to preach these things to me. **TRIS.** I rejoice, my son, that I see you bringing back many good things from the truth: which are surely immortal works. Learning this from me, proclaim the silence of virtue, sharing the mystery of regeneration with no one, lest we be considered as certain slanderers. For each of us has meditated sufficiently: I indeed discussing it, and you learning it thoroughly, from which you have known yourself and the father.

->THE ARGUMENT OF THE FOURTEENTH<-

*The fourteenth discourse asserts these things: That those things which are perceived by the senses have all been made and are continually being made. For just as they flow from another, who rules over all things as the Almighty, so also he continually sustains them, lest by their own nature, prone to falling, they should rush into nothingness: the knowledge of whom alone makes one happy and blessed. This is God, the maker of all, and the father; he himself is the sole begetter, and all other things are begotten. Between the begetter and the begotten no middle ground can be interposed: and these two are the universe. For all things are two: the begetter and the begotten. Furthermore, he asserts this: that evil does not depend on God, just as rust does not depend on the blacksmith, but follows the works created. And from human art, he raises us to conceive of divine art, and that God himself brings immortality to heaven, change to the earth, and life and motion to the whole world.*

<meta>catchword: THE FOURTEENTH</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>113</page-num>
<header>THE FOURTEENTH</header>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial Q featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or deity, within a square frame with floral motifs.</image-desc>

SINCE my son Tat, in your absence, wished to learn the nature <term>natura: the inherent character and structure of the cosmos</term> of all things, and since he wished to question me as a younger man and one recently approaching the knowledge of each thing, I was compelled to say many things so that his insight might be easy and unhindered. I have judged it worthwhile to select the better points from many and now repeat the preceding discussion to you in summary: for it is permitted to run through certain mysteries with you, as one skilled in the nature of things. All things perceived by the senses have been made, and are made daily. Things begotten <note>original: *genita* — things that are born or brought into existence.</note> are not made by themselves, but by another. Many are the things begotten, each powerful in sense, and differing, nor entirely similar. Whatever things are brought forth proceed from another. There is, therefore, some maker <note>original: *factor*</note> of these things, and he is unbegotten <note>original: *ingenitus* — meaning he was not created by anyone else.</note>, so that he is older than the things begotten: for we have said that begotten things flow from another. However, nothing except the unbegotten can be older than the begotten. The Maker is indeed more powerful, and unique, and alone truly wise in all things, since nothing at all precedes him. He also rules over the multitude, the magnitude, the continuity of action, and the difference of all begotten things. Furthermore, begotten things are visible, but he is invisible: and for this reason he acts, so that he may become visible. He is therefore always acting.

<sig>H</sig> <note>This is a signature mark for the printer to keep the pages in order during binding.</note>

<vocab>Hermes Trismegistus, Tat, regeneration, mystery, begetter, begotten, nature of things, unbegotten maker</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the lesson on spiritual secrecy and introduces a summary of the relationship between the Creator (the unbegotten maker) and the physical world (the begotten things), arguing that the Creator must be invisible and eternal to produce the visible and changing universe.</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, divinity, creation, spiritual silence, nature, creator vs created, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the fourteenth discourse of the Hermetic corpus, focusing on the relationship between God as the Creator and the physical world as His creation. It addresses the ontological necessity of both "begetter" and "begotten" and attempts to explain the presence of evil as an accidental byproduct of material existence rather than a divine creation.</meta>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"; Mercury is the Latin name for Hermes, the legendary sage to whom these texts are attributed.</note>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

It is worthwhile to understand, and through understanding to wonder, and through wondering to call oneself blessed, recognizing one’s true father. For what is sweeter than a true father? Who is he? How shall we find him? Is it fitting to attribute to him alone the title of God, or of Creator, or of Father—or perhaps all three titles together? Call him **God** because of his power; **Creator** <note>original: "factor"; literally "maker" or "doer."</note> because of his action; and finally **Father** because of his goodness. For his power is very different from the things it has produced; but his action consists in the production of all things. Therefore, having set aside the variety and vanity of speech, one must meditate primarily on these two: the <term>begetter</term> <note>Latin: "genitorem"; the one who brings something into being.</note> and the <term>begotten</term> <note>Latin: "genitum"; the thing brought into being, specifically the universe.</note>. Between these two there is no middle ground, nor is there anything else besides them. Truly, when you wish to understand all things, remember these two, and keep in mind that they are the whole of existence; nothing will be ambiguous to you, neither things above nor things below, whether divine or changeable, whether manifest or those things that lie hidden in the darkness. For all things are surely these two: the begetter and the begotten, and one cannot be severed from the other. For it is not possible for there to be a creator without a thing created: each of them exists as that very thing. Therefore, neither may be separated from the other, just as neither 

<column-break/>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>THE PYMANDER <note>The "Pymander" or "Poimandres" is the title of the first book of the Hermetic corpus, often used to refer to the collection as a whole; it represents the "Shepherd of Men" or Divine Mind.</note></header>

can be divided from itself. For if the creator is himself, and there is nothing else besides him that he makes, and he is simple <note>In a philosophical sense, "simple" means not made of parts, indivisible and pure.</note>, then as he always is, so he is always acting; he is the same to himself both in existing and in acting. Furthermore, nothing that is begotten is begotten by itself; therefore, it is not made apart from the one who makes it. He who removes one of these loses the other as well, for the nature of the one always regards the specific nature of the other. If, therefore, these two are granted—the producer and the thing produced—they are indeed united to one another, yet in such a way that the one precedes and the other follows. God the producer certainly precedes, and that which is produced follows, whatever that thing may finally be. Nor should anyone distrust what we have said, being terrified by the variety of things, as if the construction of so many diverse objects were either difficult or unworthy of the divine majesty. For the only glory of God is the constitution of all things, and this creation is, as it were, the body of God. From the creator himself, nothing is evil, and nothing is shameful. For these are <term>passions</term> <note>In ancient philosophy, "passions" are things that happen to or affect a substance, often implying a lack of control or a secondary effect.</note> that follow upon created works, just as rust follows bronze, or mud follows living bodies. But the blacksmith did not introduce the rust, nor did the begetter of the living body introduce the filth and dirt. In the same way, God did not create evil. Rather, the continuation of the process of generation <note>The physical process of being born and changing over time.</note> allows evil to persist...

<summary>This text explains the inseparable relationship between God as the Begetter and the universe as the Begotten, arguing that a creator cannot exist without a creation. It uses the analogies of rust on bronze and mud on bodies to argue that evil is an accidental byproduct of material existence, not an intentional creation of God.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Pymander, Creator and Creation, Begetter and Begotten, Origin of Evil, Divine Majesty, Ontology, Hermeticism</keywords>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS, PYMANDER <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI, PYMANDER"; Mercury is the Latin name for Hermes. "Pymander" refers to the Divine Mind or the "Shepherd of Men" who reveals these truths.</note>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS, PYMANDER</header>

<sig>H 3</sig>

[God] compels [decay] to creep in, and for that reason God instituted change in things, as if it were a certain cleansing of <term>generation</term> <note>Latin: "generationis"; the process of coming into being or the physical world of birth and death.</note> itself. Furthermore, if a painter is permitted to depict the sky, the earth, the sea, gods, men, beasts, trees, and things lacking life, shall the one God lack the power to do such things? O madman, O blind one, and one devoid of all divine knowledge! Nothing is more ridiculous, O <term>Aesculapius</term> <note>The Latin name for Asklepios, the Greek god of medicine, here portrayed as a disciple of Hermes.</note>, than what happens to this man <note>The "man" refers to someone who thinks God is too lofty to be involved in the material world.</note>. For while he claims to worship God by freeing Him from the labor and care of creating, he utterly ignores God Himself. And what is worse, he ascribes to Him the passions of mortals: envy, pride, ignorance, and weakness. For unless He makes all things, He is either proud or rather weak; both of which are wicked thoughts. For God has a single and proper nature, and that is the Good itself. But one who is good is neither proud nor powerless. For God is the Good itself. Indeed, the Good is the very power of doing all things. Whatever is begotten is begotten by God—that is, by the Good, and by Him who can do all things. See how He acts, and how those things are made. If you wish to understand this, you may look upon a very beautiful and most similar image. I pray you, look at the farmer sowing seeds into the lap of the earth,

<column-break/>

sowing here wheat, there barley, and elsewhere seeds of other kinds. See him digging around and pruning vines, apple trees, and fig trees. In the same way, God Himself sows immortality in heaven, change upon the earth, and finally life and motion into the whole world.

# ASCLEPIUS,
## THE DIALOGUE OF HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "HERMETIS TRISMEGISTI"; Hermes "the Thrice-Greatest," a legendary sage who was thought to have lived in ancient Egypt and discovered the secrets of the universe.</note>,
## AS TRANSLATED BY LUCIUS APULEIUS
## OF MADAURA, A PLATONIC
## PHILOSOPHER. <note>Apuleius (c. 124–170 AD) was a famous Roman writer best known for "The Golden Ass." Historically, he was credited with this Latin translation of the Greek original.</note>

<image-desc>A large historiated initial 'A' depicting two human figures, possibly celestial or mythological beings, flanking the letter. At the bottom of the initial is a decorative lattice or net-like pattern.</image-desc>
**A**SCLEPIUS, that man is like a Sun-god to me. God has brought you to us, O Asclepius, so that you might be present for a divine discourse, and indeed one which—by a sense of religious devotion—rightly seems more divine than any of those we have previously produced, or which were inspired in us by divine gift. If you perceive this with an understanding mind, you will be most fully filled with all good things. But since there are many "goods," and not just one in which all things reside—for one is recognized as belonging to another—it follows that all things belong to the One, or that the One is all things. For the one is so connected to the other that the two cannot be separated. But you will come to know this through diligent attention in the coming discourse. Now, O Asclepius, go forward a little and call for the one who should be present. <note>Hermes is asking Asclepius to fetch Tat, his son and another disciple.</note> When he had entered, Asclepius also
<meta>catchword: Ammon</meta>

---

<header>ASCLEPIUS.</header>
<page-num>119</page-num>

suggested that Ammon <note>Ammon, often associated with the Egyptian god Amun, is the third disciple in this sacred circle.</note> should be present. Trismegistus said: "No envy prevents Ammon from being with us, for indeed we remember many things written by us in his name, just as we wrote many things to our most beloved and good son regarding <term>physics</term> <note>original: "physica"; in this context, it refers to the study of the natural world and the laws of the universe.</note> and very many things regarding <term>ethics</term> <note>original: "ethica"; the study of moral conduct and the soul's character.</note>. This treatise, however, I shall ascribe to your name. But call no one else besides Ammon, lest the most religious discourse on so great a matter be violated by the intervention and presence of many people coming in. For it is the mark of an irreligious mind to publish to the consciousness of the many a treatise that is most full of the majesty of the entire divinity." 

When Ammon also had entered the <term>adytum</term> <note>original: "adytum"; the innermost, most sacred part of a temple, restricted to priests.</note>, and that holy place was filled with the devotion of the four men <note>The four men are Hermes, Asclepius, Tat, and Ammon.</note> and the divine presence of God, then—amidst a fitting and venerable silence, with the hearts and minds of each man hanging upon his words—divine Love <note>original: "Cupido"; referring here to a divine inspiration or the personification of the desire for spiritual truth.</note> began to speak through the mouth of Hermes in this way:

> "O Asclepius, every human soul is immortal: but not all are so in a uniform way; rather, they differ by manner or by time." 
> 
> ASCLEPIUS: "But is not every soul of a single quality, O Trismegistus?" 
> 
> TRISMEGISTUS: "O Asclepius, how quickly you have fallen away from the true consistency of reason! For I did not say that 'all things are one and one is all things' in such a way that they were [identical] in the creator..."

The text begins to explore the relationship between the One (God) and the many, specifically addressing the nature and immortality of the human soul.</summary>

<meta>This continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius. Having established that the One is all things, Hermes now explains the hierarchy of the universe, the movement of the elements, and how the divine influence flows down from the heavens into the physical world.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>
# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"; Here the Latin uses the name Mercury, the Roman equivalent of the Greek Hermes.</note>

...all things, before he had created all things. Nor is he wrongly called "all," for all things are his members. Therefore, throughout this entire discussion, take care to remember him who is the One-All, or who is himself the creator of all. From heaven, all things descend into the earth, into the water, and into the air. Only fire, because it is carried upwards, is life-giving; that which moves downward is subservient to it. But truly, whatever descends from the heights is generative; that which emanates upwards is nourishing. <note>Hermes is describing a cosmic "breath" or cycle where energy moves up and down between the heavens and the earth.</note> The earth alone, standing firm in itself, is the receiver of all things, and the restorer of all the kinds of things it receives. This whole, therefore—as you remember—is either of all things or is all things. The soul and the world, being grasped by nature, are set in motion, varied by such a multiform quality of images that infinite species of qualities are known to exist through their differences; yet they are united to this end: that the whole might appear as one, and that all things might seem to come from the one. Thus, the elements by which the whole world was formed are four: namely, fire, water, earth, and air. There is one world, one soul, and one God. Now, be present for me with all your being—as much as your mind is able, and as much as your subtle skill allows. For the reasoning of the Divinity, which must be known by a divine intention of the senses, is most like a torrential river, headlong and falling from the heights with a grasping...

<meta>catchword: rapacity</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
# ASCLEPIUS

...rapacity <note>original: "rapacitate"; meaning a swift, forceful pulling or seizing motion.</note> in its current, which causes it to bypass the attention not only of the listeners, but even of those who treat the subject themselves, because of its swift velocity. Therefore, Heaven is a <term>sensible god</term> <note>A "sensible god" (deus sensibilis) refers to a divinity that can be perceived by the physical senses—specifically the visible sky, stars, and planets.</note> and the administrator of all bodies, whose increases and decreases are allotted to the Sun and the Moon. But of Heaven itself, and of the soul, and of all things that are in the world, the governor is he who is the maker of all things: God. For from all the things mentioned above, of which the same God is the governor, there comes a frequent influence flowing through the world and through the soul of all kinds and all species, and through the nature of things. The world, moreover, was prepared by God as a receptacle for all-formed species. Nature, then, depicting species through the world, has led them through the four elements all the way to heaven, so that all things might be pleasing to the sight of God. All things hanging from above are divided into species in the following manner. The <term>genera</term> <note>Genera (singular: genus) refer to the broad, overarching categories or "families" of being.</note> of all things follow their own species, so that "solidity" becomes a genus, while a "species" is a small part of that genus. Therefore, the genus of the gods, the genus of demons, and likewise the genus of humans, and similarly of birds, and of all things which the world contains within itself, generates species similar to itself. There is also a genus...

<summary>Hermes explains that the universe is a unified system where God is the "All," and influence flows from heaven down through the four elements. <page-num>122</page-num>

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>

...[is] another kind of living thing, a <term>genus</term><note>The Latin *genus* refers to a broad "category" or "kind" of being.</note> indeed without a soul, yet not lacking in senses. For this reason, it rejoices in benefits and is diminished or corrupted by adversity. I speak of all those things that live through the health of their roots and stems throughout the earth, whose <term>species</term><note>The Latin *species* refers to the specific "form" or "appearance" of a thing.</note> are scattered across the whole world. The heaven itself is full of God. Moreover, the aforementioned kinds of beings inhabit their places down to the locations of their forms, and the forms of all these things are immortal. 

For a "form" is a part of a "kind," just as a human is a part of humanity; it must necessarily follow the quality of its kind. From this, it follows that although all kinds are immortal, not all forms are immortal. For the kind of the Divinity—both the kind itself and its forms—are immortal. 

As for the other kinds, whose eternity lies in the kind itself, although the kind may perish through its individual forms, it is nonetheless preserved by the fertility of birth. Therefore, forms are mortal; thus, a human is mortal, but <note>original: "immortalis humanitas"</note> humanity is immortal. Yet, the forms of all kinds are mixed among all kinds—some which were made before, and some which were made from these. 

Thus, the things that are made are made either by gods, or by <term>demons</term><note>In this context, *daimones* are not "evil spirits," but intermediary spiritual beings that bridge the gap between gods and humans.</note>, or by humans; and all these forms are similar to their respective kinds. For it is impossible for bodies to be shaped without the divine will, or for forms to be 

<column-break/>

<page-num>123</page-num>

## ASCLEPIUS

to be shaped without the help of demons, or for animals to be established and tended without humans. 

Therefore, whichever demons, flowing down from their own kind, are joined by chance into a form, they are considered similar to gods because of their proximity and <note>original: "cõfortio" (consortio)</note> fellowship with some form of the divine kind. Truly, those demons whose forms remain within the quality of their own kind are called "demons who love humans," for they possess a reason similar to that of humans, or even greater. 

The form of the human kind is multiform and varied; arriving from the aforementioned fellowship from above, it creates many connections—almost out of necessity—with nearly all other forms. Because of this, the human who has joined himself to the gods through divine religion—using the mind by which he is linked to the gods—approaches close to the gods. He also approaches the demons to whom he is joined. Truly, those humans who are content with the middle-ground of their kind, and all the remaining forms, will be similar to those things with whose forms they have joined themselves.

Because of this, O Asclepius, **man is a great miracle**, a living thing to be adored and honored. For he passes into the nature of God as if he were God himself. He knows the kind of the demons, as he recognizes that he originated from the same source. He even despises the part of human nature within himself,
