# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>

...relying on the divinity of his other part. O, how great is the nature of humans! When happily tempered, a person is joined to the gods by a kindred divinity. In that part of himself where he is earthly, he looks down upon all other things <note>The "other things" refers to the purely material or animal world.</note>; yet those same things, to which he knows himself to be necessary by celestial arrangement, he binds to himself with a bond of love. Thus, he looks up toward heaven. 

In this way, he is placed in the happier position of a <term>midpoint</term><note>This refers to the "Great Chain of Being," where humans act as the bridge between the spiritual and material worlds.</note>, so that he may love what is below him and be loved by those who are above him. He cultivates the earth; he mingles with the elements by his swiftness; by the sharpness of his mind, he descends into the depths of the sea. All things are open to him. Heaven does not seem too high for him, for he looks into it by the sagacity of his spirit as if from nearby. No mist of the air confuses the focus of his spirit; the density of the earth does not hinder his work; the deep altitude of the water does not blunt his vision. He is all things, and he is the same everywhere. 

Of all these kinds that are living beings, some have roots reaching down from above; others are like living beings from the bottom that grow wild into the heights by a living root <note>This imagery suggests a hierarchy where some beings are rooted in heaven and reach toward earth, while others (like plants/animals) are rooted in earth and reach toward the divine.</note>. Some are nourished by double elements, others by simple ones. Nourishment is twofold: for the soul and for the body, from which living beings are composed. The <term>World Soul</term> <note>original: "Anima mundi"; the vital spirit that animates the entire universe.</note> is nourished by restless, perpetual movement. Bodies are nourished by the water and earth of the lower world

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>

...if, however, I speak of all sensible things, and also of the spirit, then I will provide the whole account to you. For man alone is a living being <note>The Latin 'animal' here refers to any 'animated' or living being.</note>, and one part of him is simple: that which, as the Greeks say, is **essential** <term>original: οὐσιώδης (ousiodes); meaning a thing's true, spiritual substance</term>, or what we call the form of divine likeness. However, man is fourfold: we call the other part "worldly," out of which the body was made. This body acts as a covering for that which we have already said is divine in man. In this divine part, the upright divinity of the mind may rest within itself, alone with its kindred—that is, the senses of the pure mind—as if enclosed by the wall of the body.

**ASCLEPIUS.** Why then, O Trismegistus, was it necessary for man to be established in the world, and not to spend his life in that part where God is, in the highest blessedness?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** You ask rightly, O Asclepius. For we too pray to God that he might grant us the ability to provide an account for this. Since all things depend on his will, this is especially true of those things which concern the highest matters, which is the very account we seek in this present discussion. 

Listen then, Asclepius. The Lord and Shaper of all, whom we rightly call God, made from himself a "second" being who could be seen and felt. I call this being the "second sensible god" <note>In Hermetic philosophy, the physical Universe itself is often called the 'Second God.'</note>—not because this being itself perceives (for whether it perceives or not, we shall discuss at another time), but because it meets the senses of those who look upon it.

<meta>catchword: perceives,</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>127</page-num>

# ASCLEPIUS

Since, therefore, God made this world first from himself and second after himself, and it appeared beautiful to him—as it is most full of the goodness of all things—he loved it as a part of his own divinity. Therefore, because the world was so great and so good, God willed that there be another being who could gaze upon that world which he had made from himself. At that same time, he created man to be an imitator of his reason and his care. 

For the will of God is itself the highest perfection, since he completes both the willing and the perfecting in one and the same point of time. Thus, when the **essential** <term>original: οὐσιώδης (ousiodes)</term> human noticed that he could not be a caretaker of all things unless he were covered with a worldly garment, God covered him in a bodily home. He commanded all humans to be such, blending and mixing both natures into one, in the amount that was sufficient. 

Thus, he shaped man from soul and body—that is, from an eternal and a mortal nature—so that the living being thus shaped might be able to satisfy both of its origins: to wonder at and pray to celestial and eternal things, and to inhabit and govern earthly things. Now, when I say "mortal things," I do not mean the water and the earth (the two of the four elements which nature has made subject to humans), but rather those things which are produced by humans, either within themselves or from them...

<page-num>128</page-num>

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>

...come to be, such as the cultivation of the earth itself, pastures, buildings, ports, voyages, communications, and mutual exchanges—which is the firmest bond of humanity <term>original: humanitas; meaning here the shared social and civilized nature of the human race</term>. That part of the world which is water and earth is preserved by the knowledge and use of arts and sciences; without these, God did not wish the world to be complete. For necessity follows God’s decree, and the result accompanies his will. Nor is it believable that what pleased God will ever displease him, since he knew long beforehand both that it would exist and that it would please him.

But O Asclepius, I notice that you are rushing with a swift desire of mind to hear how man can have affection for, or worship of, heaven and the things within it. Listen then, O Asclepius: the affection for the God of heaven, and for all the things within it, is a constant practice of religious service. No other living being—neither divine beings nor animals—does this, except man alone. Indeed, heaven and the heavenly beings are delighted by the admiration, adorations, praises, and services of men. And not without merit has the choir of the Muses <note>The nine Greek goddesses of the arts and sciences; here described as a gift from God to make the world more beautiful.</note> been sent down by the highest divinity into the assembly of men, so that the earthly world should not seem too uncultivated if it lacked the sweetness of melodies; but rather, that it might be celebrated with the songs and praises of men, who 

<column-break/>

<page-num>129</page-num>

# ASCLEPIUS <note>In the original text, the header changes to the name of the student, Asclepius, though Hermes continues to speak.</note>

alone [praise] all things, or who is the father of all; and thus, through these heavenly praises, the sweetness of harmony would not be lacking even on earth. 

However, some few, endowed with a pure mind, have been allotted the venerable task of looking up at the sky <note>This refers to the practice of contemplation and astronomy/astrology as a form of worship.</note>. Yet those who, because of the dual confusion of their nature, have weighed down their inner intelligence with the mass of the body, are appointed to care for the elements and these lower things. Therefore, as a living being, man is not lesser because he is partly mortal; but perhaps he is even more aptly and effectively composed, appearing to have been enhanced by mortality for a specific purpose. This is because he could not have sustained both roles if he were made from only one material; instead, he was formed from both so that he might be able to care for earthly things and maintain his love for divinity.

O Asclepius, I desire you to grasp the reasoning of this discourse not only with keen attention but also with a liveliness of spirit. For this reasoning is unbelievable to many, but it is to be grasped in its whole and true form by the holiest minds. Therefore, I shall begin from here: 

The Lord of Eternity, God, is first. 
The World is second. 
Man is third. 

God is the maker of the world and of all things within it, governing all things together, while man is established as the governor of those things composed [on earth].

<page-num>130</page-num>

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>

Taking all this upon himself, man accomplishes his task through his own diligent care, so that both he and the world may serve as ornaments for each other. Because of this divine composition of man, the world seems more correctly called *kosmos* in Greek <term>kosmos: a Greek word meaning both "the universe" and "orderly beauty" or "ornament"</term>. He knows himself and he knows the world, so that he may remember what is fitting for his own parts; he recognizes which things he should use and which things he should serve. Offering the greatest praises and thanks to God, he venerates God's image, being well aware that he himself is the second image of God—for God has two images: the world and man. 

Consequently, because he is a single composite being, man seems able to ascend to heaven in that part which is divine—composed of soul, sense, spirit, and reason, as if from the higher elements. Yet in his worldly part, which consists of fire, water, and air, he remains mortal on earth, so that he may not leave all the tasks entrusted to his care abandoned and deserted. For in this way humanity was made partly divine and partly mortal, existing within a body. The measure of both these parts in man is, above all, religion, which is followed by goodness. This goodness only appears to be perfect if it is fortified by virtue and despises the desire for all things that are foreign to it. For there are

<column-break/>

<page-num>131</page-num>

# ASCLEPIUS

alien to all parts of divine kinship all those things which people possess through earthly, bodily desire. These are rightly called "possessions," because they were not born with us, but were only later possessed by us. Therefore, all things of this kind are alien to man—even the body itself—so that we may despise both the things we crave and that part of us from which the vice of craving arises. 

For as the focus of my reason leads my mind, man's purpose ought to be this: that through the contemplation of divinity, he should look down upon and despise that mortal part joined to him, which exists because of the necessity of maintaining the lower world. Indeed, so that man might be complete in both parts, observe that he has been formed with four elements for each part: he has two hands and two feet, along with other bodily members, so that he may serve the lower, earthly world. And for the other part, he has the four qualities of the mind—the senses, memory, and foresight <note>original: *providentia*; the capacity to look ahead or plan</note>—by which he may know and look up to all things divine. From this it follows that man pursues the differences, qualities, effects, and quantities of things with a searching investigation. Yet, slowed down by the heavy and excessive vice of the body, he cannot fully perceive these causes of nature which are truly his own...

<page-num>132</page-num>

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>

Therefore, for one who has been so fashioned and shaped, and appointed to such a service and obedience by the highest God—provided they keep the world pure and clean, and worship God with piety and dignity, obeying the divine will in both aspects—what reward do you believe is due to such a person? Since the world is the work of God, and the person who diligently preserves and increases its beauty joins their own effort with the will of God—arranging with the help of their body that beauty which God formed through divine intention—what reward do you believe they deserve? It can be none other than the one our ancestors received, which we also hope to receive through our most pious prayers, if it pleases the divine mercy: that is, having completed their service <note>original: "emeritos"</note> and being stripped of their worldly guardianship, released from mortal bonds, God restores them—pure and holy—to the nature of their higher part, which is the divine.

# ASCLEPIUS

You speak justly and truly, O Trismegistus. For this is the reward for those who live piously under God and diligently with the world. For those who live otherwise and impiously, their return to heaven is denied, and they are sentenced to a foul migration <term>migration: the movement of the soul into other bodies, often referred to as reincarnation or metempsychosis</term> into other bodies unworthy of a holy soul. According to how this reasoned discourse has proceeded, O Trismegistus, the souls in this human life are endangered by the hope of the future

of future eternity. But to some this seems unbelievable, to others a mere fable, and to others perhaps something to be ridiculed. For in this bodily life, the fruit gathered from possessions is a sweet thing. For this reason, it holds the soul fast "by a twisted neck," <note>original: "obtorto... collo," a Latin idiom for being dragged or held by force</note> as they say, so that it clings to that part of itself which is mortal. How then do many people make philosophy incomprehensible, or in what way do they confuse it with various sorts of reasoning? 

# TRISMEGISTUS

O Asclepius, they do it in this way: by mixing it, through clever commentary, into various disciplines that are not easily grasped, such as Arithmetic, Music, and Geometry. <header>HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note></header>

...the depths of the sea <note>The text begins mid-word: "maris profunda"</note>, and the power of fire, and knowing the effects and nature of all these things, he may marvel at, adore, and praise the art and the divine mind. Truly, to understand **Music** is nothing else than to know the order of all things, and what divine ratio <note>or "reason"</note> they have been allotted. For the order of individual things, gathered into one by the reason of the Artificer <note>the Creator or Demiurge</note>, will produce a certain harmony, sweet-sounding and most true, in a divine melody.

**ASCLEPIUS.** What then will humans be after us?

**TRISMEGISTUS.** Deceived by the cunning of sophists <term>sophists: here referring to those who use clever but fallacious arguments to obscure the truth</term>, they will be turned away from true, pure, and holy philosophy. For to worship divinity with a simple mind and soul, to venerate its works, and also to give thanks to the will of God—which alone is most full of goodness—this is the philosophy unviolated by any meddlesome curiosity of the mind. And let this be enough on these subjects. Concerning the spirit and similar matters, let the beginning be taken here.

There was God and **Matter** <note>original: "hyle", a Greek term for the primordial substance of the universe</note>, which we believe to be the world; and spirit accompanied the world, but not in the same way it accompanies God. Nor are these things from which the world is made actually God. Therefore, they did not exist when they had not yet been born, but even then they were already within that source from which they were meant to be born. For not only are those things called "unborn" which have not yet been born, but also those which lack the fertility of generating,

<column-break/>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>ASCLEPIUS</header>
<sig>I 4</sig>

generating <note>original: "nerandi," completing "generandi" from the previous page</note>, so that nothing can be born from them. Therefore, whatever things exist in which the nature of generating resides are "generable," from which things can be born, even if they were born from themselves <note>referring to self-caused or eternal substances</note>. For it will not be doubted that from those things which were born of themselves, other things can easily be born, since they are the source from which all things arise.

Therefore, God, the everlasting, the eternal, neither can be born nor could have been; He is, He always was, He will be forever. This, therefore, is the nature of God: that which is entirely from itself. However, **Matter** <note>original: "Hyle"</note>, or the nature of the world, and spirit, although they seem to be born from the beginning, nevertheless possess from within themselves the power of being born and procreating, and the nature of fertility. For the beginning is in the quality of nature, which possesses in itself the power and the substance of conception and birth. This nature, therefore, is the only thing generable without the conception of another.

But truly, those things which have only the power of conceiving—arising from the connection with another nature—must be distinguished so that this "Place" of the world, with those things that are in it, appears to be "unborn," for it certainly contains within itself the power of all nature. Now, I call it "**Place**" in which all things are. For all these things could not have existed if Place were lacking, which is able to sustain all things. For all things that have existed, care must be taken for their place; for neither qualities, nor quantities, nor

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note></header>
<meta>catchword: for the <note>original: "enim"</note></meta>

...positions, nor could the effects of those things which do not exist have been discerned. Thus, therefore, the world as well—although it was not born <note>The author suggests the world is eternal or uncreated in its essential substance, even if its forms change</note>—nevertheless contains within itself the nature of all things, seeing as it provides most fertile wombs for the conception of all things. This, then, is the totality of quality. And **Matter** <term>Matter: here "creabilis" or "creatable," meaning it has the potential to be shaped into forms</term> is capable of being created, even though it has not [strictly] been created. For just as the quality of fertility is inherent in the nature of matter, so too is the nature of malignancy equally fertile.

Nor did I say, O Asclepius and Ammon, what is said by many: that God could not have cut off and turned away malice <note>original: "malitiam," referring to evil or harmful intent in the natural order</note> from the nature of things. To these people, no answer at all need be given. Nevertheless, for your sake, I shall follow up on the cause and the argument I had begun, and I will provide the reason. For they themselves say that God ought to have liberated the world from malice in every way. Yet [malice] is in the world in such a way that it seems to be like a limb of the world itself, provided for and guarded against rationally by the supreme God—specifically at that time when He deigned to gift the minds of humans with discipline, perception, and intelligence. For by these things, in which we stand above the other animals, we alone are able to avoid the frauds, deceits, and vices of malice. For he who avoids these things by sight before he is entangled in them, that man is fortified by divine intelligence and prudence. For the foundation

<column-break/>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>ASCLEPIUS</header>
<sig>I 5</sig>

of discipline consists in the highest goodness. Moreover, all things in the world are served and quickened <note>or "given life," original: "uegetantur"</note> by the **Spirit**, and the world is subjected to the will of the supreme God like a tool or a machine. 

Therefore, let the Supreme be understood by us thus far through the mind alone; He is the **Intelligible One** <note>original: "intelligibilis," referring to the God known through the intellect rather than the senses</note>, the ruler and governor of the **Sensible God** <term>Sensible God: the physical, visible universe, which the Hermetic tradition often views as a second, created divinity</term>—that is, the one who embraces within himself every place, every substance of things, the entire matter of things being born and created, and everything whatever it is, however great it is. Truly, every species in the world is moved or governed by the Spirit, each according to its own nature distributed to it by God. 

Now, <term>Hyle: the Greek term for primordial matter</term> <note>original: "Hyle"</note> or the world is the receptacle of all things, and the site of the movement and frequenting of all things, of which God is the governor, dispensing to all human affairs as much as is necessary for each. The Spirit fills all things, of whatever nature their quality may be. For the hollow roundness of the world is in the manner of a sphere, itself its own cause of its quality or form, and entirely invisible in its totality. Indeed, since whatever highest point you choose within it for the sake of looking down, you could not see from there what is at the bottom; because of this, it is present in many places and is believed to have quality. For through forms, only by appearance...

<summary>Hermes explains the problem of evil, arguing that God provided humans with intelligence and "discipline" to navigate a world where malice is an inherent part of the structure. He distinguishes between the "Intelligible God" (the supreme mind) and the "Sensible God" (the physical cosmos), both of which are animated by a universal Spirit.</summary>

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>
### Page 138

...of which, because it appears engraved with their images, it is believed to be visible, since it is shown depicted. But in truth it is always invisible to itself, since its place or part is in the sphere which in Greek is called *aeidēs* <note>original: ἀειδής, meaning "unseen" or "invisible," often used as an epithet for Hades</note>. Indeed, *eidein* <note>original: εἰδεῖν</note> is the Greek word for "to see," and because the primal parts of the sphere lack sight, they are called species for the reason that they are invisible forms. Therefore, from the fact that they are deprived of sight, they are called *aeidēs* in Greek; and from the fact that they are in the bottom of the sphere, they are called the "Underworld" <note>original: "inferi"</note> in Latin. These, therefore, are the principal and more ancient things, and as it were the beginnings or heads of all things that are in these, or through these, or from these.

**ASCLE.** Are all these things, then, which you call "of the world" <note>original: "mundana"</note>, O Trismegistus, the total substance of all the species contained within each thing?

**TRIS.** The world, then, nourishes bodies; the spirit nourishes souls; and the sense—by which heavenly gift humanity alone is blessed—nourishes the mind. Nor are all people so blessed, but only those few whose minds are such that they can be capable of so great a benefit. For as the world is lit by the Sun, so the human mind shines by that divine light, and even more so. For whatever the sun illuminates is sometimes deprived of its light by the intervention of the earth and the moon when night comes; but when Sense has once been mingled with the human 

<meta>catchword: soul,</meta>

<column-break/>

# ASCLEPIUS
### Page 139

soul, there arises a single matter from that well-fused mixture, so that errors of darkness may never block such minds. For this reason, they have rightly said that the "senses" are the souls of the gods. I do not mean the souls of all gods, but of those great ones which are the principal ones.

**ASCLEP.** Whom do you call the "heads of things" or the beginnings of the primordial elements, O Trismegistus?

**TRIS.** I am revealing great things to you, and uncovering divine mysteries, the beginning of which I make with the help of desired heavenly favor. There are many kinds of gods, and of all of them, one part is called "intelligible" <term>intelligible: things that can be understood by the intellect but cannot be perceived by physical eyes</term> and the other part "sensible" <term>sensible: things that can be perceived by the physical senses like sight or touch</term>. They are called "intelligible" not because they are thought to be outside our perception—for we actually perceive them more than those we call "visible," as this discussion will thoroughly teach. For sublime reason, being more divine and standing far beyond the minds and intentions of men, will fly away and flow past (or rather flow back and mix itself with its own powerful fluids) unless it is received by the ears of the listeners with more attentive obedience. Therefore, there are gods who are the chiefs of all species. These are followed by the gods whose chief is Substance <note>original: οὐσία (ousia)</note>. These sensible gods are similar to both their origins, who through their sensible nature...

<summary>Hermes explains the nature of invisible forms and the distinction between the "intelligible" gods (perceived by the mind) and "sensible" gods (perceived by the senses), emphasizing that human intellect is a divine gift akin to sunlight.</summary>

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>
### Page 140

...they complete all things, one through the other. Each one illuminates its own work of heaven, or whatever is understood by that name; the <term>ruler of essence</term> <note>original: οὐσιάρχης (ousiarchēs)</note> is Jupiter. For through heaven, Jupiter provides life to all. The <term>essence</term> <note>original: οὐσία (ousia)</note> of the Sun is light: for the good of light is poured into us through the orb of the Sun, along with thirty-six stars (the name of which is the <term>decans</term> <note>original: horoscopij; these are the thirty-six 10-degree segments of the zodiac, often seen as celestial rulers in Hermetic thought</note>) that are always fixed in the same place. The <term>ruler of essence</term> or prince of these is he whom they call *Pantomorphon* or the **All-Formed** <note>original: "Omniformem"</note>, who creates various forms from diverse species. The seven spheres have the Planets as their princes, as well as <term>Fortune</term> <note>original: τύχη (tychē)</note> and <term>Fate</term> <note>original: εἱμαρμένη (heimarmenē)</note>, by which all things follow the law of nature with the firmest stability, yet varied by eternal motion. Air, indeed, is the instrument or machine of all things, through which all things are made. Moreover, the <term>ruler of essence</term> of the air is second to mortals, and things similar to them. These things being so, and moving from the bottom to the top, all things belonging to each other are naturally so connected that mortal things are joined to mortals, and sensible things <note>things perceived by the senses</note> are joined to sensibles. The height of governance obeys that highest lord, so that they are not many, but rather one: for all things hang from the One,

<column-break/>

### Page 141
### ASCLEPIUS

and rather flow from Him; though they seem distant and are believed to be many separate things, when united they are one or two, from which all things are made and by whom all things are made—that is, from the matter out of which they are formed, and by His will, by whose command other things are brought forth.

**ASCLEPIUS:** What again is this reasoning, O Trismegistus?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** It is this, O Asclepius: for God—or the Father, or the Lord of all, or by whatever name He may be called more holily and religiously by all, which among us must be kept sacred for the sake of our understanding—in contemplation of His great divinity, we do not name Him definitively by any of these names. For if the voice is a sound made of air struck by breath, declaring the human will or sense which the mind has perhaps perceived through the senses—a name whose whole substance is composed of a few syllables, defined and circumscribed so that the exchange of voice and ears was necessary for humanity—likewise also the sense, the spirit, the air, and everything within or through them, the name of God is the whole of these. For I do not hope that the creator of such great majesty, the father or lord of all things, can be named by a single name, even one composed of many parts. This... <note>original: "Hunc ue...", likely "Hunc uero" (But this...)</note>

<summary>Hermes explains the celestial hierarchy, including Jupiter, the Sun, and the thirty-six decans, before discussing the ineffability of God, arguing that God cannot be confined to a single name because He is the source of all speech and existence.</summary>

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS <note>original: "MERC. TRISMEGISTI"</note>
### Page 142

->HERMES TRISMEGISTUS<-

[...either by] a single name, or rather by every name, since he is surely the One and also all things. Thus it is necessary either that all things be called by his name, or that he himself be addressed by the names of all things. He, therefore, being the only one who is all things, and being most full of the <term>fecundity</term> <note>Fertility or the power to produce life</note> of both sexes, is always pregnant with his own will and always brings forth whatever he has wished to procreate. His will is all goodness. This same goodness of all things was born by nature from his divinity, so that all things are as they are, and as they were, and so that nature may be sufficient to be born from itself for all things yet to come. Therefore, O Asclepius, let this explanation be given to you as to why and how all things are made of both sexes.

**ASCLEPIUS:** Do you then say that God is of both sexes, O Trismegistus?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Not only God, O Asclepius, but all living beings and even things without life. For it is impossible for any of the things that exist to be barren. If fecundity were taken away from all things that exist, it would be impossible for them to exist forever. For I say that nature, sense, and the world contain nature within themselves and preserve all things that are born. For both sexes are full of procreation, and the connection of both—or what is truer, an incomprehensible truth—you may rightly call either **Cupid** <note>original: "Cupidinem"; the Roman god of desire, representing the driving force of attraction</note> or **Venus** <note>original: "Venerem"; the Roman goddess of love and beauty</note>, or both. Therefore, this...

---

# ASCLEPIUS
### Page 143

->ASCLEPIUS<-

...is more true than any truth and more manifest when perceived by the mind: that from that God of all nature, this <term>ministry</term> <note>A service or sacred duty</note> of procreation was devised and granted to all things for eternity. Within this ministry, the highest charity, joy, gladness, desire, and divine love are innate. It would have been necessary to speak of how great the power and necessity of this ministry is, if it were not that it could be known by everyone from their own innermost feelings and through self-contemplation. 

For if you observe that final moment of time when we reach the rite <note>In this context, the "rite" refers to the act of sexual union and climax</note> from the brain, and how each nature pours its offspring into the other, the one greedily seizes the seed of the other and hides it deep within. Finally, at that time, through the common connection, the females even acquire the strength of the males, and the males grow weary in the female body. 

Therefore, the effect of this so charming and necessary mystery is performed in secret, lest the divinity of both natures be forced to blush at the mixing of the sexes if the ignorant common people were to mock it; and much more so if it were subjected to the sight of irreligious men. However, there are not many, but rather very few, who can be counted as <term>religious</term> <note>original: "religiosi"; here meaning those who are spiritually enlightened or pious in the Hermetic sense</note> in the world. Whence it happens that malice remains in many people due to a lack of wisdom and knowledge of all things that exist.

<summary>In this passage, Hermes Trismegistus explains the androgynous nature of God and how the divine power of procreation is a sacred "ministry" shared by all things. He describes the act of union as a hidden mystery that reflects a divine reality, which only the wise and pious can truly understand.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius. Having previously discussed the dual-gendered nature of God and the generative power of all things, the text now shifts to explain the unique position of humanity in the cosmic hierarchy—situated between the divine gods and the mortal world—and how humans can overcome bodily vices through divine understanding.</meta>

# HERMES TRISMEGISTUS, ASCLEPIUS.
### Page 144, 145

For from the understanding of <term>divine religion</term> <note>original: "religionis diuine"; here meaning the sacred bond and knowledge of God</note>, by which all things are established, there is born both a contempt for and a remedy for the vices of the whole world. But as ignorance and lack of knowledge persist, all vices gain strength and wound the soul with incurable sores. Once infected and corrupted by these vices, the soul swells as if with poison, unless it is treated by those whose discipline and understanding of the soul provide the highest cure. If, therefore, this knowledge will benefit only a few, it is worthy to pursue and explain this treatise: namely, why the divinity deigned to impart his intelligence and discipline to humans alone.

Listen then: God, the father and lord, when he created humans after the gods, fashioned them from both the more corrupt part of the world and from the divine part, weighing them in equal balance. Thus, it happened that the vices of the world remained mixed within their bodies. Other vices arose because of the food and nourishment we necessarily share in common with all animals; because of these things, it is inevitable that the desires of <term>cravings</term> <note>original: "cupiditatũ"</note> and the remaining vices of the mind settle within human souls.

To the gods, however—since they were fashioned from the purest part of nature and require no support from reason or discipline—their very immortality and the vigor of their eternal youth serve as their <term>prudence</term> <note>practical wisdom</note> and discipline. Yet,

<column-break/>

for the sake of the unity of reason, and so that they would not be strangers to it, God established an order prescribed by the law of necessity through an eternal law. He recognized that among all animals, man alone is capable of reason and discipline, through which humans might turn away from and estrange themselves from the vices of the body, holding out for them the intention and hope of immortality.

In short, God composed man of both natures—the divine and the mortal—making him a "good man" who is capable of being immortal. Because man was fashioned in this way by the will of God, it is established that he is even better than the gods who are formed only from an immortal nature. Among all mortals, man is joined to the gods by kinship; he venerates them with <term>religion</term> and a holy mind, while the gods, with pious affection, look upon and guard all human affairs.

But this is said only of those few men endowed with a pious mind. Of the vicious, nothing should be said, lest this most holy discourse be violated by the contemplation of them. And since our discourse is directed toward the kinship and fellowship between men and gods, learn now, O Asclepius, the power of man. The Lord and Father—or God, who is the highest—just as he is the creator of the celestial gods, so is man the creator of the gods <note>This refers to the "animating" of sacred statues in temples, a key theme in the Asclepius.</note> who...

<summary>This passage explains the dual nature of humanity, composed of both divine spirit and corruptible matter. It argues that humans are uniquely capable of reason and, through it, can rise above physical vices to achieve immortality and even a status comparable to the gods.</summary>

<keywords>Hermeticism, Asclepius, human nature, divine reason, immortality, cosmic hierarchy, vices, kinship with the divine</keywords>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS
### Page 146

...of those who are in the temples, joined in close proximity to humanity: and not only are they illuminated, but they also illuminate; nor does man only advance toward God, but he even strengthens the gods. Do you wonder at this, O Asclepius, or do you perhaps distrust it, as many do?
**ASCLEPIUS.** I am confounded, O Trismegistus, but having freely agreed to your words, I judge the human being to be most happy, who has attained such a great power of happiness.
**TRISMEGISTUS.** And not without reason, for he is worthy of wonder, who is the greatest of all the gods. For the origin of all [the gods] is manifest in their mixture, propagated from the purest part of nature, and their signs alone exist for all things, as if they were heads. But the species of gods which humanity shapes is formed from both natures: from the divine, which is prior and much more divine, and from that which is within humans—that is, from the matter out of which they were procreated or fabricated: and they are configured not only in their heads alone, but in all their limbs and their entire body. Thus humanity, always neighbor to nature and its own origin, perseveres in that imitation of divinity, so that just as the Lord and Father made the eternal gods to be like himself, so humanity might figure its own gods after the likeness of its own countenance.
**ASCLEPIUS.** You speak of statues, O Tris-

---

# ASCLEPIUS
### Page 147

-megistus? **TRISMEGISTUS.** Statues, O Asclepius. Do you see how far you yourself distrust [this]? I mean statues <term>animated</term> <note>original: "animatas"; referring to the ancient practice of theurgy, where rituals were believed to draw divine souls into physical idols</note> with sense and full of spirit, doing such great and marvelous things; statues with foreknowledge of the future, predicting it perhaps by prophets, by dreams, and by many other things; statues that bring weaknesses to humans and cure them, and bring sadness according to their merits. Are you ignorant, O Asclepius, that Egypt is an image of heaven, or, what is truer, a translation and descent of all things that are governed and exercised in heaven? And if it must be said, more truly our land is the temple of the whole world. And since it is fitting for wise men to know all things beforehand, it is not right for you to be ignorant of this.
A time is coming when it will appear that the Egyptians have in vain preserved divinity with a pious mind and diligent religion, and all their holy veneration will fall into ruin and be frustrated. For divinity will return from the earth to heaven; Egypt will be abandoned, and the land that was the seat of divinity will be widowed of religion and destitute of the presence of the gods. For when foreigners fill this region and land, not only will there be a neglect of religion, but what is harder, a penalty will be established by law against religion, piety, and divine worship.

<summary>Hermes explains how humans create "earthly gods" by ensouling statues and delivers a famous prophecy regarding the future decline of Egyptian religion and the departure of the gods.</summary>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS
### Page 148

...prohibition. Then this most holy land, the seat of shrines and temples, will be completely filled with tombs and the dead. O Egypt, Egypt, of your religions only stories will remain, and they will be equally incredible to your own descendants; only words carved into stones will remain to tell of your pious deeds. A Syrian or an Indian, or someone like them, will inhabit Egypt. For the Divinity will return to heaven, and the deserted humans will all die; and so Egypt, widowed of both God and man, will be forsaken.

I call upon you, most holy river <note>The Nile</note>, and I predict your future: you will burst forth to your very banks filled with rushing blood, and your divine waves will not only be polluted by blood, but will be entirely broken. The number of the buried will be much greater than the living; and the survivor who remains will be recognized as Egyptian only by his tongue, but in his actions he will seem a stranger.

Why do you weep, O Asclepius? Egypt herself will be persuaded to suffer even more than these, and things much worse. She who was once holy, and most loving of divinity—the only place on earth where the gods deservedly dwelt—will become a teacher of seduction and impiety, a model of the greatest cruelty. And then, through a weariness of humanity, the world will not seem worthy of wonder, nor worthy of adora-

-tion. This whole good <note>The physical world/cosmos</note>, than which nothing better is, nor was, nor will be, that can be seen, will be endangered. It is a multiform united mass that ought to be venerated, praised, and finally loved by those who see it. 

For darkness will be preferred to light, and death will be judged better than life. No one will look up to heaven. The religious person will be thought insane, the irreligious person will be considered wise, the madman brave, and the worst person will be held as "good." 

For the soul and everything surrounding it—by which it was either born mortal or presumes it will attain immortality, as I have explained to you—will not only be a target for laughter but will even be considered vanity. <meta>This page continues the "Lament of Hermes" from the previous page. The text describes the final collapse of the natural and moral order—what the text calls the "old age of the world"—followed by a divine restoration. # MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS
### Page 150

-mingled with humanity, they will drive wretched people by force to every evil of audacity—to wars, to robberies, to frauds, and to everything that is contrary to the nature of souls. Then the earth will no longer stand firm, nor will the sea be sailed; the sky will not be stayed by the courses of the stars, nor will the courses of the stars remain constant in the sky. Every divine voice will be silenced in a necessary stillness; the fruits of the earth will rot, the soil will no longer be fertile, and the very air will languish in a sorrowful numbness.

This and such a thing will be the old age of the world: irreligion, disorder, and the irrationality of all good things. When all these things have come to pass, O Asclepius, then that Lord and Father, God the primary power <note>original: "primipotens"</note> and the sole governor of the world, looking upon these customs and voluntary deeds, by his own will—which is the benevolence of God—will resist these vices and the corruption of all things. He will call back those in error, washing away all malice either with a flood <note>A reference to a cleansing deluge, common in ancient mythology</note>, or consuming it with fire, or ending it through pestilential diseases scattered everywhere through different places. He will restore the world to its ancient face, so that the world itself may again seem worthy of adoration and wonder; and God, the maker and restorer of so great a work, will be celebrated by all who shall then exist with frequent proclamations of praise and bless-

<column-break/>

# ASCLEPIUS
### Page 151
<sig>K 4</sig>

-ings. For this is the rebirth <note>original: "genitura," implying a new birth or generation</note> of the world: a reformation of all good things and a most holy and religious restoration of nature itself, once the course of time is completed—a nature which is, and was, eternal and without beginning. For the will of God lacks a beginning, since it is the same now as it has always been and is everywhere eternal.

**ASCLEPIUS:** Is the nature of God, then, the counsel of his will, the highest goodness, O Trismegistus?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** O Asclepius, will is born from counsel, and the act of willing itself comes from that will. For He does not want anything through great effort, as He is the most full of all things and already possesses those things which He wills. He wills all good things, and He has all that He wills; indeed, He both thinks and wills all good things. This [good] is God; and the image of that good is the world.

**ASCLEPIUS:** Is the world good, O Trismegistus?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** It is good, O Asclepius, as I shall teach you. For just as God is the distributor and dispenser of good things—that is, of consciousness <note>original: "sensus"</note>, soul, and life—to all the species and kinds that are in the world, so too the world is the provider and giver of all things that seem good to mortals: the alternation of the seasons <note>original: "alternationis partium"</note>, the fruits of the earth, birth, growth, maturity, and things similar to these. And through this, God, dwelling above the summit of the highest heaven,

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius. Having just described the restoration of the world after a period of chaos, Hermes now explains the divine hierarchy governing the universe and transitions into a philosophical discourse on death and the judgment of the soul.</meta>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS
### Page 152

[He] is everywhere and looks upon all things. For thus there is a place beyond heaven without stars, separate from all physical things. The Dispenser, who is between heaven and earth, holds the place which we call Jupiter <note>Here referring to the celestial governor or the "middle" air</note>. To the earth and sea, however, Jupiter Plutonius <note>original: "Iupiter Plutonius," a syncretic name combining the sky god Jupiter with Pluto, god of the underworld, representing a deity who governs the terrestrial and subterranean realms</note> rules, and he is the nourisher of all mortal and fruitful living things. By the powers of all these, fruits, trees, and the earth are quickened. But the powers and effects of others are distributed through all things that exist. Those who rule the earth shall be distributed and will be gathered in a city, in the highest border of Egypt, which shall be founded on the side of the setting sun <note>The West was traditionally associated with the land of the dead and sacred mysteries in Egyptian thought</note>, to which all the mortal race shall hasten by land and sea. 

**ASCLEPIUS:** But at this very time, O Trismegistus, where are these [divine beings]? 

**TRISMEGISTUS:** They are settled in a very great city, on the Libyan mountain. And let these things be told thus far. But now we must discuss the immortal and the mortal. For the hope and fear of death torment many who are ignorant of true reason. Death, therefore, is brought about by the dissolution of the body, which has been exhausted by labor, and by the completion of the number [of years or parts] by which the limbs of the body are fitted into a single machine for the uses of life. For the body dies when the man's vitality ceases to be able to carry it. This, therefore, is death:

<meta>catchword: of the body</meta>

<column-break/>

# ASCLEPIUS
### Page 153

the dissolution of the body and the end of bodily sensation. Concern about this is unnecessary. But another concern is necessary, which either human ignorance or disbelief treats with contempt. Hear then, O Asclepius: When the departure of the soul from the body has taken place, then the judgment and examination of its merit will pass into the power of the highest spirit <note>original: "daemonis." In this context, a *daemon* is a powerful intermediary spirit or divine judge, not an evil entity</note>. If he finds the soul to be pious and just, he will permit it to remain in places suitable to it. But if he sees it stained with the marks of sins and smeared with vices, he will hurl it down from above to the depths, handing it over to storms and whirlwinds of air, fire, and water, which are often in conflict. And between heaven and earth, the soul will be snatched away, tossed in various directions by the waves of the world with eternal punishments. Thus, in this case, the eternity of the soul is a disadvantage, because it is subjugated to an eternal judgment by an immortal sentence. 

Therefore, realize that we must be cautious, reverent, and fearful, lest we be entangled in these things. For the unbelieving will be forced to believe after their sins—not by words, but by examples; not by threats, but by the actual suffering of punishments. 

**ASCLEPIUS:** Are not the sins of men punished by human law alone, Trismegistus? 

**TRISMEGISTUS:** First, O Asclepius, all earthly things that exist are mortal. This includes all things that live by a physical logic, and which, from living...

<sig>K 5</sig>

<summary>Hermes explains the divine hierarchy governing the cosmos and describes the soul's journey after death, where a divine judge determines if it deserves peace or eternal torment in the elements. He warns that the immortality of the soul makes its punishment particularly dread, as it cannot escape the consequences of a vice-ridden life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and Asclepius regarding the fate of the soul after death. The previous page ended with a discussion on the dissolution of the body; here, Hermes explains the divine judgment that follows and the nature of God as the source of light and life.</meta>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>154</page-num>

...failing by that same physical law, all these things are subject to punishments according to the merits or crimes of their lives. These punishments are inflicted even more severely after death, especially for those things that were perhaps hidden while they lived. For since the Divinity knows all things beforehand, punishments are rendered exactly as they should be, according to the quality of the crimes.

**ASCLEPIUS:** Who are worthy of greater punishments, O Trismegistus?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** Those who, having been condemned by human laws, lose their life by violence, so that they seem not to have returned their soul as a debt to nature, but to have paid a penalty for their deeds. On the contrary, for the just man, there is a defense in his devotion to God and in his supreme piety. For God protects such people from all evils. For the Father and Lord of all, who alone is all things, willingly shows Himself to all: not where He might be in space, nor what kind He is in quality, nor how great He is in quantity, but by illuminating man with the understanding of the mind alone. When the darkness of error has been shaken from the soul and the clarity of truth perceived, the man merges himself with his whole sense into the divine intelligence; liberated by the love of this intelligence from that part of nature by which he is mortal, he conceives a confidence in his future immortality. This, therefore, shall be the difference between the good and the wicked. Each person...

<meta>catchword: enim (for)</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>155</page-num>

# ASCLEPIUS

...for each person shines through piety, religion, wisdom, and the worship and veneration of God, as if they had seen the truth with their own eyes through true reason. By the confidence of his belief, such a man stands out among other men as much as the sun outshines the other stars with its light. For the sun itself illuminates the other stars not so much by the greatness of its power as by its divinity and holiness. Believe this sun to be the second god, O Asclepius, governing all other things and illuminating all the creatures of the world, whether they are animate <note>having a soul or breath</note> or inanimate. For if the world is a living being <note>original: "animal mundus," a central Hermetic concept that the universe itself is a conscious, living organism</note> that is always living—and was, and is, and shall be—nothing in the world is mortal. Since every single part of a living thing is itself living, and since this world is a single, ever-living being, there is no place for death within it. Therefore, God Himself must be most full of life and eternity, if it is necessary for Him to live forever. Just as the world is everlasting, so the sun is always the governor of living things, or rather the constant attendant and dispenser of their entire vitality. Thus, God is the everlasting governor of the living things and the vital forces within the world, and the eternal dispenser of life itself. He dispensed this life once and for all by an eternal law, providing life to all vital things in this manner which I shall now describe.

<summary>Hermes explains that divine judgment after death is based on the soul's hidden conduct, contrasts the fate of the pious with the executed, and describes the Sun as a 'second god' that sustains the life of the world-organism.</summary>
<keywords>divine judgment, afterlife, piety, sun as second god, world as living being, immortality, Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus (here called Mercury) and his disciple Asclepius. Having previously discussed the judgment of the soul, Hermes now turns to a grand cosmological vision of how the physical world exists within the stillness of eternity and how time is regulated by divine law.</meta>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>156</page-num>

->MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS<-

The world is moved in the very vitality of eternity, and that same vital eternity is the place of the world; because of this, the world will never be corrupted, being walled around and, as it were, bound fast by a stable perpetuity of living. The world is, therefore, the dispenser of life to all things that are within it, and it is the place of all things that are governed under the sun. The motion <note>original: "commotio"</note> of this world consists of a double effect: for it is quickened from without by eternity, and it quickens those things that are within it, distinguishing all things by fixed and established numbers and times. All things are directed through the effect of the Sun and the wandering of the stars; all temporal things are inscribed by divine reason and law. 

Earthly time is recognized by the quality of the air and the variety of heat and cold. Heavenly time, however, is recognized by the return of the stars to the same places as they recur in their periodic revolution. And the world is the receptacle of time, by whose course and motion it is sustained. Time, moreover, is preserved by order. Order and time cause the renewal of all things that are in the world through alternation. Since all things are thus held, nothing is stable, nothing is fixed, nothing is immobile, neither among things being born, nor things of heaven, nor things of earth. For God alone—and deservedly He alone—is entirely full and perfect in Himself, and from Himself, and around 

<column-break/>

<page-num>157</page-num>

# ASCLEPIUS

->ASCLEPIUS<-

and around Himself; and He is His own firm stability, nor can He be moved from His place by any impulse, since all things are in Him and He alone is in all things—unless someone should dare to say that His motion exists in eternity. But rather, eternity itself is immobile, into which the motion of all times returns, and from which the motion of all times takes its beginning. 

God, therefore, was stable and always is, and eternity likewise exists with Him, holding within itself the unborn world, which we rightly call **sensible** <note>original: "sensibilem"—meaning the physical universe that can be perceived by the senses.</note>. This world was made as an image of this God, an imitator of eternity. Time, however, has the force and nature of its own stability, even though it is always in motion, by that very necessity of returning into itself. Therefore, although eternity is stable, immobile, and fixed, nevertheless, because time is mobile and its motion is always called back into eternity—and that mobility is turned and brought about by the reason of time—it results that eternity itself, though immobile, seems to be moved through time, in which it is and which is in it; for all motion is seen to be moved therein. Thus it happens that the stability of eternity is moved, and the mobility of time becomes stable by a fixed law of its course. In this way, to believe that God is moved... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "cre-" suggests "crederetur" or "credibile," likely continuing the thought that God's "motion" is a divine paradox.</note>

<summary>This passage explores the Hermetic relationship between God, Eternity, and the World, explaining how the physical universe moves and changes according to divine "Order" while remaining anchored in the unchanging stability of God.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Cosmological Order, Time, Divine Law, Sensible World, Motion and Stability, Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the Hermetic dialogue from page 157. Hermes Trismegistus is explaining the nature of God's stillness and the relationship between eternity and the moving world of time.</meta>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>158</page-num>

[In-]credible <note>original: "dibile"; likely "incredibile" continuing from the previous page</note> it is how he moves within himself with that same mobility of stability. For within His very magnitude, there is an immobile active movement <note>original: "agitatio"; refers to a state of being perfectly active yet perfectly still</note>. This being, which is of such a nature that it is not subject to the senses—being infinite, incomprehensible, and beyond estimation—can neither be sustained, nor carried, nor tracked. For from where it comes, and to where it goes, and where it is, or how, or of what kind it may be, is uncertain. For He is carried in supreme stability, and His stability is in Himself, whether they be God, or eternity, or both, or one within the other, or both within both. 

For this reason, eternity is without the definition of time. Time, however, which is defined, can be eternal through the use of numbers, or by the alternation of cycles, or by returning through the circuit of another. Both, therefore, appear infinite, and both seem eternal. For stability—being, as it were, fixed so that it may support those things which are subject to movement—rightfully holds the primacy by the benefit of its firmness. 

For God and eternity are the origin of all things that exist. The world, however, because it is mobile, does not possess this primacy. For its mobility runs ahead of its own stability within the law of everlasting movement, by possessing an immobile firmness. Therefore, every sense <note>original: "sensus"; here meaning consciousness or divine perception</note> of the immobile divinity moves itself within its own stability. It is holy and incorruptible and everlasting,

<column-break/>

<page-num>159</page-num>

# ASCLEPIUS

and if there is anything better it can be called: it is eternity, standing within the very will of the supreme God, most full of all sensible things, and consisting (if I may put it this way) of all discipline <note>original: "disciplinæ"; meaning branches of knowledge or organized wisdom</note> alongside God. 

But the worldly sense <note>original: "sensus mundanus"</note> is the receptacle of all sensible things, and of species, and of disciplines. The human sense, however, arises from the tenacity of memory, in that it is mindful of all things it has done. Yet the sense of divinity descends even as far as the human animal. For God did not wish the supreme and divine sense to be mixed into all creatures, lest it be shamed by the commingling with other living beings. 

For the intelligence of the human sense, whatever its kind or greatness, consists entirely in the memory of past things. For through that tenacity of memory, man was made the governor of the earth. However, the intellect of nature and the sense of the world can be perceived from all things in the world that are subject to the senses. 

Eternity, which is second after the sensible world, is recognized as a given sense and quality. But the intellect of quality, and the quality of the sense of the supreme God, is truth alone—of which truth not even the farthest line or shadow is recognized in this world, where there are lies, where there are births <note>original: "genituræ"; the process of being born into the material world of change</note>, and where errors are seen.

<summary>This page defines the relationship between the eternal, immobile stability of God and the cyclical motion of time and the world. It also establishes a hierarchy of "sense" or consciousness, rising from human memory to the worldly sense, and finally to the divine sense which is pure Truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the Hermetic text *Asclepius*. Hermes Trismegistus has been discussing the nature of God and eternity. He now turns to the initiation of his students—Asclepius, Tat, and Ammon—into the higher mysteries and addresses the physical nature of the universe, specifically refuting the existence of a "void" or vacuum.</meta>

# MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS

<page-num>160</page-num>

You see then, O Asclepius, in what state we are placed, what things we treat, and what things we dare to touch upon. But to You, most high God, I give thanks, who has illuminated me with the light of seeing Your divinity. And you—O Tat, Asclepius, and Ammon—keep these divine mysteries hidden within the secrets of your hearts, and preserve them in silence. For this is how intellect differs from sense perception: our intellect's intention moves ahead to understand and distinguish the quality of the world’s sense. The intellect of the world, however, reaches toward eternity and the gods who are above it. Thus it happens to humans that we see the things in heaven as if through a mist <note>original: "caliginem"; a common metaphor in Hermetic texts for the limitations of physical sight compared to spiritual vision</note>, as much as is possible through the condition of human perception. This intention <note>meaning the capacity of human focus or insight</note> is very narrow when it comes to seeing such great goods, yet it is most wide when it beholds them with the happiness of a clear conscience.

Concerning the void—which seems a great matter to many people—my view is this: there is no such thing as a void, nor could there ever have been, nor will there ever be. For all parts of the world are completely full, so that the world itself is full and perfect with bodies, diverse in quality, form, size, and appearance. One of these is larger than another, or smaller than another,

<meta>catchword: and strength</meta>

---

<page-num>161</page-num>

## ASCLEPIUS

and differing in strength and thinness. For some of them seem stronger, just as they are larger; but smaller and thinner ones can hardly be seen, or cannot be seen at all, and we only know they exist by touch. This leads many people to believe that there are no bodies in these places, but only empty spaces—which is impossible. 

Just as that which is said to be "outside the world" (if indeed there is anything there, though I do not believe it) must be full of intelligible things—that is, things similar to His own divinity—so too this "sensible world" <note>the physical world perceived by the senses</note> is completely full of bodies and living beings suited to its nature and quality. We do not see all of their faces; some are beyond measure large, and some are very short, either because of the distance of the space between us, or because our eyesight is dull. They may seem to be as they appear to us, or because of their extreme smallness, many people do not believe they exist at all. 

I speak now of the spirits <note>original: "dæmonas"; in this philosophical context, these are intermediary beings or guardian spirits, not "demons" in the modern evil sense</note>, whom I believe dwell with us, and the heroes <note>original: "heroas"; referring to semi-divine souls or those who have attained a higher state between humans and gods</note> who live in the purest part of the air above us and the earth, where there is no place for clouds and no disturbance from the movement of the stars. Therefore, O Asclepius, you should not call anything "void," unless you specify what the thing is that the object is "void" of—

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus instructs his followers to keep the divine mysteries secret and argues that the universe is a "plenum," entirely full of matter and spirits, even if they are too subtle for human eyes to see.</summary>

<page-num>162</page-num>

<header>MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS</header>

<meta>catchword: sensible,</meta>

<page-num>163</page-num>

<header>ASCLEPIUS</header>

...as you predicted, such as being void of fire, and of water, and of similar things. Even if it happens to appear so—that there could be something void of things of this kind—however small or large that which seems void may be, it cannot be empty of spirit and air <note>original: "spiritu & aëre"; in Hermetic philosophy, "spirit" refers to a subtle, life-giving breath or pneuma that fills all space</note>. Similarly, we must speak regarding "place," a word which on its own lacks meaning. For a "place" is only defined by what it contains. If the primary name <note>the name of the object occupying the space</note> is taken away, the meaning changes. Therefore, we correctly say "the place of water," "the place of fire," or similar things. For just as it is impossible for there to be a void, so too a "place" alone cannot be understood for what it is. For if you posit a place without that which belongs to it, the place will appear empty: a thing which I do not believe exists in the world. But if nothing is void, then what a place is does not appear by itself, unless you add to it lengths, breadths, or heights, just as one marks the bodies of men. Since these things are so, O Asclepius, and you who are present, know that the <term>intelligible world</term> <note>the realm of pure thought and divine forms, as opposed to the physical world we see</note> and God, who is discerned by the gaze of the mind alone, are incorporeal; nor can anything corporeal be mixed into his nature—that is, anything that can be discerned by quality, quantity, or numbers. For in Him, no such thing exists. This world, therefore, which is called

<column-break/>

**sensible** <note>the world perceived through the five senses</note>, is the receptacle <note>original: "receptaculum"; a container or vessel</note> of all sensible species, qualities, or bodies, all of which cannot be brought to life without God. For all things are God, and all things are from God, and all things are of His will. He is the totality: good, beautiful, unchanging, and wise, perceptible and intelligible to Himself alone; without Him, nothing was, nor is, nor shall be. For all things are from Him, and in Him, and through Him: the multiform qualities, the great quantities, the magnitudes exceeding every measure, and the uniform species. If you understand these things, O Asclepius, you will give thanks to God. If you observe the whole, you will learn by true reason that this sensible world itself, and all things that are in it, are covered by that higher world as if by a garment. For every <term>genus</term> of living creature, O Asclepius—whether mortal or immortal, rational, whether endowed with a soul or without a soul—just as each has its genus, so do individuals have images of their own kind. And although every genus of animal possesses the entire form of its kind, yet within that same form, individuals are dissimilar to one another. For the species <note>referring here to the "Divine Form" or "Idea"</note> which is divine is incorporeal, as is whatever is grasped by the mind. Therefore, since these two things from which all consists...

<summary>Hermes explains that space is never truly empty because it is filled with spirit, and that the physical "sensible" world is a reflection or "garment" of a higher "intelligible" divine reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius. Trismegistus is explaining how the eternal, divine "Species" or "Form" remains constant while generating an infinite variety of physical "images" or "appearances" in the material world. He then transitions into the famous Hermetic doctrine of how humans create physical statues of gods and animate them with spiritual forces.</meta>

-> # MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS <-

<page-num>164</page-num>

<header>M E R C. T R I S M E G I S T I</header>

<meta>catchword: and various</meta>

If form and body were incorporeal, it would be impossible for any one form to be born exactly like another, given the distant points of hours and climates <note>in ancient geography, "climates" refers to the latitudinal zones of the earth which were thought to influence the nature of living things</note>. Instead, they are changed as many times as the hour has moments in the revolving circle, in which resides that "all-formed" <note>original: "omniformis"</note> God we have spoken of. The species <note>here meaning the "ideal type" or "archetype"</note> therefore remains permanent, producing from itself as many and as diverse images as there are moments in the world’s revolution. This world changes in its rotation. But the species itself neither changes nor rotates. Thus, the forms of each individual genus are permanent, yet they are different from one another within that same form.

**ASCLEPIUS:** And does the world change its species, O Trismegistus?

**TRISMEGISTUS:** You see then, O Asclepius, that everything has been told to you as if you were dreaming. For what is the world, or of what does it consist, if not of all things that are born? Therefore, you wish to speak of heaven, earth, and the elements. For indeed, other things change their appearances more frequently: the heavens becoming damp, or dry, or cold, or fiery, or clear, or murky. These are the appearances that often alternate within the one species of the heavens. The earth, truly, always has many changes in its appearance—both when it brings forth fruits, and when it nourishes those same fruits with its own offspring; it then yields various and diverse...

---

<page-num>165</page-num>

<header>A S C L E P I V S.</header>

...and diverse qualities and quantities of all fruits, as well as their stages and growth cycles, extending to all qualities, scents, tastes, and types of trees, flowers, and berries. Fire also undergoes many and various conversions. For the Sun and Moon have all-formed images. These are like our own mirrors, which return the likenesses of images with a rivaling splendor. But let enough be said of such things for now. Let us return once more to man and to reason, by which divine gift man is called a "rational animal."

The things said about man are less to be wondered at—though they are still wonderful—but that which surpasses the wonder of all wonders is that man has been able to discover the divine nature and to bring it into effect. Because our ancestors once wandered in great error, being unbelieving regarding the reason of the gods and not paying attention to divine worship and religion, they discovered an art by which they could create gods. To this discovery, they added a power from the nature of the world that was appropriate to it, mixing the two. And since they could not create souls, they called forth the souls of demons <note>original: "dæmonum"; these are intermediary spiritual entities, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense</note> or angels and placed them into their statues and divine mysteries, through which alone these idols could have the power of doing both good and harm. For your grandfather, O Asclepius...

<summary>Trismegistus explains that while divine archetypes are eternal, the physical world is in constant flux, using the analogy of mirrors. He then reveals the "wonder of wonders": that humans, unable to create souls, learned to pull spiritual entities into statues to create earthly gods.</summary>
