-> # MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS <-

## ASCLEPIUS.

...the first inventor of medicine <note>original: "medicinæ primus inuentor"; identifying the god Asclepius with the Egyptian healer-sage Imhotep</note>, to whom a temple is consecrated on a mountain in Libya, near the Shore of Crocodiles. There lies his "worldly man" <note>original: "mundanus homo"</note>—that is, his physical body. For the rest of him, or rather the whole man—if indeed the whole person consists of the consciousness of a better life—has returned to heaven. Even now, through his divine power, he provides all the same aid to sick mortals that he used to offer through the art of medicine. Hermes, my grandfather, whose name I share, dwelling in his home city, helps and preserves all mortals who come to him from every side. We know how many benefits the gracious Isis and Osiris bestow, and yet how much harm they do when they are provoked to anger. For it is easy for earthly and worldly gods to be moved to anger, seeing as they were fashioned and composed by humans out of a nature that is not their own. This is why these "holy animals" are so named by the Egyptians; the souls of those whose living presence was consecrated are worshipped throughout the various cities, which are governed by their laws and called by their names. For this reason, O Asclepius, those things which seem worthy of worship and veneration to some are regarded differently by others; because of this, the cities of Egypt are often accustomed to provoke one another with wars.

<term>ASCLEPIUS</term> And what, Trismegistus, is the 
<meta>catchword: quality</meta>

<column-break/>

quality of these gods who are considered "earthly"?

<term>TRISMEGISTUS</term> It consists, O Asclepius, of herbs, stones, and spices that possess a natural power of divinity within themselves. For this reason, these gods are delighted by frequent sacrifices, hymns, praises, and the sweetest sounds, harmonizing in the manner of celestial music. This is done so that the divine element, which has been drawn down by celestial custom and frequent visitation, might be able to joyfully endure throughout long periods of time within the idols, for the sake of humanity. In this way, man is the creator of gods. And do not think, O Asclepius, that the effects of these earthly gods are merely accidental. The celestial gods inhabit the highest reaches of heaven, each one filling and guarding the rank he received. But these gods of ours help humans individually: caring for some things, providing for others, predicting certain things through lots and divination, and assisting us like friendly kin.

<term>ASCLEPIUS</term> What part of reason, then, O Trismegistus, do Fate <note>original Greek: εἱμαρμένην (Heimarmene)</note> or the Fates occupy? If the celestial gods rule and the earthly gods dwell in specific places, what is this that we call Fate <note>original: εἱμαρμένην</note>?

<term>TRISMEGISTUS</term> O Asclepius, Fate is the necessity of all things that happen, always bound together in a chain of links. She is therefore either the effector of things, or the supreme God, or proceeds from that God who...

<summary>This passage describes the Hermetic "animation of statues," explaining how humans create earthly gods using natural materials and music to house celestial spirits. It concludes by introducing the concept of Fate (Heimarmene) as the unbreakable chain of cosmic necessity.</summary>
<keywords>Asclepius, medicine, Hermes, Isis, Osiris, Egypt, statues, idols, celestial harmony, divination, Fate, Heimarmene, necessity</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the sage Hermes Trismegistus and his disciple Asclepius. Having previously discussed the nature of earthly gods and statues, Hermes now shifts the focus to the cosmic mechanics that govern the universe: Destiny, Necessity, and Order. He explains how these divine laws operate without emotion or change, maintaining the eternal cycle of the world.</meta>

-> # MERCURY TRISMEGISTUS <-

...God has been made fertile, or rather the discipline of all celestial and earthly things has been established by divine laws. This, therefore, is Destiny <note>original: "εἱμαρμένη" (heimarmene); the Greek concept of the allotted portion or fate</note> and Necessity; both are connected to each other by an inseparable bond. The first of these, Destiny, gives birth to the beginnings of all things. Necessity, however, compels into action those things that depend on those beginnings. Order follows these—that is to say, the weaving together and the distinction of time for the completion of things. For nothing exists without the composition of order. In all these things, this world is perfect. For the world itself is carried by order, or rather consists entirely of order. These three, therefore—Destiny <note>that is, Fate</note>, Necessity, and Order—are brought about especially by the will of God, who governs the world by His law and divine reason. From these forces, all "willing" or "not-willing" is completely removed by divine decree. For they are neither moved by anger nor bent by favor, but they serve the necessity of eternal reason, which eternity is irreversible, immovable, and unbreakable. First, therefore, is Destiny, which, like a sown seed, receives the offspring of all future things. Necessity follows, by which all things are forced into effect. Third is Order, maintaining the weave of those things which Destiny and Necessity arrange. This eternity, then, which neither began

<column-break/>

<page-num>169</page-num>
-> ## ASCLEPIUS. <-

began to be, nor shall cease, is preserved by an eternal motion fixed by an immutable law of progression. It rises and sets alternately through its various parts, so that with the changing seasons, it rises again with the same parts through which it had set. For such is the logic of a rolling sphere: all things are so tightly bound together that you cannot know where the rotation begins, since all things seem to both precede and follow themselves forever. Chance or fortune, however, are mixed into all worldly things. We have spoken of each of these things as far as human nature was able, as it willed, and as divinity permitted. There remains only this for us: that, blessing God and praying, we return to the care of the body. For by treating sufficiently of divine matters, we have filled the mind as if with the food of the soul. Having come out of the sanctuary <note>original: "adyto"; the innermost, most sacred part of a temple</note>, when they began to pray to God, they were looking toward the south. For at sunset, when someone wishes to pray to God, he should look in that direction; just as at sunrise, he should look toward what is called the east <note>original: "subsolanus"; literally "under the sun," referring to the direction of the rising sun</note>. Now as they were saying the prayer, Asclepius said: "While praying, let us suggest to the Father in a low voice that we should offer the prayer to God with the addition of incense and spices, as he commanded." Hearing this, Trismegistus was moved and said: "Speak better omens, O Asclepius! For this is sacrile—" <note>the text cuts off mid-word "sacrilegious"</note>

<gloss>Destiny, Necessity, Order, World, Asclepius, Trismegistus, Prayer, Divinity</gloss>

<summary>Hermes Trismegistus defines the three cosmic forces—Destiny, Necessity, and Order—that maintain the world's eternal, spherical cycle. The text concludes with the group leaving the sanctuary to pray at sunset, where Hermes rebukes Asclepius for suggesting they use material incense to honor the divine.</summary>

...is similar to sacrilege <note>original: "sacrilegiis"; the scribe begins mid-word from the previous page</note>, when you ask God to burn incense and other offerings. We give thanks to You, highest and most exalted. For by Your grace alone have we attained so great a light of Your knowledge <term>cognitio: in this context, it refers to "Gnosis," a transformative, experiential knowledge of the divine</term>. O holy Name, and the one Name to be honored, by which alone God is to be blessed according to the religion of our fathers; for You deign to provide to all a father's piety, and religion, and love, and whatever efficacy is sweeter, and You grant us sense, reason, and intelligence. Sense, so that we may know You. Reason, so that we may track You through our suspicions <note>The Latin "suspicionibus" here implies using intuition or deductive reasoning to find the hidden God</note>. Intelligence, so that knowing You through this recognition, we may rejoice.

And saved by Your divine power, let us rejoice: let us rejoice that You have shown Yourself to us in Your fullness; let us rejoice that You have deigned to consecrate us to eternity while we are still situated in these bodies. For this is the only human thanksgiving: the knowledge of Your majesty. We have known You, the greatest light, perceivable by the intellect alone. We have understood You, O true way of life, O fertile source of all that is born. We have known You most fully by Your conception of all nature; we have known

You, eternal perseverance. For in all this prayer, adoring the good of Your goodness, we ask only this: that You wish to keep us persevering in the love of Your knowledge, and that we may never be separated from this way of life. Desiring this,

## TO THE MOST REVEREND FATHER IN CHRIST, LORD GIOVANNI DE' MEDICI, *Cardinal of the holy Roman Church*, MARSILIO FICINO *the Florentine humbly commends himself*.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'C' containing a large bird, possibly a swan or crane, entwined in foliate scrolls.</image-desc>

When I had decided to write a letter to you, to congratulate you on the cardinal’s dignity <note>original: "cardinea dignitate"; referring to Giovanni de' Medici's appointment as a Cardinal at age 13, though he only assumed the full role later</note> recently conferred upon you, and I was thinking to whom I should especially give it to deliver to you—someone who would make our congratulation much more pleasing—I suddenly betook myself to the Academy <note>The Platonic Academy in Florence, sponsored by the Medici family</note>, hoping that at least in its inner chambers some greeter most acceptable to you would not be lacking. There, then, by a certain divine fate, I first encountered Iamblichus, called "the divine" among the Academics, and a great priest there. To whom indeed I said: "Greetings, Iamblichus, great priest! At present (as you see) I am about to congratulate Giovanni de' Medici, the new prelate of the Christian religion. I pray you, if I ask what is right, that you would make our otherwise meager congratulation more full with your divine words and mysteries." He nodded to my prayers, and with this sentiment, Giovanni, great priest, that Great Priest comes to you.

---

<page-num>173</page-num>
<header>ASCLEPIUS <note>This header is a holdover from the previous section or indicates the transition from the Hermetic context</note>.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

You, therefore, receive such a greeter joyfully, and after the first greeting, after the letter is read, listen more attentively (if you please) to what that divine man says. For he promised that he would speak things worthy of both his character and yours: that he would express to you in brief what the Egyptian and Assyrian priests thought concerning religion and divine matters.

## SUMMARY OF THE BOOK OF IAMBLICHUS.

Porphyry, who among the Platonists is called "the philosopher" because of his excellence, sent a long letter to Egypt to the priest Anebo, full of various and grave questions regarding all parts of philosophy, especially regarding God and angels, demons and souls, providence, fate, prophecies, magic <term>magicen: here referring to "theurgy" or divine works rather than common sorcery</term>, miracles, sacrifices, and vows. Iamblichus, his student, responded to Porphyry's questions. However, he introduces the Egyptian priest Abamon responding to Porphyry on behalf of his disciple Anebo, to whom Porphyry had sent the letter. Of this entire question and truly divine response, as I was overly occupied with Plotinus <note>Ficino was famously translating the works of Plotinus at the same time</note>, I have translated almost every sense rather than the words, so it should not be thought a wonder if the speech appears disjointed in places: especially at the very beginnings of the discussion, where the loss of words is lighter. Now from here on, the context of the words will appear more continuous—

<summary>Marsilio Ficino dedicates his translation of Iamblichus's "On the Mysteries" to the young Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici (the future Pope Leo X), introducing the work as a dialogue between Porphyry and the Egyptian priest Abamon regarding divine matters.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Giovanni de' Medici, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism, Porphyry, Egyptian religion, translation, dedication, Platonic Academy</keywords>

### ARGUMENT OF THE BOOK / IAMBLICHUS ON THE MYSTERIES

...will appear <note>The start of this sentence is on the previous page</note>. I have indeed been forced at times to interject some word of my own for the sake of continuity or clarity. However, Pierleone <note>Pierleone da Spoleto (c. 1440–1492), a renowned physician and scholar who served the Medici family and was deeply interested in Hermetic secrets</note>, a man most eager for all secret matters, was also a cause for this swifter edition. Truly, what I marvel at most of all is that he always seems to strive for those things which he already possesses. For, by a certain divinity of his own talent, he attains the secrets of both Greek and barbarian <note>original: "barbarorum"; in the Renaissance, this term usually referred to non-Greek ancient cultures like the Egyptians, Chaldeans, and Persians, often viewed as sources of ancient wisdom</note> philosophers, even before they have been read.

->IAMBLICHUS<-

<column-break/>

->On the knowledge of divine things.<-

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'A'. The letter is ornate and set against a background of foliage with two putti (winged infants) climbing and playing within the vines.</image-desc>

EGYPTIAN writers, believing all things were discovered by Mercury <note>The god Hermes, identified by the Greeks with the Egyptian Thoth and the "Hermes Trismegistus" to whom many mystical texts were attributed</note>, dedicated their books to Mercury. Mercury presides over wisdom and eloquence. Pythagoras, Plato, Democritus, Eudoxus, and many others, approached the Egyptian priests. The teachings of this book are those of the Assyrians and Egyptians, and are drawn from the columns of Mercury. Pythagoras and Plato learned philosophy from the columns of Mercury in Egypt. These columns of Mercury are full of teachings. Before any use of reason, there is naturally implanted within us a notion of the gods. Indeed, there is a certain touch of divinity that is better than knowledge; from this is stirred the natural appetite for the good, as well as our reasoning and judgment. The essential knowledge of things divine is perpetual within the soul; and in truth, this is not the kind of "knowledge" by which we enjoy God. For in knowledge there is otherness <note>original: "alteritas"; the Neoplatonic concept that "knowing" implies a distance or distinction between the person knowing and the thing being known</note>, but this is a certain essential and simple contact.

<summary>This page concludes Marsilio Ficino's dedication to Giovanni de' Medici and introduces Iamblichus's "On the Mysteries," establishing the Egyptian and "Hermetic" origins of the work. It emphasizes that a primal, direct "touch" of the divine exists in the human soul prior to any intellectual reasoning.</summary>

<keywords>Iamblichus, Marsilio Ficino, Pierleone da Spoleto, Hermes Trismegistus, Egyptian priests, Columns of Mercury, Neoplatonism, divine knowledge, innate ideas</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s translation and commentary on Iamblichus's "On the Mysteries." The previous pages introduced the Egyptian origins of this wisdom and the role of Hermes (Mercury) as the source of all secret knowledge shared with the Greeks.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

For we cannot reach unity itself, unless by a certain most unified state <note>original: "unitiſſimo quodā"</note> and a unity of the mind, which stands above the specific properties of the soul and the mind. The unity of the gods itself unites souls to itself from eternity through their own unities <note>In Neoplatonism, the soul has a "one" or "unity" at its highest point that connects directly to the Divine</note> according to a contiguity <note>a state of direct contact or touching</note> so close and effective that it appears to be a continuity. The divine intellect gives being to the soul through its own essential act of understanding. Therefore, the being of the soul is a certain kind of understanding—namely, of God, upon whom it depends. Our being is to know God: because the chief being of the soul is its intellect, in which being is the same as the perpetual act of understanding divine things.

From that chief being, the discursive powers <note>original: "potentiæ animæ diſcurrētes"; the soul's ability to reason step-by-step or move from one thought to another, as opposed to direct intuition</note> of the soul are derived. After the gods, we place demons, heroes, and pure souls: these three orders are the attendants <note>original: "pediſſequi"</note> of the gods. We cannot reach these attendants of the gods—the demons, heroes, and pure souls—through the usual discursive reasoning of human logic. Instead, it is necessary to rise to an essential and eternal intelligence. Just as the gods are always reached by an innate notion, so the powers attending the gods are only then first reached when the soul has set aside its mobile mode of cognition—which belongs to the rational power, and which is formed by the intellect and intelligence

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

is formed, which they call the acquired intellect <term>intellectum adeptum: the state of the human intellect when it has been perfected by joining with the higher, divine Active Intellect</term>. For the primary knowledge consists in the active intellect. Since knowledge occurs through adequacy <note>the "matching" or alignment of the mind to the object it perceives</note>, it follows that we reach eternal and immobile powers by a notion that is eternal, immobile, and simple. The knowledge by which we know separate substances <note>immaterial beings like gods or angels that exist "separated" from physical matter</note> is of a different kind than that by which we know other things. Knowledge of divine things has always been in the soul through a simple intuition or contact.

Some think they will attain knowledge of separate things through a certain method of opposition—for example: "material things are mobile, local, and subject to change; therefore, separate things are immobile," and so on. In this way, one certainly knows what they are *not*; but what they *are* is known through the adequacy of knowledge to that which is naturally present in the soul through the intellect. However, it is acquired in the rational power by a certain study—not so much through comparison and conjecture, as through the duty of separation and purification. There was also a method of disjunction <note>a logical "either/or" argument</note>, namely: gods and divine things are either temporal or eternal. They are not temporal; therefore they are eternal. Yet even this method, by its own mobility <note>the way the mind moves through the steps of a logical argument</note>, does not suit those things which are immobile.

### *On the properties of each of the higher beings.*

If any composition exists in contrast to separate and separable substances, it does not come from...

<summary>This page discusses how the human soul reaches the divine through unity and "acquired intellect," defining the hierarchy of gods, demons, and heroes. It argues that true divine knowledge comes from simple, eternal intuition rather than step-by-step logic or merely knowing what God is not.</summary>
<keywords>Unity, Divine Intellect, Demons, Heroes, Acquired Intellect, Separate Substances, Neoplatonism, Iamblichus, Epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s Latin translation and commentary on Iamblichus's "On the Mysteries." The previous page ended while discussing how the soul's rational powers relate to the gods and their attendants. The current text begins mid-sentence, completing a thought on how spiritual beings are defined compared to material things.</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

...in a genus <note>original: "ge-nere"</note> that is in itself indeterminate, and a defining difference. For those substances are complete species <note>In this context, a "species" is a perfect, finished form of a being</note> that are entirely in a determinate and complete act. If separable substances <note>Spiritual beings like angels or "intelligences" that exist apart from physical matter</note>, being simple, do not differ among themselves through specific differences under the same genus, then they also do not differ through individual differences under the same species. Therefore, each one is like a genus or species existing by itself. They truly differ by themselves, and by the order of their ranks, and because they relate to the First <note>The "First" refers to the Neoplatonic "One" or God, the source of all existence</note> in different ways.

Intelligences <note>original: "Intelligentię"; high spiritual minds above the level of the human soul</note> do not agree or differ among themselves by any genus or differences, for they are most simple. Instead, they differ insofar as they relate to the First by either the same or a different rationale, and some are arranged in higher ranks of perfection, while others are in the ranks that follow. The differences between things in their actions and their passive experiences must always be resolved into differences of their powers, and these into differences of their essences. One must never stop at the secondary effects, but must always proceed toward the primary causes.

Neither in the higher substances nor in our own soul is there any passive motion <note>A "passive" motion is one caused by an outside force; Ficino is arguing that the soul and higher beings are self-moving</note>; rather, everything there is an effective act. The movement of the soul by which it moves itself is not divided into a "mover" and a "thing moved," or into "moving" and "being moved." Instead, it is an essential act that progresses through time. To composite beings...

---

<page-num>179</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

...and to forms that exist within a subject <note>i.e., forms that require matter to exist, like the "form" of a chair in wood</note>, "accidents" <note>term: "accidents"; qualities that can change without changing what the thing is, such as color or weight</note> are appropriate; but accidents do not apply to immaterial forms. Whatever exists in separable forms is being and is essential, perhaps with a certain superior equality. No accident or consequence is found there. Movements and actions differ because of the differences in their natures, not the other way around. 

If we were to say there is only one "genus" of separated beings, we would throw theology into confusion. Separable substances do not belong to one single genus in which they would be distinguished by specific differences; rather, they are distinguished by themselves and by their order of ranks, insofar as they relate to the First through different levels of perfection. However, those that relate to the first and the last in a similar manner have a greater agreement among themselves.

The Good is twofold: that which is above essence, and that which is according to essence. Essence is a property of all the gods. But the Good itself is above essence; it is called the cause of the good—that is, the principle of essential good. Accordingly, the ranks of the gods are distinguished among themselves according to their ranks of essence. The lowest rational souls do not possess essential good as the gods do, but rather a participation in the good according to intellectual beauty. Yet this beauty resides in them immovably.

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic hierarchy of being, arguing that divine Intelligences are "simple" beings who differ not by biological-style categories, but by their proximity to the "First" (God). It distinguishes between the Absolute Good and the goodness that defines the essence of the gods and souls.</summary>
<keywords>Intelligences, hierarchy of being, essence vs. accident, The Good, Neoplatonism, rational soul, divine ranks, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s translation of Iamblichus's *On the Mysteries*. While the previous page discussed how simple substances (intelligences) differ by their rank and relation to the "First" (God), this page moves into the specific hierarchy of the spiritual world: Gods, Demons, Heroes, and Souls.</meta>

-># IAMBLICHUS
On the order of higher beings.<-
<page-num>180</page-num>

Our souls are counted among the types of divine beings. Heroes <note>original: "Heroës"; in Greek mythology and Neoplatonism, these are semi-divine figures or elevated souls of great virtue</note> are greater than men; therefore, demons <term>dæmones: from the Greek "daimon," meaning a spirit or divine power. In this context, they are helpful intermediaries, not the "evil demons" of later folklore</term> are much more magnificent. The first of the divine is the "Good" itself, with the gods following; the last are individual rational souls. Between these two are the middle ranks: namely, heroes (who are close to souls) and demons (who are close to gods). This is just as air and water exist as intermediates between the extremes of fire and earth. Although heroes surpass us in power, virtue, beauty, and greatness, they nevertheless agree closely with our soul and its way of life. Demons are superior to heroes and serve as the ministers of the gods, acting like assistants to architects in the management of the world. What is inexpressible and hidden within the gods, the demons express and reveal. Demons and heroes adapt the universal, simple, and unmoving gifts of the gods for lower beings; they reconcile all things and are the authors of harmonic consensus and the mutual "sympathy" <note>original: "compassioinis"; the Neoplatonic idea that all parts of the universe are interconnected and "feel" together</note> of all things with one another. They translate divine matters to us, and lead our affairs back equally to the divine. Furthermore, they balance the degrees of the gifts from the higher beings with the preparation of those who receive them. These different kinds of divine beings differ among themselves by a three-fold manner of proper-

<meta>catchword: -ties,</meta>

<column-break/>

-># ON THE MYSTERIES.<-
<page-num>181</page-num>

-ties, namely in what pertains to their essences, virtues <note>original: "uirtutes"; here meaning powers or capacities</note>, and actions. The property of the gods is unity, an unmoving remaining-within-themselves, being the causes of motions, and an eminent providence <term>providentia: the divine care or guidance of the universe</term> that has nothing in common with those things for which it provides. This property is preserved in them according to their essence, virtue, and action.

The property of souls is a leaning toward multiplicity and motion, a union with the gods, and a capacity for receiving something from others. Souls fill things with vital motion and agree everywhere with all things—partly with those that remain stable, and partly with those that flow. They blend all things within and through themselves. This property is preserved in them according to their essence, virtue, and action.

The properties of demons are to contain within themselves the gifts of the gods, though in a lower way than the gods themselves. Under that divine form and rationale, they also contain the conditions of lower things—namely, they are multiplied, yet in a unified way; they are mixed, yet without being confused; and finally, they are moved, yet in a stable manner. Heroes, on the other hand, possess unity, identity, stability, and excellence, but under the condition of multiplicity, motion, and yielding. Demons and heroes are nearest to the extremes: the former to the gods, the latter to souls.

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic "Great Chain of Being," positioning demons and heroes as essential bridges between the perfect, unified gods and the moving, multiple souls of humans. It defines each rank by its "essence, virtue, and action," emphasizing how divine power is translated down to the physical world.</summary>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Neoplatonic hierarchy, moving from general definitions to specific comparisons between the four ranks of being: Gods, Demons, Heroes, and Souls. It specifically addresses how their actions differ and how the Intellect acts as a bridge between them.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num> ->IAMBLICHUS ON THE MYSTERIES<- <page-num>183</page-num>

The differences between demons and heroes are especially these: heroes sometimes decline more towards particular and changing things than demons. Two intermediaries, namely demons and heroes, are composed of the characteristics of the extremes—that is, the gods and souls—but in demons there is more of the divine, and in heroes more of the human.

### How the actions of the higher beings are distinguished from one another.

Gods hold the highest rank of beings, and souls the lowest. Gods can do all things, being united in a single moment; souls, on the contrary, can neither do all things, nor act in a single moment, nor are they united. Gods act upon and care for all things without being "inclined" <note>original: "non inclinati"; meaning the gods act without being pulled down or distracted by the material world</note>, while souls act with a certain inclination and directed attention. Gods are the primary causes; souls depend perpetually upon the will of the gods. With the swiftest vigor, the gods grasp their innermost ends together with their beginnings <note>the gods see the beginning and the end of a process simultaneously</note>; souls proceed from one thing to another, moving from the imperfect toward the perfect. Gods cannot be grasped by any measure or form; souls are held and constrained by their inclinations, habits, and wills; they are touched by a craving for lesser things and a familiarity with them, and they contract certain qualities from that contact.

<column-break/>

### Concerning the intellect and the soul.

The Intellect, the maker <term>opifex: the craftsman or "Demiurge" who shapes the universe</term> of all things, is always present to all the gods most fully, through a single act remaining within itself. The Soul is a participant in intellect, but in a way that is more divisible and multifaceted, adapted for the task of governing; it naturally provides for inanimate things, being affected at different times by different forms. Likewise, order and beauty itself—or the causes of both—are present to the gods from even higher causes; but to the soul, order and "intellectual beauty" are present. Likewise, in the gods there is a measure that is powerful over all things; but for the soul, it is that measure which determines the soul’s limits and acts through it upon specific things.

Demons and heroes, acting as intermediaries between gods and souls in a fitting proportion, are composed of the properties of both souls and gods (which we described above) as they relate to the extremes and to themselves. If you wish, therefore, to understand the nature <note>original: "speciem"; the form or species</note> of the gods, think of the properties of the lowest rational soul and attribute the opposite perfections to the gods. If it pleases you to conceive of the nature of a demon, compose it from both, while attributing more of the divine. If, finally, you seek the nature of a hero, do so from both, yet bringing in more of the conditions of the soul. He <note>Iamblichus</note> censures Porphyry <note>original: "Carpit Porphyrium"; Porphyry was Iamblichus's teacher and rival philosopher whose views on spirits Iamblichus often critiques for being too materialistic</note> for distinguishing these separate beings only by their relationships to bodies...

M 4
<meta>catchword: Concerning the intel...</meta>

<summary>This page defines the metaphysical distinctions between Gods, Demons, Heroes, and Souls, explaining that Gods act with unified, effortless power while Souls are subject to change and material inclination. It describes Demons and Heroes as hybrid beings that bridge the gap between the divine "Intellect" and the physical world.</summary>

<keywords>Neoplatonism, Great Chain of Being, Iamblichus, Porphyry, Intellect, Soul, Hierarchy of Spirits, Divine Providence, Demiurge</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how to properly categorize divine beings. Iamblichus is arguing against the common mistake of defining higher beings (like gods and demons) based on the physical bodies or locations they govern, rather than their own inherent nature.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

...distinguishing, namely, gods by their relationship to ethereal bodies, demons by their relationship to aerial ones, and souls by their relationship to earthly ones; for surely the prior things and the causes should not be defined by the posterior things and the effects. Incorporeal substances are not *within* bodies, but rather they lead them from the outside, and they give something to the bodies without receiving anything in return. Therefore, they are neither changed along with the bodies, nor are they divided according to the distribution of those bodies. For they are not states <term>habitus: the internal disposition or "way of being" of a thing</term> of bodies, nor are they material forms; rather, they are self-existing and separable—indeed, they are already separate and they live before bodies exist. These separate and separable substances do not become what they are because they chose a certain lot or province, or because such a lot was assigned to them; rather, it is the other way around: according to the kind of life and form the soul brought forth into action within itself before entering a body, it is allotted such an organic body and a corresponding nature, which is then subjected to the more perfect life of the soul itself. The rational soul comes into a body that is already living, but only imperfectly living. Divine beings naturally generate their entire work as a distinct whole within themselves before they bring it forth. Among those beings that possess the nature of a universal principle, the lower things are produced within the higher, the corporeal within the incorporeal, and the works within the workers; and within these beings...

<meta>catchword: circularis (circular)</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>185</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

...they are directed by those who contain them in a circular fashion. Therefore, the celestial orbits are directed back to the orbit of the celestial souls. Indeed, the souls of the worlds—that is, of the spheres and the stars—as they enter into their own intellect, are completely grasped by it and are first generated within it. The particular intellect is contained within the universal intellect, and the universal intellect is likewise contained in the higher things. Since, therefore, things that follow are always "converted" <note>original: "convertantur"; a Neoplatonic term for the way a created thing turns back toward its source for its perfection</note> toward the first things, and the higher things lead the lower as their exemplary models, the lower are certainly produced by the higher. Essence and form are present in the lower things simultaneously; and in all cases, the following things are first produced within the better things. Therefore, the order and measure in the following things depend on the higher ones, and not the other way around. Thus, the properties of the higher beings are not to be assigned based on the lower ones. Consequently, the incorporeal essence cannot be distinguished by the qualities of the bodies with which it has nothing in common. Divine principles grant to their works not only their essence, but also their entire distinction and formal order. The essence which is present to bodies but not localized <note>original: "non localiter"; meaning it is not physically stuck in one spot like a solid object</note> within them, is not distinguished by corporeal places; and that which is not confined by the specific boundaries of its subjects is not at all contained by the parts of the world in a particular way. Nature...

<sig>M 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Natu (Nature)</meta>

<summary>Iamblichus argues that the hierarchy of being moves from the top down; incorporeal substances like the soul and intellect exist before bodies and are not defined by the physical matter they inhabit. He emphasizes that the lower world is contained within the higher divine principles, rather than the gods being "trapped" in the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>incorporeal substance, hierarchy of being, celestial orbits, universal intellect, soul-body relationship, Neoplatonism, causality</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's argument from page 184, further explaining that divine beings are not "contained" by the physical places they govern. He transitions from discussing the ranking of souls and demons to the nature of divine omnipresence.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num> <header>186 IAMBLICHUS | ON THE MYSTERIES. 187</header>

<note>The text begins mid-word; based on the context, the full word is likely "natura" (nature).</note> [An incorporeal] nature is suddenly present wherever it wishes, with nothing anywhere preventing it.

->*Everything divine and incorporeal is everywhere.*<-

Divinity is all the more present because it is no less everywhere, constrained by no limit. Likewise, those things which are among us cannot be made or formed unless a certain single creative power <term>uirtus opifex: the "demiurgic" or crafting power that shapes the physical world</term> and a participation in the forms <note>Iamblichus refers here to the Platonic Ideas or Archetypes that give shape to matter.</note> is diffused through all things. Finally, unless divine things are everywhere, the power of sacrifice is removed; for it consists in a certain communion of the gods with humans. The gods contain all things primarily because they are contained nowhere. Since earthly things are created and exist by the gift of the divine, as often as they are made more ready for the divine, they suddenly obtain the gods, who exist here even before the specific natures of earthly things. Furthermore, since in every god there is infinite power and an indivisible and incomprehensible nature, they deservedly cannot be confined to certain places anywhere. Likewise, there would not be a true union of universal providence among the gods unless all were equally present everywhere. It is also said that some gods are aerial, while others are aquatic, not because they are only there, but because they rule there most significantly. Priests used to call upon earthly and subterranean gods; who are certainly called such, not because they are only there,

<column-break/>

(for they are all everywhere) but because some operate more powerfully in some places than others. For even the soul of any star is everywhere, although it acts more specifically in that [star]. Through statues, a certain divine illumination is received.

### *Concerning providence, and how the gods are everywhere.*

When gods are said to specifically be allotted various parts of the world, cities, temples, or statues, understand that their essence and power—thriving everywhere in itself—illuminates this or that thing most strongly. Just as light, remaining in itself, illuminates diverse things everywhere without mixture or division of itself, so do the gods. Just as the light of the Sun is the same continuous whole everywhere, and can neither be divided into parts, nor enclosed anywhere, nor separated from its own source, nor mixed with the air even though it is present—indeed, while it leaves no trace of light behind <note>original: "nihil relinquat luminis." Iamblichus implies that light itself does not "rub off" or stay in the air like a material substance, even if its effect remains.</note>, yet heat is left behind by the thing that warms—so the light of every god is present entirely and indivisibly to the whole world; although it grants its power especially to a certain part most suited to itself. Meanwhile, however, it fills all things in a certain way, on account of its perfect power and its utterly immense causal excellence <term>excessum caausalem: the "overflow" or "surplus" of causality where a cause is vastly superior to its effect</term>, from which it perfects all things, and unites the farthest extremes with extremes through the middle, encompassing all things in itself—and itself as well—reflecting them back to itself as something completely united.

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic concept of divine omnipresence, arguing that gods are not physically located in statues or specific elements but illuminate them like sunlight. It justifies the practice of theurgy and sacrifice by claiming that gods are "contained nowhere" and thus can be present everywhere.</summary>

<keywords>omnipresence, providence, theurgy, sacrifice, divine illumination, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism, creative power, causality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's explanation of divine omnipresence. The previous page established that the gods are not restricted by physical location; this page explains how the physical world reflects that divine unity and how sacred rites (theurgy) use this connection.</meta>

<page-num>188</page-num>
<header>I A M B L I C H V S</header>
<meta>catchword: bet (completes "Quælibet" on the next page)</meta>

The world itself also imitates this duty <note>original: "munus"; referring to the divine role of ordering the universe</note> through its circular motion and through a certain connection and reconciliation of its parts into a unity, as the elements are transformed into one another in turn and the power of higher things is sent down to the lower. Anyone who accepts this manifest "statue of the gods" <note>Iamblichus uses the term "statue" (statua) as a metaphor for the visible universe, which reflects the invisible divine reality</note> which is entirely united everywhere, ought to be altogether afraid to hold views alien to the gods who are the causes of the world—namely, that they are, so to speak, locally separated. For if there were no communion of the world with the divine powers <term>numina: the spiritual powers or presiding spirits of the gods</term> regarding their essence, power, and action, and no common distribution or fellowship, then surely there could be no coextension or shared distribution according to place. Furthermore, no mutual comprehension or mingling could be imagined regarding that which pertains to their species or genus. Indeed, things that differ entirely in their whole nature admit no reciprocal distribution, mixture, or comprehension at all. When the distribution of the divine powers occurs through various regions of the world, understand that different regions receive the gifts of the gods in different ways according to their own property. Considering this, the <term>ars sacrorum operum: literally "the art of sacred works," referring to Theurgy, the practice of rituals intended to invoke the presence of the divine</term> employs specific invocations that are seen to be appropriate to such a distribution and to the property of the divine powers. Every—

---

<page-num>189</page-num>
<header>D E  M Y S T E R I I S.</header>

—ery <note>Continuing "Quælibet" from the previous page</note> part of the world receives something from every god; for every god is present as a whole to every part of the world. However, different parts receive from them in different ways: the ether receives ethereal things, while the air receives aerial things in its own manner. For by certain materials and invocations, they naturally attract the gifts of the gods.

### *That everything divine is alien to passions.*

The divine powers superior to us have nothing within themselves that is "passible" <note>original: "passibile"; meaning the capacity to be acted upon, to suffer change, or to be affected by external emotions</note>; and they possess this "impassibility" (if I may call it that) not by acquired virtue, but by nature. Nor does even the soul, which is the last of the divine beings, have anything passible within it. I speak here of the soul that is pure from bodies, which is not even touched by the disturbance of pleasure—that which is a restoration to nature—since it possesses an innate supernatural life. Nor is it touched by any dissolving pain, since it exists outside of every body and outside of the nature divided around the body, and outside the harmony descending into the body from the principal harmony of the soul. Nor does it even require the passions that precede the senses; for it is not contained by a body, nor is it forced to perceive bodies through external instruments. Indeed, since it is an indivisible and uniform essence, remaining incorporeal according to its own nature, it has nothing in common with the body.

<summary>Iamblichus explains how the physical universe acts as a unified reflection of the divine and introduces the concept of divine impassibility, arguing that gods and pure souls are beyond physical or emotional suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, Divine Presence, Elements, Passibility, Incorporeal Soul, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism, Sacred Rites</keywords>

<page-num>190</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>
<meta>Catchword: to receive.</meta>

...communicating; clearly it admits no passions <note>original: "passiones"; in ancient philosophy, a "passion" is not just an emotion, but any state of being "acted upon" or changed by an external force</note> of division, or alteration, or any change whatsoever.

->He speaks of the soul separated from the body.<-

The soul, even while it is present in the body, does not itself suffer anything, nor do the seminal reasons <term>rationes seminales: the formative, "seed-like" principles that govern the growth and organization of the physical body</term> which it imparts to the body. For these forms are simple and uniform, undergoing no disturbance or departure from their own nature. Whatever passions there may be in a living being, they are not in the soul—regardless of its nature—but in the body, which, under the soul's influence, possesses a certain quality through which it both suffers and acts. There is no passion in the soul that is either destructive or disturbing. And the soul does not only acquire this impassibility <note>the state of being unable to be harmed or changed by outside forces</note> by choice, but possesses it by its very nature, which is far superior to physical bodies. Daemons <note>original: "Dæmones"; in Neoplatonism, these are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily "demons" in the modern evil sense</note> and heroes are impassible and eternal: for they always follow the gods in preserving the order of the world. But where there is passion, perpetual order is not maintained. Porphyry <note>A philosopher who often questioned the logic behind religious rituals</note> suspected that the daemons who rule the world were in some way subject to passions because of the offerings used in sacred rites. Iamblichus denies this, because where there is perpetual order, there is no passion—which an incorporeal essence cannot receive through eternal things.

<column-break/>

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES.</header>

to receive. But perhaps he might concede that the lowest and evil daemons, who are deprived of divine governance, are more effectively touched by passions—not indeed in their soul, but in such a body as is constituted by the presence of the soul. Just as nature makes manifest things from hidden reasons, so the priest employs manifest things to signify the hidden. The priest often performs many acts by which he might effect something similar and proper to divine things, and through this kinship, he may attract something from the divine. Meanwhile, he also performs many works through which he may purify the soul and turn away evils from us. The daemons, who are superior to us, receive no natural benefit from us: for either they do not need it, or they themselves abundantly provide for themselves everywhere. Indeed, they never suffer any change from corporeal things. There are within us certain principles of disturbance which, if they are restrained too violently and suddenly from the start, eventually burst forth more sharply—like a compressed flame or stifled laughter; therefore, they must be corrected more gently. Heraclitus <note>An early Greek philosopher famous for the idea that "everything flows"</note> calls sacrifices "medicines," because they purge the soul of diseases contracted in this world of generation <note>the physical world where things are born and die</note>. Porphyry suspected that not only daemons but even gods were touched by some passion, since they are moved by invocations. The will for good in God and in the gods is more excellent than the goodnesses themselves,

<summary>Iamblichus argues that the soul and higher spirits (daemons) are "impassible," meaning they cannot be changed or harmed by the physical world. He explains that religious rituals do not "influence" the gods through emotion, but rather purify the human soul and align it with divine order.</summary>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of the gods and their relationship with the human soul. Previously, Iamblichus argued that the gods are impassible—meaning they cannot be changed or affected by external forces. Here, he explores how prayer and invocation do not "change" the gods' minds, but rather change the person praying, making them receptive to the divine goodness that is always being offered.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>
<header>192 IAMBLICHUS</header>
...and therefore their life is freer than our own life of choice regarding what is good. Thus, the gods are not moved to bestow goodness upon men simply because they are invoked; rather, they spontaneously call us toward the good, and they meet us of their own accord when we have turned toward them through prayer, revealing and granting their gifts. Men indeed believe they are free to ask; so be it: then the gods are even freer to give. Because of their free will for the good and their eternal, perfect activity, the gods benefit all things as soon as people have turned toward them through invocation. When the soul reaches a state of contemplating divine things through its supplications, it transforms its former life—which was subject to passions <note>original: "passionibus"; in this context, "passions" refers to being "acted upon" by external, physical, or emotional forces</note>—into a life free from such suffering, as it enters into the activity and life of the gods. How then can our supplications affect the gods with "passions," when these very prayers actually render our previously passive souls free from passions? Supplications make the soul fit to receive the influence of the gods, which arrives easily and of its own accord because of the divine friendship that connects all things.

### *On the anger of the gods.*

When we turn away from the beneficial care of the gods, as if turning away from the light of the sun, we are immediately in dark- <meta>catchword: ness</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>193</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES. 193</header>
-ness, and it is then said that God is "angry" with us. Therefore, to appease the anger of the gods is simply to return to them by means of prayer. This kind of "appeasement" does not bring any passion or change to God; rather, it frees the human soul from its previous state of suffering. The gods and divine daemons <note>original: "dæmones"; intermediary spirits that act as messengers between the divine and human realms</note>, when humbly adored and honored, repel many evils that would otherwise occur through the natural course of events.

### *On the necessity and liberty of the gods.*

It is necessary for the gods to be exactly as they are. This is not because of a necessity forced upon them from the outside, but because of a necessity that is natural and optimal for them. It is, therefore, a supreme act of will; they would never wish to be otherwise, even if given the choice. Porphyry <note>A rival philosopher who often questioned Iamblichus's mystical views</note> and many other philosophers claim that the gods are "pure intellects," while daemons are "animal intellects" <note>intellects that are joined to a soul or life-force</note> or participants in the intellect. However, Iamblichus objects that this does not correctly define the unique nature of daemons. For human souls are also "animal intellects" and participants in the intellect. Nor, again, does it correctly define the nature of the gods, for the gods actually preside over and rank above pure intellects.

### *On the difference between gods and daemons according to the Greeks and the most devout.*

The gods [are defined] not according to the principle of intellect itself, but according to the principles of unity, goodness, and intel-

<summary>This page explains that prayer does not change the gods, but rather transforms the human soul so it can receive divine light. Iamblichus also refutes Porphyry's definitions of gods and daemons, arguing that gods transcend even "pure intellect."</summary>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of theurgical practices—religious rituals and prayers—against the skepticism of his teacher Porphyry. While Porphyry suggests that pure, divine minds are too far removed from our physical world to hear us, Iamblichus argues that the divine is present everywhere through "The One," and that ritual acts serve to elevate the human soul rather than drag the gods down.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>
<header>I A M B L I C H V S</header>

...properly defined by the intelligible object <note>original: "intelligibilis"; this completes the word from the previous page</note>. Porphyry suspects that pure and separate intellects <note>refers to divine beings existing independently of physical matter</note> have no relationship with physical things, nor do they hear prayers. However, the primary form of worship must be offered to these separate substances; for it is through this worship that the highest part of the soul is awakened and joined to the highest beings. These supreme beings comprehend all our affairs within "The One," and they understand the actions of religious prayers within their own selves. They do not depart from their own nature, but remaining stable within themselves, they are fully present to the intention of those who pray. Indeed, it is most fitting to pray with great humility; for acknowledging our own lowly state when compared to the higher powers causes us to pray most earnestly, to turn ourselves toward them completely, and through constant habit, to become like them. Above all, those prayers that are divinely sent—which possess a divine power—are most beneficial. This is argued against Porphyry, who holds the opinion that we should specifically worship the "worldly" gods because they possess senses. Likewise, he believes that supplications <note>humble prayers or petitions</note> are not at all appropriate for the purity of divine minds. Iamblichus, however, thinks they are appropriate because of the "inner sense" <note>an intellectual or spiritual perception rather than a physical one</note> that exists within supplications, especially those given by the gods themselves. Pure intellects know physical things without the use of physical senses.

### Again on supplications.

Porphyry says that certain material things are used in sup-
<meta>catchword: plications</meta>

---

<page-num>195</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES.</header>

plications; therefore, he argues they are only used for those gods who are "animals" <note>original: "animalia"; in a philosophical sense, this refers to any being composed of a soul and a body</note>. Iamblichus responds that within those material things, beyond their physical qualities, there also lie hidden rational principles, forms, and measures <note>original: "rationes, & species, & mensuras"; these are the underlying divine patterns that structure reality</note> that are incorporeal and divine. It is through these that the materials used in ritual are made compatible with the gods. Certainly, whatever is brought forward—provided it is in some way harmonious with and similar to the gods—the gods are immediately present; they join with it, bestow their gifts, and hear the prayers. A small degree of congruence <note>a "fittingness" or sympathetic link</note> between our affairs and the gods is enough for us to draw something from them. For they are always most ready for this, because of their natural goodness and wonderful power. Indeed, a powerful cause can act even upon matter that is only slightly prepared. Porphyry distinguishes demons from gods on the basis that the former are incorporeal while the latter are corporeal <note>possessing bodies, such as the stars and planets</note>; Iamblichus says that their essential substances and properties cannot be distinguished by these physical traits alone.

### On the difference between gods and demons, and on the celestial beings.

The celestial gods can be called "incorporeal" because no hindrance arises from their bodies to interfere with their superior activity and the happiness of their life. But insofar as the gods tend toward "The One" itself, their bodies spontaneously strive toward that same goal.

<summary>Iamblichus argues that the gods hear prayers through a spiritual "inner sense" and that physical ritual objects contain divine patterns that create a bridge between the human and the divine. He challenges Porphyry's view that gods are defined by having bodies, asserting instead that celestial bodies do not hinder divine perfection.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s argument regarding the nature of the heavens. He is explaining that celestial bodies (the stars and planets) are not like earthly bodies where a soul is "trapped" in matter, but rather the bodies are entirely governed and illuminated by the soul.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>

# IAMBLICHVS

...nor do they contain souls, but they are most excellently contained by them <note>In Neoplatonic thought, the higher (soul) contains the lower (body), not the other way around</note>. The celestial body is most closely related to incorporeal <note>non-physical</note> things because of its simple, undivided, and constant nature, and its single action—that is, its circular motion, its innate life, and its light. In heaven, there is no composition of soul and body into a single third nature <note>unlike humans, who are a mix of both</note>; instead, the body is drawn entirely toward the nature of the soul, and it is like a kind of visible soul. Perhaps heaven is light itself, without matter and without dimension. For dimension appears there only because of the vastness of its presence. Thus, heaven is the soul of heaven adapting itself to our eyes and to falling things; and that circle is the movement of the soul, and its light is the intellect of that same soul. Just as in the lowest of bodies, form becomes material <note>losing its purity</note>, so in the highest, matter becomes formal <note>original: "formalis"; meaning the matter is so refined it takes on the characteristics of pure spirit</note>, and the body becomes "soul-like," rather than the soul becoming "body-like"—if indeed there is any matter at all in heaven. Just as the highest air becomes fiery, so the celestial fire becomes alive. That soul emerges as intellectual, and the intellect is made "good-form" <note>aligned with the ultimate Good</note>. Celestial living beings can be called incorporeal. All celestial beings are good and beneficial, looking only toward the Good itself, and acting eternally with this vision. Inestimable virtues <note>powers or capacities</note> have been discovered, both in celestial...

They are immutable <note>unchanging</note> everywhere, even though they govern generation <note>the process of birth and change</note> within changeable matter in a certain perpetual order, and they are always beneficial. Thus, from the flow of heaven into matter full of discord, something discordant to us results. Temporal <note>time-bound</note> things receive eternal things in a temporal way; bodies receive incorporeal things in a bodily way. These material things receive the immaterial powers of the heavens in a changeable and disordered fashion. Those things that are in the divine world are not shaped <note>original: "figurata"; having no physical form</note>, even though they are received as shaped things in heaven; likewise, celestial things are not evil, even though here they are received as evil. All the powers of the celestial beings descend from there as good, but in this mixture of opposites <note>our physical world</note> they are changed. Therefore, the quality that causes harm on earth is already different from that which arrived here from heaven. First, that which is given is one thing in the giver, but another thing outside of them, even if it remains the same in itself; furthermore, it is also diminished. Second, while it is received in a lowlier subject, it also becomes lowlier. Third, because of the different nature of the recipient, it is received in a different way. <summary>Iamblichus describes the heavens as a realm where matter is refined into "formal" light and governed entirely by soul and intellect. He explains that while celestial influences are purely good and beneficial at their source, they appear distorted or "evil" to us because of the disordered and limited nature of the physical matter that receives them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's discussion of how celestial bodies influence the earthly realm. He is arguing that the apparent "evil" or "harm" caused by planets like Saturn or Mars is not due to the planets themselves, but rather the inability of earthly matter to properly receive their pure, powerful energies.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>

# IAMBLICHVS

...things which are received equally from the same subject. Fifthly, it suffers when the subject suffers. Sixthly, from all the things contained in the subject, something else finally results. Celestial influences <term>influxus</term> <note>the "flowing in" of spiritual or astrological power from the stars to the earth</note> extend into material things. The power of Saturn is indeed containing and binding; whereas that of Mars is a moving force. The former [Saturn] frequently causes harm by accident when it is received by matter that is too cold; the latter [Mars] when received by matter that is too burning. Likewise, the former harms when it is received by way of freezing; the latter when by parching heat. This happens because of the disposition of the matter—that is, when that matter is not hot enough and is, at the same time, too dense; the latter, however, is of itself hotter and more subtle. The light and color of the Sun, although they seem to harm the weak <note>referring to those with weak or diseased eyes</note>, are nevertheless necessary for life. Similarly, all celestial influences arrive as healthy and salvific, even if the perversity of the recipient receives them perversely, or if weakness cannot easily endure the efficacy of the higher powers. All movements contribute to both the whole and to the necessary parts of the universe; even though, in the meantime, among the smallest particles under this movement, one might be harmed by another, or such particles might not easily sustain the movement of the whole. Just as in a dance <term>chorea</term> <note>a Greek-style choral dance used as a metaphor for the harmony of the spheres</note>, while individuals dance harmoniously and correspond in...

<meta>catchword: gestures</meta>

---

<page-num>199</page-num>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

...gestures with each other and with the whole chorus, a finger or a foot is pressed and hurt; and if anything weak enters the movement, it is crushed.

### *On the proximity of celestial bodies to incorporeal species.*

The celestial gods, having their origin in the intelligible world <note>the realm of pure thought and "Forms" that exists above the physical world</note>, while they contemplate their own Ideas, move the heavens by this very act of intelligence and will, which is remarkably effective and perpetual. Therefore, they rule without effort, which is clearly apparent from the always constant order of the stars. In this way, the gods are joined to these separated states <note>the pure Ideas</note> rather than to physical bodies. The governance of the heavens cannot distract the gods from their intellectual focus; indeed, they rule more powerfully and easily the more attentively they contemplate that higher realm. For the governance of the world proceeds from that contemplation. The same would also happen to our own living creatures, if they could contemplate their own cause, from which the faculty of growth and life also comes.

### *Confirmation of the above.*

The Divine Mind procreates the souls of the celestial gods through its Ideas, and through those souls it creates the celestial bodies, keeping both perpetually joined to itself. The worldly gods, who are also called "intellectual species," are united among themselves, both because all share the same exemplar <note>the "blueprint" or "pattern"</note> in which the intelligible species exist...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that planetary influences are inherently good, but can cause accidental harm depending on the "temperature" or density of the earthly matter receiving them. He also clarifies that gods rule the heavens effortlessly through pure contemplation of divine Ideas.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Saturn, Mars, celestial influence, harmony of the spheres, Divine Mind, intelligible world, Neoplatonism, Iamblichus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic exploration of divine hierarchy, moving from the physical influence of celestial bodies discussed on the previous page to the internal, metaphysical unity that binds the gods together. It transitions into a comparison between the "universal providence" of the gods and the "particular providence" of demons.</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>

# IAMBLICHVS

...behold the <term>intelligibles</term> <note>original: "intelligibiles"; things perceived by the intellect rather than the physical senses, such as pure forms or ideas</note>, because that exemplar flourishes everywhere and in its entirety within every <term>mundane god</term> <note>original: "deo mundano"; gods associated with the physical cosmos/world</note>. Neither space nor different subjects prevent incorporeal essences from being present within one another everywhere. The mundane gods are within each other and within their own <term>Ideas</term> <note>the divine archetypes or blueprints of all things</note>: partly because they come from the One and are referred back to the One; partly because the divine unity, prevailing everywhere, unites them; and partly because they proceed from one another and return to one another, receiving from each other in turn. In natural, composite things, unity is <term>adventitious</term> <note>original: "aduentitia"; external or added on, rather than being part of the thing's essential nature</note>, depending on higher things and being overcome by multiplicity. In separable forms, unity overcomes multiplicity; and in the gods, it overcomes it so thoroughly that their very being is a certain unity. I say "a certain" unity, because the first principle is itself Unity simply. Since, therefore, unity so prevails internally within the gods, it deservedly overflows from the prior ones into those that follow: so that the latter consist within the unity of the former. Through this unity, as if common to all, all are one in a certain act, just as bodies are one among themselves through a certain material potential. The celestial gods are completely united to the invisible gods; partly because celestial souls are outside their bodies, and partly because the higher gods—that is, the Ideas—possess an infinite unity, insofar as all are in any one [god]; through

<meta>catchword: quam</meta>

---

<page-num>201</page-num>

# DE MYSTERIIS.

which unity they unite the gods that follow to themselves and to each other. For since all gods are defined by unity, one common unity is spread through them all: through which all are one by the act of all toward one single object, principle, and end, at the same point and moment simultaneously.

->*On providence, and on gods and demons.*<-

The providence of the gods is universal, whereas that of <term>demons</term> <note>original: "dæmonum"; in this context, these are not "evil" spirits, but intermediary beings who bridge the gap between gods and humans</note> is particular. The first God gives all things to all; the gods who follow grant some things to all; demons and souls give only to some, and only some things. There are invisible gods, and there are visible gods in the heavens: there are also demons. Indeed, the gods visible in the heaven exist in the very union with the hidden gods; demons, however, stand far apart from them, just as the demons are more intently occupied in acting, while the gods are pure contemplators: for this reason, they also stand far apart from the visible gods. Likewise, the essence of both types of gods, as well as their power and providence, is universal, reaching through all things; whereas the nature of demons is a particular providence, concerning certain things and provinces. Likewise, the gods so greatly transcend their own bodies, and their bodies are so obedient to them, that they are said to be separate from their bodies; demons, however...

<summary>This passage describes the metaphysical unity of the gods, explaining how they exist within one another through the "One." It also distinguishes between the universal providence of the gods and the specific, localized actions of demons.</summary>

<keywords>Neoplatonism, Divine Unity, The One, Mundane Gods, Providence, Demons, Celestial Bodies, Iamblichus</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's defense of theurgical practices, moving from the internal unity of the gods to their relationship with the lower hierarchies of demons, heroes, and human souls. It specifically addresses the confusion between divine nature and human emotion in religious ritual.</meta>

<page-num>202, 203</page-num>

# IAMBLICHVS

...they are more affected by the contrary reasoning and are joined to bodies. Likewise, the gods are the architects, while the <term>demons</term> <note>original: "dæmones"; in Neoplatonic thought, these are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily evil beings</note> are the ministers. Demons are hidden from the senses, and the gods are hidden even from human reason. For if the gods should happen to appear upon the earth, they will still be superior to the demons of the air: for a change of place or duty does not change the divine nature. Finally, the gods are free from the powers flowing into <term>generation</term> <note>original: "generationem"; the realm of physical birth, change, and decay</note>; demons, however, are by no means so. He refutes what Porphyry <note>A Phoenician philosopher and student of Plotinus who questioned the efficacy and morality of theurgic rituals</note> had brought forward from history—namely, that many things are done to the gods as if they were <term>passible</term> <note>original: "passibiles"; capable of feeling "passions" or being affected by external influences</note>. This is because the law of sacred rites commands one to be free from <term>passions</term> <note>original: "passionibus"; emotions or physical disturbances</note> during sacrifices: which law indeed was given by divine authority, even from the first father of the world, from whom also all symbols in sacrifices signify something most hidden. Finally, those things which are performed correctly in sacred rites have a divine cause, so remote from passion that not even reason can reach it. The common people cannot grasp the reason for ceremonies divinely instituted, yet they attempt to assign one. By assigning reasons based on their own passions, they attribute passions to the gods. The common people use gestures of veneration toward powerful men and use the same gifts toward the gods: but toward men, they do this both because of their own passions and because of the passions of the superior. But they think 

<column-break/>

->DE MYSTERIIS<-

and similar passions exist in the gods as exist in powerful men: and that because of similar passions in subjects and worshipers, such things are offered to the gods. But in truth, ceremonies divinely instituted are not performed out of any passion, but rather out of admiration and veneration, and an entirely constant intelligence and a very similar joy.

### *How demons, heroes, and souls differ.*

There are in the gods generative and crafting powers; there are also life-giving powers, which are contained more sublimely in the former. The first are spread more widely than the second; the second are defined by the first. Demons begin after a long progression of these, where they are now diverted into many partitions. <term>Heroes</term> <note>original: "Heroës"; semi-divine beings or souls of great merit that act as intermediaries</note> are defined through the second kind—that is, the life-giving—before they reach the last stage: for in the last stage of these, souls begin. Therefore, the duty of demons is the craftsmanship concerning <term>mundane</term> <note>of the physical world</note> natures, to pierce through into individual things and simultaneously perfect them, and to bind souls to bodies. But the role of heroes is to give life and to reason, to guard souls, and to release them from bodies. The duty of demons is broader than that of heroes. The former work more regarding the universe, the latter more regarding souls. We do not say that souls are created, but that they receive something from demons and heroes...

<summary>Iamblichus distinguishes the roles of gods, demons, and heroes, arguing that while gods remain impassive and architectonic, demons act as intermediaries who bind souls to the physical world. He further refutes Porphyry’s claim that gods are moved by human-like emotions in sacrifices, asserting that religious rites are based on divine reason beyond human grasp.</summary>

<keywords>Iamblichus, Porphyry, hierarchy of being, demons, heroes, sacrifice, passions, divine architecture, soul-body relation</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of the soul's unique position in the cosmic hierarchy. Having previously discussed how the gods and demons differ in their nature and relationship to the physical world, he now explores how the human soul can ascend through these ranks and how different spiritual beings manifest themselves to a practitioner of theurgy.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

...and from itself it can apply many things besides, and begins its own duty near the boundary of divine things. This is not because divine things do not act further, but because souls are admitted at the furthest reaches of divine offices. Likewise, the soul can bring forth from itself such varied forms of living that it may inhabit individual regions of the world according to their suitability. It can also meet with those it wishes, and again return into itself, and join itself to both gods and inferior beings, insofar as it brings forth the <term>principles</term> <note>original: "rationes"; the underlying logical or spiritual structures</note> of divine things as an inferior would. And although the soul often joins with the gods through <term>demons</term> <note>original: "dæmones"; intermediary spirits between humans and gods</note> and <term>heroes</term>, it can also—on account of a certain middle distinction of its own—join itself to the gods directly. The soul sometimes reconciles itself to divine things through different harmonies of its essence and actions than those by which demons join themselves to the higher powers. Even if the soul does not maintain a life so consistently similar to the divine, nevertheless, often illuminated by the beneficent will of the gods, it transcends the level of demons toward an angelic life rather than a merely animal one. Although the soul may seem to exhibit all principles and all <term>forms</term> <note>original: "species"</note> within itself, it is nevertheless always determined according to some one thing—that is, one specific form—and meanwhile, it coordinates itself through various preceding causes, moving itself.
