### *When different deities appear and act differently.*

All those above, when invoked, come into view; but some appear under different forms and powers than others. The souls of the stars also stir the sight in certain new ways. The visions of the gods appear uniform and most simple; those of the archangels are somewhat less uniform or simple; those of the angels are also less so; those of the demons are varied; those of the <term>principalities</term> <note>original: "principatuum"; a specific rank of spiritual rulers in the celestial hierarchy</note> are more varied, but in a miraculous order of variety; those of the heroes are almost similar to these; those of the <term>princes</term> <note>original: "principes"; lower-ranking spirits who oversee specific regions or elements</note> are very varied and poorly ordered; the visions of particular souls are uniform. For since those above are diverse in their natures and actions, and appear to each in their own way, they rightly meet us in diverse ways.

The gods come into view in a manner that is entirely <term>salutary</term> <note>original: "salutares"; health-giving or beneficial to the soul's salvation</note>; the archangels are both terrifying and gentle; the angels are milder; the demons are to be feared; the heroes are revered to a lesser degree; the principalities are astounding; the princes are harmful and hostile; souls are similar to heroes, although inferior. The images of the gods appear entirely immutable in magnitude, form, and figure, as well as in their action. Those of the archangels and angels are gradually less immutable, but remain close to the nature of the gods. Those of the demons are indeed changeable in magnitude and form, for to these...

They appear differently at different times, yet recognize themselves as the same.
Those of the <term>Principalities</term> <note>original: "Principatuum"; high-ranking celestial rulers in the Neoplatonic hierarchy</note> are immutable; those of <term>heroes</term> <note>ancient semi-divine spirits or elevated human souls</note> are almost like those of <term>demons</term> <note>intermediary spirits between gods and humans</note>; those of the <term>princes</term> <note>original: "principum"; lower-level ruling spirits often associated with the material world</note> vary in many forms; those of souls are far more mutable than those of demons. The Gods appear with a certain quiet and order; archangels with the same, but they add an efficacy to them; angels with the same—namely, quiet and order—but they add an ordered and moderate motion. Demons appear with a disturbance that transgresses order; principalities with a stability and constancy within themselves; heroes with motion and haste; princes with tumult; souls appear almost like heroes, but with less stability and order. The beauty of the Gods occurs as inestimable, incomparable, admirable, and pleasing beyond measure. That of archangels and angels is similar, but unequal in degree. The spirits of demons and heroes, appearing by themselves, have beauty in determinate <term>forms</term> <note>original: "speciebus"</note>; but the former according to the <term>principles</term> <note>original: "rationes"; the underlying spiritual logic</note> of their essence, the latter according to an act of fortitude. The beauty of the Principalities is primary and native; that of the princes is fashioned, adorned, and elaborate. The beauty of souls consists in determinate principles indeed, but more divided than in the form of heroes, yet comprehended under a single

<page-type>text</page-type>

form. The actions and effects of the Gods are faster than the intellect can grasp, yet meanwhile they remain thoroughly stable. The speed of archangels is mixed in some way with effective actions; that of angels is with motion; nor do they possess it in such a way that they may perfect a thing as soon as it is spoken. The actions of demons appear faster than they are in effect and truth. Heroes indeed march magnificently toward action, yet they complete it more slowly than demons. Principalities come to act with authority and command. The effort of the Princes indeed has much <term>outward display</term> <note>original: "emphasim"; a Greek-derived term meaning a vivid appearance or semblance that may lack substance</note>, but it falls short of the effect. The actions of souls are more mobile than those of heroes, but weaker. The magnitude of the appearing Gods seems to occupy the whole heaven; as they advance, the earth does not think it can sustain them. When archangels appear, certain parts of the world are moved, and a light precedes them that is divided within itself; they indeed, according to the magnitude of their dominion, display a magnitude of light and figure. The angelic form and light is both smaller and more divided. The magnitude and form of demons is more divided and smaller still. The heroic magnitude is again narrower, and of a magnanimous appearance. Principalities are very great and vast; princes are inflated

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how different classes of divine and spiritual beings reveal themselves to human perception. ### IAMBLICHUS, ON THE MYSTERIES.

...occurring to us, is attributed to the higher classes. Now, you should assign that pre-effulgent quality, which remains firmly within itself, to the **Gods**. That which shines and remains indeed, but as if it were in another, assign to the **archangels**; and that which remains in another, to the **angels**. Likewise, that which is mobile and wandering, and full of foreign natures, should everywhere be attributed to the inferior orders. **Demons** have mixed within themselves the vapors of the world, and are carried unstable along or beside the motion of the world. To **heroes**, generative compositions of spirits are mixed, around which they themselves are moved. The <term>Principalities</term> <note>original: "Principatus"; high-ranking celestial rulers who govern nations or large groups</note> remain in the same way, showing the worldly nature they possess. The <term>princes</term> <note>original: "Principes"; lower-level ruling spirits specifically tied to the governance of material elements</note> are full of material fluids <note>original: "liquoribus"; likely referring to the elemental humors or physical substances of the sublunary world</note>. **Souls** are filled with superfluous stains and foreign spirits; whenever they appear, each of these classes shows itself by these traits. The **Gods** consume matter <note>the physical substance of the world</note> instantly; **archangels** do so little by little; **angels** release the soul from it and recall it to the heights; **demons** diligently adorn matter; **heroes** fit themselves to it, caring for it vigorously and suitably with appropriate measures everywhere. The <term>Principalities</term> stand over matter, pre-eminent, and in this way they show themselves. The <term>princes</term> show themselves to be entirely filled with matter. 

<column-break/>

**Souls** indeed come apart from matter; but impure ones show themselves to us enveloped in matter. Moreover, different beings grant us different gifts. The **Gods**, when present, give health to the body, virtue to the soul, and purity to the mind; they recall all our parts to their own original principles. What is cold and death-bringing in us, they destroy; they foster heat and make it more effective for life. They also cause all things of the soul and mind to agree and be measured by the same standards, and they pour out light through an intelligible harmony. They show that which is not a body as if it were a body to the very eyes of the soul, through the eyes of the physical body. **Archangels** do the same things, but not as sufficiently or permanently, nor beyond our human proportion. **Angels** distribute even more particular goods in a divided manner, and produce an effect that is greatly lacking in their innermost vigor. **Demons**, by their presence, weigh down the body, afflict it with diseases, or punish it in other ways; they also drag souls down toward nature, and do not depart from bodies and their related physical senses. Furthermore, they detain those who strive toward the celestial or divine fire in a lower place, and they do not release them from the <term>bonds of fate</term> <note>the deterministic laws of the material and astrological world</note>. **Heroes** do other things similar to **demons**.

...they possess this specific quality: they rouse us toward certain noble and great works. The <term>Principalities</term> <note>original: "Principatus"; high-level cosmic administrators in the Neoplatonic hierarchy who govern broad regions or nations</note>, when they appear, bestow worldly goods and supply all the necessities of this life. The **Princes** <note>original: "Principes"; spiritual governors specifically tied to the management of the material elements</note> grant material and earthly goods. When **souls** appear—if they are pure souls established within the **angelic order**—they call the observer upward, providing health to the mind, a sacred hope, and those sacred gifts for which one hopes. Impure souls, however, twist the observer down toward <term>generation</term> <note>original: "generationem"; the state of being caught in the cycle of physical birth, decay, and material existence</note>; they frustrate hope, taking away its fruits, and they fill observers with passions that bind the soul to the body.

The rank which each entity holds is also designated by how they appear. **Gods** show other gods or angels standing around them. **Archangels** present angels either preceding them, surrounding them, or following them, or else some other crowd of angels acting as a bodyguard encircling them. **Angels** present works that are consistent with their own order. **Good demons** bring their handiwork and the benefits they grant into view. **Avenging demons** <note>original: "dæmones ultores"</note> show various forms of punishments. Other **wicked demons**, whenever they are encountered, appear surrounded by deadly and ravenous wild beasts. The **Principalities** show certain provinces of the world alongside themselves. The **Princes**...

<meta>catchword: condition</meta>

<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES.</header>

...bring the condition of **matter**, which is lacking in order and wandering, into view with them. A **soul** that is whole and not held by any particular form shows a fire without a definite shape around the whole world; by this, the nature of the **World Soul** is signified—undivided, one, and bound to no specific form. A **purified soul** shows a fiery figure, a pure fire and a glittering light that is simultaneously stable; it follows a sublime leader with a joyous and good will, thus making manifest the rank it holds through its works. But the **soul that falls downward** appears dragging signs of bonds and punishments with it; it shows itself weighed down by a composition of material spirits and occupied by the chaotic disturbances of matter, submitting itself to the rule of **demons** who favor the process of physical generation.

Each class, when they appear, shows the provinces and lots <note>original: "fortes"; the designated "allotments" or regions assigned to them by divine law</note> which they inhabit. The **aerial** ones show an aerial fire; the **earthly** ones show an earthly and black fire; and the **celestial** ones show a more splendid fire. Within these three boundaries, all their orders are distributed. The **Gods** demonstrate the principles of all orders within themselves when they appear. **Angels** declare that they depend upon the **archangels**. **Demons** and **heroes**...

they reveal that they serve the higher powers: although these do so differently than those. The **Principalities** <note>original: "Principatus"; cosmic governors</note> and **Princes** show their prefecture <note>or "governance"</note>: the former over the world, the latter over matter. **Souls** declare that they occupy the last rank of the superior beings; hence, together with themselves, the primary ones designate the first places, the secondary ones the second, the third ones the third, and the others follow in that same order. **Gods** shine forth with such a great subtlety of light that the eyes of the body are unable to grasp it, and are affected in the same way as fish that are pulled from murky and thick water into the thinnest air. For those who behold the divine fire, being unable to breathe the subtlety of that divine fire, fail in their strength as soon as they begin to look, and their innate spirit <note>original: "innato spiritu"; the "pneuma" or vital breath connecting the soul to the body</note> is entirely cut off. **Archangels** also emit a splendor more subtle and pure than can be endured, yet it is not as intolerable as that of the gods. **Angels**, when they appear, make the mixture of the air tolerable, so that the priests are able to reach out to it. When **Demons** <note>original: "Dæmones"; here meaning intermediate spirits, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense</note> appear, the whole air is not affected at all, nor does the air surrounding them become more subtle; neither does a light run before them in which their form shines out, anticipating the air, nor does the air shine all around them. When **Heroes** appear, certain regions

of the earth are moved, and sounds ring out all around. Yet the whole air does not become more subtle, nor does it become so unsuitable for the priests that they cannot endure it. When **Souls** appear, the visible air is more akin to them, and it receives their description by being adapted to them. The dispositions of those who invoke them bring back an exceeding perfection and action when the **Gods** appear, along with divine love and an incredible joy. From **Archangels**, they receive an intellectual contemplation that is supremely pure, and an immutable power. From **Angels**, they receive a rational knowledge and virtue arranged in the highest order. From **Demons**, a desire for <term>generation</term> <note>original: "appetitum generationis"; the drive toward physical birth and material existence</note>. From **Heroes**, similar things, and additionally, a zeal for civil government. From **Principalities** or **Princes**, they experience in the soul worldly or material movements, and the fulfillment of the works of fate. From **Souls**, they receive the desire for generation and a zeal for caring for bodies. The appearance of the **Gods** [brings] truth, power, and actions...

<page-num>216</page-num>
# IAMBLICHUS

strengthens to the highest degree, and provides the most ample benefits. The gifts of **Archangels** are similar, but more specific; they provide knowledge and power not in a universal sense, but restricted to a particular thing, place, time, or manner. The gifts of **Angels** are even more defined and limited. Those of **Demons** <note>original: "Dæmonum"; here referring to intermediate spirits, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense</note> are not goods of the soul, but rather goods of the body and physical things, granted only when the worldly order permits. The **Heroes** grant gifts pertaining to the worldly or earthly "republic" <note>meaning the management of human society and terrestrial affairs</note>. The **Principalities** <note>cosmic governors</note> provide worldly benefits and all the comforts of life. The **Princes** provide lower things, that is, an abundance of more material goods. Finally, **Souls** provide those things which contribute to human life through a thoroughly familiar encounter.

Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre, a student of Plotinus and critic/interlocutor of Iamblichus</note> related an opinion which claims that it is common to gods, angels, demons, and indeed all superior beings, that when they appear, they show themselves off and boast wonderfully, producing an imaginary likeness of themselves to impress the viewer. Iamblichus refutes this entirely. A God, an angel, or a good demon, when they appear, teach a person what their essence truly is and what it is like, speaking nothing beyond or short of the truth. For just as light naturally and constantly follows the Sun, so truth follows God and his attendants; especially since they are perfect and full, they neither need to, nor are they able to, claim more for themselves than they actually are. When in **theurgy** <note>original: "theurgia"; the practice of divine rituals intended to unite the soul with the divine</note>, that is, the deifying...

<column-break/>

<page-num>217</page-num>
# ON THE MYSTERIES

...deifying and priestly faculty, men fail <note>original: "delinquunt"; meaning a mistake in the ritual procedure</note>, then the good divinities which are desired do not appear, but rather inferior and evil spirits encounter us under the pretext of being good. For evil spirits often transform themselves into the likeness of good ones; they come boastfully and claim for themselves more than they are. Truly, where a corrupted first beginning arises, a great deal of lying proceeds from that perversity. 

Priests must examine the apparitions of spirits by the rule of the entire cosmic order, and remember that boastful arrogance is in no way a characteristic of true and good spirits. They must take care never to wander from the sacred rite. And if, through an error in the apparition, some insolent beings should suddenly arrive, it is not right to judge the entire common nature and condition of the divinities based on the behavior of those intruders. For it is a sacrilege to rashly judge and change those things which have been proven in sacred matters over many centuries and with painstaking labor, based on a few things happening suddenly by chance. And even if false spirits lie, the good spirits are nonetheless truthful and legitimate concerning the fire <note>likely referring to the "theurgic fire" or divine light used in rituals</note>. For indeed, in every class of being, the principal members begin first from themselves, and provide for themselves what they exhibit to others; and this occurs in their essence, their life, and their motion.

<summary>Iamblichus categorizes the specific material and spiritual gifts granted by different ranks of beings and defends the inherent truthfulness of high spirits against Porphyry's skepticism, blaming "lying" spirits on ritual errors by the practitioner.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Porphyry, theurgy, spiritual hierarchy, archangels, demons, ritual error, divine essence, truthfulness</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's defense of the honesty of divine revelations. Having previously argued that higher beings provide specific gifts to the soul and body, he now refutes the idea that gods use deceptive "phantasms" or illusions to appear to humans, asserting instead that they reveal their true essence.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

Similarly, the higher beings, presenting truth to all, first speak true things about themselves, and they reveal their very essence especially to those contemplating them. Therefore, they duly demonstrate to the priests the fire by itself and in its own form to be observed. For it is not the nature of heat to make things cold, nor of light to darken and hide, nor of anything that acts through its essence to ever do the opposite. A **phantasm** <note>original: "phantasma"; here meaning an illusory appearance or phantom</note>, that is, an image sent by someone without its own nature, but only in the manner of a reflected <term>idol<gloss>from the Greek 'eidolon,' meaning a mere image, shadow, or double lacking substance</gloss></term>, vainly draws the mind of the observer; it deceives while it pretends to be something whose nature it does not possess. It is, therefore, a cause of falsehood, which in no way belongs to any true essence, and thus not to the gods. Instead, they and their followers reveal images of themselves that are not thin and empty, but natural and true—not indeed reflected (as I said) and vain ones. For why would they send such things? Not to demonstrate their essence and power, for those images fall short of that through their whole nature. Nor even for the utility of the observers; for what utility is there in a lie and a feigned deception? Finally, they do not send them because it is their nature to thus send an idol from themselves; for their essence is firm, consisting in itself, and so it is to the rest of the essence 
<meta>catchword: of truth</meta>

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

of truth, which as a cause in no way sends into another any fallacious and deceptive imitation of itself that would pretend to be something whose nature it does not possess. Therefore, neither the gods nor their followers transform their nature into any likenesses, nor do they produce these from themselves into another; rather, they illuminate and demonstrate the true forms of themselves in the true characters of the soul. <note>Note: similar to this is the idea that in the blessed homeland <note>referring to the celestial or divine realm</note> the mind sees the divine essence, not through an image created by it which is entirely deficient, but through that same essence; just as one who sees light through light in light, not like one who sees color through light, or a body through a mirror.</note> Separate substances <note>immaterial beings existing independently of physical matter</note> do not have any common genus among themselves. Likewise, gods, angels, and good demons do not appear in a phantastic <note>illusory or imaginary</note> manner, but in a way that is entirely their own and true; whereas evil spirits appear in a phantastic and deceptive way. Nor is it proper for us to judge the way the good spirits form the soul by their presence based on the condition in which the evil ones appear. Porphyry <note>the Neoplatonist philosopher and critic of Iamblichus</note> says that knowledge of divine things is indeed a sacred matter, but ignorance is profane. Iamblichus confirms these things: because since the gods act upon beings through intelligence, we rightly through...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that divine beings reveal their true essence to priests and practitioners rather than using deceptive illusions (phantasms). He distinguishes between the truthful self-revelation of gods and the deceptive appearances of evil spirits.</summary>

<keywords>theophany, phantasm, divine essence, Porphyry, Neoplatonism, sacred knowledge, deception, idols, light, separate substances</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s argument from the previous section, where he established that divine beings reveal their true essence rather than deceptive images. Here, he pivots to the "how" of divine union, distinguishing between intellectual study (philosophy) and ritual action (theurgy).</meta>

...the understanding of beings pleases the gods and is approved by them. But he places **religious operation** <note>original: "operationem religiosam"; this refers to theurgy, the practice of sacred rituals to communicate with the divine, as opposed to mere prayer or study</note> above understanding, as that which is alone effective for acquiring the soul’s **deific union** <note>original: "deificam unitatem"; a state where the soul becomes like or one with the divine</note> with God. So great is the power of sacred rites performed correctly for God that even if you are ignorant of them, yet observe them piously, they have no less efficacy. For it is not understanding that joins the sacrificers to God; otherwise, philosophers would achieve the deific union [instead of priests].

### On the power of the sacraments.

The proper observance of the precepts and divine works, which exceed all understanding, and the wondrous power of the symbols and **sacraments** <note>original: "sacramentorum"; used here in the broader sense of "sacred mysteries" or theurgic rites rather than the specific Christian sacraments</note>, grants us deific union with the gods alone through vows. When we work in sacred rites, we do not perform the sacraments through understanding; otherwise, their action would be intellectual and would originate from us. On the contrary, even when we do not understand them, these rites carry out their own work, and the power of the gods—to which these rites are referred—recognizes its own ineffable images within them, not being stirred into action by our intellects. For universal causes are not moved by particular effects; thus, neither are 

<column-break/>

divine things principally moved to act by our intellects. However, it is necessary for our intellects, the best of dispositions, and purity to precede these rites as certain **co-causes** <note>original: "concousas"; secondary factors that prepare the way for the primary cause to act</note>. But what principally provokes the divine will are the divine sacraments themselves; and thus divine things are incited by themselves, and do not receive the beginning of their action from any subordinate source. Do not think, therefore, that the whole authority of the effective reason in the sacraments lies within us, nor that their true work is completed by the truth of our understanding, or that they are rendered false by our deception. For even if we should know what properly follows each kind of rite, we do not suddenly achieve the truth of those things in practice. Indeed, without knowledge, an effective unity with the divine never arrives, yet they are not entirely the same thing. Therefore, divine purity is not achieved through right knowledge—just as the purity of the body is achieved through health—but it is a unity and a purified purity above knowledge; that is, it is achieved through a power superior to knowledge. Therefore, nothing else that is human in us contributes anything to the end of divine actions. This concludes the opinion of Iamblichus, the Egyptians, and the **Assyrians** <note>original: "Assyriorum"; a term often used by Iamblichus to refer to the Chaldeans and their oracles</note>. Just as in agriculture, medicine, and the art of **images**... <note>original: "arte ima[ginum]"; likely referring to the making of sacred statues or talismans</note>

<summary>Iamblichus argues that theurgy (ritual action) is superior to philosophy (intellectual understanding) for achieving union with the gods, as rituals possess an inherent power that operates independently of the practitioner's intellect. He asserts that while purity of mind is a necessary preparation, the "sacraments" themselves are what prompt divine action.</summary>

<keywords>theurgy, deific union, sacraments, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism, ritual efficacy, Egyptian wisdom, Chaldean oracles, divine will, purification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of theurgy (sacred ritual) as a divine gift rather than a human invention. He is contrasting the roles of the human practitioner and the divine power in the effectiveness of rituals and prophecy.</meta>

->I A M B L I C H V S<-

...of origins, in human intercourse, man appropriately fits the material, but a superior and universal cause applies the form. In the same way, in sacrifice, when symbols and **tokens** <note>original: "synthemata"; these are material objects or words believed to contain divine power or "signatures"</note> are used—that is, signs and **sacred mysteries** <note>original: "sacramenta"; used here in the Neoplatonic sense of ritual acts that connect the physical and divine realms</note>—the priest provides certain material things, arranged in a becoming order; but God impresses the effective power upon these mysteries.

### *What prophecy is and from where it comes.*

**Prophecy** <note>original: "vaticinium"; refers here to divine revelation or the art of foretelling the future through divine communication</note> itself is produced neither by human art, nor by nature, nor by natural or animal reasons or movements; nor is it made in any human way. Instead, it is eternal, sent down to us from the divine. The entire power of foreknowledge is referred back to the gods, and all its authority resides in them. It is handed down from there and is perfected through divine works or signs. It possesses divine spectacles and contemplations endowed with true knowledge. All other things are subjected like instruments to the gift of foreknowledge sent from heaven—whether they pertain to the soul or the body, or exist in the nature of the whole world, or in individual natures. Places and certain other factors are required for prophecy only in the manner of "matter" <note>meaning they are the physical vessels but not the cause of the prophecy</note>. It is not fitting to derive prophecy without cause, namely from those who have no foreknowledge in themselves; rather, it comes from the gods, who contain within themselves the boundaries of all knowledge

<column-break/>

->DE MYSTERIIS<-

of things. It is right to contemplate prophecy as being distributed throughout the whole world and throughout all the distinct natures in the world. Thus, the cause of prophecy is primary and most universal, possessing in itself whatever it exhibits to those participating in it. It especially provides the truth which prophecy requires, and holds within itself the essence and cause of those things that happen, from which the perpetual sequence of foreknowledge necessarily proceeds. **Porphyry** <note>Iamblichus’s teacher and intellectual rival, who was more skeptical of ritual</note> says: "When we sleep, we sometimes foresee the future without any awareness of our present state, even if we are not at all moved into a state of ecstasy." He wishes to argue, I believe, that this foreknowledge comes not so much from an external divine influence flowing in at that moment, as from the nature of the soul and mind itself. Iamblichus says: Dreams that arise from human causes—whether through our own nature, or through emotions, thoughts, and actions—reach the truth only very rarely and too obscurely, and more often reach it only by a kind of chance.

### *On dreams.*

Dreams are sometimes stirred within us by innate understandings or reasons, or by imaginations and daily cares, which occasionally attain the truth, but mostly deceive. These arise from human principles while people are sleeping...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that true prophecy is a gift from the gods, not a product of human skill or nature, and distinguishes divine revelation from common, unreliable dreams produced by the human mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of how divine prophecy differs from human imagination. He is moving from a general definition of prophecy to the specific mechanisms of divine dreams and the state of the soul during revelation.</meta>

->JAMBLICHVS<-

...happen to those who are awake. **Dreams sent by God**, however, do not happen like this, but arrive either between wakefulness and sleep, or between sleep and wakefulness, or even during wakefulness itself. In those moments, brief voices are heard, advising on what should be done. Sometimes, a certain spirit—not physical <note>original: "non corpulentus"; literally "not fleshy" or "not bulky," meaning a spiritual presence without a dense body</note> and not tangible—surrounds those who are resting. It cannot be seen by the eyes, but is perceived by another sense and awareness that feels through a **shared resonance** <note>original: "consenşu"; referring to the Neoplatonic idea of "sympathy," where the soul vibrates in harmony with higher powers</note>. This spirit enters with a certain force, surrounding the person on all sides without physical contact, and miraculously drives away the afflictions <note>original: "passiones"; the emotional or physical disturbances that weigh down the soul</note> of both soul and body. At other times, a pure and quiet light shines out, under which the physical sight is closed, yet the soul remains open. Meanwhile, the other senses remain fully awake and perceive how the gods reveal themselves in the light. They perceive what the gods say and do with a steady awareness. This is discerned even more perfectly when the sight also perceives it, and the strengthened intellect follows what is occurring, and the observers are similarly moved. Such vast differences as we have described are not of human origin. They occur when our sleep is not merely a heaviness of the head, or a drooping and closing of the eyes, or some middle state...

<column-break/>

225

->DE MYSTERIIS<-

...between waking and sleep, or a sudden awakening, or even wakefulness itself. All these states are divine, suitable for receiving the gods, and are sent from the heavens. Indeed, such occurrences precede the gods, acting as parts of the divine manifestation <note>original: "apparitionis"; the appearing or revelation of the deity</note>. Dreams that arise from human causes suffer from two defects: they happen to those who are completely asleep, and at that time we do not clearly or fully observe either the things that occur or our own state in that moment. However, when dreams are sent from God, we are not truly sleeping; we follow and observe things more clearly than we usually do when we are awake. For this reason, **prophecy** <note>original: "vaticinium"</note> is primarily found in dreams of this kind. The soul possesses a **double life**: one lived alongside the body, and another that can be separated from all bodily form. When we are awake, we mostly employ the life held in common with the body, unless we are occasionally separated from it because we are engaged in pure understanding or thinking with abstract reason. But in sleep, we can be entirely set free, as if the chains bound to the soul were removed, and we can then employ that life which is separate from the world of **becoming** <note>original: "generatione"; referring to the material world of birth, death, and change, as opposed to the eternal world</note>. When the soul is released from the body during sleep in this way, then that form of life, acting according to its own nature...

<summary>Iamblichus distinguishes between ordinary human dreams and divinely sent dreams, describing the latter as a state of heightened awareness where the soul, freed from the body's "chains," can perceive the gods and receive prophecy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of how the human soul, when detached from the body’s influence during sleep or trance, can access divine knowledge and universal truths. It moves from the mechanics of the soul's "inner principles" to historical examples of divine intervention through dreams.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>
->JAMBLICHVS<-

...remaining separable and separate, whether it is intellectual or even divine: it immediately awakens within us and acts according to its own nature. Since the intellect also contemplates, and the soul encompasses the **principles** <term>rationes; the underlying logic or "blueprints" of things</term> of created things within itself, it rightly foreknows the future, which is ordered according to these preceding principles within itself. We have spoken of the forewarnings of the soul as it returns to itself in a state similar to sleep. I say "into itself," meaning into its **seminal and intellectual principles** <term>rationes seminales; the "seed-principles" that Neoplatonists believed directed the growth and destiny of all things</term>. However, up to this point, it remains under a form specific to the present life—that is, the form of this particular action and life. But if the soul soon unfolds the universal form of principles and thoughts according to which it once lived, it will then bring back forewarnings pertaining more to the universal condition of worldly affairs, within which it will be able to perceive individual details here and there. Furthermore, if through such a universal form of its own, it is joined to the very minds of the gods, it will see what belongs to the higher world and what belongs to this one. Likewise, through those gods as the primary causes, the soul will understand more perfectly; in this state, not only will its thought be perfected, but its imagination will also possess
<meta>catchword: imagina-</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>227</page-num>
->DE MYSTERIIS<-
<note>On the Mysteries</note>

pure images <note>original: "imaginamenta syncera"; mental pictures unclouded by physical sensation</note> of both bodily and divine things. Since the principles of created things exist in the soul according to its power—both its power to act and its power to know—and since these principles depend on the principles that exist in the gods, the soul, when joined to the gods, rouses these principles within itself into an act of vast knowledge. This happens under the mode of divine providence, through which the soul considers the past, the future, and all temporal things, as well as their actions and experiences, the remedies for those experiences, and the cures for diseases and vices of the soul, and the established laws and arts necessary for the human race. In this way, dreams are received in the temple of **Aesculapius** <note>The Greco-Roman god of medicine (Asklepios); patients would sleep in his temples hoping for a "healing dream"</note> by which diseases are cured, and the very art of medicine is compared to divine dreams. The army of **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great</note> escaped the danger of a plague that was about to destroy them because of remedies revealed in a dream by the god **Dionysus**. King **Lysander** <note>A famous Spartan general</note> had besieged the city of Aphytis, but he lifted the siege when ordered to do so in a dream by **Ammon** <note>An Egyptian and Libyan deity often identified with Zeus/Jupiter</note>. Many similar things happen daily that are beyond human speech and reason.

### *Concerning the divine inspiration that occurs during wakefulness.*

**Porphyry** <note>The student of Plotinus who originally asked the questions Iamblichus is answering in this book</note> touched upon two types of divine inspiration <note>original: "afflationes"; literally a "breathing upon" or "influence"</note>: one that is commonly said to happen in sleep, and another that happens while awake, where those who are inspired...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 227 regarding how the soul, when divinely inspired, transcends its ordinary human functions. It moves from the internal state of the soul to the external physical manifestations of divine possession, specifically focusing on the suspension of human reason and physical sensation.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHVS</header>

...stirred, they foretell the future, so awake that they even use their senses, yet they are not meanwhile in possession of human reason. Iamblichus says that truly foretelling and inspired souls do not sleep, even when they seem to be sleeping; and when they wake, they do not wake in the proper sense, nor do they use their senses in the manner of men. Souls divinely inspired and stirred have submitted their lives entirely to the god who inspires them. Therefore, whether they have submitted themselves as a vehicle or an instrument <note>Latin: *vehiculum... instrumentum*; Neoplatonic terms describing how the human becomes a vessel or tool for divine action</note>, they have laid aside their former mode of life and have either exchanged their own life for a divine life, or they direct their own life entirely toward the god. For this reason, they neither use their senses nor are they awake in the same way as those who have alert senses; nor do they foretell or move by some human impulse and custom. They do not notice their own state, nor do they produce any knowledge or action of their own, but everything there is conducted under a divine form and action.

<margin>Many miracles are performed by the prophets.</margin>

Moreover, it is clear that those who are divinely inspired do not live by the **animal life** <term>animali vita; the biological life force shared with animals, distinct from the higher rational or divine life</term> itself at that time, because many of them are not burned when fire is applied—the god breathing within clearly repelling the fire. Or if they are burned, they do not feel it, nor do they perceive stabbings, scrapings, or any other torments. Likewise, because

<meta>catchword: because</meta>

---

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES.</header>

because their actions are not human, it is certain, since they walk through pathless places, and are carried through fire untouched, and cross rivers miraculously—which the priestess herself does in Cataballa <note>The city of Castabala in Cilicia, where the priestesses of Artemis Perasia were famous for walking on hot coals without harm</note>. Through these signs it is clear that they live neither a human nor an animal life using senses and impulse, but a divine one, as if their soul were resting, and the god were there in place of the soul, moving them as a more excellent soul. There are various kinds of those divinely inspired, both because of the variety of the gods—for different gods inspire in different ways—and because of the difference in the modes of inspiration. These modes are as follows: for either the god himself occupies us, or we devote ourselves to him and are made entirely his, or we perform a common action with the god. Likewise, we either attain mastery of the lowest divine powers, or the middle, or the supreme. Furthermore, sometimes the presence of the god is **bare** <term>nuda; simple, unadorned, or unmediated</term>; sometimes a communion occurs beyond presence; and occasionally a union is achieved beyond this. Again, either the soul alone becomes possessed of the inspiration, or the body also thereafter becomes a participant in some way, or the **living creature** <term>commune animal; the composite entity formed by the union of soul and body</term> as a whole is possessed equally. According to these different varieties, there are various signs, effects, and works of the inspired. According to the aforementioned [variety] of the inspirers, or the inspiration...

...diversity, some who are inspired are moved either in their whole body or in certain limbs, or conversely, they remain still. Likewise, they perform dances and harmonious songs, or the opposite. Again, their body seems to grow in height or in breadth, or to be carried through the heights <note>original: *per sublimia ferri*; referring to levitation</note>, and the opposite. Also, they give forth voices, either steady and continuous, or uneven and interrupted by silence; and now they lower their tones, now they raise them.

->*The inspired person is free from their own action and has the god in place of a soul.*<-

Truly, the greatest sign of divine inspiration <term>afflatio; literally a "breathing upon" or "blowing into" by a deity</term> is that he who draws down and introduces the deity beholds the spirit descending, both how great it is and of what kind, and by it he is mystically taught and guided. Moreover, he who receives the deity sees a certain appearance of fire before the reception. Sometimes this is even seen by those present, either when the god is arriving or departing. From this sign, those skilled in these matters discern most truly what the power of the deity is, what its order is, and concerning what things it speaks the truth, and what things it may perform. But those who, without such spectacles, secretly introduce spirits, are like those who grope in the dark; they do not understand what they are doing, except for certain small signs—

<meta>catchword: signs in</meta>

---

<page-num>231</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

—in the body of the inspired person himself. Therefore, since the excellence of divine fire and a certain ineffable appearance of light broods upon the one who is occupied from without, and fills him entirely, and dominates him within, and embraces him on all sides within itself—so much so that he cannot perform any action of his own—what sense of perception or personal awareness can be present in the soul receiving the divine fire? What human motion, passion, or distortion of the imagination and alienation can there be, such as some suspect? Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Plotinus who often questioned the nature of theurgy</note> defines divine inspiration thus: "A motion of the mind together with demonic inspiration." Iamblichus says that in a soul divinely inspired, neither the human mind nor human thought is moved, if it is truly contained by the god; nor is it an inspiration of demons, but of the gods. Again, it should not be defined as a "departure" or "alienation" <note>original: *alienatio*; the state of being "other" or out of one's mind</note>, for these terms signify a fall into a worse state; rather, it should be called a "leading back" and a "restoration to a better state." Divine inspiration, therefore, is nothing other than the souls being entirely occupied and contained by the god. From this, "ecstasy" <term>extasis; literally "standing outside" oneself</term> follows later—that is, a certain departure or alienation—as a secondary effect following the inspiration. Plato, in the *Phaedrus*, says that the ancients called prophecy "mania," that is, "fury" or "madness," because to the divine alone...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine possession and inspiration, specifically examining the role of the mind versus the divine. It explores the etymology of prophecy and critiques various theories regarding whether the source of inspiration is the soul, the body, or a combination of both, ultimately presenting Iamblichus's view that it is purely a gift of the gods.</meta>

<page-num>232</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

...seized by frenzy, they truly prophesy. Later people, however, by adding the letter "t," called it *mantia* <note>original: "mantiam"; a reference to Plato's *Phaedrus* where he links *mania* (madness/frenzy) with *manteia* (prophecy)</note>. Likewise, priests, sibyls, and prophets, as long as they are of sound mind <term>prudentes; meaning rational and in control of their faculties</term>, predict very little and obscurely, or nothing at all; but when they are snatched away by God, they truly prophesy. Hence, among many nations, they have foreseen dangers threatening by divine will, warned the people, and through sacrifices have appeased the divine Nemesis <note>The goddess of divine retribution and justice</note>. He also says that a prediction made by skill is called a "conjectural estimation" <note>original: "putationem coniecturalem"</note>, and it is of no importance if it is compared with divine inspiration and prophecy.

Iamblichus says that divine inspiration and prophecy are not in the mind or soul, either as a quality in a subject or as an action in an agent, but that the whole is an act of God himself, depending simply on God. I say it is an "act" performed by God in the mind or soul simply, as if in instruments that are free from any movement of their own, and in this way true foreknowledge is given. But if any movement from the body or soul, or any such quality, should be introduced in the meantime or should precede it, the visions are confused; neither will they be true prophecies, nor is there a legitimate divine inspiration there, where anything human is mixed in.

There were generally three opinions concerning divine inspiration and prophecy. The first referred the cause 
<meta>catchword: of it</meta>

---

<page-num>233</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

of it to the soul, the second to the body, and the third to the composite <note>The "composite" refers to the union of body and soul together</note>. The first places it in that state in which the whole soul is resolved into the divine spirit, or into the mind, or when its action has become more vehement or fuller; or when thought has become sharp and very swift in motion, or when the mind has grown hot. The second places its cause in the body, when it has a certain complexion <term>complexionem; the balance of "humors" or bodily fluids thought to determine temperament</term>—namely, either melancholic <note>In ancient and Renaissance thought, a "melancholic" temperament was often associated with genius and prophetic insight</note> or tempered—or a certain elemental quality, or perhaps a special one, or a certain spirit. The third says its cause is a certain proportion by which the body and soul are joined to one another.

Iamblichus says that it is not the work of the soul, or the body, or the composite, nor does it have its cause in them. For that prophetic inspiration acts both in predicting and in performing beyond all custom of human nature. However, a man, or anything belonging to a man, cannot progress beyond his own limits toward divine limits; nor can more excellent things be generated by inferior ones. Therefore, the cause of this is only divinity itself—namely, when lights descend into us from the gods, and spirits are handed down from them, and the lordship and power from that place are present with us.

<sig>P 5</sig>

<summary>This section discusses the nature of prophecy, distinguishing between human conjecture and true divine inspiration, which Iamblichus argues must be entirely external to the human mind and body. It reviews three common theories of inspiration (soul-based, body-based, or a combination) before rejecting them in favor of a purely divine origin.</summary>
<keywords>prophecy, Iamblichus, divine inspiration, mantia, Nemesis, soul and body, melancholic temperament, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s argument that divine inspiration is a total possession by the deity. The previous page concluded by stating that if any human movement or thought interferes, the prophecy becomes false. Here, he explains that the divine presence must completely displace the human faculties to be authentic.</meta>

<page-num>234</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

...absolute, encompassing all things that are within us, and utterly banishing our own awareness and motion. It produces speech through the human being, certainly, but not by the human's own thought; rather, the deity pronounces these things through the mouth of one in a frenzy <note>original: "furentis"; referring to the state of *divine madness* described by Plato</note> who has been placed outside of himself. This occurs while everything within us clearly serves the deity entirely and yields to the sole, dominating divine action.

Porphyry <note>A Phoenician-born philosopher and student of Plotinus who often questioned the mechanics of theurgy</note> says that music induces passions in the soul and likewise calms them; he also says that different sounds suit different souls and characters. Likewise, he claims that music can change the complexions and affections of the body, stirring up fury or restraining it. Iamblichus concedes these points, but he denies that these are the *causes* of divine inspiration. This is because such effects are merely human—partly natural and partly artificial—and they possess nothing divine in themselves. 

<margin>⦿</margin>
Porphyry seems to suspect this [human cause], noting that some are inspired when they hear flutes, cymbals, or drums, while others are moved by different melodies. In truth, various species of motions in the world correspond to the various ranks of the gods, with specific motions linked to specific deities. From these, various melodies flow forth which, through their own movements, are similarly congruent with the specific divine principles of motion. Since these principles are everywhere,

<column-break/>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

and since they impart their own qualities chiefly to their own followers, they are especially present in those melodies that are most congruent with them. Working their way into our spirits <term>spiritibus; in Neoplatonism, the "spirit" is the subtle, airy vehicle that connects the soul to the physical body</term> when they have been affected by these melodies, the gods occupy the human being and soon fill him entirely with their own essence and power. 

The cause of this inspiration is not so much the human passion excited by the music, but rather the congruence of the music itself to the god, to which the god is naturally present. Things that come to us through sight are too much like "images" <note>original: "imaginaria"; meaning they are tied to the faculty of imagination and visual phantasms</note> and refer more to things understood by the mind. Things perceived by the senses lower than hearing are too material. However, things perceived by hearing are "middle" things and suit the soul. Once they have worked their way into the spirit through motion, emotion, and meaning, they affect it vehemently—the soul on one hand, and the body on the other. 

Hence, the whole human being, according to the properties of the melodies, becomes a "receptacle" <note>original: "susceptaculum"</note> for this deity or that. Those who are inspired behave differently in their movement, their stillness, and their other states, depending on the diversity of the deities by whom they are primarily inspired, rather than by the difference in the music itself. For the effect of the sound has already ceased here once the god acts. The soul once heard divine harmony in the "intelligible world" <note>The realm of pure intellect and eternal truths, existing above the physical world</note>, which it remembers here when it hears melodies that possess a vestige of that divine harmony. By remembering it, the soul is then vehemently moved...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that divine inspiration is not caused by the emotional effects of music, but by music's mathematical alignment with divine orders, which allows a god to displace human consciousness. He argues that hearing is the primary sense for this connection because it occupies a middle ground between the material and the intellectual.</summary>
<keywords>divine possession, Porphyry, music, spiritus, harmony, intelligible world, theurgy, hearing vs sight</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s refutation of Porphyry’s materialist explanations for divine inspiration. The previous page established that while music might prepare the soul, it is not the actual cause of a prophetic state. Here, Iamblichus argues that inspiration comes from the soul’s connection to its celestial "homeland" and the direct presence of a deity, rather than from physical or psychological factors.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

among the number of souls which especially contemplated the idea of harmony itself in their homeland <note>original: "patria"; in Neoplatonism, this refers to the celestial realm or the "intellectual world" from which souls descend</note>. By such an affection, having become familiar with the god, the soul is now breathed upon by a certain singular presence of the god, from which she performs wonders. Therefore, the cause of this inspiration <note>original: "afflationis"; literally a "breathing-upon" or divine breath</note> and miracle is not a passion brought in from sounds, nor the nature of the soul being composed of harmony, but a likeness to the god and the presence of the god. 

It should also be said much less that inspiration consists in this: that through music and the inspiration itself, certain superfluities hardened in the soul and body are purged. The power of prophecy <note>original: "vaticinii vis"</note> does not arise from a certain physical temperament <note>original: "complexione"; the balance of bodily humors</note>; for the gifts of the gods are not breathed in various ways according to bodily variety. For the divine gift is impassible <note>meaning it cannot be affected or changed by human or material conditions</note>, and it is given above the power of nature through the god's own action. 

Neither water, nor air, nor any other physical thing provides prophecy; but rather the sacred rites performed around these things—indeed, the purification of the mind along with the purification of the vehicle <note>original: "vehiculi"; referring to the "astral body" or spirit-shell that conveys the soul into the physical body</note>, and the focusing of intent upon the god—receives the divine power, which is everywhere and always ready. Since the inspired mind both speaks and acts above and contrary to the custom of nature, the cause of such inspiration is not natural; it does not arise from our body, nor the quality of a place, nor the celestial arrangement <note>referring to astrology or the position of the stars</note> at a person's birth or during their work:

---

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

but these things happen from the presence of the god. Hence, the various types of inspired people do not follow the natural variations within us, but rather the different species of the inspiring deities themselves, as well as the different modes by which we participate in the divine presence. 

Therefore, the fact that some divinely inspired men remain still, while others are stirred up, and some seek out mountains and deserts, and others behave in different ways: this does not proceed from the differences of natural causes within us, or of places, or of the music which they have just heard or are hearing. Instead, it proceeds from the various properties of the gods by whom the inspired are governed, and from the various modes of inspiration. 

Porphyry <note>The philosopher whose questions Iamblichus is answering in this book</note> brings forward three examples out of many oracles: one at Colophon <note>An ancient Greek city in Ionia, home to the oracle of Apollo at Claros</note>, where prophecy came by drinking water; another at Delphi, where the priestess prophesied while sitting at the mouth of a cave; and a third at Branchidae <note>Also known as Didyma; an oracular shrine near Miletus run by the Branchidae clan</note>, where they prophesied by inhaling vapor from the water. Each of these acts out its effects and works according to the mode of its own nature; therefore, the spirits themselves which, hanging from the gods, arouse and stir up men, exceed all natural and human motion in this very work. And this very action of theirs is by no means similar to the accustomed actions known to man. It was at Colophon...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that divine inspiration is a supernatural gift that transcends human biology, geography, or musical influence. He maintains that different prophetic styles (such as those at Delphi or Colophon) result from the specific nature of the deity involved rather than the physical environment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of how divine inspiration occurs at specific oracular sites. He is moving from general principles to specific historical examples, such as the Oracle of Apollo at Colophon and the Pythia at Delphi, to show that physical elements like water or vapors are merely tools for purification, not the source of the god themselves.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS 
### ON THE MYSTERIES

->**Chapter 11**<-

...there was an underground place where there was a spring. On designated nights, the priest would perform sacrifices; afterwards, he would taste the water, and finally, he would prophesy there, having become invisible to those present. It might seem to someone that a divine spirit passed through the water into the priest, yet it is not so. For the divine itself does not travel through participating things according to distance or division, but rather encompasses them from without, illuminates them from within, and fills the water with a certain power that contributes to prophecy—specifically, a <term>purgative</term> <note>purifying or cleansing</note> power. Thus, when the priest drinks, the water purifies the <term>luminous spirit</term> <note>original: "luminosum spiritum"; in Neoplatonic thought, this is a "vehicle" or "astral body" that allows the human soul to interact with divine light</note> implanted within us and prepares it for God. Through this purification and preparation, we are made capable of receiving God. 

However, there exists another presence of God besides the power infused into the water—one that is prior, superior, and illuminating from both around and within. This presence is absent from no one, provided they can reach it through <term>congruity</term> <note>original: "congruitatem"; a state of being "in tune" or naturally fitted to the divine frequency</note>. It arrives suddenly and uses the prophet as an instrument. In the meantime, the prophet is no longer his own master; he does not notice what he says and is ignorant of where he is. Consequently, after the response, he hardly recovers himself. Indeed, even before drinking the water, he abstains from food for a day and a night and withdraws into certain sacred rites inaccessible to the common people. Therefore, because of this sequestration

<column-break/>

from human affairs, he renders himself pure and fit for receiving God. From these things, it is concluded that a person is prepared for God here in two ways: first, through the purgative water made so by God, which purifies the spirit; second, through sobriety, solitude, the separation of the mind from the body, and a focused intention toward God. 

The **Sibyl** <note>referring specifically to the Pythia, the priestess at the Oracle of Delphi</note> in Delphi received God in two ways: either through a certain thin and fiery spirit <note>original: "spiritum... igneum"; often interpreted by ancient writers as the vapors rising from the earth</note> that erupted somewhere from the mouth of a certain cave, or while sitting in the <term>adyton</term> <note>the innermost, restricted "holy of holies" area of a temple</note> upon a bronze seat having three or four feet and dedicated to the god. In both cases, she exposed herself to the divine spirit, whereby the light of the divine fire was illuminated. Thus, sometimes a great deal of fire flying out of the cave all at once surrounds her on all sides and fills her with divine splendor; at other times, while she remains on the seat sacred to the god—through which she is prepared for him—she is joined to the stable prophecy of the god. In both ways, the Sibyl becomes entirely possessed by God. Suddenly, the god is present, yet remains separate, being something other than the fire, the spirit, the specific seat, and all the apparatus of that place, whether artificial or natural. 

Furthermore, the prophetic woman among the **Branchidae** <note>the family of priests who oversaw the Oracle of Apollo at Didyma</note> either sits upon an axle or holds in her hand a staff given by some god...

<summary>Iamblichus describes the rituals of the oracles at Colophon and Delphi, arguing that physical elements like spring water and vapors are merely preparatory tools that purify the priest's "luminous spirit" to receive a separate, superior divine presence.</summary>

# IAMBLICHUS, ON THE MYSTERIES.

...or dips her feet or the hem <note>original: "limbum"; the border or edge of a garment</note> of her robe into the water, or breathes in a vapor from the water, and in these ways she is filled with divine splendor, and having obtained the god, she prophesies. For by all these things she is made <term>adapted</term> <note>original: "accommodata"; becoming a suitable vessel</note> to the god, whom she receives from without. However, that the prophecy occurs not from any power of the body or the soul's own vital force, nor from the power of places or instruments, but from the presence of the god coming from outside, is evident from this: the priestess herself, before giving oracles, performs many sacrifices according to the rite, observes holiness, bathes, and abstains from food for three full days. She lives in seclusion, and already begins to be gradually illuminated and to rejoice wonderfully. Through these signs, it is clear that the god is summoned from the outside to arrive, and does arrive from without; and that even before the priestess comes to the place of the water, she is in some way inspired, and is also inspired in that very place while a certain spirit rises up from the water of the spring. It is certain that the god is something else besides these physical things—something separated from all of them—and is the cause of such a place, of the spring, and of the whole prophecy. The power that inspires prophecy in us is <term>absolute</term> <note>original: "absoluta"; meaning "set free" or "unconditioned" by physical constraints</note> from places and times; otherwise, it could not express all places and times simultaneously. For if it were inseparably attached to the nature of places or matter, or if it proceeded according to a movement determined
<meta>catchword: by [tum]</meta>

<column-break/>

by number, it would not be able to foreknow those things which are everywhere and always, nor be equally present everywhere and always. But if it is freed from these things, as being superior to things that happen in time or are contained in a place, it is equally present to all things wherever they may be; and it is present simultaneously from all sides to those things that happen in time, and it comprehends the truth of all things at once in a single point, because of its <term>essence</term>, which is entirely separate and surpasses all things. 

This divine power, the origin of prophecy, is whole everywhere and whole always. This power is not derived from the body, nor from the soul bound to the body, but from the "absolute" ones—that is, the <term>angels</term> <note>In the context of Neoplatonism, "angels" (angelos) were viewed as messengers or intermediary spirits between the highest gods and the human realm</note> and gods, who are everywhere. Or at least the power itself is present wherever any of the angels are, and they are everywhere; and that power, being separated from all bodily things, is present to all, not contained by place or body or by a soul that is bound to a specific limited form. Rather, this very prophetic power of the gods is both separate from all things, and therefore is present as a whole everywhere at once to those who are suddenly able to participate in it. It illuminates from without and fills all things, running through all the elements and leaving no animal or part of nature behind into which it does not imprint something of itself (according to that creature's capacity), contributing to the gift of <term>presage</term> <note>original: "praesagium"; a foreboding or prophetic sense</note>. Indeed, it can fill all things equally because...

<sig>Q</sig>

<summary>Iamblichus argues that prophetic inspiration comes from a transcendent divine source that is "absolute" (independent) of time and space, rather than from the physical properties of oracular sites. He explains that while rituals like fasting and bathing prepare the priestess, the god remains a separate, omnipresent entity that illuminates the soul from the outside.</summary>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s argument that divine prophecy is entirely independent of material conditions. He transitions from discussing the physical environment of the priestess to addressing Porphyry's concerns about the use of ritual symbols or "characters" and the role of the imagination in receiving divine visions.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

...neously. It is likewise absolutely unconditioned by all things. Porphyry, on account of prophecies celebrated publicly, mentions some privately—those, namely, who profess that they will attract spirits to prophecy through certain <term>characters</term> <note>original: "characteres"; symbolic marks, sigils, or "tokens" believed to hold theurgic power to draw down divine presence</note>. Iamblichus rejects this: Because since they neglect all religion and holiness, trusting only in characters, they are unable to attain the very presence of any god or good divinity, through which true prophecies are usually given. But in this they are deceived: for while through characters that are indeed suited to the gods and through the intention of the mind, they may bring back some imaginary, small, and obscure appearance from the higher beings. And since they meanwhile neglect holiness, they come under the power of evil demons who lie and deceive; and however much of signs and appearances happen to come from the gods, they pervert and confuse them. But those who obtain the true presence of the gods bring back a true and unchangeable foreknowledge, which cannot be disrupted by evil spirits. Just as when the Sun arrives, the darkness cannot endure its presence but suddenly flees, in no way hindering the sun; so when the power of the gods shines forth from every side, filling all things with good, every disturbance that is accustomed

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES.

is accustomed to happen from evil spirits has no place, but is suddenly destroyed. For when the good divinities are fully present, the evil spirits vanish; they are neither able to move themselves nor to obscure the divine splendor. He condemns those who neglect all religious contemplation and holiness, trusting in characters alone, by which they hope they will enclose the very substance of some god, in whose presence they might prophesy. Porphyry also disapproved of the confidence of these people. Porphyry also recounts that, besides these, there are others in whom the <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "phantasia"; in Neoplatonic thought, this is the "mirror" of the soul where divine light is reflected as images</note> is sometimes stirred when they have applied certain things; even if they are otherwise of sound mind, they are raving in their imagination during the sacred rites. Namely, when they have either hidden in the darkness, or drunk certain potions, or used chants or certain <term>constructions</term> <note>original: "constructionibus"; likely referring to the ritual arrangement of objects or talismans</note>. Then, while raving, they imagine hidden and future things—some seeing them in water, others on a wall, others in the open air (that is, in the clear sky), others in the Sun, and others in other stars. This divine light comes specifically into the imagination; and if we see something wonderful from the outside, it happens through the sight following the imagination. The gods, by their presence alone and without any medium, move the soul whenever they wish—that is, a soul that is prepared—or [they move it by] light...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that ritual symbols (characters) are useless without holiness and often attract deceptive demons rather than gods. He explains that true divine presence instantly dispels evil spirits, just as sunlight dispels darkness, and discusses how the imagination acts as a vessel for divine light.</summary>

<keywords>Theurgy, Sigils, Porphyry, Divine Light, Demons, Imagination, Prophetic Vision, Ritual Purity</keywords>

# IAMBLICHUS

...they pour something from themselves into the <term>ethereal vehicle</term> <note>original: "aethereum animae vehiculum"; in Neoplatonism, this is a "subtle body" or spirit-body that serves as the intermediary between the immaterial soul and the physical body</note> of the soul, by which imaginations are moved in the <term>fantasy</term> <note>original: "phantasia"; the mental faculty that receives and stores sensory images</note> according to the gods' own will; and in both cases, the divinity is never enclosed within the soul. When the aforementioned conditions suitable for prophecy are applied, the attention and thought of the mind notices what is happening, even when the divine light does not properly reach the <term>rational powers</term> <note>original: "rationales potentias"; the logical, discursive parts of the human mind</note>; but meanwhile, the imagination rages in an alienated state, because it is incited from above beyond human custom.

Sometimes they keep the divining person in darkness, for nothing in the shadows resists the coming light. At other times, they keep him in the light when they are preparing him, so that there may be a greater likeness to the divine light; and then that light is received in the light of the Sun, or the Moon, or during a clear night, or in water prepared for its transparency, or upon a wall prepared with sacred <term>characters</term> <note>original: "characteribus"; symbolic marks or sigils believed to have theurgic power to anchor divine energy</note>. In the latter case, because of the wall's solidity, that which is received from the divine is retained for a longer time. 

Furthermore, they bring forward certain things related to the gods being introduced, and they offer to the one about to divine certain potions, compositions, and songs or incantations suitable for preparing the <term>receptacle</term> <note>original: "susceptaculum"; the person or object that "receives" the divine presence</note> and accommodating the presence and appearance of the gods—specifically, so that their hidden light might appear 

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES.

somewhere to the senses. Yet this always happens according to the will of the gods, whether the light is drawn down from the ether, the air, the Sun, the Moon, or some other celestial sphere, as far as it pleases the gods who give freely as the primary authors. They move the imagination, and through it sometimes the senses using appropriate objects, and sometimes they move the imagination through the senses. 

Magical images made of solid matter preserve their power longer. The beings above give gifts to those who are prepared, not merely through natural processes, but through the intellect and the free will of the gods themselves. The gods provide portents of the future in <term>entrails</term> <note>original: "extis"; the internal organs of sacrificed animals, studied by "haruspices" for omens</note> and birds, and in new wonders among the stars, from which human sagacity conjectures events based on the proportion of the signs' arrival: just as from the fall of a bird one predicts the fall of a man; from a defect of limbs in the entrails, a failure; or from a falling star, the downfall of leaders. 

The gods imprint signs of future things upon the world through the ministry of universal nature and particular natures, or through the obedience of <term>demons</term> <note>original: "daemonum"; in this context, these are intermediary spirits or "daemons" who carry out the divine will, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense</note> serving the gods and ruling over the elements, animals, and all similar things that act easily according to the will of the gods. And if they declare the will of God and future events symbolically through signs—as <term>Heraclitus</term> <note>An ancient Greek philosopher known for saying that the Lord whose oracle is at Delphi "neither speaks nor hides his meaning, but gives a sign"</note> says—they meanwhile represent the manner of the divine workmanship. For just as through firm images...

<summary>This passage explains how the gods project divine light into the human imagination and use physical mediums—like water, walls with sigils, or animal entrails—to communicate the future. Iamblichus emphasizes that prophecy is a gift of the gods' free will rather than a purely natural or human skill.</summary>
