--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's explanation of how gods communicate with humans through external signs. It follows the discussion of how divine light affects the human imagination, moving now to physical phenomena such as animal entrails, the flight of birds, and atmospheric events.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

...[the gods] bring things about and signify future events, thus through new <term>portents</term> <note>original: "ostenta"; extraordinary or supernatural events viewed as omens</note> and signs of every kind; and meanwhile, for the same cause and occasion, they sharpen our own perception. In the case of portents, the entrails in animals are changed against nature by their own soul, and by the <term>daimon</term> <note>original: "dæmone"; in this context, a mediating spirit or guardian, not an evil entity</note> presiding over them, and by the motion of the air and the motion of the heavens—occurring contrary to nature and according to the will of the gods for the purpose of warning mankind. That this is done divinely is clear, because often certain principal organs necessary for life are not found within the animals, having been suddenly removed by miracle, though it is necessary that they were not missing shortly before. In the taking of <term>auguries</term> <note>original: "augurijs"; the practice of interpreting the will of the gods through the flight or behavior of birds</note>, birds are miraculously moved by their own soul and by a presiding daimon, and by the motion of the air and the heavens, from which a power descends through all the air, shaping and moving it according to the will of the gods for the purpose of producing signs. I speak of a will signified and decreed from the beginning. Thus, birds often throw themselves down, or strike themselves, or otherwise kill themselves while they contribute to the act of signifying; this they do not of their own accord, but drawn by divine power. Certain flames resembling stars, created divinely, are sometimes easily produced in the air at the command of the celestial gods as signs of future things, by igniting the air there

# DE MYSTERIIS
<margin>Q 4</margin>

at the highest point, which is most easily led and shaped, especially by the gods, into fire. Meanwhile, certain influences of the celestial bodies descend into this air, leading to the creation and movement of stars of this kind, similar to the celestial ones in light, power, and motion. The <term>cosmic animal</term> <note>original: "Mundanum animal"; the Neoplatonic concept of the universe as a single, living, breathing organism where all parts are interconnected</note> is so united with itself that its members suffer together in turn, and those things which seem remote from each other are moved as if they were close together, as if they were members of the selfsame animal. And this union and <term>sympathy</term> <note>original: "compassio"; the natural resonance or "feeling-together" of different parts of the universe</note> allows the gods to show signs to men, first through the heavens, and subsequently through the air. The gods do not make signs for men immediately, but through intermediate instruments—namely daimons, souls, natures, and all things depending therefrom—starting from a single principle and thereafter diffusing a descending motion from themselves to wherever they wish. Therefore, they rule these individual things without being bound by or coordinated with the things that are generated. And just as in their craftsmanship and providence, so in the showing of signs, their intellect is not turned toward our level; rather, while that intellect remains within itself, it converts all signs and prophecy to itself, and notices that they proceed from itself. All prophets confess that prophecy occurs through the gods or daimons.

<summary>Iamblichus explains that divination through animal entrails, birds, and atmospheric phenomena (like meteors) works because the universe is a single "cosmic animal" tied together by divine sympathy. He asserts that gods use daimons and natural forces as instruments to send signs without losing their own higher status.</summary>
<keywords>divination, augury, extispicy, portents, daimon, cosmic animal, sympathy, celestial influence, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's defense of divine divination. He is responding to Porphyry's skeptical questions regarding why the high gods would involve themselves in mundane methods of fortune-telling, such as using flour or pebbles.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

...[to] obtain, because they cannot see future things unless they are the causes and masters of the future, through whom others may also see. Porphyry wonders why the gods would so greatly serve humans that some <term>prophets</term> <note>original: "uates"; those who speak for the gods</note> even practice <term>divination through flour</term> <note>original: "per farinam"; specifically referring to aleuromancy, an ancient practice of interpreting patterns in flour</note>. Iamblichus responds that the gods do not perform this out of any obedience to us, but out of an abundance of power and goodness. Since the cause of all things is contained within them, they remain within themselves without having to "turn" toward the lower subjects they govern. Instead, they bestow gifts and signs according to the "level of preparation" of the recipient—just as the sun provides light to transparent objects and to the eyes, and through this light, provides color. Because we see that humans must focus their attention and be "distracted" by various tasks to care for their subjects, laboring in the process, we mistakenly suspect that the gods provide for us in the same way. On the contrary, insofar as the gods are turned toward themselves and their own single <term>form</term> <note>the "form" here refers to a divine, unified essence that serves as the blueprint for lower reality</note>—which is the cause of all lower things—they act, move, and rule everything. They are not pulled apart by different tasks, but from a single principle and a simple "nod," they set all things in motion, which then hang from them in a sequence. They do not bend down to their subjects; rather, they remain in their own state and most fully make all things similar to themselves. God supplies various modes of <term>prophecy</term> <note>original: "uaticinij"</note>; he is not distracted himself, but suggests all modes through an indivisible command and a simple form. He suggests various modes at various

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

times, yet he is not varied through time himself. Instead, he inspires all things with a single, eternal stroke of mind and will, even though these signs are unfolded to us at different times. Neither is he contained within nor distracted by <term>portents</term> <note>original: "oſtentis"; extraordinary signs or omens</note>; rather, embracing all signs within his own single form, he expresses them from himself while remaining fixed in his own being, handing all things down through a single act of will. Such is the abundance of <term>providence</term> <note>original: "prouidentiæ"; the protective care and foresight of God</note> in showing us signs that he provides indications of the future even in pebbles, rods, wood, stones, grain, and flour. In these instances, he wonderfully seems to provide a soul to the inanimate, motion to the immobile, and a kind of intelligence to those things lacking reason—as if these objects actually "knew" the future when they reveal it to us. And just as God often speaks wisdom through <term>fools</term> <note>original: "fatuos homines"</note>, declaring that it is not the man speaking but God, so he portends the future through the most lowly things. In this, he is entirely superior to these instruments, and through all things, he instructs our own intellect. Through the lowest things which lack knowledge, God makes clear things that are superior to knowledge, thereby witnessing that he far transcends nature. While he offers various signs, he is not confined by them, nor is he distracted by them, but stands within his own single and indivisible form.

<summary>Iamblichus explains that gods communicate through mundane objects like flour or stones not out of servitude, but through an overflow of divine power that animates the inanimate. He argues that the divine remains unified and undisturbed even while producing a variety of signs across time.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Porphyry, divination, aleuromancy, providence, Neoplatonism, divine signs, prophecy</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of how the divine "nod" or will governs all things. He is refuting Porphyry’s suggestion that the gods are somehow subservient to human rituals or material objects.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

[the divine] soul contains there all signs, and from there, with a single and simple nod, unfolds all things, and provides us everywhere with evidence of past, present, and future events.

->*No work of any effectiveness occurs in sacred rites except through the power, or rather the presence, of some divinity.*<-

In religion, no work of any wondrous efficacy can occur unless one of the higher beings is present there, as both an observer and a fulfiller of the work. Indeed, the gods direct the most perfect works, <term>angels</term> <note>original: "angeli"; in the Neoplatonic hierarchy, these are spirits that bridge the gap between gods and the lower world</note> direct the intermediate ones, and <term>daimons</term> <note>original: "daemones"; spiritual entities that mediate between the divine and the mortal; they are not necessarily "evil" in this context</note> the third level. Human nature cannot even receive a true understanding or speech concerning the gods without the gods themselves, much less be capable of performing divine works without them. For human nature is so weak and dull that it has no remedy for its own "nothingness," except for a certain portion of divine light descending from above; without this, nothing we do can be divine. Just as those who believe they can produce a soul from inanimate things or a mind from the mindless are deceived, so too are those who believe they can produce divine things from non-divine ones. They cannot perform the specific works of the gods—such as <term>prophecies</term> <note>original: "vaticinia"; divine oracles or predictions</note>, miracles, and similar things like divine appearances and rites of purification—without the gods.

<meta>catchword: Divinities</meta>

<column-break/>

# 251

# ON THE MYSTERIES

->*Divinities are present to us when invoked, not because they are forced, but because they are willing.*<-

It is acknowledged that the effective works of religion are completed by the presence of gods, angels, and daimons. Porphyry, however, suspects that the <term>divinities</term> <note>original: "numina"; spiritual powers or divine presences</note> are drawn to these acts by some necessity of the invocation. Iamblichus denies that any necessity can be imposed on the divinities, either by man or by the world. Since they are immaterial, they do not receive an order imposed from the outside. Likewise, invocation and sacred operation actually draw the priests toward the divinities through a certain likeness and familiarity; this is not a violence inflicted upon the priests, let alone upon the divinities. God, and the entire chorus of divinities joined with God, is above all necessity coming from the outside. Prophecy is fulfilled while the priest performs sacred rites; at that time, no <term>passion</term> <note>original: "passio"; here meaning the state of being acted upon or moved by an external force</note> falls upon the priest or the diviner when they are divinely inspired. Much less does such passion affect the divinities, who do not flow into minds unless those minds are free from earthly passion. Nor should it be said that the prophetic soul is a middle and effective instrument of divine things, or that the invoking priest is the effective cause of divine works. On the contrary, God is all things, can do all things, and fills all things with Himself—

<summary>Iamblichus argues that sacred rites and prophecies are only possible through the voluntary presence of the gods, rather than through any human "force" or "necessity." He clarifies that humans do not control the gods; rather, rituals align the priest with the divine nature.</summary>
<keywords>divine presence, hierarchy of beings, angels, daimons, prophecy, Neoplatonism, Iamblichus, Porphyry, sacred rites, divine inspiration</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s argument that divine works, such as miracles and prophecy, do not originate from human nature or material causes, but are acts of grace from the gods themselves. He is specifically refuting the idea that astrology or the human psyche can produce these sacred effects.</meta>

<page-num>252</page-num>

# IAMBLICHUS

by [the divine] itself. Human nature truly has no efficacy in these matters, for it is a mere plaything <note>original: "ludus"; meaning human effort is trivial or like a child's game compared to the power of the gods</note> compared to divinity. We laugh at those who say that God is present to some people by a certain chance, through the power of a celestial configuration at their birth. For it is not fitting for unbegotten and universal causes to be enslaved to anyone through the process of birth, nor to its cycle, nor to causes foreign to their nature. 

Some people, because they did not wish to burden God with chores, and because they did not perceive any mode of craftsmanship or <term>providence</term> <note>original: "providentiae"; the protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power</note> other than that which arises from human worry, denied that the gods act or provide for us at all. Similarly, those who cannot see the exalted way in which the gods are present—without labor and without changing themselves—would deny that the gods take part in prophecies and miracles. Yet, the mode peculiar to the gods through all these things is known only to the gods themselves. 

<term>Porphyry</term> <note>a philosopher and student of Plotinus who was often skeptical of the literal reality of theurgical miracles</note> says that divine works, such as prophecies and miracles, are sometimes certain "passions" <note>in this context, "passions" refers to internal movements or states of the mind/soul</note> of the soul excited by small sparks, and that the soul is able to imagine, speak, and perform these things by its own power. 

Iamblichus, however, says that a divine work is neither performed by chance (since it is orderly), nor by a human being who is far inferior; for it is necessary that a divine work be performed by divinity. Therefore, all works similar to divinity do not sprout from human nature, but from the divine nature.

<meta>catchword: Works</meta>

---

<page-num>253</page-num>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

->*The works of miracles and prophecy are not by any power of the stars, the body, or the soul, but by the pure and free power of God.*<-

The human soul is both constrained within a single species and is too greatly overshadowed by the decaying body; therefore, it possesses no effective power for divine work. If we ever truly seem to be capable of such a thing in some way, we only perform it through a divine illustration <note>original: "illustratione"; a spiritual "lighting up" or inspiration</note> flowing down from above, through which we appear to be participants in the divine action. 

If divine works were performed by the soul, then by the very nature of the soul and by its own power, any soul possessed of virtue and prudence—or even every soul—would perform such things. But in reality, not every soul does so; and even the soul that appears perfect is still imperfect when compared to divine things. Therefore, the soul is not the cause, but divinity itself is the cause of divine works. Otherwise, <term>religion</term> <note>original: "religio"; here meaning the formal practice of worship and rites</note> would not be necessary for us to achieve these things; rather, we would be capable of divine works without the worship of the gods if they were performed by the power of the soul itself. 

Finally, if the soul performed divine things by its own power, then either every soul would do them, or those which possessed the excellence and perfection proper to the soul: namely, knowledge, art, prudence, and the character acquired through prudence. But as it stands now...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that miracles and prophecy are pure acts of God rather than products of the human soul, astrology, or psychic states. He refutes Porphyry’s psychological explanations, asserting that human nature is too weak to produce divine effects without direct inspiration from above.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, Iamblichus, Porphyry, Divine Providence, Miracles, Prophecy, Human Soul, Grace, Astrology, Religious Rites</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s rebuttal of Porphyry’s theories regarding the origin of divine works. Having previously argued that human nature and astrology cannot produce sacred effects, Iamblichus now addresses Porphyry’s specific suggestions that the soul might act as an instrument, a primary cause, or a component of a divine-human hybrid in theurgy.</meta>

<page-num>254</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

Those who perform these acts are not of that sort <note>meaning they are not acting through mere human skill or mundane psychological states</note>, but are rather those who are moved and drawn away <note>original: "abstracti"; referring to being spiritually detached from the physical world</note> from the material. <term>Porphyry</term> had proposed three possibilities regarding divine works. First, that for these acts, the soul is an **instrumental cause** <note>original: "causam instrumentalem"; a tool used by a higher power</note>. Second, that the soul is sometimes the **principal cause**. Third, that the soul can be an equal **co-cause**, such that from the soul and the divine spirit, a certain third type of substance or existence is produced.

Iamblichus says that when a single thing is made from many, those many parts must be both related to one another and capable of being changed from what they are into something else. In this way, the four elements are mixed into a single fifth thing <note>the "quintessence" or ether</note>; and multiple souls can merge into one whole—perhaps the vegetative, sensory, and motive souls within us yield to the **intellectual soul** <note>original: "intellectuali"; the highest part of the human psyche</note> and are brought into its activity. Or perhaps from all of these, a "vital harmony" results within the body. Or perhaps souls in their **fatherland** <note>original: "patria"; a common Neoplatonic term for the divine realm from which the soul originates</note> unite into one single Idea of all souls. 

But **Divinity**, being entirely exempt from all things, does not join with the soul to form a "third" thing—especially with a soul that has already fallen from its original divine state. Because divinity is so vastly different, it does not enter into a mixture; and because it is **immutable** <note>unchanging</note>, it cannot be changed into something else that is common to both itself and the soul. It had been said previously that by tiny sparks...

<meta>catchword: awakened</meta>

---

<page-num>255</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

...divine forms are **awakened** in the soul. Let it be so; but these would not be transformed into purely divine forms if they were merely bodily and natural in origin. Finally, when it is said that the soul and divinity meet to form a "third" entity, the soul is being made equal to God. Furthermore, it would mean that God is changed and receives something from the soul, just as the soul is changed and receives something from God—which is **absurd**. 

This is especially true because such a view admits that the gods, like mere elements, would combine in their effects to create a single form that is made "in time" <note>original: "temporaliter"; meaning something that has a beginning and an end, unlike the eternal gods</note> and out of eternal things, and that they would be contained within a temporal thing. 

When it is claimed that from the soul and God (or from various gods) a single temporal thing is produced, we must ask: does something new result from this meeting, or not? If nothing new results, then there is no true union, nor a true unity—just as in a work of art, which depends only on the artist's skill but does not become one with the artist. If something new *does* result, then **eternal things** would fall from their high status to become something temporal; for unless they are changed, a third thing cannot result. 

Indeed, it is completely absurd to claim that something produced by a temporal birth is actually eternal. Yet, it is also not fitting to call it temporal, for then eternal things would be made to serve the temporal. Nor is it fitting to suggest that this union is **dissoluble** <note>original: "solubile"; capable of being broken apart or destroyed</note> on both sides...

<summary>Iamblichus refutes Porphyry's theories that the soul and the divine can merge into a "third" substance, arguing that the gods are immutable and cannot be diminished by mixing with temporal, human nature.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Porphyry, Neoplatonism, theurgy, soul, divinity, instrumental cause, immutable, temporal vs eternal, union</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical debate between Iamblichus and Porphyry regarding the origins of divine inspiration and the nature of intermediary spirits known as "daemons." Iamblichus is refuting the idea that these spirits can be "manufactured" or summoned through mere material means or psychological states.</meta>

<page-num>256</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

made from eternal things. And yet, through a new generation, it seems to be dissolvable. Therefore, that opinion is absurd. <term>Porphyry</term><note>Porphyry (c. 234–305 AD) was a student of Plotinus and a critic of certain ritual practices, prompting Iamblichus's response.</note> says that the soul sometimes generates a **daemonic power and essence** <note>original: "essentiamque dæmonicam"; in this context, "daemons" are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily evil</note>, which imagines future things through such motions. I say "generates" when it brings together certain materials that possess such powers within themselves, especially materials taken from living creatures. 

<term>Iamblichus</term><note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who defended theurgy, or "god-working" rituals.</note> denies that **daemons** are generated or destroyed. For he denies that daemons—who are superior to both bodies and the soul—can be generated by the soul from bodily powers. He also denies that the actions of a particular soul, when enclosed in a body, can proceed into essences that subsist on their own outside of the soul. He denies that the powers of bodies, which are entirely bound to matter, can be released from them to generate daemons. 

Furthermore, he asks what power it could be that releases these material forces from their matter and melts them back together into one to generate a daemon; for such a power would itself seem to be daemonic, and thus the daemon would exist even before it is generated. A soul—especially a **prophetic** <note>original: "fatidica"; fate-speaking or predictive</note> one—cannot be generated from things lacking life and the gift of prophecy. What is more excellent cannot be procreated from the inferior. No essence or substance can be established from the motions of the soul and the 
<meta>catchword: and by the powers;</meta>

---

<page-num>257</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

and by the bodily powers, which are neither substances nor essences. Likewise, nothing that is sown through generation possesses anything greater than what is handed down to it by its primary generating cause. Therefore, since neither the body nor the soul possesses **prophecy** <note>original: "uaticinium"; the ability to foretell the future</note> by itself, the soul cannot procreate a prophesying daemon from its own motions and bodies. 

Perhaps someone will say that daemons are not generated by us, but that they preside over living beings in a certain order; and so, when materials from the animals entrusted to such a daemon are applied at the opportune time, this daemon is drawn toward them as if through **shared suffering** <note>original: "compatientem"; referring to "sympathy," a Stoic and Neoplatonic concept where parts of the universe react to one another</note>. Iamblichus says that daemons who "suffer" to such an extent cannot prophesy the truth. For true prophecy does not belong to a nature that is passive <note>original: "passibilis"; capable of being affected or suffering change</note> and enclosed in some body, place, or time, but is instead free from all these things, so that it may look forward to things done in any place or time equally and simply.

### *Concerning divine and human dreams.*

Porphyry says that the forewarnings of **dreams** are partly from within ourselves and partly from an external source. Iamblichus says they come solely from the outside: because we often strive and do many things to "hunt" for this forewarning, and yet
<sig>R</sig>

<vocab>Porphyry, Iamblichus, On the Mysteries, spirit/daemon, prophecy, dreams</vocab>

<summary>Iamblichus argues that spirits (daemons) and the gift of prophecy cannot be created from physical materials or human psychology, as the cause cannot be inferior to the effect. He also begins to distinguish divine dreams, which he claims come exclusively from external divine sources, from ordinary human ones.</summary>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical dispute between Porphyry and Iamblichus regarding the nature of divine revelation. While the previous page discussed whether spirits (daemons) are generated by the soul, this page focuses on whether prophecy is a psychological "passion" or a gift from the gods.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS, ON THE MYSTERIES.
## R 2

It is given; and often it is given to those who are not seeking it. For if the effect of dreams arose from our own nature and human will, it would occur when we provided for it. However, it is sent by the gods according to their own will—namely, when and how they wish—to demonstrate the future through a certain kindness. The Orphic hymn <note>The *Orphic Hymns* were a collection of ancient Greek poems used in mystery religions, often attributed to the mythical poet Orpheus.</note> declares the same thing. **Porphyry** says that the cause of **prophecy** <note>original: "uaticinij"; the art of predicting the future through divine inspiration</note> is a certain **passion** <note>original: "passionẽ"; in this context, "passion" refers to an external influence or an emotional state that the soul "undergoes" or "suffers," rather than modern romantic passion</note> of the soul. **Iamblichus** denies this, saying that passion is by its very nature unstable, disordered, and disturbed, whereas prophecy ought to be entirely stable and ordered; therefore, it cannot be found within a state of passion. Likewise, when the soul is prudent and constant, and acts according to its more excellent powers—namely the intellectual and rational ones—it does not foresee the future. How, then, will it attain these things when it "suffers" according to disordered and disturbed movements? Furthermore, passion has no capacity for the contemplation of true beings and truth, but instead brings about an impediment. Moreover, if the things of the world were themselves constituted by passions, then a "passion" might reach them through some similarity. But since they are constituted and governed by reasons and forms, there is surely some other kind of foreknowledge that is alien to all passions.

<column-break/>

Again, passion perceives only present things. Since prophecy also attains the future, it does not involve passion. **Porphyry** says that prophecy is a certain passion of the **imagination** <note>original: "phantasiæ"; the faculty of the mind that produces mental images</note>, arising either from our own thoughts, or by the instinct of the bodily nature within us, or occurring in a similar way to how the frenzied <note>original: "phreneticis"; those suffering from "phrensy" or mental agitation</note> happen to prophesy. To support this, he offers signs: that in the state of prophecy, the imagination acts vehemently, while the senses are meanwhile occupied and restrained. Also, that **fumigations** <note>the use of ritual incense or vapors</note> are used for the one about to divine, clearly to affect the imagination. Also, that **invocations** are used to incite this same state. Finally, he notes that not everyone is suited for this, but only simpler youths who are fit for the task. **Iamblichus** argues that the "occupation of the senses" is actually a sign that no human mental image is present at that time. He claims that vapors are used to create a harmony <note>original: "congruitatem"</note> with the god, not to affect the soul of the person watching. Invocations do not excite either thoughts or bodily passions in us; for they are unknown to us, and known only to the god who is invoked. Only the simpler people <note>original: "simpliciores"; those who are guileless or less intellectually rigid</note> are exposed to the divine spirit coming from the outside.

<summary>This page presents Iamblichus's rebuttal to Porphyry's psychological theory of prophecy, arguing that divine revelation is a stable gift from the gods rather than a byproduct of emotional disturbance or imagination.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Porphyry, prophecy, divination, passion, imagination, ritual incense, Orphic hymns, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of divine inspiration against Porphyry’s skeptical psychological explanations. The previous page argued that prophecy is not a disordered "passion" of the soul; this page distinguishes between "alienation" (ecstasy) caused by disease or intoxication and that caused by the gods.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS
## 260

[that it] is a passion of the imagination <note>original: "phantasiæ"</note> such as happens to the melancholic and the foolish who are often soothsayers: he [Porphyry] misuses the divine gift of prophecy <note>original: "uaticinij"</note> when he compares the alienation <note>original: "alienationē"; a state of being "outside oneself" or in an ecstatic state</note> produced by the divine for the better, with the alienation occurring from diseases, drunkenness, and rage, which happens for the worse. The alienation of the mind happening from our passions casts the mind down below the human state; but that produced by God lifts the human above the human and joins them to God. The former renders one unstable and completely immerses them in matter; the latter [renders one] constant and a master of order, and releases them from matter. In no way should it be said that divine inspiration <note>original: "afflationem"</note> has anything similar to the alienation produced by the humors <note>The "humors" refers to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which ancient medicine believed caused mental illness or personality shifts when imbalanced.</note>, drunkenness, or rage: for these are at a distance from God beyond what can be imagined. Therefore, such insanity is just as far from divine inspiration; they have nothing similar or in common. Alienation produced by bodily causes is a depravity of the mind happening from weakness: but that produced divinely is the salvation of the soul happening from the fullness of power. In the former indeed, while the soul attempts its own operations, it produces them perverted and turbulent: but in the latter, while it is at leisure according to its own life and intelligence, it exposes itself to be used by the higher [power]. Finally,

---

# ON THE MYSTERIES.
## 261

how much divine things differ from the lowest natural things, so much do their actions and effects [differ] from the effects of natural things. Therefore, the alienation induced divinely has nothing similar to that which occurs humanly. Therefore, when you think of that [divine alienation], separate entirely from it all defects and all accidents of this [human alienation]. Furthermore, when you meditate upon the sacred sobriety and vigilance received divinely, do not compare it with the sobriety of men. And in both cases, remove from the imaginations divinely applied to the soul whatever is in any way similar to any imaginations which humors and similar causes excite. Likewise, the ambiguous state of a man between a sober mind and an alienated one—a middle state—do not think it similar to the state of a sacred soul, beholding God with a single gaze. Nor should you believe the most certain insights of divine things to be similar to imaginations excited by the poisonous art <note>original: "arte uenefica"; here referring to sorcery or "low" magic that uses potions or charms to induce hallucinations.</note>. For what we imagine when fascinated has no truth of action or essence beyond the imaginings themselves. For the end of such magic is not simply to do something, but to stretch out imaginings until they appear [real]. It is not to be thought that the divine prophet <note>original: "uatem"</note> happens upon the truth by chance, as the insane are often accustomed [to do]: nor even by a certain natural passion, as

<summary>Iamblichus argues that divine ecstasy (alienation) is a superior state that connects humans to God, distinguishing it sharply from medical insanity, drunkenness, or the deceptive illusions of common sorcery.</summary>
<keywords>divine inspiration, ecstasy, alienation, phantasia, prophecy, magic, humors, Neoplatonism, Iamblichus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s argument that divine prophecy is distinct from natural instincts or human arts. He is responding to Porphyry's suggestion that "foreknowledge" might simply be a natural byproduct of our physical or mental state.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS
## 262

...and animals are often accustomed to be affected by certain parts and powers [of the world] that are beginning something in the future, and are already acting in the air in a certain way before they act upon the earth. And so, by being affected in this way, they sometimes sense future earthquakes and storms, especially those animals that are either easily affected or have acute senses. For foreknowledge <note>original: "Prænotio"</note> arising either by chance or by such an affection is rare, disordered, inconsistent, and utterly obscure.

But divine prophecy <note>original: "Vaticiniū"</note> beholds the order of the entire universe with a continuous, firm, and connected reason. Even if we have some innate faculty for perceiving the future, that is not for that reason prophecy; for this faculty is not always true, but only sometimes; nor is it in everyone, but only in some. But prophecy is always true, and in all cases.

Likewise, foreknowledge achieved through art <note>This refers to technical divination, such as reading bird flight or entrails, which Iamblichus considers a human skill rather than a divine gift.</note> searches for the future from signs that are not entirely reliable—because they do not always have the event joined to them—but rather [is achieved] by divine providence. It beholds effects from causes with a firm and present and entirely continuous intuition, connecting all things that are changeable and temporal and distant from each other, anticipating future things as if they were present. Someone will say that the future can be predicted from four gifts

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES
## 263

->R 4<-

joined together: namely, from a certain human nature, from an art applied to it, from the common sympathy <note>original: "compassione"; the Neoplatonic concept of "sympatheia," the idea that the universe is a single living organism where all parts react to one another.</note> of things for one another—as if they were parts of the same living creature—and from that disposition of bodies by which some provide signs to others. 

But in truth, when signs are clearly perceived through these combined factors, even this perception has something similar to prophecy; and in general, it has traces <note>original: "uestigia"</note> of prophecy everywhere, but not everywhere clearly. Nevertheless, just as the image of the Good itself in all things points to God, so in all things there is some trace of prophecy. Yet, the divine species of prophecy—pure and unmixed—is not to be defined by its images cast down into the realm of generation <note>The "realm of generation" refers to the physical, changing world of birth and death, as opposed to the eternal, divine realm.</note>, and much less by the imaginings of those who are raving <note>original: "delirantium"; Iamblichus is distinguishing between divine ecstasy and mental illness.</note>. For all these things are deceptive. 

Prophecy itself must be defined through a single reason, a single order, a single divine form, and a single intelligible and immutable truth. On the contrary, all changeable perception must be separated from it as something indefinite, and in no way suited to the gods and divine things. Whenever we perform any work natural to us, certainly the seeds and preparations for it were granted from the beginning by nature itself, which is the cause of such work. Therefore, there are no...

<summary>Iamblichus distinguishes between true divine prophecy—which is orderly, constant, and certain—and natural foreknowledge or technical divination, which he views as mere "traces" or unreliable reflections of the divine gift.</summary>
<keywords>divine prophecy, foreknowledge, providence, universal sympathy, divination, nature, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's argument that divine prophecy is fundamentally different from natural human instincts. He is refuting Porphyry's suggestion that prophecy might be a natural capacity of the human body or soul, rather than a gift from the gods.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS
## 264

...preparations and seeds provided by nature, whether universal or human, for that prophecy <note>original: "vaticinium"</note> which is above nature and human custom; for nature lacks intellect. Nor is nature the primary author of those things that happen, especially insofar as nature is within us. Instead, prophecy is more than intellectual; it touches the causes of events and, in a single and sudden intuition, encompasses the order of the universe. A work of this kind is characteristic of a god acting within us, while we are at that time vacant from our own personal action.

If any foreboding <note>original: "Præsagium"</note> occurs to us, it is carried out by natural instinct or by human skill <note>original: "arte"</note>; it can certainly have a preparation and an aptitude within our nature. But this kind of foreboding is extremely narrow and obscure, and is subject to being "more or less" <note>This implies it is inconsistent and varies in intensity, unlike divine truth which is absolute.</note>. Divine prophecy, however, is most broad and certain, singular and stable—that is, it holds the highest rank everywhere—and therefore it could not have been implanted in us by nature.

Porphyry conjectured that a certain affection of our nature, whether of the body or of the soul <note>original: "animalem"</note>, contributed to prophecy. He based this primarily on the fact that those who summoned prophecy into a person used to carry certain small stones and herbs, and would tie sacred knots and then untie what they had tied; they would also change the 

<meta>catchword: circumstances</meta>

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES
## 265

->DE MYSTERIIS<-

circumstances <note>original: "posita"</note> of those who had received such things from them, turning bad situations into better ones. 

But Iamblichus turns these observations and effects back toward his own argument: that the inspiration of prophecy comes from outside and from above. For when such things, which are in harmony with certain deities <note>original: "numinibus"</note>, are applied to us, they adapt us to receive their inspiration. 

Porphyry says that we should not despise the art which, by using certain vapors over fire under the favorable influences of the stars, creates "idols" <note>original: "idola," referring here to phantasms or ritual apparitions</note> of the gods that appear immediately in the air. These are in some way similar to the gods and possess a similar kind of efficacy. Iamblichus, however, says that these idols are held in low esteem by priests who have seen the true appearances of the gods. He argues that no excellent good can be found in something that sprouts from matter and the forms within it, or from the powers of such forms combined with human art. For what is made by man is inferior to man; and what is made as an "idol" is a kind of shadow <note>original: "adumbratio"</note>, appearing rather than truly existing, and vanishing as soon as it is made. It is not produced by the "craftsman's art" <note>original: "arte opificia," referring to the creative power that makes real things</note> which creates true essences, but by an "imitative art" which merely renders images of essences. 

Indeed, the God who is the maker of the world did not [create] through celestial motions, nor through particular matter, nor...

->R 5<-

<summary>Iamblichus argues that true prophecy is a divine act that transcends human nature, dismissing Porphyry's idea that it arises from natural instincts or the manipulation of physical objects like herbs and stones.</summary>

<keywords>Iamblichus, Porphyry, Neoplatonism, prophecy, divination, theurgy, idols, divine inspiration, nature vs. intellect</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's response to Porphyry's questions. Having previously discussed the nature of prophecy, Iamblichus now addresses the creation of "idols" or animated statues, distinguishing between divine creation and the lower, artificial "art" of making images that merely capture the lowliest celestial influences.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS
## 266

...[the Divine] creates all things through powers divided in this way, but with intellects, wills, and immaterial forms, and through an eternal, supermundane soul—as well as a mundane soul—He brings worlds into being. The maker of such idols <note>original: "idolorum"; here referring to animated statues or images used in lower forms of magic</note> is said to work through the stars; yet he does not work through the stars themselves in the way that might perhaps seem. For since there are innumerable powers in the celestial gods, the lowest of all is natural power. Within that natural power of the celestial gods are "reasons" <note>original: "rationes"; these are the *logoi*, the rational, formative principles that govern nature</note> which are both seminal <note>generative of forms</note> and others that are immobile, preceding the seminal ones, which coordinate the forms. Therefore, the authority of both types of "reasons" precedes the entire process of generation. After these, there are qualities, forces, and movements in the celestial bodies, and from them flows a dominance—under the guidance of those higher reasons—over the whole ordering of mundane things. Indeed, the lowest power of the celestials, from among those forces I mentioned, leads the generation of earthly things. Many arts, such as medicine, gymnastics, and agriculture—whichever arts communicate in their operation with the operation of nature—use these qualities and forces transmitted to lower things through celestial influxes <note>original: "defluxus"; the downward flow of cosmic energy</note>. And yet...

<meta>catchword: idoloficus</meta>

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES
## 267

->DE MYSTERIIS<-

...the idol-maker <note>original: "idoloficus"</note> himself draws a certain generative portion from these celestial influxes, however small and weak it may be. For he does not use the motions of the heavens themselves, or the forces within them, or the forces naturally established around celestial motions; nor can he reach them. Instead, he deals with certain forces that have flowed down from their nature to the very end, falling to the lowest place of the world. He indeed uses a certain art, but it is not one that is pious and god-like <note>original: "deifica"</note>. These lowest celestial forces, working together around some particular matter, can transmute, transfigure, and transform it in various ways. Furthermore, they undergo a transposition of those powers which exist in particular things. When someone makes such idols from many materials and under celestial influences, then the variety of celestial actions on one hand, and material powers on the other, produces something; but this action falls far short not only of divine craftsmanship <note>original: "opificio diuino"</note> but even of natural effect. For Nature performs its own work with a single steady course and a simple action. This making of idols, therefore, is an artificial mixing involving the final <note>meaning the lowest or most distant</note> influx of the celestials, and involving those...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that while divine creation is a high intellectual process, the "art" of making animated idols is a low-level practice that only taps into the weakest, most distant remnants of celestial power.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Neoplatonism, theurgy, idols, celestial influx, seminal reasons, nature, divine craftsmanship</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's rebuttal to Porphyry regarding theurgy. Iamblichus is distinguishing between true divine contact and "idol-making" or "eidolon-making"—the creation of magical statues or illusions that only capture the lowest, most fleeting cosmic influences.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS
## 268

...which are moved by and recede from the celestial nature to the furthest degree. Man, since he is a better being and procreated from better origins, ought not to devote himself to the manufacturing of idols <note>original: "idolis"; here referring to artificial statues or magical images used in lower forms of sorcery</note>, which are not truly animated, but are merely inflated with a certain outward show of life. Nor do they contain an inward and true harmony, but only an external and fabricated one, destined to last for only a short time. Nor do they possess anything true or authentic, since they are fashioned solely by human art. Indeed, only that which is simple and uniform in action or composition prevails within them, and for that reason they are greatly deficient, since they are piled together from widely diverse and contrary qualities. Nor do they possess any perfect power, because a multitude of such influxes <note>original: "defluxuum"; the downward flow of cosmic energy from the stars</note> is captured from many sources and mixed into something adventitious <note>meaning external or non-essential</note> that is utterly thin and weak. Nor do these idols have any stability; indeed, they vanish more quickly than the reflections seen in mirrors. For when incense <gloss>suffumigation</gloss> is applied with exhaling vapors, they are immediately formed; but when the smoke and vapor spread through the entire air, they suddenly dissolve. The manufacturing of such monstrous illusions is entirely unworthy of a seeker of truth. For he who contrives these things, whether he recognizes their utility

<column-break/>

<page-num>269</page-num>
# DE MYSTERIIS

yet delights in them, becomes like them. Or if he worships them as gods, he is guilty of a sin in deed and word that is unspeakable; nor does any divine light shine into a soul that is occupied with the dark phantasms <note>original: "phantasmata"; illusory images or mental shadows</note> of idols. Porphyry says that the makers of idols observe the course of the celestial bodies during their manufacture, claiming that depending on which celestial body is primarily running, and with which or with whom it concurs, true or false prophecies <note>original: "vaticinia"</note> will result. Likewise, they ask whether what happens there is significant or effective, or on the contrary, void of significance and efficacy. Although they construct idols from chosen materials through the observation of the stars, these things nevertheless possess nothing divine within them. For the materials that are moved here by the heavens are the lowliest in the order of generation, and they suffer <note>original: "compatiuntur"; they are passive recipients of influence</note> the very last of the celestial influxes. For such phantasms, which are so easily changed that at the slightest nod they are either inert or, on the contrary, prophetic, significant, or effective—certainly such easy passivity and rapid mutability declare that they have nothing of divine power. There are no powers in matter that are the basic elements of demons <note>the "daemones" are intermediary spirits between gods and men in the Neoplatonic hierarchy</note>. Nor can these spirits be generated from particular bodies; on the contrary, these bodies are generated and preserved by the spirits. Nor...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that the magical creation of "idols" or animated statues is a low, unstable art that captures only the weakest celestial influences, unlike true theurgy which connects with the divine. He warns that focusing on these fleeting phantasms prevents the soul from receiving true divine light.</summary>

<keywords>theurgy, idol-making, celestial influx, Porphyry, phantasms, daemones, Neoplatonism, divination</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's defense of theurgy against Porphyry’s skepticism. In the preceding section, Iamblichus argued that man-made "idols" or magical statues are mere fleeting illusions, lacking the true, stable essence of divine beings.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS
## 270

...man cannot produce the forms of demons themselves by his own devices. On the contrary, we are formed by demons according to the physical body. It must not be thought that some multitude or composition, having a power inherent to demons, flows together from a union of elements. For the demon itself—being simple, uniform, and self-consistent—gathers diverse elements into one and generates compositions from them, and whatever stability exists within them, he himself bestows it. Nor is it proper to call idols "demons." For the author of idols is one thing, and the great leader of demons is another. The very substance of demons is different from the phantasms <term>phantasmatis; meaning deceptive appearances or empty shadows</term> of idols, by which—as you, Porphyry, also admit—neither a god nor any demon is dragged down from above. Therefore, this manufacture of idols, since it is devoid of both god and demon, brings neither true prophecy nor any sacred work.

But those who are ruinous, and who meanwhile insolently invade the sacred rites against the order and custom of the sacrifices, surely cannot reach the gods because of the weakness of their action and defect of power. Instead, because of certain defilements, they are both kept away from the gods and are matched with evil demons, by whose most wretched breath <note>original: "flatu"; meaning a spiritual or pneumatic influence</note> they are entirely filled. <header>230 IAMBLICHUS</header>

...busy themselves constantly. These men, therefore, being full of passions and depravity, draw evil spirits to themselves through a natural kinship. Once occupied by these spirits, they are incited again toward every kind of iniquity. Here, one assists and strengthens the other in turn, like a circle joining the beginning to the end, and returning an equal exchange in the same manner. Thus far the Chaldæans <note>original: "Hucusq́; Chaldæi"; Iamblichus often cites the Chaldean Oracles as a primary authority on theurgy.</note>. When the profane handle sacred rites improperly, the outcome is frustrated: for instead of the true gods, others are celebrated, and in place of good spirits, evil ones are introduced. Therefore, the errors of these people should not be commemorated when discussing legitimate sacred rites, nor should what happens to them be considered a law. Just as sacrilege is the opposite of divine worship, so those occupied by deceptive evil demons—the authors of intemperance—are the opposites of legitimate priests. From these priests, every wicked spirit, depravity, and passion is far removed. Rather, the presence of good deities is at hand, and from above they are filled with truth by the divine fire. To these priests, evil spirits bring no hindrance to the goods of the soul; nor is the smoke of pride or soft flattery suggested from that source, nor are they deceived by any fumigation, nor disturbed by any force. Instead, all these evils, by the power of the good pre-

---

<page-num>273</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES. 273</header>

-sence of the deity, are immediately scattered and vanish as if struck by a lightning bolt. For such people, a truthful prophecy results, with which nothing else can be compared in any way; this must be studied with the proper sanctity we have described. For by this means alone is both unshakable truth acquired in prophesying and perfect virtue attained in the soul. By these two things indeed, the priest ascends to the intelligible fire <term>ignem intelligibilem; the highest, non-physical realm of divine light and pure thought in Neoplatonism</term>, which ought to be proposed as the goal of every presage and sacrifice. You have thought it necessary, O Porphyry, to mention all too rashly the opinion of those who think every prophecy is granted by an evil demon when discussing divine vaticination. For these people, having always lived in darkness from the beginning, can neither discern the true from the false, nor understand the principles from which these things come.

### *Invocations and commands of men*
### *against spirits.*

Porphyry doubts why we sometimes humbly invoke spirits as if they were our superiors, yet at other times issue commands to them as if they were our inferiors. Iamblichus distinguishes them thus. The gods indeed, and all those superior to our nature...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that while wicked practitioners attract evil spirits through their own depravity, true priests are purified by divine fire, allowing them to access genuine prophecy. He begins to address the paradox of why theurgists use both humble prayers and authoritative commands when interacting with spirits.</summary>

<meta>This translation continues from page 273, where Iamblichus explains how the purity of a priest attracts good spirits and repels evil ones. On these pages, he begins to address the specific question of why a theurgist—a practitioner of divine rites—might address spiritual beings with both humble prayers and authoritative commands.</meta>

<page-num>274</page-num>
-> IAMBLICHUS <-

<meta>catchword: they carry</meta>

...beings, through the fullness of goodness, the will of the good, and a certain kindness, grant things appropriate to worthy souls. They choose the religious as their own offspring <note>original: "genituras"; referring to those born into or destined for a spiritual life</note>, fosterlings, and disciples, and they take pity on those who labor. There are, furthermore, certain middle classes of deities who are overseers <note>original: "ephori"; a term for magistrates or supervisors, here acting as cosmic guardians</note> and presidents of discernment and judgment; they advise on what things are proper to do and what to avoid. They assist the just, recall people from the unjust, and often justly turn back upon wicked men the injuries they attempt to inflict, so that they themselves suffer what they were striving to do. There is, again, another class of spirits surrounding us that is indiscriminate and unthinking, which has been allotted one single power in number, because of its distribution from the individual parts of the universe; hence, one is devoted to only one task. For just as a sword only divides and does nothing else, so among the spirits distributed in the world—due to the very necessity of their divisible nature—this one only divides, while that one only collects the things that come to be and are generated. Certain hidden spirits, therefore, having each been allotted a different power, perform individually only that to which they have been ordered. If anyone, therefore, taking those things which, in their distributed order, contribute to the whole—

---

<page-num>275</page-num>
-> ON THE MYSTERIES <-

<note>S 2 - Printer's signature</note>

carry <note>original: "ferunt"; the catchword from the previous page</note> them, should divert them elsewhere and transfer something as if by transgression, the harm of this very act falls upon the one using it badly. But let us return to the question. To answer the question: commands and violent orders are directed toward spirits who neither use their own reason nor possess the principle of judgment and discernment. For since our thought possesses the nature of reasoning and discerning the condition in which things exist, and also encompasses many powers of life within itself, it is accustomed to command spirits that do not use reason and are determined toward only one action. The priest <note>original: "Inuocat eos"; literally "He invokes them"</note> invokes them as superiors, because he seeks to draw from the whole world that contains us those things which contribute to the whole, concerning certain things contained in particular and divided parts. He commands them, again, as inferiors, because our intellectual nature is more excellent than that which lacks intellect, even if those spirits have a wider range of action in the world. Likewise, there is a double persona <note>original: "figura"; the "mask" or role the priest adopts during a ritual</note> or garment in the priest: one indeed human, but the other divine, which he puts on through the sacred rites <note>original: "sacramentis"; referring here to theurgic rituals or mysteries</note>, in which the divine power lies hidden, lifting the mind upward to divinity and joining it to God. And therefore—

<summary>Iamblichus explains that different classes of spirits exist, ranging from judging overseers to unthinking forces that perform a single function. He justifies the priest's use of commands by explaining that the human intellect is superior to unreasoning spirits, and that the priest assumes a divine persona during rituals.</summary>

<keywords>Iamblichus, De Mysteriis, spirits, daemons, theurgy, priesthood, reason, sacred rites, divine persona</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of theurgy—the practice of rituals intended to invoke the presence of the divine. On the previous page, he discussed how different classes of spirits are assigned specific tasks in the universe and how the character of the priest influences the success of a ritual. Here, he addresses the paradox of why a human priest might command a god.</meta>

<page-num>276</page-num>
-> IAMBLICHUS <-

...by applying a **divine form** to the soul, with which he is clothed, he can, under the pretext of a god, command such spirits as if he were a superior. Nevertheless, as a human, he is accustomed to ask by invoking them, insofar as they are superior, to the extent that he invokes powers from the universe. But we solve the question more truly in this way: a certain communion of consenting benevolence and an indissoluble embrace of unity contains every **sacred expedition** <note>original: "sacrorum expeditionem"; likely referring to the performance of sacred rites or theurgic operations</note>, so that this effect is divine and exceeds every measure of human effort. Therefore, we ought to remove all human effort from here. 

Therefore, the priest does not summon the deity as if he were absent, nor does he command anything to be performed by a separate entity, as humans usually do among themselves. Rather, a single and same action of **divine fire** <note>original: "divini ignis"; a reference to the celestial fire or the energy of the gods in Neoplatonic philosophy</note>, spontaneously ready and present through itself, acting from itself, shines forth and acts in the same way through all things at once—both through those things which are subsequently passed on to others and those which are able to receive something from it. Therefore, the divine works which are fulfilled in sacred rites are not transfigured through the opposition of one thing to another as **generated things** <note>original: "generabilia"; things in the physical world that are born and die</note> are, nor through the difference of the one calling or moving and the one summoned and moved, as human things usually are. Instead, that whole is fulfilled by identity, union, and consensus; and with discrepancy removed,

<meta>catchword: discrepancy,</meta>

---

<page-num>277</page-num>
-> DE MYSTERIIS <-

discrepancy, that very thing which acts, and that by whose power it is acted upon, is everywhere something common and simple. 

**Porphyry** <note>Iamblichus is responding to a series of skeptical questions posed by his predecessor, Porphyry of Tyre</note> asks why the higher beings often command their worshipers to be just, yet sometimes, when entreated, they inflict injury. **Iamblichus** replies that they do not inflict injuries, but rather **just punishments**; and if by chance they are unjust, they are not gods, but wicked **demons** <note>term: "demons" (Latin: *daemones*); in Greek philosophy, these are intermediate spirits between gods and humans, capable of being either benevolent or malevolent</note>. 

The gods do not behold only a certain part of this life, but the whole; and not this life alone, but the entire life of the soul **from age to age** <note>original: "ab æuo in æuum"; a reference to the soul's journey through multiple incarnations or lifetimes</note>. And so, they often punish in a subsequent life according to the merits contracted in a preceding life, whether they are asked to punish or not. Meanwhile, we, who see nothing beyond the present, suppose that adversities sometimes happen to us without merit; while yet we often suffer in this life for what we have already inflicted in another, and what we do now, we shall one day suffer. 

Furthermore, many sins of men are hidden from us, which are by no means hidden from the gods. Likewise, we define justice as everyone doing their own duty according to the present laws of the homeland. But the gods define it much more broadly, looking to the whole order of the universe, and looking especially to that reason by which the judgment of souls is conducive to the gods.

<sig>S 3</sig>

<summary>Iamblichus explains that theurgic ritual works through divine union rather than human command, and he defends the gods against charges of injustice by explaining that divine punishment accounts for the soul's actions across multiple lifetimes.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, theurgy, divine fire, Porphyry, justice, reincarnation, divine punishment, demons, sacred rites, soul's journey</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of how divine justice operates in the context of theurgy. Having previously discussed how a priest might appear to "command" spirits, he now addresses the deeper question of why divine rewards or punishments might seem "unjust" to human eyes and clarifies that evil originates from lower, deceptive spirits rather than the gods themselves.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS | ON THE MYSTERIES

...perform. And so great is the diversity between **divine judgment** regarding justice itself and our own, that we cannot reach an understanding of it. Furthermore, the gods themselves weigh what is just differently when looking at a single soul, and differently when looking at the whole kinship of souls. For if a communion of the same nature exists among souls—both those living in a body and those situated outside the body—it creates a certain shared and common bond for all toward the entire life and order of the world. Therefore, it is necessary to seek the execution of judgment from the whole—that is, from the universal order—and from the relationship of souls among themselves. This is especially true where the magnitude of crimes committed by a single soul exceeds the punishment that would naturally follow from its individual sins. 

The gods, therefore, even when entreated, never inflict punishments unless they are just. Since they possess the **Good** according to their very essence, they cannot be the cause of evil or injustice. But if someone should perhaps prove, by pointing to curses <note>original: "imprecationibus"; formal prayers or rituals intended to bring harm to an enemy</note> made to the higher powers, that certain adversities happen to people unjustly, we will be compelled to look for other causes besides the gods and good spirits. If we happen not to find those causes, we still ought not to suspect anything of the gods that is unworthy of the 

<column-break/>

**divine nature**, nor of that certain knowledge of the goodness of the gods which is innate to our intellects—a truth in which, without doubt, all Greeks and barbarians <note>original: "Gręci omnes barbaricꝫ [barbaris]"; in this context, "barbarians" simply refers to all non-Greek peoples, such as Egyptians or Chaldeans, whom Iamblichus often respects for their ancient wisdom</note> agree. 

Consequently, since the types of evils are diverse and conflicting, they are not to be referred to a single cause, but to various ones—assuming that what I was saying about idols and **wicked demons** <note>original: "improbisqꝫ dęmonibus"; Neoplatonists believed in a hierarchy of spirits (daemones). While some were good, others were lower, "material" spirits that could be deceptive</note> is true. From this, indeed, arises the manifold origin of evils. For these spirits simulate the presence of the gods and good demons; they even command their worshiper to be just, so that they themselves might appear to be good, just like the gods. 

However, because they are evil by nature, when they are asked to inflict harm, they do so willingly and lead us toward unjust actions. These are entirely the ones who both lie in **oracles** and deceive, and who counsel and perform shameful things. The gods and good demons, however, never deceive, nor do they contribute to iniquity. Moreover, the nature of evil demons is inconsistent within itself, unstable and at odds with itself, persuading one thing at one time and another at another. The nature of the higher beings, however, is consistently constant to itself, and always preserves the same tenor in its actions. The corporeal parts of the world are not void of power <note>original: "virtute"; here meaning inherent power or efficacy rather than just moral virtue</note>, but the greater, more beautiful, and more perfect they are compared to our bodies, the greater powers and actions they possess. Therefore, each one...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that divine justice considers the collective kinship of all souls rather than just individuals and asserts that evil or deceptive oracles originate from low-level "wicked demons" rather than the gods.</summary>
<keywords>divine justice, kinship of souls, evil demons, theurgy, oracles, divine goodness, Neoplatonism, Iamblichus</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s discourse on divine justice and the order of the cosmos. He is explaining why individual parts of the world (like human beings) might suffer even when the universe as a whole is governed by a perfect and harmonious divine law.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS

...has its powers differing separately and produces various actions. Likewise, by a certain mutual disposition, they can accomplish many more things together. Furthermore, a multiform action descends from the whole parts of the world into the individual particles; it descends quite easily because of the similarity of powers—to the extent, that is, that the lower powers correspond step-by-step to those preceding them, especially where the recipient is further adapted to the agent. 

Therefore, due to the necessities of the body, certain **evils and destructive things** happen to specific parts from the whole. These are indeed beneficial to the wholes and in harmony with the **universal symphony**, even if they are meanwhile harmful to the parts. This happens either because the parts cannot withstand the actions of the wholes, or because of the mixture occurring further down, or the natural weakness inherent in the lowest things, or because the parts are not proportionate to one another. 

Furthermore, beyond the body of the world itself, the very **nature of the world** can do much. For the concord of similar things and the discord of dissimilar things produce many effects. Indeed, the union of many things into one living whole, and the powers thriving in the world—however many and of whatever kind they may be—act one way in the wholes and another in the parts, due to the very weakness of the parts, which are distributed and divided to the furthest extent.

<column-break/>

### ON THE MYSTERIES

S 5

**Friendship** of the whole, as well as love and strife and similar things, are **actions** in the wholes themselves, but in the particles, they are **passions** <note>original: "passiones"; in this philosophical context, this refers to "being acted upon" or receiving an influence, rather than just "emotions."</note>. Likewise, those things which are **Forms** <note>original: "species"; the ideal, divine blueprints of things.</note> and pure **rational principles** <note>original: "rationesque"; the formative laws or 'logoi' that organize matter.</note> in the Divine Intellect, participate in a certain material poverty within the nature of the universe; finally, they reach a state of formlessness in individual things. And those things which are united in the wholes are divided in the particular parts. 

Furthermore, certain particles are destroyed so that the wholes, established according to nature, may be preserved. Some parts are also crushed and pressed, while the wholes from which they arise remain meanwhile free from such suffering. 

When some **sorcerer** <note>original: "ueneficus"; historically meaning a poisoner or a practitioner of "black" magic.</note> brings down a calamity upon someone through his curses, by using certain natural and bodily powers for this purpose, the transmission of such an action comes from above without any choice or wickedness <note>The author argues that the higher cosmic forces are neutral and "blind" in their descent, following natural laws.</note>. Although the evildoer meanwhile uses this transmission, he diverts it to entirely contrary and evil ends. And this transmission and action descending from above is stirred by a certain **compassion** <note>original: "compassione"; here meaning "cosmic sympathy" or the natural resonance between different parts of the universe.</note> because of the similarity of lower things to the experiences of their opposites. The sorcerer, however, through his own choice, draws what has been transmitted toward evil, beyond what is just; yet the transmission itself remains consistent with the single **harmony of the world**.

<summary>Iamblichus explains that while the universe is governed by a harmonious whole, individual parts may suffer due to their inherent weakness or the misuse of natural forces by sorcerers. He clarifies that evil arises from the "descending" and "fragmented" nature of the lower world, not from the divine intellect itself.</summary>

<keywords>Cosmic Harmony, Divine Intellect, Universal Sympathy, Sorcery, Whole vs. Part, Neoplatonism, Natural Law, Passions</keywords>
