<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of the divine order. He is addressing Porphyry's critique regarding why the gods or spirits seem to facilitate "immoral" acts like illicit sexual unions, explaining that the fault lies in human abuse of cosmic forces rather than the gods themselves.</meta>

<page-num>282</page-num>
### IAMBLICHUS
<meta>catchword: mutually,</meta>

...brings it about that things which are most distant nevertheless work together. If someone, meanwhile, observing this, attempts to unfairly draw certain parts or powers of the whole into other parts, thereby causing harm, the cause of such damage is not the universe, but human abuse. This abuse transgresses the order of the universe and perverts its legitimate laws. Therefore, the gods do not implant evils in us; rather, it is the natures and bodies that depend on the divine that do so. And yet, even these do not send down evil outflows to the lowest depths, but rather good ones that are beneficial to the world. Those who receive them, however, transform these gifts through their own mixture and perversity, so that they receive what was given differently, under contrary conditions. 

After this, you ask why the higher natures <note>referring to the gods or celestial beings</note> do not hear the one who invokes them unless he is pure, yet they themselves meanwhile, when besought, aid illicit sexual unions <note>original: "concubitus"</note> and are not ashamed to incite their worshippers to shameful lusts. When, by hidden causes, we are incited to a union that is not "legitimate"—that is to say, according to human laws—perhaps it is "legitimate" according to cosmic laws, especially when a necessary human birth results from it. Now, the instinct toward <term>Venus</term> <note>original: "Venerem"; the power of attraction, procreation, and the planetary influence of Venus</note> in the universe tends toward friendship and harmony; but in the individual parts, it becomes more vehement because these, due to their own temperament, are all too easily and <insert>mutually</insert>

<column-break/>

<page-num>283</page-num>
### ON THE MYSTERIES

mutually affected, and they "feel with" <note>original: "compatiantur"; from the concept of *sympatheia*, the interconnectedness of all parts of the cosmos</note> the whole. Finally, that which descends from heaven for a good purpose is here often transferred to an evil one. The world is a single living being in which the parts, although distant in location, are nevertheless drawn toward one another because of their single nature. 

And yet, the very reconciling force of the world, and the common cause of the mixing of all things, draws the parts toward each other by its own nature. This kind of drawing and appetite can be increased by a certain <term>art</term> <note>referring to theurgy or natural magic, which manipulates these hidden sympathies</note> beyond the natural order. This reconciling force infused into all things is good, and is everywhere the cause of fulfillment, communion, harmony, mutual love, and the cosmic union which exists and occurs by the command of the governing power. However, in the parts—due to their distance from each other and from the wholes, and because of their weak and needy nature—it brings about a mutual coming together through "shared feeling." From this, a sharper appetite is born in many. 

Thus, Art, discovering this force of "binding" <note>original: "coaguli"; a substance that curdles or binds things together</note> scattered everywhere in nature, contracts it from all sides, collects it together, and directs it toward a single point. In this way, it takes the appetite for a necessary union—which is naturally moderate—and makes it unbridled through its own mechanisms, while it also applies matter especially conducive to procreation and mixes together different natural powers from all sides, of which...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that "evil" actions like illicit lust result from humans perverting good, cosmic forces of harmony (Venus) through theurgical art or personal weakness. He argues the cosmos is a single living organism where celestial gifts are often corrupted by the recipient's lower nature.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, De Mysteriis, sympathy, cosmic law, Venus, theurgy, sexual ethics, divine providence, natural magic</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's explanation of why the gods are not responsible for human immorality. He argues that while the cosmic order is inherently good, individual beings and earthly materials can be manipulated or misused by lesser spirits or through human error.</meta>

<page-num>284</page-num>
->**IAMBLICHUS**<-

...governs by mixture at its own discretion and completes the unions intended for procreation. Furthermore, since there are powers in herbs and stones—some capable of dissolving and others of binding—and since these powers are much greater in larger things and higher natures, the drive toward intercourse can certainly be intensified by applying such things. Indeed, through these very means, love can be ignited and attractions formed without any specialized artifice. We have discovered that wicked demons <note>original: "dæmones improbos"; in Neoplatonism, these are lesser spirits associated with the material world who can influence human emotions</note> possess immense power over procreation and over earthly and human affairs. Therefore, it should not seem surprising that the fire for illicit intercourse is frequently kindled in us by them. Certain souls can also contribute to this same end: both those still living in this body, which possess a wonderful power of attraction, and also those souls already released from the body but still wrapped in a turbid and moist spirit <note>original: "spiritu turbido humidóque"; refers to the "astral body" or pneumatic vehicle of a soul that remains heavy with material desires after death</note>. These souls still wander around the places of procreation and thus commit such acts. Therefore, let no one say that either the gods or the demons who follow them are the causes of illegitimate love and intercourse; rather, the causes are either our own depraved natural instincts, the art of sorcery, evil demons, or souls that are not yet purified.

### **On sacrifices: where they derive their power, and what they contribute.**

The question is asked: by what reasoning are sacrifices both suitable to the gods above <meta>catchword: above</meta>

---

<page-num>285</page-num>
->**ON THE MYSTERIES**<-

and beneficial to men, and what role do they play in the world? Porphyry <note>A student of Plotinus and critic of some ritual practices whom Iamblichus is answering here</note> says that not only the prophets, but also the <term>hypophetas</term> <note>original: "hypophetas"; from the Greek, meaning those who interpret or announce the prophecies received from others</note>—that is, all the priests who deliver a prophecy received from others—ought to abstain from animal flesh, lest the gods be contaminated by the vapors exhaling from them. However, a common saying is contrary to this opinion: namely, that the gods are greatly soothed by the scent <note>original: "nidoribus"; specifically the smell of burning fat or meat from a sacrifice</note> of animals as if by a bait, and are thus captured. Iamblichus condemns both views: namely, that the gods are either infected by the smells of meat or ensnared and enticed by them. The World Soul <note>original: "Anima mundi"</note> and the celestial beings, by the very fact that they are present to their own bodies, neither suffer passions from them nor are they distracted from their intellectual contemplations. Individual souls, however, suffer both. He would err greatly who attributed similar defects to the higher souls; for the condition of universal and particular things, or of principles and effects, is not equal. Indeed, for us to feed upon bodies that were once joined to a soul undoubtedly brings a burden and pollution upon us; it creates a proclivity in the soul through which it may melt and succumb to pleasures, and it generates many other diseases in the soul. But the vapor exhaling from animals and the sacred incense-offering <note>original: "suffumigatio"</note> when rightly performed, [is suitable] to the gods...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that illicit lust stems from evil demons or unpurified souls rather than the gods, and begins a defense of animal sacrifice against Porphyry’s critiques.</summary>
<keywords>demons, procreation, sorcery, astral body, Porphyry, sacrifice, ritual purity, World Soul, animal consumption</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s argument that the gods are transcendent and unaffected by the material world. He is specifically addressing the misconception that ritual practices or earthly substances could somehow "soil" or "influence" the divine nature, leading into a discussion on the true purpose of sacrifice.</meta>

<page-num>286</page-num>
->**IAMBLICHUS**<-

...and nothing hinders mundane and universal causes. For the soul does not contain and order the higher beings toward itself, but is contained by them and coordinated with the whole as well as with the gods. Therefore, by their command, it can be harmoniously adapted and congruent with the higher things. Thus, the religious abstain from animals <note>original: "animalibus"; this refers to the practice of vegetarianism in certain philosophical and priestly traditions to maintain ritual purity</note>, yet not because the gods could be defiled by the vapors of animals. For the power of the gods, before any material thing can touch it, disperses it even without contact. Not only does this power destroy and erase corporeal things before they come near, but even the celestial bodies themselves do not take in any material element. They neither admit anything foreign nor lose anything of their own; indeed, the vapors of earthly things, having barely progressed five stadia <note>A stadium was an ancient Greek unit of distance; five stadia is approximately 3,000 feet or 0.6 miles. This reflects the ancient belief that the atmosphere was very thin and limited.</note>, flow back down. Nor do they infect or nourish the immaterial and rotating body <note>original: "immateriale uolubileq; corpus"; referring to the celestial spheres or the "fifth element" (aether) which moves in circles</note>, which has no capacity for change within itself, possesses nothing contrary to itself, and is not moved toward or away from the center, but must either rest or revolve. Therefore, the bodies below the moon, since they are composed of clashing powers and motions, and are entirely subject to change—moving both up and down—have nothing in common with heaven, neither in nature nor in... <note>original catchword: "tem," the beginning of "potestatem" on the next page</note>

---

<page-num>287</page-num>
**ON THE MYSTERIES.**

...power, nor in action. Thus, no vapor from them nor anything else reaches heaven, being entirely separated from them. It is right then that those things which are unborn <note>original: "ingenita"; referring to eternal, uncreated divine beings who exist outside of time and change</note> take no change from general things. Is it possible, then, that the nature of the gods can be defiled by such vapors, when they repel material vapors with a single blow (so to speak)? Such things are not harmful to the higher beings, but to us. For these divisible and material things, which are akin to us, share something in common in their suffering or action; but against the celestial and divine things, which exist in another category of being, endowed with different natures and powers, and utterly exceeding us, they can neither act nor communicate.

### **What is the reason for sacrifices, and what is their utility?**

Porphyry <note>A Phoenician Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) who questioned many traditional religious practices in his "Letter to Anebo," to which this book is a reply</note> thinks that sacrifices are offered to the gods for this reason: so that we may honor them with such gifts, just as we often do for our elders, and also so that we may return thanks for the goods received from the divine, giving back good things for good things. Iamblichus <note>The author of this work, who defends the spiritual efficacy of ritual</note> says that this explanation is drawn from the common customs of humans toward humans, and does not suit the nature of the gods, which surpasses human ways beyond measure. Furthermore, such explanations and declarations of sacrifices do not demonstrate the greatest efficacy of sacrifices at all. Efficacy...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that the divine and celestial realms are entirely separate from material pollution and earthly vapors. He then begins to critique Porphyry's human-centered view of sacrifice as mere gratitude, suggesting it has a deeper, more powerful spiritual function.</summary>
<keywords>celestial bodies, vapors, ritual purity, sacrifice, Porphyry, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism, theurgy</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s response to Porphyry’s "Letter to Anebo." Having argued that the gods are not harmed or fed by material vapors, Iamblichus now begins to investigate the true mechanism behind ritual efficacy, moving from naturalistic explanations (like universal sympathy) to the higher, supernatural causes of theurgy.</meta>

<page-num>288</page-num>

# IAMBLICHUS

...I say, without which we are not liberated from plague, famine, or scarcity, nor do we obtain timely rains; rather, it is through this [ritual] that we also achieve the purification and perfection of our souls, and freedom from this realm of generation <note>The "realm of generation" refers to the material world characterized by birth, change, and decay, from which the soul seeks to ascend.</note>. Thus, someone following your assignment of reasons will not much approve of these modes of sacrifice, as if you had assigned a worthy cause to their effects. And if by chance he does approve, he will certainly accept them in a secondary place as consequences, and as being adapted in a secondary manner to the primary and most ancient causes of such works. We desire, therefore, the reason why sacrifices have efficacy in accomplishing things, and from where they are adapted to the gods—the primary causes and principles of those things which occur. Someone might say that because the world is one living being <note>original: "animal unum"; a reference to the Neoplatonic "World Soul" where the universe is viewed as a single, interconnected organism.</note>, and has one and the same life everywhere, it moves similar things through the communion of like powers, or even the distance of unlike ones, or certainly a certain aptitude of the agent to the patient... just as this life runs through a single sympathy <note>original: "compassionem"; used here in the technical sense of "cosmic sympathy," where distant parts of the universe react to one another.</note> even from the most remote to the most distant, as if these too were close together. For even between these things themselves, the communion of life is an equal assignment of this kind. Truly—

<column-break/>

<page-num>289</page-num>

# DE MYSTERIIS

<sig>T</sig>

—indeed he speaks, and what necessarily follows concerning sacrifices. However, the true mode of sacrifices is not shown in this way. For the essence of the gods itself is placed neither in nature, nor in natural necessities, by which obligation it might be excited by natural passions, or moved by powers extended through all nature; rather, outside of these things, it is determined according to itself, having nothing in common with them either in essence, power, action, or passion. Some add to the previous assignment a certain congruence in lower things with the higher, and therefore contributing to the efficacy of sacrifices; for instance, in the crocodile, the number six <term>hexagonarius numerus: the number six, which was sacred to the Sun in certain Egyptian and Pythagorean systems because of the crocodile's perceived solar nature</term> agrees with the Sun. Again, in the dog and the baboon <term>cynocephalo: literally "dog-head," referring to the baboon sacred to Thoth/Hermes, believed to have a physical or "physical reason" connection to the Moon's phases</term>, certain physical reasons and powers are common reasons relating to the Moon. Likewise, in other things, there are certain material species—just as in sacred animals, colors, figures, and forms suit one of the higher beings—or some limb of an animal, such as the heart of a rooster <note>The heart of a rooster was often used in ancient divination and ritual because the rooster was considered a solar animal that "heralds" the light.</note>. And other similar natural things employed in sacrifices are the causes of wonderful works. From the above assignment of causes, the divine cause is not assigned—which is entirely supernatural—nor is it bound by natural vows...

<meta>catchword: indeed</meta>

<summary>Iamblichus discusses the efficacy of sacrifices, rejecting purely naturalistic explanations like "cosmic sympathy" in favor of a higher, supernatural divine cause. He examines how certain animals and numbers (like the baboon or the number six) are traditionally linked to celestial bodies but argues these are only secondary to the true power of theurgy.</summary>

<keywords>theurgy, sacrifice, cosmic sympathy, purification, Neoplatonism, World Soul, sacred animals, solar symbolism, lunar symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of ritual efficacy. He moves from discussing the physical elements of sacrifice to explaining how ritual acts act as a bridge between material nature and the higher, divine hierarchy of causes.</meta>

<page-num>290</page-num>

# IAMBLICHUS

...is moved, but he assigns a certain natural power contained within matter and bodies. Therefore, it is excited by corporeal motions and material things, and in turn it is calmed—naturally, I say—and these things occur together among those things that concern nature. If indeed any such result follows from the sacrifices, it is adapted to the preceding causes as a "con-cause" <note>original: "concausa"; a secondary or contributing cause that is necessary for an effect but not its primary source.</note> without which the work might not be accomplished. It is better, therefore, to say that the marvelous effects of sacrifices proceed as if from a primary cause through a certain friendship <term>amicitia; an affinity or spiritual attraction between different levels of reality</term>, adaptation, and a conciliating disposition that links the creators <term>opifices; the demiurgic or "craftsman" powers that shape the world</term> to their works, and parents to their offspring. When, therefore, following and being led by this common principle, we take some animal <note>original: "animal"; here meaning any living being or ensouled creature.</note>, or something else animate and born from the earth, which sincerely and exactly preserves the will and purpose of its creator, then through such a medium we properly move the creative cause that is set over this specific mixture of matter. Since, indeed, there are many such causes—some of which are intimately present without an intermediary, such as demons; while others are ordered higher than these, such as the divine causes in their proper sequence, and one certain most ancient cause presides over all of these—it follows that in a perfect sacrifice, all these causes are moved in their proper order. According to the
->order<-

<column-break/>

<page-num>291</page-num>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

order which each cause has been allotted, they are familiarly adapted and (so to speak) appropriated to the sacrifice by a kindred condition. But if the sacrifice should be imperfect, it ascends only to a certain level but does not transcend further. Hence, many people believe that sacrifices are made only to "good demons"; many others think they are for the lowest powers of the gods; others still think they are for the mundane or earthly powers of demons or gods. These people are describing only a part of the reality of sacrifices, for they are ignorant of the total power of these rites and of all the "goods" <note>The "goods" (bona) refer to the divine blessings or energies that descend through the hierarchy of being.</note> which extend themselves from the divine realm into everything. We, however, admit all these views and encompass them all at once. Natural things—those which are mutually moved as if within a single living being <note>Referring to the *Anima Mundi* or World Soul, which connects all physical things through a shared life force.</note> according to their aptitude, sympathy <term>compassio; a technical term for the resonance or "feeling-together" of distant parts of the cosmos</term>, or reaction—we accept as being simply subject to, following, and serving the effective cause that appears in the sacrifices. As for the demons and the divine powers surrounding the earth and the material world, we accept them as the ones set over our own order as the most immediate supervisors. But we judge that the most powerful causes of truly effective sacrifices belong to the most perfect and creative powers of the universe. Because those highest creative powers embrace all the lower causes within themselves in an orderly fashion...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that sacrifice works through a hierarchy of causes, from natural "sympathy" to the highest creative gods. He argues that while rituals may involve earthly powers or demons, a perfect sacrifice engages the entire divine chain of command.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, sacrifice, hierarchy of causes, cosmic sympathy, demons, creators, demiurge, ritual efficacy</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of theurgy against the rationalist critiques of Porphyry. The previous section established that ritual acts (sacrifices) are not mere human gestures but are linked to the hierarchy of divine causes. Here, Iamblichus argues that sacrifices do not "feed" the gods or demons through physical vapors, but rather align the human soul with the higher order of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>

# IAMBLICHUS

...therefore, we believe that all efficient causes are moved together at once by a single stroke: since in a perfect sacrifice, the causes following in order are moved together with the highest causes and the creators <term>opificibus; the demiurgic or "crafting" powers of the cosmos</term>. Then from all of them a common utility proceeds into the whole of generation: sometimes through cities, peoples, and all kinds of nations, or greater or smaller boundaries than these; sometimes, however, through households or each individual man, good things occur, with the gods above bestowing with an abundantly beneficial will. 

They do not exhibit their distribution through passion <note>original: "passione"; meaning a reactive emotion or physical sensation</note>, but judge with an impassible mind that gifts must be given according to propriety, familiarity, and kinship, while a certain single friendship <term>amicitia; a spiritual affinity or sympathetic resonance</term> which contains all things accomplishes this binding through a certain ineffable communion.

These things are certainly truer, and participate more in the essence and power of the gods, than what you suspect—namely, that the gods above are primarily enticed by the savory smells <note>original: "nidoribus"; the "nidor" or fatty steam rising from a burnt sacrifice</note> of animals in sacrifice. For if demons are enveloped in any body—which some believe is nourished by sacrifices—that body is certainly immutable, impassible, and lucid, and not lacking in anything, so much so that nothing flows out from it, and nothing is necessary <meta>catchword: to flow in (sit)</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>193</page-num>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

to flow in. And yet even if by chance it were necessary, why is it necessary for it to flow in through sacrifices, when the world and the air within it have an inexhaustible exhalation from earthly things spread all around? Now indeed, if the bodies of demons were to waste away <note>original: "effluant"; literally to flow out or diminish</note>, sacrifices would not restore them in an equal portion, nor would they preserve the quantity and similar proportion between the natural excess and deficiency for the demons. 

Finally, since the craftsman <term>faber; the demiurge or creator of the material world</term> of the world provided abundant and ready nourishment for all earthly and marine animals, did he desert those beings superior to man in the matter of nourishment? And when he established food for other living things, which is provided abundantly by their own daily workings, why did he want for demons alone an external food to be provided by humans? And yet if we should happen to neglect nutrition of this kind, the bodies of the higher beings would hunger and perish. Consequently, if we nourished the demons, we would also be their creators, and we would be superior to them.

But the matter is quite the opposite: For they themselves nourish us, and superior things everywhere nourish the inferior; thus celestial things nourish terrestrial things. Indeed, the soul is perfected and nourished by the intellect <term>intellectus; the divine Mind or Nous</term>, and the soul in turn perfects nature. Furthermore, the offering of sacrifices through fire consumes matter...

<summary>Iamblichus refutes the idea that sacrifices physically nourish divine beings, arguing instead that ritual acts facilitate a spiritual "friendship" and that the higher always sustains the lower, never the reverse.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, Sacrifice, Demons, Divine Providence, Hierarchy of Being, Demiurge, Intellect</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s response to Porphyry’s doubts about the nature of sacrifice. Here, Iamblichus argues that the fire of the altar is a purifying force that transforms material offerings into an immaterial state, mirroring how divine fire elevates the human soul toward the gods.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS, ON THE MYSTERIES

<sig>T 4</sig>

and [the fire] completely assimilates it to itself, it does not present itself as similar to matter, but leads it back to the divine, celestial, and immaterial fire, nor does it in any way decline downward toward matter and generation <term>generationem; the world of physical birth, decay, and change</term>. For if the exhalation of vapors from sacrifices were meant to be a material nourishment, it would be necessary for the matter to remain intact; for in that way, a more abundant influx would come to those intended to receive it. 

But now, all the matter is burned and consumed, and is completely transformed into the very purity and subtlety of fire, which signifies the exact opposite of what you yourself were saying. For the higher beings are free from passion <term>passionis expertes; meaning they are not affected or moved by external, physical stimuli</term>, seeing as it pleases them for matter to be dispersed and consumed through fire, and they make us strangers to passions, making whatever is within us like the gods. 

Just as fire makes all hard and opaque things similar to subtle and bright bodies, so too it elevates us upward through sacrifices; through the consumption of the offerings by fire, it raises us to the fire of the gods, just as fire raises fire and brings heavy and hard things upward toward the divine and celestial. You suggest a bodily vehicle for the daemons <note>original: "dæmonibus"; in Neoplatonism, these are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily evil spirits</note>, yet it exists neither from matter, nor from the elements, nor from any body known to us. Therefore, it cannot be made into nourishment for daemons from an elemental 

<column-break/>

essence, as such a thing would be entirely foreign to them. But just as the gods divide matter with lightning-fire <note>original: "igne fulmineo"; referring to the celestial fire associated with Zeus or the highest creative powers</note> and separate from matter all those things that are indeed immaterial by essence but bound to matter—making the impassible out of the passible—so too our ritual fire, imitating the action of the divine fire, destroys whatever material it finds in the sacrifice. It purifies the things brought to it and releases them from the bonds of matter; because of this purity of nature, it makes them fit for communion with the gods. 

In the same way, it absolves us from the bonds of generation <note>the cycle of physical life and death</note>, assimilates us to the gods, makes us fit for the friendship of the gods <term>amicitiam; a spiritual resonance or harmony between the human and divine</term>, and raises our material nature to an immaterial nature. This general reasoning regarding sacrifices is, I believe, sufficient for the clever, who are able to use this common rule to sagaciously investigate the specific nature of every individual sacrifice. 

Nevertheless, indulging those of slower wit, we shall add certain clearer specifics to these points, bringing them into the open. Indeed, the best principle for all these matters is that which declares the context of sacrifices as being linked in a consequential order to the hierarchy of the gods. We posit that some gods are material and others immaterial. The material gods are those who contain matter within themselves and adorn it. The immaterial gods, however, are those who are entirely and utterly separated from matter.

<meta>catchword: essence</meta>

<summary>Iamblichus explains that sacrificial fire is a purifying tool of transmutation that releases the soul from material bonds, and he begins a distinction between material (cosmic) and immaterial (hyper-cosmic) gods.</summary>
<keywords>sacrifice, fire, transmutation, purification, daemons, material gods, immaterial gods, generation, Neoplatonism, Iamblichus</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of theurgy, specifically addressing why material sacrifices are necessary for some gods but not others. He transitions from discussing the purifying nature of fire to the classification of gods based on their relationship with matter.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS

<page-num>196</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

Therefore, according to the **priestly art** <term>sacerdotiam artem; often called theurgy, the practice of rituals intended to invoke the presence of or unite with the gods</term>, it is fitting to begin sacrifices with the material gods; for the ascent to the immaterial gods is not otherwise open to us. These material gods, therefore, have a certain communion with matter, insofar as they preside over it and dwell within it. These same gods also rule over everything that occurs within the realm of matter, such as division, striking, rebounding, change, birth, decay, and all material bodies. 

If anyone, therefore, wishes to worship these gods rightly, they must surely offer a form of worship according to their nature and their sovereignty—I mean offering **material worship** to material beings. For in this way, we shall be led into a proper intimacy with them, as whole beings to whole beings through whole things, and we shall offer to them a kinship in worship that is suitable to their rank. 

Therefore, bodies deprived of life, the slaughter of animals, and the manifold destruction and change of bodies—and indeed the casting away of previous matter—is fitting for these material gods. This is not for their own sake, but for the sake of the matter over which they preside. For although they are separated from matter to the highest degree, they are nevertheless simultaneously present to it; and even if they contain matter within an immaterial power, they still exist alongside it. 

Furthermore, things that are governed are not foreign to their governors, and similarly, things that are adorned are not foreign to their adorners.

<meta>catchword: adorners. Furthermore</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>197</page-num>
<header>ABOUT THE MYSTERIES</header>

Furthermore, to those who use them, the things they provide are easily adapted as instruments in proper proportions. For this reason, it is foreign to the **immaterial gods** to employ matter in sacrifices, but it is entirely fitting for all the material gods. 

In addition to these points, it is worthwhile to consider the **dual state of our life**. Sometimes we become entirely soul, positioned outside the body and elevated by the mind; we raise ourselves through the sublime heights together with all the immaterial gods. At other times, however, we are bound by the earthly body and the "smoke" of the body—that is, we are encompassed by matter. 

Similarly, there will be a twofold method of divine worship. One will be simple, incorporeal, and pure from all **generation** <term>generatione; the cycle of physical birth, change, and decay</term>, fitting for pure souls. The other, however, is full of bodies and entirely material action, suitable for souls that are not yet pure and not yet cleansed from all the effects of physical birth. 

Therefore, there are two species of sacrifices. The purest sacrifices belong to men who are highly purified; this indeed "rarely happens to one, or to very few," as **Heraclitus** <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for his doctrine of change and his elitist view of wisdom</note> says. But material sacrifices, and those involving change, are for those still occupied with the body...

<sig>T 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: occupied</meta>

<summary>Iamblichus explains that because humans exist in a dual state (sometimes spiritual, sometimes physical), worship must also be twofold: immaterial for pure souls and material for those still bound to the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, material gods, immaterial gods, sacrifice, Heraclitus, purification, dualism of the soul, generation</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of the "priestly art" or theurgy. Having established that material gods require material worship, he now argues that humans, who are still bound to physical bodies, cannot leap directly to purely intellectual worship without first purifying their physical state through appropriate rites.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS

<page-num>298</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

...seek. Therefore, for those states <note>The Latin *civitatibus* literally means cities, but in this Neoplatonic context, it refers to the "polity" or organized condition of the soul or human community</note> not yet purified from their generative lot and bodily companionship, unless you offer sacrifices of this kind, you will fail to obtain both immaterial and material goods. For the immaterial gods cannot receive such material offerings, yet for the material gods, people do not offer what is proper and fitting. Added to this is the fact that everyone ought to take care of their own essence according to what it is, not according to what it is not. One should not, therefore, transgress this proper measure and the nature of the worshiper. I judge that a constitution properly fitted between the humans who perform the care and the powers that are cared for will choose a mode of worship suitable to itself.

For a constitution of that kind, if tempered immaterially, chooses an immaterial way, insofar as it joins itself by purely incorporeal powers to purely incorporeal beings. However, a constitution of that kind which is joined to bodies in a corporeal way chooses a similar worship for the essences presiding over bodies. 

Often, for the necessary use of the body, we vow and perform something toward the gods who govern the body and the **good daemons** <term>dæmones; here referring to intermediary spirits or guardians, not "demons" in the modern sense of evil spirits</term>, specifically when we strive to purge the body of certain stains and old filth, or to release it from diseases and bring health, or to remove heaviness and...

<meta>catchword: sluggishness</meta>

---

<page-num>299</page-num>
<header>CONCERNING THE MYSTERIES</header>

...sluggishness, and to bring lightness and efficacy, or to provide some other good for the body. At such times, therefore, we will not treat the body rightly through some intellectual and incorporeal reasoning. For the body is not so designed by nature as to be cared for in those ways; rather, when it obtains what is kindred to it, then the body is cared for and purged by bodies. 

Therefore, the rite of sacrificing will also necessarily be corporeal for such a use; in this way, it will purge the superfluities of the body, supply what is lacking according to necessity, and lead whatever is disordered back to proportion and order. 

We often sacrifice asking the higher beings for things adapted to human life—specifically those things necessary for food, or pertaining to those things we desire for the sake of the body. What, then, will come to us from the gods themselves, who are entirely segregated from all human generation, that might contribute to driving away poverty or sterility, or bring us abundance, or lead to other necessities of life? Nothing, truly. For those who are absolved from all things do not handle such gifts. But if someone should say that those immaterial gods comprehend all material gods within themselves, and embrace their gifts according to one primary cause...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that because humans are physical beings, they must use material sacrifices to interact with the gods who govern the body and the physical world. He maintains that purely intellectual worship is insufficient for physical needs like health and sustenance.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, material sacrifice, purification, daemons, bodily health, Neoplatonism, ritual order</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s discussion on the different levels of divinity and the corresponding types of worship. He previously explained that because the gods exist in a hierarchy—ranging from the purely immaterial to those who oversee the material world—humanity must utilize different forms of theurgy (ritual action) to reach them.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<meta>catchword: turalia (naturalia)</meta>

One would certainly say that a certain abundance of divine gift descends from that higher realm to the lower. However, it must not be permitted for anyone to say that those who handle these matters perform actions that are closely bound to human life and its activities. For the administration of the sorts of things that exist here in the material world is divided and particular, and it is carried out with a certain turning toward these things; it is not entirely separated from bodies, nor can it admit a pure and unmixed governance or oversight. 

Therefore, the mode of sacrificing that is suitable for such matters is one that has commerce with bodies and **generation** <term>the cycle of birth, decay, and physical change in the material world</term>, rather than one that is entirely separated from matter and the body. For he who is entirely pure transcends such material concerns and holds no proportion or relation to them. But he who makes use of bodies and their powers is most closely related to all such things; he is capable of providing the prosperous things of life, turning away the adversities that threaten us, and securing a balanced and moderate climate for the human race. 

The vast multitude of mankind is subject to universal nature; they are governed by natural powers and lean downward toward the works of nature. They fulfill the dispensation of **fate** <note>In this context, Fate refers to the rigid, deterministic laws of the physical and astrological world which the soul seeks to transcend</note> and accept the order of fated works; they exercise practical reason only in regard to na-

---

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

-tural things. Very few, relying on a supernatural power of the mind, depart from nature and betake themselves to a separate and unmixed **intellect** <term>the *nous*, or higher divine mind that perceives eternal truths directly without the need for physical senses</term>, whereby they rise above natural powers. 

Some, however, hold a middle ground between pure intellect and nature. Of these, some follow each in turn, while others lead a life that is a mixture of both; others still are released from the lower things and transfer themselves to the better. 

Therefore, those who are governed by universal nature—living a life enslaved to their own physical nature and enjoying natural powers—will practice a worship suitable to nature and consistent with bodies moved by nature. For this purpose, they choose specific locations, qualities, the atmosphere of the air, the powers of matter, bodies, and the qualities and habits of bodies, as well as the appropriate movements and changes of those things involved in **generation**, along with similar elements both in the other parts of divine worship and in the ritual of sacrifice. 

But those who live by the mind alone, freed from the bonds of nature, will meditate upon an intellectual and bodiless rule of worship in every part of their religion. Those who lead a middle life between intellect and nature will likewise follow a middle way of religion,

<vocab>Iamblichus, On the Mysteries, sacrificing, nature, intellect, fate, religion, body, matter</vocab>

<summary>Iamblichus categorizes humanity into three groups—those bound to nature, those who live by pure intellect, and those in between—arguing that each group requires a different form of religious ritual (theurgy) to connect with the divine.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, hierarchy of souls, material sacrifice, fate vs intellect, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism, ritual purity</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s discussion on the hierarchy of divine beings and how human worship must mirror these levels. He is explaining that because humans exist in a "middle" state—between the purely spiritual and the purely physical—our religious rites must adapt accordingly to reach different classes of gods.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS

<page-num>302</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

And according to the differences of this "middle life," different people will pursue modes of worship through middle means. For they will either deal with both types of worship in turn, or they will lean from one toward the other, or they will touch upon the lower things so that through them they may also attain the better, believing they cannot otherwise reach the more sublime heights. 

In the genus of divine essences and powers, some indeed have the soul and nature subject to them, serving their creative works <term>original: opificiis, referring to the demiurgic or world-building activities of the gods</term> to the extent that the gods themselves wish. Others, however, are entirely separated from soul and nature—I mean not only from the worldly and **generative** <term>the cycle of physical birth and change</term> soul, but even from the divine soul and its nature. Finally, others hold the middle ground and reconcile the communion between these extremes, either through one indissoluble bond, or through an abundant tradition passed down from the greater powers, or through the unimpeded reception of the lesser ones, or through a bound consensus of both. 

When we worship the gods who rule over soul and nature, it is not out of place to offer natural powers to them, and to consecrate to such gods the very bodies that are governed by nature. For all the works of nature both obey them and contribute something to their administration. But

<meta>catchword: quando (when)</meta>

---

<page-num>303</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

when we desire to venerate the gods who are far removed and gathered in their own unity, we must worship them with honors that are entirely released from matter. For intellectual <note>In Platonic philosophy, "intellectual" (intellectualia) refers to the realm of pure, immaterial thought or "Nous," far above human reasoning.</note> gifts are owed to these: an incorporeal life, perfect virtue, consummate wisdom, and the specific duties belonging to them. 

However, for the "middle" gods—the leaders of middle goods—middle offerings are appropriate, just as we have said. Starting then from another principle—namely, from the world, the mundane gods, and the distribution of the four elements in the world—we find an easy access to the truth of sacrifices through the common lot assigned according to the measures of the elements, and from the circumference revolving in order around its centers. 

For if we are in the world, and are contained within it as parts—first because we are generated by it, and are perfected by its total powers, and are composed of its four elements, and possess a certain portion of life and nature obtained from it—we certainly must not overlook the world and the **mundane orders** <note>The "mundane orders" (mundanos ordines) are the structural hierarchies of the physical cosmos, including the planets and elements.</note> in our divine worship. Let us therefore posit that around every part of the world there is a certain body that we can see, and that there are moreover incorporeal powers distributed in order within those bodies; thus, the law of sacred rites joins like to like...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that worship must be tailored to the specific rank of the god: material offerings for gods of nature, and purely "intellectual" or spiritual offerings for the highest, most unified deities. He argues that since humans are part of the physical world, we cannot ignore the rites associated with the cosmos and its elements.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, hierarchy of gods, mundane gods, four elements, intellectual gifts, soul and nature, Neoplatonism, ritual sacrifice</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of the hierarchical nature of religious worship. He argues that because the divine realm is structured in levels—from the highest "supermundane" gods to the lower "mundane" spirits—the rituals humans perform must be equally diverse and complete to be effective.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS

<page-num>304</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

properly distributes [all things], and proceeds from the highest to the lowest through all the intermediate stages: applying bodies to bodily things and incorporeal to incorporeal things—namely, rendering to each its own proper share. Nevertheless, if a religious person attains a **supermundane** divinity <note>original: "supermundanū numen," referring to gods who exist entirely beyond the physical universe</note>—though this happens very rarely—this person alone, being set apart from bodies and matter, pursues such a mode of worship because he is united with that supermundane power and the supermundane gods. 

However, that which happens to one person, and then only with great difficulty at the very peak of the priesthood, ought not be established as a common rule for everyone, nor for those at the beginning or middle stages of their religious life. For these people must perform a bodily worship in some manner. Therefore, anyone skilled in the priesthood <note>theurgy or the practice of divine rituals</note> will admit that it is necessary to offer to the higher beings a worship that is neither partial nor imperfect, but rather fitting and whole. 

Since, therefore, before the gods are present and descend to the earth, all the powers that lie beneath them are stirred and precede them like forerunners and ceremonial processions: surely he who does not distribute to all what is fitting for each, nor honors each with their specific proper honor, will not attain the presence of the gods themselves. But he who renders all the divinities favorable to himself by offering to each one pleasing and most si-

<column-break/>

<page-num>305</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

milar <note>original: "similima," completing the word "similima" from the previous column</note> gifts, this person is certainly not frustrated in the outcome, but receives the divine choir with a complete and full presence. 

Since these things are so, the rite of religion should not be simple or composed of only a few elements, but must be multiform and consisting of every **harmony**, established from all the things of the world (as we have said). For if the being who is called upon in the priesthood and moves toward us were something simple and belonged to only one order, then a simple mode of sacrifice would suffice. But in truth, no one knows how great a multitude of powers is usually stirred up when the gods descend and move, except for those priests who have already experienced it in the work itself. 

Therefore, only these priests know what constitutes the perfect fullness of worship, and how much the outcome frustrates us if even a few things are omitted. Just as in a **harmony** <note>a musical metaphor for the cosmic order</note> if one string is broken, the whole suddenly sounds dissonant: so if one divinity is passed over in the ritual, the common religion itself does not reach its desired end, for while this one part is missing, the sacred work lacks its proper structure and integrity. When the divine powers are about to descend visibly to our senses, a manifest loss comes to us if we pass over any of those higher beings.

<summary>Iamblichus explains that successful religious ritual (theurgy) must be comprehensive and harmonious, honoring every level of the divine hierarchy rather than just the highest gods. He uses the metaphor of a musical instrument, noting that skipping a single divinity is like breaking a string on a lyre, which ruins the entire harmony.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, divine hierarchy, supermundane, mundane, religious ritual, cosmic harmony, priesthood, sacrifice</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that religious ritual must be complete and hierarchical. If a practitioner honors some divine powers but neglects others, the entire sacred operation is compromised, much like a broken link in a chain.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS
### ON THE MYSTERIES

<page-num>306</page-num>

...[to leave them] unhonored. Similarly, when divine powers arrive secretly during a sacred rite, it is dangerous if you offer honors to some, yet do not honor another according to his dignity and rank. For he who cheats any power of its proper honor throws the entire work of religion <note>original: "religionis opus," referring here to theurgical practice or ritual</note> into confusion by dissolving a single, unified arrangement. It is not, as some might think, that only the act of receiving the divinity is then imperfect; rather, the structure of the whole ritual perishes. 

But what of this? Does not the very summit of holiness return to the highest principle of the entire multitude of divinities—to the **One**—and in that One, does it not worship all divine essences and powers together? Undoubtedly. However, a summit of this kind arrives quite late in one’s development and is granted to very few; we should consider ourselves fortunate if such a thing happens to us at the very end of our lives. 

The present discussion, however, does not set down laws for such a rare man, for he is superior to the law. Instead, it enacts these sacred laws for those who still require some law. The multitude of divinities <term>numina: the various ranks of gods, spirits, and angels that inhabit the cosmos</term> is like a world unto itself, having many ranks that harmonize together into a single order. In the same way, sacrifices must reflect the order of these divinities, likewise harmonizing into a single order, so that each part properly corresponds to each divinity.

<column-break/>

<page-num>307</page-num>

Similarly, those things that are around us and within us are manifold; they should not respond to divine causes from just one side, but from every part of our being, and thus ascend to the supreme leaders. 

Religious observance, therefore, performs various actions within and around us. It either perfects us as if by growth, or it cuts away what is unnecessary, or cleanses us, or brings order to things lacking it, or separates us further from mortal error. Finally, it brings all things into a state of familiarity and friendship with the heavenly powers. 

When both sides meet together into one—the divine causes from above and the equal human preparations from below—then perfect religion accomplishes all things and brings back great benefits from the sacrifices. Indeed, the exuberance of power in the highest causes of the universe is such that, just as it surpasses lower things in all other matters, it also greatly surpasses them in the very abundance of its presence. The greatest causes of all are most present to all things, so much so that the power of each single cause is always present everywhere as a whole, standing apart from any impediment. By this reasoning, the first principles of all things shine down even upon the lowest <note>original: "subrutilant," suggesting a glowing or shimmering light from above</note>; and immaterial principles [interact] immaterially with matter...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that ritual must be holistic and respect the divine hierarchy, as neglecting any part of the spiritual order ruins the entire rite. He notes that while a rare few may reach the "One" directly, most must work through the structured ranks of the gods to achieve spiritual perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, Hierarchy of Being, Ritual Order, Divine Presence, Sacrifice, The One, Spiritual Purification, Iamblichus</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's defense of theurgy (ritual practice). Having previously argued that ritual must be complete and follow a divine hierarchy, he now addresses the role of physical matter—often seen as "low" or "evil" in other philosophical schools—explaining how it can be purified and prepared to host divine presence.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS
### ON THE MYSTERIES

<page-num>308</page-num>

...are present in material things. No one should wonder if we say there is a kind of matter that is pure and divine. For since matter itself was made by the Craftsman and Father of all <note>original: "opifice patreq; omniū," referring to the Demiurge or creator deity</note>, it has deservedly acquired a certain perfection of its own, suitable for receiving the gods. Furthermore, nothing prevents higher beings from spreading their light to lower ones. Nor do they allow matter to be devoid of higher things. Therefore, whatever part of matter is perfect, pure, and god-like <term>deiform: having the form or quality of a god</term> is not unfit for the reception of the gods. 

For since it was necessary that even earthly things should in no way be without a share in divine communion, the earth itself also received a certain divine portion or lot within itself, sufficient for containing the gods. This sacred wisdom, having contemplated these things, and thus carefully finding suitable receptacles <note>original: "susceptacula"</note> for each of the gods according to their harmony, often combines into one: stones, herbs, animals, spices, and other similar things that are sacred, perfect, and god-like; and from all these, it then constructs a receptacle that is pure and whole. For it is not right to detest all matter, but only that which is alien to the gods. It is proper to choose matter that is specific to them, as it is able to harmonize 

<column-break/>

<page-num>309</page-num>

and to contribute to the buildings of the gods, the foundations of statues, and the works of sacrifices. For otherwise, no possession or portion of the divine can reach earthly places and the men living here, unless such a foundation has first been made. We must believe the secret discourses <note>original: "arcanis sermonibus," likely referring to the Orphic or Chaldean oracles</note> which testify that, through blessed visions <note>original: "beata spectacula"</note>, a certain kind of matter was handed down from the gods. This matter is therefore known through those who handed it down. 

A sacrifice of such matter rouses the gods to show themselves, and immediately calls them to be understood; it captures their presence and demonstrates it perfectly. One can learn these same things from the very distribution of divinity which is carried out according to specific places, and from the governance that is divided among individual things—whatever ranks, whether greater or lesser, have obtained such lots here and there. 

For it is clear that for the gods who are the rulers of certain places, a sacrifice made from the things that are born in those same places will be most appropriate; and the things governed will please the governors, and whatever pertains to them will be welcome. Works are always especially pleasing to their creators; and those who first brought something into being find such things most acceptable in return. Whether, therefore, [it be] ani[mals]...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that matter, being a creation of the divine Father, can be purified into "receptacles" (stones, herbs, or statues) to host the gods during ritual. He explains that specific material offerings are effective because they correspond to the specific divine governors who oversee those materials and locations.</summary>

<keywords>theurgy, matter, Demiurge, receptacles, sacred stones, sacrifice, divine distribution, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's explanation of how physical objects—animals, plants, and minerals—serve as links between the human and divine realms. Following the discussion on page 309 about matter being a suitable receptacle for the gods, he now details how specific beings and ritual acts establish a "prefecture" or spiritual jurisdiction that allows for communion with higher powers.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS

<page-num>310</page-num>

...certain animals, whether plants or other things which are seen to be dispensed by the higher powers, have at the same time obtained a prefecture <term>prefectura: a spiritual jurisdiction or authority</term> under them, and they reconcile for us an indivisible communion with those above. Therefore, some of these, insofar as they are preserved and kept, increase the familiar property of those holding them toward the gods—specifically, all those which, by virtue of remaining whole, preserve the power of communion between men and gods. Such are certain animals among the Egyptians, and the sacred man everywhere. Others, however, when sacrificed and burnt, make that familiarity clearer, namely all those which effect a resolution into the principles of the first elements, making them more akin to the causes of the higher powers and, according to the rite, more sacred. For everywhere, the more that the specific property and familiarity are perfected, the more perfect are the goods that descend to us from there. Nor are these merely human inventions, nor have they gained authority through human customs; rather, God himself, rightly called upon among the sacred rites, is the author of such laws, around whom many gods and angels <note>original: "dij angeliqʒ"</note> revolve, and under whom, throughout whatever nations on earth, some divinity <term>numen: a divine power or presiding spirit</term> has obtained a common prefecture and province. Thence, a specific divinity is assigned to each temple. Likewise, of the sacred rites which we perform to the gods, some god is the spectator 
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>311</page-num>

### ON THE MYSTERIES

and governor. Of those rites directed to the angels, an angel [is the governor]; those to demons <term>daemon: here referring to intermediary spirits, not necessarily evil</term>, a demon; and in other cases in the same manner, a perfected being has obtained a kindred prefecture according to its own kind. Since, therefore, we perform sacrifices under the divinities who are observers and authors of the rites, we ought to revere the laws of divine holiness and the legitimate sacrifices; and for that reason, we should hope in these things, because we sacrifice under the leadership of the powers and the gods. And meanwhile, we must take the utmost care not to offer anything either unworthy of the higher powers or foreign to them. Finally, we advise this in summary: that we should diligently look around at each thing that is within us and around us, and those things in the world, as well as the gods, angels, and demons distributed among the nations; and in this way, we should similarly offer a friendly sacrifice to each. For only by this method is the holiness and religion worthy of each divinity preserved. A vow, or adoration and prayer, completes every work of sacrifice and contributes above all to the whole of religion, establishing a sacred and indissoluble communion for souls with the gods. Of this [prayer], there are indeed three kinds: The first gathers us together and provides us with contact and knowledge of divinity. The second binds a harmonious communion to the divine,

<summary>Iamblichus explains that spiritual hierarchy is mirrored in physical reality, where specific animals, plants, and rituals are governed by divine "prefects." He emphasizes that prayer and correctly performed sacrifices create an indissoluble bond between the human soul and the gods.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, spiritual hierarchy, Egyptian animals, sacrifice, numen, angels, demons, prayer, religious law, divine communion</keywords>

<meta>This translation continues Iamblichus’s discourse on theurgy—the ritual practice of connecting with the divine. On the previous page, he discussed how physical objects (animals and plants) serve as links between the human and divine. Here, he moves into the psychological and spiritual mechanics of "adoration" (ritual prayer) and how it prepares the soul for union with the gods.</meta>

### IAMBLICHUS

<page-num>312</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHUS</header>

and it calls forth for us gifts sent down from the divine, which, even before we speak or understand, bring our every work to completion. The third stage <note>Iamblichus is describing three stages of prayer: introductory, bonding, and unifying</note> accomplishes an ineffable unity, places all authority in the gods, and perfectly establishes and confirms our soul within them. Now indeed, within these three limits by which all divine things are measured, harmony forges a friendship for us with the gods and brings to us a threefold sacred utility from them: one pertains to illumination, another to the shared execution of works, and another to a perfect fullness derived from the divine fire. Likewise, adoration <term>adoratio: translated from the Greek *proseuche*, meaning ritual prayer or invocation</term> and its power sometimes precede the sacrifices themselves, sometimes are woven into the middle of the sacrifices, and sometimes complete the final goal of the sacrificing. Indeed, no work in the sacred rites is ever performed without the supplications of adoration. Finally, adoration practiced over a very long time nourishes and perfects our intellect; it makes the soul much larger and more capable of receiving divine things; it reveals the divine to humans; it accustoms the soul to grasping the splendors of divine light; and it gradually perfects those things within us that allow us to touch the divine powers, until it leads us to the summit. Furthermore, it gradually withdraws the human habits of our thought
<meta>catchword: latim (gradually)</meta>

---

<page-num>313</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES</header>

gradually, and inserts divine habits: it produces persuasion, communion, and friendship that is perpetual and far from any interruption. It increases divine love once again, and it ignites and elevates that which is divine within the soul. It purges whatever is contrary and adverse in the mind, and from the ethereal and splendid spirit itself which is poured around the soul, it shakes off whatever leans toward generation <note>the material realm of birth, decay, and constant change</note>; it even provides good hope and perfects faith regarding the light. Finally (to speak briefly), for those who pray frequently, the power of adoration makes them the familiar companions and disciples of the gods. Since adoration is of such a kind and has such a connection with sacrifices, it is clear that the goal of sacrifices participates in a contact with the Creator <term>opificis: referring to the Demiurge, the divine craftsman of the universe</term>; for indeed, through these works, the soul is adapted to the gods. And so, the benefit of sacrifice is as great as that which is sent from the creative causes to the human race. From this, the power of adoration appears once again as reductive <note>the power to lead the soul back to its divine source</note>, perfecting, and fulfilling—showing how it is effective and a maker of union, and how it holds a common bond from the gods. It reveals how adoration and sacrifice mutually confirm each other and confer power upon one another: these are the admirable effects of religion. From here again, it appears—

<sig>V 5</sig>

<summary>This passage describes the transformative power of theurgical prayer (adoration), explaining how it purifies the soul's "ethereal vehicle" and gradually elevates a human from the material world to a state of friendship and union with the gods. Iamblichus argues that prayer and sacrifice are codependent rituals that allow the soul to participate in the creative power of the Demiurge.</summary>

<keywords>theurgy, prayer, sacrifice, spiritual purification, divine union, Iamblichus, Neoplatonism, Demiurge, illumination, ethereal spirit</keywords>

<page-num>314</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHVS</header>

...that the priestly discipline and operation possess a harmony in their parts no less than a living animal has in its limbs, and that no part of it can complete its work without the cooperation of the whole.

### *Concerning the sacrifices of animals.*

Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234–305 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a letter questioning the validity of ritual magic, to which this book is a response.</note> is uncertain as to why the laws of sacred rites require that one who is to behold divine things must not touch a dead body at that time. Nevertheless, invocations and the fulfillment of divine matters are quite often performed through the bodies of dead animals. Iamblichus resolves the matter thus: The prophets command that the beholder of divine things must not touch a fallen <note>original: "caduca," referring to a body that has died naturally or is decaying</note> body that has not been consecrated; however, they permit touching what is consecrated. Furthermore, they forbid even looking upon human bodies which the soul has already abandoned. For a certain trace, or image, or manifestation of divine life is extinguished in the human body through death. But no religious law forbids touching the dead bodies of other animals, since they did not share in a more divine life while living. Furthermore, insofar as we rise up toward the immaterial gods, it is beneficial not to have touched such a body; but insofar as we strive to approach the material gods, it will often be beneficial to touch

<meta>catchword: them</meta>

---

<page-num>315</page-num>
<header>DE MYSTERIIS.</header>

them. For we are accustomed to calling upon gods of this sort through animals—namely, those who preside over such animals, especially those who govern them closely and by a kind of kindred condition. Likewise, for humans caught in matter, bodies deprived of life inflict a certain stain—namely, the non-living to the living, the filthy to the pure, and that which lacks a state of being to that which possesses it, and the dead to that which has the potential for dying, because of a certain common relationship between these things. But to a spirit <note>original: "dæmoni". In this context, a daemon is a middle-tier spiritual being, not an evil entity.</note> who is incorporeal and entirely beyond suffering, such bodies do no harm; for he stands far above all perishable things.

### *Concerning divination through animals.*

When divination <term>uaticiniũ: specifically refers to prophecy or foretelling the future through signs</term> is performed through the bodies of sacred animals, such as hawks <note>original: "asturum," likely referring to the goshawk or similar birds of prey used in augury</note>, it must not be thought that the gods themselves descend to these things—that is, the universal providers attending to a divided, particular, material, and proportional care for individual animals. Rather, it is spirits <note>original: "dæmones"</note>, and these quite divided ones, assigned to each individual species of animal, who have obtained the immediate care and presidency over them. For these have obtained a leadership that is not entirely sufficient on its own and is not free from matter; therefore, they are accustomed to appropriately touch such instruments. To this end, therefore...

<summary>This page addresses the apparent contradiction of using dead animals in rituals while forbidding contact with corpses, explaining that consecrated animals serve as links to material deities. It also clarifies that divination through animals is governed by intermediary spirits (daemons) rather than the supreme, universal gods.</summary>

### IAMBLICHVS

<page-num>316</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHVS</header>

...so that we may obtain divination of this kind from demons through animals, it is necessary to prepare for them such a seat through which they might be able to move among men and interact with us. This seat must be incorporeal, so that the incorporeal demons may be present to us through it. This seat will therefore be the soul of such animals, now released from the body. Insofar as it is also in some way separated from the body, it is compatible with the demons; yet it is also compatible with us because of a certain kinship of life. Therefore, as a medium, it serves the presiding demon in suggesting a prophecy to a human, in declaring the demon’s commands, and it reconciles us with the demon. Truly, the soul using this kind of divination does not only hear or receive the prophecy, but it also contributes no small amount from itself toward its execution regarding the works to be done. For it is moved and cooperates, and at the same time foresees through a certain necessity of shared sensation <note>original: "compatientem neceſſitatem," referring to the concept of "sympathy," where all parts of the universe resonate with one another.</note>. But divination of this kind stands furthest from the divine truth; it is concerned with small and brief matters which are placed in the divided realm of nature, and whatever things are now carried along within the process of generation, exhibiting motions from themselves to those capable of receiving them,

<meta>catchword: and passions-</meta>

---

<page-num>317</page-num>
<header>CONCERNING THE MYSTERIES.</header>

and they inflict passions <note>In this context, "passions" refers to being affected by external forces or emotions rather than suffering.</note> in many ways upon those things which are naturally exposed to such resonance. Through this sympathy, therefore, such a prophecy is produced. However, perfect foreknowledge cannot occur through passion. For that which is immutable and pure in the world is what is primarily accustomed to attaining the future. But that which has commerce with the material, the passive, and the dark is overwhelmed by ignorance. Therefore, we judge this artificial machine of prophecy to be neither worthy of the name of legitimate divination, nor to be treated seriously, nor should the one who uses it be believed as if he were a speaker of truth.

->*How those who offer prayers used to threaten the demons.*<-

Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre, a student of Plotinus who questioned the morality of "forcing" gods or spirits to act through ritual.</note> relates that priests were accustomed to use certain violent threats against the higher powers: such as, "unless you do this," or conversely, "if you do something else, I will shatter the heavens," or "I will disclose the secrets of Isis," or "I will divulge the arcane mystery hidden in the abyss," or "I will stop the *baris*" <note>A sacred Egyptian boat used in religious processions and associated with the sun god's journey.</note>—that is, a certain boat sacred among the Egyptians—"or I will scatter the limbs of Osiris to Typhon" <note>Typhon is the Greek name for Set, the Egyptian god of chaos who murdered Osiris and scattered his limbs.</note>, and other similar things. Iamblichus adds that those who offer execrations <note>ritual curses or forceful invocations</note> were not accustomed to direct such threats toward the Sun and Moon and other celestial beings, as Porphyry thinks, but there are in the world...

He also begins to address Porphyry's confusion regarding why Egyptian priests appear to threaten the gods during rituals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of theurgical practices—the ritual art of divine communication. Having previously discussed how souls of animals can be used as mediums, Iamblichus now addresses why theurgists (priests) use threats against lower spirits and how these spirits maintain the cosmic order.</meta>

### 318 IAMBLICHVS

...there is a certain class of powers that is highly divided, indistinct, and unreflective; it receives reason or discourse from another and listens, but possesses no intelligence of its own. It distinguishes neither truth from falsehood, nor the possible from the impossible. Thus, when this class is provoked by threats, it is immediately agitated and stunned, for it is naturally moved by vehement words and leads and drags others along through a kind of dull and unstable imagination.

Furthermore, the priest often commands and threatens the worldly spirits <note>original: "numinibus," referring to lower-tier divinities or spirits that govern the physical realm.</note> not through human virtue, but through the divine and ineffable power of the **sacraments** <note>original: "sacramentorum." In the context of theurgy, these are not Christian sacraments but "holy rites" or "divine tokens" (synthemata) that connect the priest to the gods.</note>. The priest is then established within the rank of the superior gods; therefore, he uses commands and threats appropriate to that higher nature. Yet he does not do this as if he were actually going to carry out the threats he has become accustomed to using, but rather to declare how much power he has obtained through his union with the gods—a power gained from the knowledge and possession of those ineffable symbols or seals which we call **sacraments**.

Likewise, the demons are divided by provinces and regions, guarding different parts of the world. Each has such great care for the parts entrusted to them that they cannot tolerate even a single word contrary to that order and dispensation

<column-break/>

->**CONCERNING THE MYSTERIES. 319**<-

to tolerate; for they watchfully guard the perpetual and immutable perseverance of worldly things. They have undertaken this immutable order of theirs because the order of the gods remains immobile within the same state. Therefore, in the state where these demons exist—whether they are ethereal or distributed around the earth—they cannot even bear to hear themselves being assailed by threats.

Consequently, these demons zealously provide for the guardianship of the ineffable and arcane **mysteries**, with such great observance because they understand that the order and dispensation of the universe is primarily contained in mysteries of this kind. To the extent that these words allegorically designate the deities and the worldly orders: for this reason, the parts of the world remain in their order because the beneficial power of **Osiris** <note>The Egyptian god of the afterlife and order; Iamblichus uses Egyptian mythology to explain cosmic stability.</note> remains pure and untouched, and is not mixed with contrary tumult and error. Moreover, the life of all things remains always pure and incorrupt, insofar as the life-giving and beautiful **reasons** <note>original: "rationes," translating the Greek "logoi," the underlying rational principles of nature.</note> which are hidden within **Isis** <note>The Egyptian goddess of nature and magic, here representing the source of the world's generative life-force.</note> persist in that same place. From there, the world is driven, yet these principles do not descend into this body visible to the eyes. But all things remain immobile and simultaneously eternal, belonging to the realm of generation...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that priests use ritual threats against lower spirits not out of human pride, but by invoking divine authority and the cosmic order symbolized by Osiris and Isis. These spirits (demons) lack independent reason and must be directed by the "sacraments" or holy tokens of the theurgist.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, demons, Osiris, Isis, sacraments, cosmic order, spiritual hierarchy, Egyptian mysteries</keywords>
