<meta>This page continues Iamblichus's defense of theurgical practices. He has been explaining why priests use "threats" against certain spirits, clarifying that these threats are not meant for the highest gods but for the lower, worldly spirits who maintain the physical order of the universe.</meta>

[page 340]

->IAMBLICHVS<-

...succession, since no one can ever stop the course of the Sun. Furthermore, they remain intact because those things which are hidden in the **abyss** <note>original: "abyisso." In Neoplatonic thought, this refers to the profound, unmanifest depths of the divine or the primordial foundations of the world.</note>—that is, in the depths—are never revealed. Therefore, it is in this preservation that all things, and indeed the entire universe, find their safety: namely, in the fact that things which are naturally hidden are kept hidden and remain so, and that the most secret essence of the gods never undergoes an opposite fate, nor is it mixed with its opposites. 

I say that the **earthly spirits** <note>original: "circumterranei daemones," literally "spirits who dwell around the earth."</note> cannot bear to hear or even listen to things being handled otherwise, or to have these secrets profaned by words; and for this reason, the use of such "threatening" words possesses a certain power over spirits of this kind. However, no one ever uses such threats against the higher gods, nor are such prayers or entreaties established for them. For this reason, the **Chaldean** <note>The "Chaldeans" refers to the practitioners of the *Chaldean Oracles*, an ancient text of wisdom highly respected by Iamblichus.</note> priests—among whom the pure discourse directed only toward the gods has been judged and decreed—never use threats. The **Egyptians**, however, who mix demonic languages with divine **sacraments** <term>sacraments: here referring to "theurgic rites" or "holy tokens" used to connect with the divine</term>, sometimes turn to these kinds of threats.

### Exposition of the symbols of Egyptian theology.

The Egyptians, imitating the very nature of the universe and the craftsmanship of the gods, also display certain images of mystical and hidden notions through the creation of **symbols**.

<meta>catchword: certain</meta>

---

[page 341]

->ON THE MYSTERIES<-
<sig>X</sig>

Just as nature expresses hidden **reasons** <note>original: "rationes," referring to the *logoi* or the underlying blueprints of creation.</note> in visible forms as if through symbols, and just as the gods explain the truth of **Ideas** <note>Platonic Ideas: the perfect, eternal patterns of which all physical things are mere copies.</note> through manifest images, so too do the Egyptians. For since they perceive that all higher things delight in the similarity of lower things, and moreover, since they desire to be filled with goodness from the higher powers (to the extent that they can imitate them), they rightly offer a mode of action to the higher powers that is suitable to them. They do this by inserting hidden mysteries into manifest symbols. 

In interpreting these symbols, disregard the literal words and focus on the inner meaning. For example, when they name **mud** <note>original: "lutum," referring to the primeval silt or slime of the Nile, often used in Egyptian creation myths.</note> in their sacred rites, understand this to mean the body of the world and **matter**, as well as the generative power inserted into it, which is, as it were, agitated and flowing along with it. It can also represent the principal cause and the foundation placed beneath the **elements** and their elemental powers. 

Since this is the case, God himself is the cause of generation and of all the powers of nature that are implanted in the elements; yet, because he stands above all these things, he is himself **immaterial**, indivisible, immovable, and unbegotten. Being entirely from himself and entirely within himself, he clearly precedes all these things and leads them, embracing all things within himself. And from the fact that he embraces...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that while Chaldean priests never threaten the gods, Egyptian priests use "threats" to command lower spirits who guard the world's order. He then begins explaining how Egyptian symbols, like "mud," represent the material foundation of the universe and the generative power of God.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian theology, Chaldean priests, symbolism, matter, divine essence, theurgy, Neoplatonism, the abyss</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of Egyptian priestly symbols. On the preceding pages, he discussed why theurgy uses "threats" toward lower spirits and began explaining how divine symbols (like the lotus and the sun) represent the relationship between the transcendent One and the material world.</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS

[He] embraces all things, and from his own bounty bestows upon worldly things, shining upon all from his own essence. Because he stands above all, he shines forth as one set apart from all worldly things, proceeding alone by himself through the heights. This is confirmed by the symbol that follows, where God is presented sitting upon an aquatic lotus—which is a tree. This signifies that God, in his sovereignty, transcends the worldly **mud** <note>original: "lutum." In Neoplatonic thought, "mud" or "slime" represents the heavy, chaotic, and drowning nature of material existence.</note>, and does not touch it in his governing, but instead exercises a rule that is entirely intellectual and **empyrean** <term>empyrean: relating to the highest, most pure heaven, often associated with divine fire and pure intellect</term>. For all parts of the lotus are round—both the fruit and the leaves—by which is signified the circular motion of the **Mind** <note>original: "mentis" (the Greek *Nous*). For Iamblichus, the Divine Mind moves in a perfect circle, always remaining the same and returning to its source.</note> through the same things and in the same manner. God himself stands above this very action, residing within himself, surpassing such a sovereignty; he is holy, venerable, and entirely at rest within himself—which is indeed signified by the act of sitting. Furthermore, where they present God as the **pilot of a ship** <note>original: "navis gubernatorem." A common ancient metaphor for divine providence steering the universe like a captain steers a vessel.</note>, they signify his sovereignty as the governor of the world. For just as a pilot, though separate from the ship and the rudder, moves and rules and directs the ship from everywhere in one easy moment, so God himself directs the world from above—namely, from the primary principles of nature—providing the fundamental causes of all motions. Be-
<meta>catchword: cause</meta>

---

# DE MYSTERIIS.

-cause all the parts of the heavens, the celestial creatures, the entire motion of the universe, and time itself (according to which the world moves)—in short, all things contained within the whole—receive the powers descending from the **Sun**. Some of these powers are mixed within the things themselves, while others transcend such mixing. For this reason, the symbolic method of representation introduces these things, describing the Sun in words as being figured according to the **celestial animals** <note>original: "animalia coelestia." This refers to the signs of the Zodiac (from the Greek *zodiakos*, meaning "circle of animals").</note> and changing its forms according to the seasons and hours. Meanwhile, however, this symbol demonstrates the Sun's immutable and total transmission of power throughout the whole world. But since those things which look up to God are carried in different ways around his indivisible transmission—and they themselves, according to the diversity of their own natures and motions, receive multiform powers from the Sun—the symbolic tradition therefore maintains that one God enters through a multitude of gifts, and that one divine power is present through many forms of power. On this account, he says that the One himself remains the same; however, he asserts that the changes of forms and transformations result from those who look up to him. Therefore, he says that the Sun is changed according to the "animals" and the hours, as if such variations happened around God himself, whereas they actually follow the different **recipients** <term>recipients: or "receptacles" (Latin: *susceptacula*); the idea that physical things receive divine influence only according to their own capacity and nature</term> of God.

### *On the divine names.*

Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre was a student of Plotinus who wrote a skeptical letter to an Egyptian priest named Anebo, questioning the logic of ritual magic. This book is Iamblichus's response.</note> asks why priests use certain names that signify nothing. Iamblichus responds that all names of this kind signify something to the gods, even though the meanings of some are unknown to us. However, there are certain known names whose interpretation we have received by divine inspiration; yet, in general, the manner of their significance in these matters is **ineffable** <term>ineffable: too great or extreme to be expressed or described in words</term>. It is not according to human imaginations, nor according to rational discourse, but according to the **Intellect** <note>original: "intellectum" (the Greek *Nous*). In Neoplatonism, this is the part of the soul that can perceive divine truth directly without needing to "think through" steps of logic.</note> which is divine within us—or rather, in a simpler and more excellent way, according to the Intellect united with the gods. Therefore, one must remove from divine names all human inventions, rational arguments, and natural comparisons of the voice itself that are born from things found in nature. Just as the symbolic character of a divine likeness is intellectual and divine in itself, so we must assume and accept this very character in all these names. Because the meaning here

---

# DE MYSTERIIS

is unknown to us, this is the very thing about it that is most to be venerated: for it is too excellent to be divided into our common knowledge. Now indeed, in those divine names for which we have received an explanation from the gods, we have the entire knowledge of their essence, power, and divine order compressed within the name itself; and furthermore, we preserve the mystical, ineffable, and complete image of the gods within the soul. Through these names, we lift the soul up to the gods, and once it is elevated, we join it to them as much as our power allows.

Next, the question is asked why we prefer "barbarian" <note>original: "barbara." To the Greeks, "barbarian" simply meant anyone who did not speak Greek, specifically the Egyptians and Assyrians whom Iamblichus considered more ancient and closer to the gods.</note> names over others among divine things. This is because the gods have approved the entire language of sacred nations, such as the Egyptians and Assyrians, as being most fitting for sacred rites. For this reason, we believe that common expressions and ways of speaking should be applied to the power and speech that is kindred to the gods, especially because this way of speaking existed as the most ancient and primary form. Those who first received the names from the gods mixed them with their own tongue, tempering them with a voice that was familiar and consistent to them, and thus they handed them down to us to be kept forever. We, in turn, must properly preserve the very rule of this tradition...

<summary>Iamblichus explains that "meaningless" ritual names have divine power because they are recognized by the gods, even if humans do not understand them. He defends the use of ancient Egyptian and Assyrian "barbarian" languages in prayer as being more sacred and ancient than Greek.</summary>
<keywords>divine names, barbarian names, Egyptian rituals, Assyrian language, Porphyry, theurgy, sacred tradition, Intellect (Nous)</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of theurgical language and ritual names. He is responding to Porphyry’s skepticism regarding why "barbarian" (non-Greek) names are used in prayers and whether the meaning or the specific sound of the word is what matters to the gods.</meta>

<page-num>346</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHVS</header>

...we. For if anything else is fitting for the gods, it is that which is eternal and immutable, which is most akin to the gods above all else. Among these points, Porphyry says: "The person who hears the sound of divine names looks toward their meanings; therefore, the concept itself, which remains the same everywhere, is sufficient, regardless of the form of the speech." Iamblichus responds in this manner: It is not as you think. For if names were established by human agreement and convention, it would make no difference to exchange one for another. But if they were adapted to the very nature of things, then those names which are most congruent to that nature are also most dear to the gods. From this, it clearly appears that the speech of the sacred nations <note>Iamblichus refers to the Egyptians and Assyrians as "sacred nations" because he believed their cultures and languages were closer to the original divine revelations.</note> is reasonably preferred over others. For names do not preserve exactly the same mind <note>original: "mentem" - referring here to the inner essence or "thought" of the word</note> when translated into another language; rather, there are certain properties unique to individual nations that cannot be signified to other nations through sound alone. Added to this is the fact that even if we could interpret the properties of the meanings through another language, they still do not preserve the same **power** <term>power: "potentia" in Latin, referring to the inherent ritual efficacy or "charge" a word carries</term> when they are changed. Furthermore, **barbarian names** <note>In the ancient world, "barbarian" simply meant non-Greek, specifically referring here to Egyptian and Chaldean/Babylonian languages.</note> possess great **emphasis**, which is to say an efficacy in how they insinuate and demonstrate their meaning; they also possess a concise brevity. Indeed, the least amount of 

<meta>catchword: ambiguity</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>347</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES.</header>

ambiguity, variety, and multitude of words is, for all these reasons, most fitting for the higher beings. Therefore, set aside those suspicions that fall away from the truth—those by which you suspect that the Egyptian language is used only because an Egyptian is present, or because the one who invokes the gods happens to use the Egyptian tongue. Rather, consider that since the Egyptians were the first of all people to be allotted the presence and participation of the gods, the gods especially approve of the one who invokes them when they are called upon according to the Egyptian rite. Nor should it be said that these "barbarian" and obscure divine names are merely the devices of jugglers or sorcerers. For in what way could those things which are most closely joined to the gods—and which join us to them, and which possess powers almost equal to the higher beings—be "fantastic fictions," without which no work in the sacred rites can be accomplished? Nor are these sorts of coverings and veils created from our own human passions and reputations to be attributed to the divine. For we do not start from the things that we ourselves suffer; on the contrary, starting from the properties of the gods themselves, we attribute to them the utterances that are suitable to them according to their nature. Nor do we hold notions about the divine that are contrary to how the divine truly is, but rather we speak according to how its nature exists, and...

<vocab>Porphyry, Iamblichus, On the Mysteries, barbarian names, Egyptian language, divine names, theurgy</vocab>

<summary>Iamblichus argues that sacred "barbarian" names (specifically Egyptian) are not arbitrary human inventions but are naturally linked to the gods and lose their ritual power if translated. He defends the use of these ancient, obscure names against the charge that they are merely the tools of fraudulent magicians.</summary>

<keywords>theurgy, sacred language, Egyptian ritual, barbarian names, Porphyry, divine invocation, translation of names, ritual efficacy</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of ancient ritual practice. Having established that the specific sounds and origins of "barbarian" (Egyptian and Assyrian) names possess inherent divine power, Iamblichus now contrasts the stability of these ancient traditions with the "fickle" nature of Greek innovation. He then transitions to answering Porphyry’s specific questions regarding the nature of the "First Cause" in Egyptian theology.</meta>

<page-num>348</page-num>
<header>IAMBLICHVS</header>

Just as those who first handed down to us the laws concerning the sanctity of religion achieved the truth about it, so we must persevere in them. For if anything else in sacred laws and rituals is pleasing to God, stability itself is what pleases Him most and is especially fitting. It is therefore necessary to keep the ancient rites of worship always untouched, as things that are sacred; one must neither take anything away nor add anything from elsewhere. For this <note>the act of changing rites</note> has recently become the reason why all names and prayers have become weakened—because, through this very transgression and the desire for novelty, they are constantly being changed and never cease to be altered. For the Greeks are by nature eager for new things; they are carried away headlong everywhere like a ship lacking ballast, possessing no stability, and they do not preserve what they have received from others. Instead, they quickly discard it, and because of their instability, they tend to transform everything for the sake of a new way of speaking. But the **Barbarians** <note>original: "Barbari." In the ancient world, this term referred to any non-Greek speaker. Here, Iamblichus uses it with great respect to describe the Egyptians and Assyrians, whom he considers the keepers of original, uncorrupted wisdom.</note>, just as they are serious and firm in their character, so they persevere firmly in the same words. Indeed, because of this stability, they are friends to the gods, and they offer prayers that are accepted by them—prayers which it is never lawful for anyone to change for any reason.

<meta>catchword: Opinion</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>349</page-num>
<header>DE MYSTERIIS</header>

<margin>The opinion of the Egyptians regarding God and the gods.</margin>
Shortly thereafter, Porphyry <note>Porphyry was a student of Plotinus and a critic of ritual practice; this entire book is Iamblichus’s response to a letter of inquiry Porphyry wrote.</note> asks: what do the Egyptians believe the **first cause** to be? Is it **Intellect** <term>Intellect: "intellectum," referring to the divine Mind or "Nous", the first principle of being</term>, or something above Intellect? Furthermore, is it one thing alone, or is it combined with something else, or others? Again, is it incorporeal, or perhaps corporeal? Moreover, is it the same as the **Maker of the world** <term>Maker: "opifice," often called the Demiurge, the divine craftsman who shapes the physical universe</term>, or is it superior to the Maker? And did all things flow from one source or from many? Finally, do they believe the first foundation of all things is matter entirely without form, or some kind of bodies? And is matter itself uncreated or created? To these things, Iamblichus replied thus: I will tell you first the reason why the sacred and ancient writers of the Egyptians have felt differently about these matters, and why, moreover, the wise men of this current age do not speak of them with the same reasoning. For since there are many essences in the universe, and they differ from one another in many ways, it is only right that many principles have been handed down concerning them, holding different ranks; and different priests have handed down different things—indeed, principles that are whole or universal, as Seleucus relates. **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius." This refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage to whom thousands of mystical and philosophical works were attributed.</note> himself handed down these things in twenty thousand volumes, or as Meneteus records, he handed them down in thirty

<sig>X 5</sig>

<summary>Iamblichus argues that religious rituals and names must remain unchanged to retain their power, contrasting Greek "fickleness" with "barbarian" stability. He then begins addressing Porphyry's questions about the Egyptian concept of the First Cause and the vast corpus of Hermetic literature.</summary>

# IAMBLICHVS

...thousands, and likewise six thousand five hundred and twenty-five <note>original: "sex millibus & quingētis atque uigintiquinque." Iamblichus refers here to the vast number of books attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, symbolizing the totality of Egyptian wisdom.</note>, and in them he perfectly demonstrated all things. While various other ancient writers have introduced different specific principles for particular levels of existence, it is necessary to declare the truth about all of them briefly, and first, to answer what you asked first. 

The first god is before being, and is alone; he is the father of the first god <note>Neoplatonists often distinguished between a "First One" (completely beyond reach) and a "Second One" or "Demiurge" who begins the process of creation.</note>, whom he begets while remaining in his own solitary unity. That first principle is **beyond the intelligible** <term>superintelligibile: that which exists above the level of the mind or intellect</term>, and is the "exemplar" <note>the original pattern or blueprint</note> of the second god. This first principle is called the father of himself, the son of himself, the "one-father," and the truly good god. 

For he is the greater, the first, the fountain of all things, and the root of those things which are first understood and which first understand—namely, the **Ideas** <term>idearū: the eternal, divine forms that serve as the templates for the physical world</term>. From this One, the self-sufficient god unfolded himself. Therefore, he is called self-sufficient, his own father, and his own ruler. For he is the principle, the god of gods, a unity derived from the One, the principle of essence existing above essence: for from him comes essence itself, and for this reason he is named the "Father of Essence." He is the being who exists above all being, the principle of all things that can be understood by the mind.

These are the most ancient principles of all, which **Mercury** <note>Mercurius: the Latin name for Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage and purported author of the Hermetic corpus.</note> places before the etheric, empyrean, and celestial gods. He composed one hundred books concerning the **empyrean** gods <term>empyreis: relating to the highest, most refined heaven of pure fire</term>, an equal number concerning the **etheric** gods, and a thousand books concerning the **celestial** gods. According to another order,

<column-break/>

# DE MYSTERIIS
### (ON THE MYSTERIES)

he places the god **Emeph** <note>Emeph: an Egyptian deity mentioned in Hermetic texts, often associated with the creative mind or soul of the universe.</note> as the leader before the celestial gods. He says Emeph is the intellect that understands itself and turns its own thoughts back into itself. Before this one, he places a single, indivisible unit, which he calls the "first exemplar" or the "image," and names it **Icthon** <note>likely "Eicton" in other transcriptions; a representation of the first manifested monad.</note>. Within this Icthon is the first thinking being and the first thing thought of, which is worshipped only through silence. 

In addition to these, other leaders preside over the creation of visible things. For the artisan-intellect <note>the Demiurge or world-maker</note>, who is the lord of truth and wisdom, is called **Amun** in the Egyptian language when he proceeds into the hidden world of generation and brings the power of latent reasons into the light. 

However, when he accomplishes all things without falsehood and with artistic skill combined with truth, he is called **Phtha** <note>Ptah: the Egyptian creator god of craftsmen and architects.</note>. The Greeks call this god **Vulcan** <note>the Roman equivalent of Hephaestus</note>, though they consider him only in his capacity as a craftsman. 

Insofar as he is the maker of good things, he is called **Osiris**; and he has other names based on his different powers and actions. There is also among the Egyptians another dominion over the entire set of elements involved in the process of birth and change, and over their powers. Of these powers, four are masculine and four are feminine <note>This refers to the "Ogdoad," a group of eight primordial deities in Egyptian mythology representing the forces of chaos before creation.</note>; they attribute this dominion to the **Sun**. Furthermore, there is another dominion over...

He cites Hermes Trismegistus as the ultimate authority who documented these divine orders in thousands of books.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Egyptian divine hierarchy, moving from the description of the "First God" to the specific ranks of intellects and the creation of the physical world as explained by Hermes Trismegistus (Mercurius).</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS

...all nature which is involved in the process of generation is attributed to the moon. **Marsilio** <note>Marsilio Ficino, the 15th-century scholar who translated this work into Latin and provided these explanatory bridges between the authors.</note>: You may arrange the order of these things according to **Proclus** <note>A later Neoplatonist philosopher who systematized the complex hierarchies of gods and intellects.</note> in this way: First is the **One** itself, which is above all being and above the reach of the mind. Second is the **One-Being**, or the unity of being, in which unity is placed before its own being; this can also be called the **Intelligible Intellect** <term>intellectus intelligibilis: the level of divine reality where thought and the object of thought are the same</term> and the father of itself. Third is the intellect which is both **Intelligible and Intellectual** at once, the united intellect and the first expression of the One, called **Ictho** <note>original: "icthō," a name derived from Egyptian theology used in Neoplatonic hierarchies.</note>. Fourth is the **Intellectual Intellect**, now fully revealed as the leader of the celestial gods, called **Emeph**. Fifth is the **Artisan Intellect** <term>intellectus opifex: the "Demiurge" or craftsman-god who constructs the physical universe</term>, the immediate leader of those who build the world—the gods of the highest fire, the stars, and the heavens. This is perhaps the intellect of the **World Soul**, which is named **Amun**, **Phtha** <note>Ptah, the Egyptian creator-god of craftsmen</note>, **Vulcan**, or **Osiris**. **Iamblichus**: Those who divide the heaven into two, four, twelve, or thirty-six parts <note>referring to the divisions of the zodiac and its decans</note>, or double these numbers, or in any other way, also assign various ranks of rulers to them. Again, they place one leader over all these; and thus the Egyptian treaties on the first principles, proceeding from the highest down to the lowest, descend from the **One** into the **multitude**, which is governed everywhere by a single god. In this system, an indefinite nature is held...

<meta>catchword: under a</meta>

---

# DE MYSTERIIS.

...under a certain defined limit and commandingly grasped, especially by the exalted unity which is the cause of all things. God produced **Matter** through "materiality"—that is, derived from "essentiality"—which the **Artisan**, taking up its vital power, used to make the simple and unchanging spheres. The very last portion of this matter he took to create bodies that are born and die. With these things clearly distinguished, those doubts you say you felt while reading Egyptian books are easily resolved. For those books circulated under the title of **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurij," referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage to whom thousands of Egyptian texts were attributed.</note> contain "Mercurial" <note>Hermetic</note> opinions, even if they often speak in the style of Greek philosophers. For they were translated from the Egyptian language into Greek by men well-versed in philosophy. I would wish, however, that whoever touches upon the first causes of worldly things would also explain the final principles. Likewise, those who discuss the planets, the zodiac, the **decans** <term>decanos: the 36 spirits or stars that rule over ten-degree segments of the zodiac</term>, the **horoscopes**, and the stars as powerful leaders, should hand down the specific distributions of these principles. Accordingly, the doctrines found in the **Salameschiniaka** <note>original: "salaminïacis," referring to ancient Egyptian astrological texts containing tables, omens, and the ritual duties of priests.</note> contain only a very brief part of the "Mercurial" arrangements. Likewise, those things treated concerning the appearance or hiding of stars, or the waxing and waning of the moon, hold the lowest place of assignment among the Egyptians.

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s defense of Egyptian theology against Porphyry’s skepticism. The previous page established a hierarchy of divine intellects (the One, the Intelligible, and the Artisan/Demiurge). Here, Iamblichus explains how the Egyptians distinguish between the physical laws of nature and the higher realm of the soul and intellect.</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS

...possess the power of causes. Indeed, the Egyptians do not believe that all things are merely natural, but they distinguish both intellectual life and animal life from the realm of nature: not only in the world at large, but also within human beings. And since they have placed intellect and reason—existing in their own right—in the position of leadership, they believe that whatever is created is done so according to these higher principles. They assert that the first and most excellent father of all things involved in the process of generation <note>The cycle of birth, growth, and decay in the physical world.</note> is the Artisan <term>opifex: the Creator or Demiurge who fashions the universe according to divine patterns</term>. They also recognize a vital power, both that which exists in the heavens and that which stands above the heavens. Likewise, they place a pure intellect above the world, one that is indivisible within the whole world, and another that is distributed through all the celestial circles. Now, they are not accustomed to contemplating these things with mere words and naked reason; rather, they further advise and teach that our nature must progress to higher and more universal things, rising above fate toward God himself and the Creator of the world—provided that the soul carries no material baggage with it, nor anything else besides the observation of the proper timing <note>referring to astrological or ritual timing</note>. They teach that we achieve this through sacred works <note>Theurgy: ritual acts intended to unite the soul with the divine</note>, and at the same time, these works make it a reality. Mercury <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage and purported author of the Hermetic texts</note> himself also handed down this path to us; but the prophet Bitis <note>An Egyptian priest mentioned in the Hermetic tradition as a translator of sacred texts</note> interpreted it for King Ammon after finding it in the inner sanctuaries of the temple in the Egyptian city of Sais <note>original: "Saym," referring to Sais, an ancient cult center for the goddess Neith</note>, where it was inscribed in sacred letters. This path also

---

<page-num>355</page-num>

# ON THE MYSTERIES.

provided us with the name of God which runs throughout the entire universe. There are also many other ranks of these same beings. Therefore, you do not seem to me to be correct in trying to reduce all Egyptian teachings to purely natural causes. For among them, there are many things involving many different essences; there are also supramundane <term>supermundanae: powers existing beyond or above the physical universe</term> powers, which they have worshipped with especially religious sanctity.

### *On free will, and how we are released from fate.*

After this, Porphyry says that many of the Egyptians also subjected our very free will to the motion of the stars. Iamblichus responds from the Hermetic teachings in this manner: Man has two souls, as the Hermetic writings <note>original: "mercuriales literæ"</note> teach. One soul is from the first Intelligible <term>intelligibili: the realm of pure being and divine thought, accessible only to the mind</term>, and it partakes in the power of the Creator himself. But the other soul is bestowed upon us from the circuit of the heavenly bodies, into which the soul that contemplates divine things creeps. Therefore, the soul descending into us from the worlds <note>referring to the planetary spheres</note> follows the circuits of those worlds. But the soul that comes from the Intelligible realm is present in an intellectual way; it stands above the generative circuit <note>the cycle of reincarnation and physical fate</note>. Through this higher soul, we are both released from fate and we ascend to the Intelligible gods, and we possess a religion that reaches toward eternal things. Therefore, one should not think—as you were doubting—that all things are bound by the unbreakable chains of necessity, which they call Fate.

<summary>Iamblichus argues that Egyptian theology recognizes a divine realm above nature and fate, specifically describing a "dual soul" where one part is subject to the stars and the other is free and divine.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Porphyry, Hermes Trismegistus, Sais, free will, Fate, dual soul, theurgy, Egyptian mysteries, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s explanation of how the human soul relates to Fate and the Divine. The previous page established that while the physical world is governed by the laws of nature, the human soul possesses a higher intellect that allows it to rise above these constraints through divine connection.</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS
## ON THE MYSTERIES.

to be bound together. For the soul has a principle proper to itself, by which it may betake itself to the intelligible <note>The realm of pure thought and eternal truths, as opposed to the physical world.</note>, and depart indeed from those things which are born <note>The world of "generation," where things are born, change, and die.</note>, and approach Being itself and the divine. Nor again have we attributed fate to the gods, whom we worship in temples, sacred rites, and statues as those who release us from fate. The gods themselves dissolve fate. But the lower natures descending from them, which are already implicit in the generation and the body of the world, carry out the decrees of fate. Rightly, therefore, we offer all holiness to the gods, so that they—who alone dominate necessity through intellectual persuasion—may dissolve the evils impending upon us from fate. For not everything is bound within the nature of fate; rather, there is also another principle of the soul, more excellent than both nature and generation, through which we can be joined to the gods, overcome the worldly order, and participate in the eternal life and action of the super-celestial gods. Through this, therefore, we can exempt ourselves from fate. For when the more excellent parts within us are active, and the soul is recalled to its own superior nature, then it is utterly separated from those things which bind it in the cycle of birth, and it ascends from lower things, exchanging one life for another, <margin>seque</margin> <note>original: "seçp", an abbreviation for "seque", meaning "and itself".</note>

<column-break/>

and <insert>the soul</insert> inserts itself into another order, while it completely abandons the former order. What then? Is it possible to free oneself from fate through the revolving gods <note>The planetary or celestial gods whose movements were thought to govern physical destiny.</note>, and at the same time consider these same gods to be the leaders and authors of fates, binding lives deeply with indissoluble chains? Perhaps indeed nothing prevents it from being so. For since the gods embrace many essences and powers within themselves, what prevents there being within them other differences and contradictions that are beyond our estimation? Now it is also permitted to say this: in each of the gods—even those who are visible <note>The stars and planets.</note>—there are certain intelligible principles of essence, through which flight and freedom from the world of change may happen for souls. If, however, someone does not admit two orders of gods for the duty of liberation—namely, the mundane <term>mundanos: gods residing within the physical universe</term> and the super-mundane <term>supermundanos: gods existing beyond the physical universe</term>—at least through the super-mundane ones, liberation will eventually occur. But these things are declared more diligently in the treatise on the gods: which gods especially are the ones who lead us back to the higher realms, and how they recall us, by what powers, and by what reasoning they dissolve fate; and through which specific "returns" accomplished by sacred operation <note>Refers to Theurgy: ritual acts intended to unite the soul with the divine.</note> they act, and what the order of worldly nature is, and in what way the perfected intellectual action rules over this order.

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s response to Porphyry’s "Letter to Anebo." Having just explained that the soul has a principle that can transcend Fate through divine connection, Iamblichus now addresses the specific mechanism of prayer and the nature of the "Personal Daemon" or guardian spirit.</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS

Therefore, that which you brought forward from the verses of Homer—namely, that the gods are "flexible" <note>Iamblichus refers to *Iliad* 9.497: "Even the gods themselves are flexible [στρεπτοί], though their virtue and honor and strength are greater."</note>—we judge to be a wickedness to say. For the holy works of religion have long been defined by pure and intellectual laws, and they dissolve inferior things by a greater order and power; and as we are brought into a better lot, to that extent a departure from inferior things occurs. Nor does anything happen because of this that is contrary to the rule established from the beginning, as if the gods were "bent" because of a sacrifice or worship performed later; rather, from the first descent, God sent souls down on the condition that they might return to him again. Nor do the ascents of souls and their descents conflict with one another. For just as in the intellectual universe, generation <note>In Neoplatonism, "generation" refers to the entire world of physical change, birth, and decay.</note> is correctly connected together with essence—indeed, even this whole—so also in the disposition of souls, the release from generation is in harmony with their care for generation.

->On each person’s own guardian spirit.<- <note>original: "De suo cuiq; dæmone." The term *daemon* here refers to a personal tutelary spirit or "higher self" that guides an individual's life.</note>

Iamblichus responds to these things thus: The discourse concerning the guardian spirit proper to each person is twofold: one is indeed sacred, or deific; the other is technical. The former invokes the guardian spirit from celestial causes; the latter from the cycles

which are involved in generation. The former [sacred] method does not use an exploration of each individual’s birth-chart <note>original: "genituræ." In this context, it refers to the astrological configuration at the moment of birth.</note>, but the latter [technical] explores such things diligently. The former worships and cares for a universal guardian spirit above nature, while the latter cares for a particular one according to nature. Since these things are so, you seem to me to have absurdly transferred a more perfect form of worship to a merely human level, and to have exercised your questions only within this latter category. Furthermore, where you seek or worship a guardian spirit by a natural way only, you seem to have grasped only a certain small part of such a way. For those who skillfully investigate and work in an orderly fashion concerning nature are accustomed to call upon the guardian spirit from the "decans" <term>decans: thirty-six 10-degree segments of the zodiac, each ruled by a specific deity or spirit</term>—that is, from those who act and from the dispensers of influences, from the images of heaven, the stars, the Sun, and the Moon; likewise from the poles and all the elements and the world. You, however, incorrectly selecting a very brief part of these—namely, the lord of the birth-chart itself—introduce questions about this alone. whether a guardian spirit <note>original: "dæmon". In this context, it refers to a tutelary spirit or "higher self" assigned to a person at birth, not an evil entity.</note> exists or not, and whether the lord of the [astrological] figure <term>lord of the figure: the dominant planet in a person's birth chart that was believed to govern their life and character.</term> can be found or perhaps cannot. He asserts, finally, that he will be blessed who, having found the figure of his birth, has escaped the things of fate, having thus looked upon his own guardian spirit. To me, however, these things seem to disagree with one another and to fall short of the truth. For if the guardian spirit is assigned to us by the very figure of our birth, and we know him from there, by what means shall we escape the things of fate through knowledge of a guardian spirit who was himself bound to us by fate? And yet, if we expel necessary things from ourselves through the guardian spirit, as you affirm, how are these things still destined for us through fate together with the guardian spirit?

->*On the judgments of astrologers and the guardian spirit.*<-

Porphyry says that if anyone should know the figure of his birth and the lord of that figure, he would find his own guardian spirit, and through him would be released from the fate of birth. But he adds: it is impossible to know that figure, and to find this spirit, and that the rules of astronomy and astrology are incomprehensible and uncertain, as witnessed also by Chaeremon <note>An Egyptian priest and Stoic philosopher who wrote on the religion of Egypt.</note>. Iamblichus says that the guardian spirit is neither known nor given by that figure and its demonic power; otherwise, he would not release us from the fate of birth. Rather, he is given by a more ancient and higher principle, namely from the superior gods
<meta>catchword: superior gods</meta>

---

<page-num>361</page-num>
<header>ON THE MYSTERIES.</header>
superior gods, and as soon as the soul is inclined toward the world of the senses before conception; and that we are released from fate not so much through the guardian spirit as through the worship of a higher divinity. Truly, not only do those sayings of Porphyry disagree with themselves, but they also disagree with the truth. For the guardian spirit is not entirely assigned to each person by the specific figure of their birth, but this beginning is even more ancient. Likewise, if the guardian spirit were recognized only from this source, he who recognized the guardian spirit of his birth would not for that reason be happy. And who would receive this spirit as a leader to flee the things of fate? If he himself is assigned to fulfill these fated things, you entirely overlook the principal questions concerning the essence and the order of the guardian spirit, and instead you touch upon the final matters—namely, from whence he descends. You say, moreover, that innumerable rules of astrology are circulated, and doubts concerning the birth-figure: from which it appears that knowledge about these things cannot be held. I, however, judge first that the most certain truth about the stars can indeed be held through divine prophecy <note>original: "vaticinium". Iamblichus argues that spiritual insight is more reliable for understanding the stars than mere mathematical calculation.</note>; and that we do not entirely need the counting of rules nor artificial predictions. And I add further that you do not demonstrate that knowledge from there is entirely impossible, so far as it pertains to mathematical science, because diverse

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s refutation of Porphyry’s skepticism regarding astrology and the "Personal Daemon." In the preceding text, Iamblichus began addressing whether a guardian spirit is truly tied to one's birth chart or if it represents a higher power that allows one to transcend fate.</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS

Opinions are bandied about among them, and [he notes] what Chaeremon <note>An Egyptian priest and Stoic philosopher who wrote on the religion of Egypt.</note> and anyone else says in contradiction. For in this way, in every science and art, because of conflicting opinions, we would be forced to say that no science exists. But indeed, everywhere not only do false things disagree among themselves, but the false also disagrees with the true, so that everywhere the truth can be found alongside the false.

Porphyry says that even the rules of astronomy cannot be grasped, let alone those of astrology, and that there are many disagreements within them, and they are attacked by many. Iamblichus responds: Divine prophecy can teach the nature and effect of the stars without the use of [human] art. Astronomy and other arts, once given by the gods, have been confused over a long period by human opinions, and they retain but a tiny portion of divinity and truth in the end. In astronomy there is something of the truth, however small: which is evident through the effects of the stars arising from their measurements in the heavens, just as was predicted regarding the eclipse of the Sun and Moon and the aspects <term>aspects: the geometric relationships between planets that astrologers believe influence earthly events.</term> of the planets. The rules of measurements—the magnitude of distances, progressions, comets, and similar things—observed for a long time among the Chaldeans <note>original: "Chaldęos". Ancient Mesopotamian astronomers and astrologers famous for their long-term celestial observations.</note> and among us, testify that the science of astronomy is true. Porphyry says that the lord of the [astrological] figure <term>lord of the figure: the dominant planet or "ruler" in a person's birth chart believed to govern their life.</term>, or the lords if there be several, is to be

---

# ON THE MYSTERIES

found to be impossible even by the judgment of astronomers and astrologers, yet from that source the personal guardian spirit <note>original: "proprium dæmonem". In Neoplatonism, this is a tutelary spirit assigned to a soul to guide it through its earthly life.</note> must be sought. Iamblichus says that astrologers provide rules—some five, others fewer, others more—for finding and discerning the lords of the figures. I do not say, I repeat, that rules are [successfully] held, found, and discerned, but rather that it is thought so and passed down by them, contrary to Porphyry’s statements. 

What indeed prevents the lord of the figure and the guardian spirit from being found with difficulty through astrology, yet very easily through divine prophecy and sacrifices? Nor should it be thought that the guardian spirit is assigned solely by the lord of the figure. For there are principles of this spirit more numerous and more ancient than that. Nor can we find our personal guardian spirit through human arts, but through divine ones. A personal guardian spirit is not distributed to each person by some particular arrangement of the sky or the elements, but by the whole arrangement of the world, when the soul descends while that world is reigning, and it provides a certain guardian spirit as the executor of the life which the soul has chosen. This spirit reconciles the soul to the body, cares for the life communicated to the body, directs the life proper to the soul, constantly presents the principles for thought, and we perform such things as he leads...

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s discussion on the nature of the "Personal Daemon" (guardian spirit). On the previous page, Iamblichus argued that human sciences like astronomy are fallible because they are human interpretations of divine truths. He now shifts to how a soul receives its specific guardian and how this spirit guides the soul through its physical life until it can be unified with God.</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS

[...brings] into the mind, and he governs us to that extent, until, having been perfected by sacred rites, we exchange our daemon <term>daemon: in this context, a "daimon" is not an evil spirit, but a tutelary or guardian spirit assigned to a person at birth to oversee their destiny.</term> for a divine Lord. Then the daemon, yielding to God, either rests from his labor or assists in the same purpose. A specific lot <term>lot: "sors" in Latin, referring to the specific destiny or "hand" dealt to a soul upon its entry into the world.</term> is assigned to each individual soul while it descends from the whole body of the world, and life—as it then stands in relation to that soul—is guided and fulfilled by a certain daemon. Therefore, it is impossible to judge specific events with certainty, since we cannot grasp the concurrence of all causes, unless we attain this knowledge through divine inspiration.

### Whence the personal daemon comes, what it does, and whether it is unique.

The personal daemon is not assigned from just one specific part of the heavens or from the elements; rather, he is assigned from the whole world, from the manifold life within it, and from the manifold bodies through which the soul passes as it descends into generation <term>generation: the process of a soul descending into the material world to take on a physical body.</term>. A certain lot is specifically discerned and decreed for us, distributed according to our own particular governance. This daemon, in fact, existed in the exemplary realm <note>The "exemplary realm" refers to the world of Platonic Forms or divine archetypes that exist before physical manifestation.</note> before souls fell into generation. Since the soul first received him as its leader, the daemon stands by immediately as the one who fulfills the soul's various lives. This daemon, as I have said, binds the soul to the body as it descends, and he himself cares for the common 

---

# DE MYSTERIIS.

living creature <note>original: "commune animal". This refers to the composite being formed by the union of the soul and the physical body.</note>. He directs the soul's own life and provides the principles of reasoning for whatever we contemplate. We act in such ways as he brings into the mind, and he governs us until, having been purified by sacred works <note>original: "sacris operibus". Iamblichus refers to theurgy, or "god-working" rituals, intended to elevate the soul.</note>, we receive God as our leader in place of the daemon. Hereafter, the daemon yields to God in such a way that he either remains idle in His presence or contributes to the same divine end. 

You then asked whether many daemons are present for each person, with different ones governing different aspects of the human being. To this we respond that the daemon does not preside over only one part of us, but is present to the whole at once and leads the whole, just as he was assigned by all the orders that exist in the world. You conjectured that many daemons are present for us according to the number of our parts. Furthermore, you suggested that even within the body itself there are different daemons—one caring for health, another for beauty, others for a certain condition among these—and that one daemon presides over these equally in the body. Thus, you also thought that one daemon rules the body, another the soul, and another the intellect. It is better, however, to use such a conjecture for this purpose: namely, to recognize that the body, the soul, and the intellect are all governed by the same daemon. Do not, therefore, divide them so that there is one daemon for the body, another for the soul, and another for the intellect. For it is absurd 

<summary>Iamblichus explains that the personal daemon is a singular guardian assigned from the totality of the universe to guide a soul's reason and physical life until it achieves divine union. He refutes the idea that different parts of a person (body, soul, mind) are governed by different spirits, insisting on a unified guidance.</summary>

# IAMBLICHVS

...to think that the living being is one thing, but that the daemon presiding over the living being is multiform <note>Iamblichus is arguing against the idea that our guardian spirit is made of many parts. He believes the governor must be simpler and more unified than the person it rules.</note>. For those things which hold the leadership are everywhere simpler than those they rule. Furthermore, it is even more absurd if many ruling daemons, acting as parts of a daemon, were not fused into one but were separated from each other. You also introduce a contradiction among them, as if some ruling daemons were good and others evil; yet nowhere do evil spirits hold the lot of leadership, nor are they distributed in equal authority or number in opposition to the good. After this, you suspect that the personal daemon is a certain part of the soul—namely, the intellect—and that the man who possesses a wise intellect is *eudaimona* <term>eudaimona: the Greek term εὐδαίμονα, meaning "fortunate" or "happy," literally "having a good daemon."</term>, that is, fortunate. If this were so, there would no longer be another order of being more excellent than man, presiding over man. Instead, among the divided parts of the soul, the higher part would merely rule over the lower ones. Yet where all parts are of the same kind, no part can rule over the others as an absolute master. After this, you introduce the worship of the daemon as if it were directed toward many, partly as if toward two daemons, and partly as if toward three. It is a sacrilege, however, to divide the causes presiding over us, and not to reduce that leadership to one; for unity dominates in all things. Now truly

and he who divides the daemon according to the body and the management of the body reduces its leadership to the narrowest portion. Therefore, it is not fitting to consider religious worship regarding a daemon divided in such a way, since the very opinion upon which that idea is founded wavers in its weakness. Therefore, there is one personal daemon for each of us, a unique governor. It is not right to think of a daemon that is entirely common to all men, nor to imagine one that is common in itself but joined to each person by a specific condition. For the very division according to species and the difference of matter does not admit the community and identity of those things which are incorporeal in themselves.

<margin>Invocation of the daemon.</margin>
You ask then why we are accustomed to call upon the daemon with a certain common invocation among all people. It is because the invocation of daemons acts through the one Lord God, who from the beginning defined for each person their own daemon, and who in the sacrifices reveals to each person their own daemon according to His own will. For always in the order of sacred operations, the lower powers are called upon through the superior deities. Therefore, even among the daemons, there is one certain leader of them who holds the lordship over the process of generation <term>generation: the process of a soul entering the material world and taking on a physical body.</term>, and who sends his own daemon to each...

<summary>Iamblichus argues that the personal daemon must be a single, unified entity that is superior to the human soul, rejecting the idea that it is merely the human intellect or a collection of different spirits. He explains that we call upon these spirits through the authority of a single supreme God who assigns them to us.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Iamblichus’s response to Porphyry regarding the "personal daemon" or guardian spirit. Having previously established that the daemon is an external, superior entity, Iamblichus now moves to explain how this spirit guides the individual toward divine union and defends the reality of theocratic practice against the charge that it is merely a product of human imagination.</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS

...sends down. Therefore, after his own guardian spirit is present to each person, it then reveals the worship suitable to itself, and makes known its own name and its own proper mode of its invocation. And this is the fitting order of daemons: for one order is related to those who invoke it, another proceeds from more ancient causes, and another creates a harmonious cooperation <note>original: "conspirationē"; here referring to a "breathing together" or cosmic sympathy</note> among them, contributing together toward the same end. Do not, therefore, think that divine invocations are like human ones, or compare the unutterable to the utterable, nor compare those things which surpass every limit and every indeterminate mode to human commands and mandates, whether these are defined or completely undefined. For the things that exist among us have nothing in common with the whole genus [of spirits], nor with those things which exceed us according to their entire order and surpass our whole essence and nature. Indeed, from this source, the greatest errors often happen to men: when out of human weakness they are accustomed to reason about the dominations of daemons, and weigh the vast, highly valued, and perfect things by the small, worthless, and divided ones.

### On Happiness.

Do you perhaps suspect that some other way to happiness <note>original: "beatitudinem"</note> lies hidden besides divine worship, and do you ask what that way might specifically be?

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

...be hidden, and you ask what that way might specifically be? But surely if the essence and perfection of all goods is contained in the gods, and the primary power and the primary principle of goods is there, then it is with good reason that we priests alone—and those who have similarly devoted themselves to the higher powers and have legitimately attained union with them—diligently pursue the beginning and end of all goods. In this state, the contemplation of truth is present, as well as the completion of intellectual knowledge; and together with the knowledge of the gods, there follows a turning toward ourselves and a recognition of our own nature <note>Iamblichus argues that knowing God is the only way to truly know oneself.</note>. In vain, however, do you suspect that perhaps the religious man depends on human opinions and favors. What leisure can there be for a soul dedicated to the gods to listen to human praise?

### That Religion is True.

Nor do you doubt correctly in what follows, fearing perhaps that the religious man himself is deceived when you say that the soul often fabricates great things from chance. What beginning can there be for fictions among things that truly exist? Is it not that power of imagination <term>phantastica: refers to the 'phantasia' or the image-making faculty of the mind, which Iamblichus distinguishes from pure intellectual insight.</term> within us that fashions and transforms idols? And when the intellectual life acts perfectly, no imagination arises; is it not the case that truth itself exists essentially among the gods?

<summary>Iamblichus explains that the personal daemon reveals its own unique form of worship and warns against judging divine matters by human standards. He concludes that true happiness and self-knowledge are found only through theocratic union with the gods, which is a matter of essential truth rather than human imagination.</summary>

# IAMBLICHVS

exists together with them: is not that which is founded upon the harmony in intelligible things <note>referring to the Platonic realm of pure forms and intellect, beyond the physical world</note> much more so? Therefore, such things are spread abroad by you and others in vain. Furthermore, when you and others slander religious men as if they were magical tricksters <note>original: "praestigiatores"; referring to charlatans who use sleight of hand to mimic divine acts</note> and vain boasters, you depart further from the truth. For just as in other arts, so also in religion there are those who falsely profess it; and this profession departs further from religion than anything else. For evil is more contrary to the good than to that which is merely not good.

### *On natural, artificial, and divine*
### *foreknowledge.*

If any natural aptitude is innate in us for the foretelling of the future, just as animals possess a natural foreknowledge of earthquakes, storms, and cold, such a fore-notion does not seem to have anything venerable <note>or "worthy of worship"</note> about it. For such natural foreknowledge follows from the sharpness of the senses, or from a certain sympathy <note>original: "compassionem"; here meaning a natural physical resonance between parts of the universe</note>, or some other commotion of natural powers. Nor, again, if someone [predicts] according to the discourse of reason, or the observation of signs of preceding effects—just as doctors are accustomed to foresee a fever from the contraction of the pulse and

<column-break/>

# ON THE MYSTERIES

rigor <note>stiffness or shivering</note>—does such foresight seem to be particularly honorable: for it proceeds from a human, suspect, and corporeal condition. Only divine prophecy <note>original: "vaticinium"</note>, therefore, arriving from the gods, truly exhibits a divine life to us, while it possesses both foresight and divine intelligences; it renders us truly divine and brings us a legitimate good. For the most blessed intelligence of the gods is abundantly filled with all good things. You may perhaps suspect that prophets who are divinely inspired might foresee what is to come, yet not be happy because of it: for they may foresee, yet not know how to make use of those future things. But it must be said to the contrary: that every fore-notion inspired by the gods is good and filled with goodness, partaking of order and decorum, and brings utility to us. For the gods, along with the foreknowledge, also suggest the faculty for avoiding and declining the inconveniences naturally threatening us. And when it is necessary to exercise virtue, and the ignorance of the future contributes to that end, they themselves hide future things so that the soul may be better for it. But when this is of no consequence, yet it is expedient for the soul to foresee—specifically so that the soul might return safely to the heights <note>the ascent of the soul to the divine realm</note>—then

<summary>Iamblichus distinguishes between lowly natural or medical foresight and true divine prophecy, arguing that divine inspiration is inherently good and intended for the soul's moral and spiritual elevation.</summary>

# IAMBLICHVS; ON THE MYSTERIES.

they deeply instill prophecy into the very essences of souls.

<margin>->*The Path to Happiness.*<-</margin>

You asked next what the way to happiness might be, and in what thing happiness itself consists. Listen, then, to this. Man was once a contemplative intellect <note>The "intellect" (original: *intellectus*) in this philosophy refers to the highest part of the human spirit that can perceive divine truths directly</note> who, in his own nature, was joined to the contemplation of the gods. But then, he entered into another soul <note>referring to the soul's descent into a physical body</note> which was shaped or tempered according to the human form. And because of this, he was bound within the very chain of necessity and fate. It is therefore proper to consider by what primary means he can be released from such bonds. There is no solution except the knowledge of the gods themselves. For the very idea of happiness is to know the "Good" <note>the "Good" is the ultimate, supreme principle in Platonic thought</note> just as it is; and the idea of evils is the forgetfulness of good things and the deception regarding what is evil. That former knowledge proceeds together with the divine; but this forgetfulness and deception is inseparable from that which is fleeting and transitory. Likewise, the former measures the essences of intelligible things <note>realities that can only be understood by the mind, not the senses</note> rightly through the sacred ways of holiness; but the latter, falling away and being rejected from these principles, throws itself down to measure the physical, bodily form. The former is the recognition of one's own parent <note>referring to the divine source or creator of the soul</note>, but the latter is a departure, joined with forgetfulness, from God the Father

<column-break/>

himself—the most holy, self-sufficient, and far-removed. The former preserves true life, calling it back to the Father; the latter leads man, the chief of creation, down toward that which never remains stable but is always caught in a perpetual flux of change. This, then, shall be your first path to happiness: possessing the intellectual fullness of divine union within souls. Yet, the sacred and deifying <note>original: *deifica*; literally "god-making," meaning the process of becoming more like the divine</note> tradition of happiness is called the "gate" to God the Architect <note>original: *opifex*; a term for the creator of the physical universe, often called the Demiurge</note> of all things, or the "place," or the "hall of the Good." It possesses a power: first, a pure and sober holiness of the soul, which is far more useful than a sober purity of the physical body. Second, it offers the alignment of thought toward the participation in and vision of the Good, while simultaneously driving out all things contrary to it. Third, it provides union with the gods, who are the givers of all good things. Once it has joined the soul to the various parts of the world and the divine powers spread through those parts—both specifically and individually—it then joins the soul to the Architect in his entirety. It provides a total harmony with Him from every side, and places the soul outside of all matter, joined now with the sole eternal reason <note>original: *ratione*</note> of the universe. For example: in the very power of the Architect, which is self-begotten, self-moving, and supports all things—

<summary>Iamblichus describes the descent of the human soul from divine contemplation into the "chains" of the physical body and fate. He argues that true happiness is found only through "deifying" rituals and the knowledge of the gods, which lead the soul back to union with the Creator.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the final discourse of Iamblichus's "On the Mysteries." On the previous page, Iamblichus was describing how the soul, once descended into the physical realm, seeks to measure and understand its reality. He continues here by explaining how divine power lifts the soul back to its creator.</meta>

# IAMBLICHVS

completely remaining and elevating, both intellectual and adorning all things alike, likewise recalling the soul to the intelligible truth <note>The "intelligible truth" refers to reality as it exists in the divine mind, beyond what the physical senses can perceive.</note>. Furthermore, this power—being perfect and perfecting through itself, as well as being the primary actor—gradually fits the soul to the other powers of God the Creator <note>original: *dei uiribus opificis*; referring to the Demiurge or the crafting force of the universe</note> according to the specific property of each, so that the soul, purified and made sacred, is perfectly established within their intellectual actions and creative faculties. Then, and only then, does he place the soul within God the Creator as a whole.

### *The Conclusion regarding religion and happiness among the Egyptians.*

And here, among the Egyptians, is the end of the religious and sacred elevation of the soul. They consider the "Good" itself <note>The "Good" is the ultimate principle of Neoplatonic philosophy, the source of all existence.</note> to be of two kinds: one is divine, namely the God who exists before all intellect; the other is human, which is the union with that very Lord. This is exactly as Bitis the prophet <note>A legendary Egyptian figure said to have interpreted the wisdom of Hermes for King Ammon.</note> explained from the Hermetic books <note>original: *libris mercurialibus*; these are the "Books of Hermes," a collection of sacred texts on philosophy and magic attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. Therefore, this part of the path has not been overlooked by the Egyptians, as you suspect, but has been duly passed down to us in a manner consistent with holiness. Nor, for that matter, do the priests trouble the divine intelligence regarding trivial things, but rather concerning those matters which pertain to the purification, the release, and the salvation of the soul. They do not dwell on difficult theories that are useless to mankind; rather, they focus on the concerns of the soul, which are the most 

<column-break/>

# DE MYSTERIIS.

useful of all. Nor are the priests deluded or obscured by some deceitful and seductive demon; they entirely transcend the deceptive demonic nature in all things and ascend to the intelligible and divine reality. 

Now that these matters have been thoroughly discussed, I pray to the gods that they may grant to me and to you a true guardianship and observance of the divine intelligences, which shall never fail at any time. May they provide us with the truth of eternal things for all eternity, and make us partakers of more perfect understandings concerning the gods. In these things, the most blessed goal of all good things is set before us, as well as the entire authority and foundation of our mutual friendship and harmony.

## THE END OF IAMBLICHVS ON 
## THE MYSTERIES.

# EXCERPTS OF MARSILIO
## FICINO FROM THE GREEK COMMENTARIES OF PROCLUS ON THE FIRST ALCIBIADES OF PLATO.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring a winged cherub or putto figure entangled in swirling floral and vine-like ornamentation.</image-desc>
**P**hilosophers consider the primary and most firm foundation of the Platonic dialogues, and indeed of all philosophy, to be the knowledge of our own nature. For once this is correctly understood, we will be able to know more perfectly what good is suited to us and what evil is at odds with us. Nature is certainly arranged in such a way that, just as the essences of things differ among themselves, so too do the specific perfections of those things. Therefore, so that we may find what the perfection of our nature is and how it is obtained, we must first recognize our own essence. This goal is indeed proposed in the *Alcibiades* <note>Plato’s *First Alcibiades* is a dialogue traditionally used by Neoplatonists as the entry point into philosophy because its central theme is "Know Thyself."</note> and is the ultimate aim of all Plato’s teaching. When we consider the essences of things, we must examine whether the essence of a thing is among those that are indivisible, or of the genus of those things that are divided around bodies,
<meta>catchword: divided around bodies</meta>

---

<header>ON THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.</header>
<page-num>377</page-num>

divided around bodies, or among those that are placed in the middle. Likewise, we must ask whether the essence is eternal, or existing through all time, or if it comes into being during some specific part of time. Again, we must consider whether it is simple and existing before any composition, or if it is indeed composed but joined by unbreakable bonds, or if it can, in turn, be resolved back into the elements from which it was composed. And if we consider each thing in this way, we can then more easily judge what kind of "good" belongs to it. For it is clear that for an essence that is entirely indivisible <note>An "indivisible essence" refers to divine or purely intellectual beings that do not have parts and do not change.</note>, the good is eternal; for a divisible essence, however, the good is involved in time and motion. Finally, for the middle essence <note>The "middle essence" refers to the human soul, which stands between the eternal divine realm and the changing physical realm.</note>, its perfection is weighed according to the measures of the substance itself. For a perfection of this kind needs time, but "primary time" <note>original: *tempore primo*; in Neoplatonism, this refers to a higher, intellectual form of time that measures the movements of the soul rather than the physical world.</note>, which can truly and fittingly measure the incorporeal circuits of the soul. We are indeed ignorant of ourselves, because we are occupied by a generic forgetfulness <note>original: *obliuione generica*; the idea that the soul "forgets" its divine origins upon being born into a physical body.</note> and struck down by the disturbances of the life of rational species. Meanwhile, we think we know many things that we do not know, because we possess the "reasons" <note>original: *rationes*; here meaning the "ratios" or "blueprints"—the innate intellectual principles that God or the Demiurge used to create the world, which remain hidden within the human soul.</note> of things innate to our essence. There are three species of conversion <note>original: *conuersio*; the Neoplatonic concept of *epistrophe* or "turning back," where a soul or being redirects its attention from lower things back toward its divine source.</note>. For whatever is turned, is either turned toward something worse than itself, while...

He categorizes the soul as a "middle essence" that exists between the eternal and the temporal, possessing innate knowledge that is often obscured by the trauma of physical birth.</summary>

->PROCLUS ON<-

...it falls from its own perfection, or rises to a better state because of the vigor of its own life and natural action, or it reflects back upon itself because of a knowledge that is akin to it and a middle form of motion. A "turning" <term>conversio: the Neoplatonic concept of the soul returning to its divine source</term> toward the worse is a certain suffering of the soul as it casts away its wings and, already placed in forgetfulness, loses sight of itself and higher things. Yet turning both toward oneself and toward the better is not only within the power of souls, but also of the divine beings themselves, as Parmenides <note>A reference to the philosopher Parmenides, the subject of one of Plato's most difficult dialogues on the nature of "The One."</note> himself teaches us. In the prologue, Plato indicates among other things that a mind devoted to truth produces forms of knowledge that are well-suited to the things being known: neither an unchangeable knowledge of changeable things, nor an ambiguous and wavering knowledge of things that are necessary and stable. Instead, as Socrates taught in the *Republic*, the mind knows all intellectual things together through a simple, uniform, and intellectual awareness. Through scientific and thoughtful knowledge, by unfolding and composing specific forms, it contemplates the intermediate reasons of things. Third, through the power of the senses touching upon physical objects through their own instruments, it judges things that can be sensed; and finally, through the power of imagination, it apprehends the images of those sensible things. And now indeed

<column-break/>

<page-num>379</page-num>

->THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.<-

Timaeus <note>The main speaker in Plato’s *Timaeus*, who describes the creation of the universe.</note>, distinguishing types of knowledge by a certain proportion to the things being known, commands us to judge true beings with intelligence and reason, but to judge with opinion and sense those things which are not true beings, but are constantly becoming and perishing. He teaches that the rational accounts of things that truly exist are fixed and unmixed, while the accounts of things flowing in the world of "generation" <term>the physical world where things are born and die</term> are imaginary and subject to change. For everywhere, accounts and speech imitate the things they explain. Furthermore, Socrates says in the *Cratylus* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the origin of names and language.</note> that regarding eternal things, names touch the nature of the thing more closely; but regarding changeable things, names also change often and depend more on human convention. Everywhere, different properties exist for different gods: for some are the craftsmen of the universe, providing the substance for the arrangement of the forms of being; others propagate life and generate various vital energies; others preserve the unchanging order of things and guard the unbreakable connection; finally, others, having obtained a different power, preserve all things by their own specific benevolence. Similarly, the entire "order of love" <term>amatorius ordo: the hierarchy of Eros that pulls the soul upward</term> is the cause of the return of all things to divine beauty and form, leading back...

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic theory of knowledge, where the soul’s ability to understand depends on whether it is looking at eternal truths or the changing physical world. It also introduces the hierarchy of divine beings—from creators to guardians—who facilitate the soul's return to divine beauty.</summary>
