<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's movement and its relationship with divine beauty. It focuses specifically on the role of Love (Eros) as a mediator between the divine and the material world, building on the Neoplatonic idea of the soul's "return" to its source.</meta>

<page-num>380</page-num>
<header>->PROCLUS ON<-</header>
...all things following it, joining them to it, and confirming those things joined, and soon filling the subsequent things from there, and radiating through all things the gifts of divine light proceeding from there. For this very reason, in the *Symposium* <note>Plato’s famous dialogue on the nature of Love and desire.</note>, he teaches that Love is a "great spirit" <note>original: "magnum dæmonem." In this context, a "daemon" is not an evil being, but a mediating spirit between gods and humans.</note>: since first of all, Love demonstrates such a mediating power in itself, and is the middle point between the object of desire itself and the one who desires it, and is the cause of the "turning back" <term>conversio: the Neoplatonic process where a soul or being returns its attention toward its divine origin</term> of later things toward prior ones. Therefore, the entire army of love is established in the vestibule of the cause of beauty, calling all things toward it, and creating a middle path between the beloved and those who are called back by love. Because of this, Love declares in itself the pattern of the entire spiritual order: holding this middle position among divine things, just as spirits hold a place between the divine and the mortal. Since, therefore, the entire army of love has this property among the gods, we conceive of its highest point as single and hidden, joined to the first and "intelligible beauty" <note>Beauty as it exists in the realm of pure thought, before it is manifested in physical shapes.</note> among the highest gods; but its middle progression is among the supermundane gods, shining with intellectual character; and its third progression is distributed in many ways around the world, producing many orders and [pow-]
<meta>catchword: -ers (res)</meta>

---

<page-num>381</page-num>
<header>->THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.<-</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>
-ers from itself, and distributing such gifts to different parts of the world. For after the single and first principle of love, and after the three-part substance perfected from it, a varied crowd of loves now shines forth. From here, choirs of angels are filled with love. Even crowds of spirits, full of this god, follow the gods, having been called back to intelligible beauty. An army of heroes, together with spirits and angels, revel wildly around beauty itself. Finally, all things are stirred up, they revive, they flourish again, and they grow warm around the influx of beauty itself. Now indeed the souls of humans, touched by such inspiration and naturally related to God, move continually around the beautiful, and they glide down into the province of "generation" <note>The realm of physical birth, death, and change.</note> to offer a benefit to imperfect souls, and to provide for those who seem to be in need of salvation. The gods themselves and the followers of the gods, remaining in their own proper state, do good to those that follow them and turn them toward themselves. But the souls of humans, having fallen downward and touching the world of generation, show forth the beneficial providence of the gods according to their strength. Just as other souls established according to another god preside purely over the decaying provinces, and to souls...

<summary>This passage describes Love (Eros) as a mediating force that bridges the gap between the divine realm and the physical world. It explains how Love flows through a hierarchy of beings—gods, angels, spirits, and heroes—to inspire human souls to return to their divine origin.</summary>

<page-num>382</page-num>
<header>->PROCLUS ON<-</header>

...for those wandering here, some are helped by more imperfect means through prophecy <note>original: "uaticiniũ"</note>, others through expiations and sacrifices, others through the divine help of healing. So too, souls that have chosen the amatory life <note>A life dedicated to the pursuit of beauty and love as a spiritual path.</note> are moved by the god presiding over beautiful things toward the care of talented souls. From the beauty appearing to the senses, they are led back to divine beauty, calling their beloved ones along with them. But souls that fall from the heavenly gift due to perverse education—though otherwise possessing a nature suited for love—stumble upon images of beauty. Due to ignorance of true beauty, they admire and love bodily forms, being ignorant of their own passion. For just as in Plotinus <note>A foundational Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 204–270 AD) who influenced Proclus.</note> a downward flow of the mind creates cunning, and a fallen portion of wisdom creates sophistry <term>sophisticã: the use of clever but false arguments intended to deceive</term>, so too the gift of love, falling into a perverse vessel <note>original: "susceptaculum"</note>, produces a life that is intemperate and tyrannical.

*The higher cause acts in a work earlier and later than the lower cause, and together with it, and is more present to the work.*

Those things which are everywhere more perfect, and which have the character of a model <note>original: "rationem exemplaris," referring to the Platonic idea that physical things are copies of perfect divine forms</note>, thoroughly encompass the actions of their images, to the extent that inferior things are present nowhere except where the more perfect things...

<meta>catchword: also</meta>

---

<page-num>383</page-num>
<header>->THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.<-</header>

...also, not much earlier, have delivered something from themselves. Furthermore, through all the orders of things, the One itself <note>The ultimate, absolute source of all existence in Neoplatonism.</note> encompasses other things, and acts before them, after them, and together with them.

*Evil spirits also occur in sacred rites, and deceive under the pretext of good things.*

We have found it to be true in expiations and sacrifices that something from the lower orders everywhere puts on an image of perfection, through which it may entice souls not yet perfected toward itself and turn them away from higher things. Therefore, just as in physical contemplation <note>The study of the natural world.</note> the life of sophistry is a hindrance—a leaning that draws the weaker soul away from the contemplation of true beings toward the imagination familiar with fleeting things—so in the elevation through love, the crowd of "popular lovers" <note>Those who pursue physical gratification rather than the spiritual beauty of the soul.</note> is a hindrance, feigning the true lover and twisting the soul of the youth away from heavenly goods into earthly and shadowy things. Similarly, in all the most holy expiations and rites, before the god himself is present, the attacks of certain earthly spirits <term>dæmonum... terrenorum: in this context, "daemons" are supernatural beings; "earthly" ones were thought to be lower-ranking and often deceptive or distracting</term> openly occur, with strange appearances that disturb those being purified and perfected, deflecting them from pure goods toward matter. For this reason, the gods command that one should not first...

<summary>Proclus explains how the impulse of love can either lead a soul back to divine beauty or, if corrupted, lead to an obsession with physical forms. He also warns that in sacred rituals, lower or "earthly" spirits often mimic divine beauty to distract the soul from true spiritual progress.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s commentary on the relationship between the soul and higher guiding forces. Having previously discussed the nature of Love (Eros) and the different ways souls are led back to the divine, the text now focuses on the "daemon" as a guardian and the role of the Intellect in providing silent guidance.</meta>

<page-num>384, 385</page-num>
<header>->PROCLUS ON THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.<-</header>

...look upon them, once you have bound your eyes with the power of the sacred rites. For thus the divine oracles <note>original: "diuina eloquia," likely referring to the Chaldean Oracles, a text highly revered by Neoplatonists</note> say: *It is not beneficial for you to look upon them before you have purified and hallowed your body,* since those spirits, by soothing the souls, turn them away from the sacrifices everywhere. Just as in sacred rites the divine first appears only when we have expelled all this demonic influence <note>original: "dæmoniũ," here referring to lower, distracting spirits or "material daemons" that pull the soul toward physical concerns</note> that drags souls into matter, so too the divine lover, after the rejection of common lovers, meets the beloved. Likewise, just as each person's good daemon <term>daemon: in Neoplatonism, a guardian spirit or higher guiding consciousness, not a "demon" in the modern sense</term> guards them from childhood and examines all their actions and words, and provides for them before their parents do, and remains after their parents as the constant governor of their whole life, and after death is our guide to the judges: in the same way Socrates conducts himself toward Alcibiades. Finally, just as the good daemon is present to us mostly in secret, providing for us privately, advising us in silence, and ineffably correcting us, so Socrates is to Alcibiades.

### *The Intellect always acts within us by illuminating, yet we do not always notice it.*

The Intellect itself indeed always acts around us, and always offers the light of understanding, even before we fall into an irrational state. And when we spend our life in passions, and are sometimes defiled by passions, we do not always notice it; but when we have been purged from the great tumult of generation <note>original: "generationis," referring to the world of physical birth, change, and decay</note> and live tranquilly: Then the intellect shines upon us, then it speaks with us as it were, then it communicates its voice to us, though previously it was present in silence.

<meta>catchword: passions</meta>

<column-break/>

### *On the good daemon, and on earthly and material daemons. Likewise how each of us is considered in two ways, namely as the soul simply, and as the soul using the body.*

Socrates indeed relates to Alcibiades as a good daemon; the other lovers are like material and earthly daemons, who must be driven away so that the soul may be more exposed to the good daemon. One can consider Alcibiades in two ways: both as the soul simply, and as the soul using the body. For the two are not the same, just as the man in himself is not the same as the man who is a pilot; nor is the man simply the same as the charioteer. In general, that which is unparticipated <term>imparticipabile: a technical term for a higher principle that remains pure and independent from the things it influences</term> differs from that which is participated. And that which exists according to itself differs from that which is judged as being together with something else. And finally, that which is transcendent <note>original: "exemptum"</note> differs from that which belongs to a shared order...

<meta>catchword: order</meta>

<summary>Proclus compares Socrates' guidance of Alcibiades to the way a "good daemon" or guardian spirit protects and silently corrects a soul, contrasting this with the distracting influence of "material daemons" or base lovers. He further explains that the Intellect is always providing light to the soul, though it is only heard when the soul is purged of worldly passions.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Socrates, Alcibiades, daemon, guardian spirit, Intellect, Neoplatonism, purification, generation, unparticipated</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between Socrates and Alcibiades as a metaphor for the soul's relationship with the divine. The previous page established Socrates as a "good daemon" who provides silent, providential guidance. This page expands that analogy to the cosmic level of the Intellect and the triad of Faith, Truth, and Love.</meta>

<page-num>386</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...[destiny] has been allotted to no other. Since, therefore, Alcibiades is understood in two ways—both as the soul simply, and as the soul using a body—in the sense that he is simply a certain soul, Socrates plays the part of the **Intellect** <term>intellectus: in Neoplatonism, the higher mind (Nous) that perceives divine truths directly</term> for him. However, in the sense that he is a soul using a body, Socrates plays the part of the **good daemon**. And thus, he himself is both a daemon, in relation to the man, and an intellect, in relation to the soul. Indeed, when we consider the "demonic middle-ground," we place it between gods and men; but when we consider the Intellect, we suspend the soul from the Intellect at the highest point, and the body from the soul. 

Indeed, you have one division in the *Symposium* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the nature of Love</note>, where it is taught that the daemon exists as a middle point between gods and men. You have the other division in the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s dialogue on the creation of the universe</note>, which says that the soul holds the middle position between the Intellect and the body. Therefore, the Intellect and the daemon are the same: it is considered an **Intellect** in relation to the soul, but a **daemon** in relation to the human being.

->*How Love the god is neither first nor last, and how in divine matters there is the good, the wise, and the beautiful:*<-
->*Also Faith, Truth, and Love.*<-

We ought to place the love of the master neither among the first of

<column-break/>

<page-num>387</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>

beings, nor among the last. Not among the first, because the "beloved" itself is higher than love; not among the last, because the "lover" is a participant in love. It is necessary, therefore, to establish **Love** <term>amor: often translating the Greek 'Eros', the divine force of attraction and return</term> between the beloved and the lover. We must assert that it is later than the **Beautiful** <note>original: "pulchro"</note> itself, but prior to all things that are "lovers." 

Where, then, did it exist from the beginning? In what way does it proceed through all things, and with what unities did it come forth? Since there are three substances <note>or "hypostases"</note> in the intelligible and hidden gods:
1. The first is sealed by the **Good**, understanding the Good itself from itself, where—as the sacred oracles <note>original: "sacra eloquia," likely the Chaldean Oracles</note> teach—there is a **Paternal Unity**; 
2. The second is designated by the **Wise**, where the first Intelligence flourishes; 
3. The third is defined by the **Beautiful**, where the most beautiful of intelligible things exists, as the *Timaeus* says. 

Undoubtedly, three unities exist according to these intelligible causes; according to their cause, they consist uniformly among intelligible things, but they shine forth first in the ineffable order of the gods: namely, **Faith**, **Truth**, and **Love**. **Faith** strengthens all things in the Good; **Truth** unfolds all knowledge among beings; and finally, **Love** converts all things and gathers them into [the nature of the Beautiful itself].

<summary>Proclus explains that Socrates acts as both an Intellect and a guardian spirit for Alcibiades, mirroring the cosmic hierarchy where the soul sits between the divine mind and the physical body. He also introduces the divine triad of Faith, Truth, and Love, which connects the soul to the ultimate levels of Goodness, Wisdom, and Beauty.</summary>

<page-num>384</page-num> <note>The OCR lists 388, but contextually this precedes page 385.</note>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>
<meta>catchword: gator</meta>

...the nature of the beautiful. A trinity <note>Original: "trinitas." In Proclus’s philosophy, this refers to the triad of Faith, Truth, and Love, which are the primary virtues connecting the soul to the Divine.</note> of this kind indeed proceeds from here through all the orders of the gods, and irradiates everything toward that intelligible union. But it declares itself differently through different orders, everywhere weaving its own powers together with the specific properties of the gods. In one place, it exists ineffably, unknowingly, and uniquely; in another, by way of containment and connection; in another, as if perfecting and providing forms. Here, again, it acts intellectually and paternally; there, it is entirely moving, life-giving, and effective; in another place, it acts as if commanding and assimilating; there, it is entirely absolute and pure; and elsewhere, it is already multiplied and divided. 

**Love**, therefore, descends from the highest intelligible realms down to the mundane world, calling all things toward divine beauty. **Truth** also proceeds through all things, illuminating everything with forms of knowledge. **Faith** proceeds through the entire universe, establishing each individual thing within the Good. Hence, all the divine oracles <note>Likely a reference to the Chaldean Oracles, a sacred text for later Neoplatonists.</note> hand down the teaching that all things are governed by these three, and consist in them. For this reason, the gods command those performing sacrifices to join themselves to God through a trinity of this kind. The "intelligibles" <term>intelligibilia: the highest level of reality, consisting of pure Being and divine ideas</term> themselves, on account of their inestimable unity, do not need the medium of love. But where there is both a unity of beings and a distinction of separate things, there love exists: for it is a [binder]...

<column-break/>

<page-num>385</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>
<sig>Aa</sig>

...binder <note>The Latin catchword "gator" from the previous page completes "colligator."</note> and a reconciler of those things which exist after it and those which are above it. It is a converter of following things into the prior things, and a reducer and perfecter of those things that are imperfect.

---

->*Providence runs through each individual thing down to the smallest, and meanwhile is subject to none.*<-

Those who are more diligent in assigning the reasons for how things work hand down these two most powerful elements of divine and demonic providence. **First**, that providence proceeds from above through all things to the very last, and does not overlook any place, even the smallest, as if it were devoid of itself. **Second**, that it receives nothing at all from the things it governs, nor is it filled by their lower nature, nor mixed with them anywhere. For it is not mixed with the things it governs simply because it saves and arranges them; for it possesses a divine and demonic nature, which is not subject to the passions <note>passiones: in this sense, "passions" refers to the capacity to be affected or changed by external influences.</note> of particular souls. Nor yet, while it remains separate and towers over the things that follow it, does it leave anything among the inferior things unordered or neglected. Indeed, for this very reason, it adorns all things with decency and is yet kept free from the things it dispenses. For it is beneficial and pure at once, the one who adorns all things...

<note>original Latin terms used in the summary: Proclus, Trinitas (Trinity), Amor (Love), Veritas (Truth), Fides (Faith), Providentia (Providence), Daemon (Divine Guide)</note>

<summary>This page describes the Neoplatonic triad of Faith, Truth, and Love as the governing forces of the universe and explores the dual nature of Providence, which touches everything while remaining untainted by the physical world.</summary>

# PROCLUS ON
# THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.

<page-num>386, 387</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON / THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.</header>

...and meanwhile freed from cultivated things. It runs through all things, especially because it is nowhere restrained. Moreover, divine men <note>Likely referring to enlightened philosophers or "theurgists" who practiced divine rituals.</note> provide for lower things most diligently and at the same time calmly. What then must be said of the gods themselves and the good spirits <term>demons: from the Greek 'daimon,' meaning intermediate spirits that act as messengers between gods and humans, not evil entities</term>? Are they not present to all things yet free from all things? Filling all things with themselves, they mingle with nothing anywhere. Traversing the universe, they have nowhere surrendered their own life <note>Meaning they remain pure and do not lose their divine nature even when interacting with the physical world.</note>. Shall we not confess that the gods love their own offspring? Just as the poets imagine, such as Jupiter <note>Original: "Iovem."</note> loving a girl, and Venus herself. For indeed, love of this kind is a provider, a preserver, a perfecter, and a sustainer of things divinely loved. Meanwhile, it is both beneficial and pure and unmixed, with no attachment to external things. Tell me, I pray, from where could such a privilege of love have fallen to the divine souls of men sometimes, if it did not first exist in the gods themselves? For whatever good and health-giving quality belongs to souls has its definite cause from the gods. Wherefore Plato proves that the models of all virtues and bodily goods—such as health, strength, justice, and temperance—precede us and reside with God. How much more, then, shall we confess that the primary cause of the gift of love

<column-break/>

is among the gods? Especially since this affection of love, as Socrates says in the *Phaedrus* <note>A famous dialogue by Plato where Socrates discusses the nature of the soul and divine madness/love.</note>, is granted by a certain divine gift. Therefore, gods love gods—the older ones indeed love the lesser; but by the gift of providence, the greater love the lesser in turn, evidently by a certain duty of turning back <note>Original: "conversio." In Proclus’s philosophy, this describes the way lower beings look back toward their source to find perfection.</note>. To perfect lesser things and provide for inferiors is also appropriate to souls, by the very nature of being souls. For it is for this very reason that they descend: for the sake of providing for things that are born and for the care of mortals. Yet to provide in such a way that the provider is neither mixed with those governed, nor affected as if enslaved, nor receives anything from them—this is the property of the gods and good spirits. And whenever this happens to human souls, it happens by divine or demonic bounty. Priests in the most sacred sacrifices are accustomed, whether through words or through things shown to the eyes, to lead those present into admiration and amazement. For amazement <note>Original: "stupor." In this context, it refers to a state of ecstatic awe or spiritual "shock" that opens the soul to divine influence.</note> is accustomed to expose the soul to God. Socrates would never have lived a life of love so correctly had he not chosen it by a certain inspiration <note>Original: "afflatu."</note> of a spirit <note>A reference to the famous "daimonion" or inner voice of Socrates.</note>, and held to it once chosen.

<sig>Aa 2</sig>

<summary>This page argues that love and providence originate in the gods and flow down to humans and the material world. It explains that gods can care for the world without being "contaminated" by it, and suggests that human love and priestly rituals are echoes of this divine activity.</summary>

<page-num>392</page-num>

->*Love is the mediator between the beloved and the lover: it is a great spirit <term>daemon: from the Greek 'daimon,' referring to a mediating spirit, not an evil entity</term> binding all things together, and whatever things desire, Love, as the mediator, first joins them to the object of their desire.*<-

Therefore, the sacred utterances <note>Likely referring to the "Chaldean Oracles," which Proclus regarded as divine revelation.</note> indicate that this love of the Lord binds all things together and resides within all things. Indeed, if this Love connects all things, it also joins us to the ranks of the spirits. Diotima <note>The priestess and teacher of Socrates in Plato's "Symposium."</note> called this a "great spirit" because it fills every middle space between those who desire and those who are desired. That which is to be loved claims the first rank for itself, while the lover holds the third rank, distant from the beloved. Finally, Love occupies the middle space between both, gathering and binding together the seeker and the sought, and filling the lesser with the better. Now truly, among the intelligible <term>intelligible: referring to the highest realm of reality that can only be understood by the mind, not the senses</term> and hidden gods, Love unites the intelligible intellect to the first and secret beauty through a certain life that is better than intelligence itself. Thus, the theologian of the Greeks <note>Probably referring to Orpheus.</note> calls that Love "blind." However, in those things which exist outside the intelligible realm, Love does not yet illustrate the unbreakable bond to all things perfected by it. For a bond is a kind of union, but one that exists alongside many distinctions. For this reason, the sacred utterances are accustomed to calling the fire of this love a "uniter": from the in—

---

<page-num>393</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.</header>

—telligible intellect, for proceeding from there, it binds all following things to each other and to that source. It therefore reconciles all the gods to intelligible beauty, joins the spirits to the gods, and joins us to both spirits and gods. In the gods, Love exists primarily <note>original: "primo"</note>; in the spirits, secondarily; and in particular souls, it exists through a certain third process originating from the principles. Again, in the gods, it is above essence <note>Meaning it exists in a state of pure being beyond physical or even spiritual definitions.</note>, for the entire race of gods is of this kind. In spirits, it exists through their essence. Finally, in souls, it exists through the effect of illumination. And this threefold order seems similar to the threefold power of the intellect. For one intellect is "absolute" <term>imparticipabilis: literally 'unparticipated,' meaning an original form that does not lose its purity by being shared with lower things</term>, being free from all particular kinds; another is "shared," of which the souls of the gods partake as something better than themselves. Yet another is infused into souls from this one, which is indeed the very perfection of those souls. Timaeus <note>Referring to the narrator of Plato's dialogue of the same name.</note> himself signifies these three aspects of the intellect. Therefore, understand that the love implanted in the gods is consistent with the absolute intellect; for that Love is also free from all things that receive it and are illuminated by it. Compare the spirit-like <note>original: "daemonium"</note> love to the shared intellect. For that Love is essential and perfect within itself.

<summary>Proclus describes Love as a cosmic mediator that binds the universe together, moving from the highest gods down to human souls. He aligns the different ranks of Love with the different levels of the divine Intellect as described in Platonic philosophy.</summary>

<page-num>394</page-num>

than the pre-participated intellect <term>pre-participated: a Neoplatonic term for a pure principle that exists on its own before it is shared or "participated in" by lower things</term> which sits most closely within souls, or finally, than the intellect received according to a fixed habit. You ought to consider the third kind of love as that which is infused into souls by divine illumination. Nor is it by chance that we consider Love to be joined to the entire intellectual range: for in the intelligible intellect, it holds its first and hidden substance. Indeed, if it came forth from there, it is also founded there by its primary cause. Moreover, it seems to me that Plato also discovered this god in the works of Orpheus, where that same being is called both Love and a "great spirit" <term>daemon: from the Greek 'daimon,' a mediating spirit between gods and humans, not an evil entity</term>, and Plato himself seems to have been satisfied with this hymn. Justly, therefore, does Diotima <note>The wise woman in Plato’s "Symposium" who teaches Socrates about Love.</note> call Love a great spirit. And where Socrates disputes concerning love, he also disputes concerning spirits.

☞ *First, concerning spirits in general; Second, concerning familiar spirits; Third, concerning the spirit of Socrates.*

Next, we must speak first in general about spirits, then about those who have been allotted to govern us, and thirdly about the spirit of Socrates. Therefore, spirits—having been allotted their substance from the first life-giving goddess <note>In Proclus’s theology, this usually refers to Rhea or Hecate as the source of souls and spirits.</note> and flowing from there as if from a fountain—obtained a soul-like essence,

<meta>catchword: soul-like</meta>

<column-break/>

and those who possess a greater and more perfect substance are indeed more intellectual; whereas those who possess the middle ranks are less intellectual and rather more rational. Those of the third and demonic order, holding the lowest positions, have a nature that is more varied than rational, and is material in character. Being established in a substance of this kind, they are divided according to the division of the gods, inasmuch as they perform the role of servants to the gods. For they respond and serve the gods in different ways: some serve the entirely absolute gods, who begin and lead all things before the world was made. Others serve the mundane <term>mundane: relating to the physical cosmos or 'mundus'</term> gods, who preside most closely over the parts of the world and are distributed according to the twelve super-celestial gods. These spirits are distributed according to all the properties of the mundane gods. For every mundane god commands a certain order of spirits, to whom he imparts his own neighboring power. For a creator-god bestows a creative force; an unchanging god exhibits an uncontaminated faculty; and a perfector-god likewise bestows a perfecting power. Around each and every god there is an innumerable multitude of spirits, and this multitude bears the same names as the gods who lead them. They truly rejoice when they are called "Apollonian" <note>Spirits serving under the god Apollo.</note>...

<summary>Proclus defines Love as a mediating spirit found in both Orphic and Platonic traditions and begins a systematic breakdown of "daemons" (spirits). He explains that these spirits act as intermediaries, mirroring the hierarchy and powers of the various classes of gods they serve.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s discourse on the hierarchy of spirits (daemons) and their relationship to the gods. The text follows the Neoplatonic view that spirits act as essential mediators between the eternal divine realm and the changing mortal world.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON

<page-num>396</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...[Saturn]ians, Mercuries, and Jupiters <note>original: "Mercurij, Ioues." Proclus is explaining that spirits are often named after the specific god whose qualities they represent.</note>: since they express the specific nature of their own gods within themselves. Accordingly, mortal things obtain divine influences through these spirits <term>daemon: from the Greek 'daimon,' referring to a mediating spirit or protective power, not an evil entity</term>, and thus plants and animals are fashioned. Some of these display the images of certain gods, and others of other gods. From close at hand, these spirits bestow the gifts of their leaders <note>The "leaders" here are the primary gods.</note>, while more eminently the gods preside over the spirits from above. For this reason, the lowest things agree with the first in their experiences: for the marks of the first are found in the last, and the causes of the last are contained in the first. 

Indeed, the intermediate kinds of spirits fill all things and connect them at once; they themselves maintain the communion of all, since they participate in the gods while being participated in by fleeting, mortal things. Therefore, if anyone says that the craftsman of the universe established the order of spirits as the bond of the whole, he will not wander from the truth. Since even Diotima <note>The wise woman in Plato’s "Symposium" who instructs Socrates on the nature of Love.</note> attributes this order to them—namely, connecting divine and mortal things together, drawing down the essences flowing from above, referring all subsequent things back to the gods, and finally filling the universe according to the connection of this very middle-ground.

<meta>catchword: Confutes</meta>

---

<page-num>397</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.</header>

->*He refutes two opinions: the first saying that there are no spirits other than our own souls released from the more solid body; the second, that of Amelius, who thinks that spirits are certain wandering gods.*<-

Therefore, we shall not admit those who say that spirits are our own souls which have already finished this life and moved into another. For it is not fitting to treat a spirit considered through a certain disposition <gloss>a soul that has temporary spirit-like qualities</gloss> as the same thing as a spirit that is one by its very essence. Nor should one establish the eternal middle-ground of all worldly things from a life that changes itself in many different ways. For the demonic guardianship itself always stands in the same way, keeping all things together. 

But the soul does not retain its order in this way, as Socrates says in the *Republic* <note>Referring to the Myth of Er in Plato's "Republic," where souls choose their next lives, moving between different ranks of existence.</note>. For at different times, it chooses different lives. Nor, again, shall we approve of those who think that some of the gods are spirits—as if they were wandering gods, as Amelius <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher and student of Plotinus.</note> thinks. Instead, we shall follow Plato himself, who calls the gods the masters of the universe, but subjects the flocks of spirits to the gods. And everywhere we shall approve the reasoning of Diotima, who assigns to spirits the middle rank between gods and mortals.

<summary>Proclus defines spirits (daemons) as essential mediators that link the divine and mortal realms, refuting the ideas that they are merely human souls or low-ranking gods.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, daemons, spirits, Diotima, Amelius, Plato, Republic, hierarchy of being, mediation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s systematic classification of the hierarchy of spirits (daemons), detailing the five specific classes and their roles in linking the eternal gods to the physical world and individual human souls.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON

<page-num>398</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

### On familiar spirits. Likewise, he numbers five types of spirits and their various duties.

Since, therefore, spirits are positioned in the middle—as we have said—the first and highest among them are the **divine spirits**. These frequently appear as gods because of their excellent resemblance to the divine. Indeed, in every rank, the first member displays the form of the rank above it. For just as the intellect presents itself as a god, and the most ancient of souls is intellectual in nature, so too the highest class of spirits—being closest to the gods—is uniform and divine. 

The **second** class after these are the spirits already endowed with "intellectual" properties <note>original: "intellectuali proprietate," referring to the capacity for pure reason and contemplation of higher truths</note>. These preside over the ascents and descents of souls, and they declare and hand down the workings of the gods to all. 

The **third** are the spirits who distribute the effects of divine souls into the things that follow, and they complete the bond of those who look upward, allowing the divine flow to reach them. 

The **fourth** transmit the effective powers of universal natures into things that can be born and die <note>original: "generabilia," things subject to the cycle of birth and decay</note>. They breathe life, order, and reason into particular natures, and oversee the entire production of mortal things. 

The **fifth** are, in a certain way, corporeal <term>corporeal: having a physical or bodily form, as opposed to being purely spirit or mind</term>, and they are bound to the furthest extremes of bodies. For in what way could eternal bodies

<meta>catchword: na</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>399</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.</header>

agree with decaying ones? How could causes connect with their effects, unless a mediator of this kind binds them together? For a nature <note>original: "Natura," here referring to the animating force of the physical world</note> of this kind rules over physical goods and provides for all natural resources. Many of their number are occupied with matter; they contain and weave together the powers descending from celestial matter into the material world, guarding it thereafter and preserving the variety and "painting" of forms within matter itself.

### The five duties of spirits: the first spirits join souls to their own gods.

Since, therefore, spirits are many and multiform—as Diotima says <note>Referring again to the priestess in Plato’s "Symposium" who explains the nature of spirits as messengers</note>—the highest among them join those souls that proceed from the Father to the gods who are their leaders. For every god leads, first of all, a group of spirits, and then a group of particular souls. For as Timaeus says <note>Referring to the narrator of Plato’s "Timaeus," a dialogue concerning the creation of the universe</note>, the craftsman-god sowed some souls in the sun, others in the moon, and others in the various other gods. These spirits are themselves "godly," having been allotted souls according to their essence, and they join those souls to their proper leaders. 

And yet, every soul—even if it revolves around its own god—stands in need of the benefit of such a spirit. The **second** class of spirits manages the ascents and descents of souls, and from these spirits, choices are provided to many souls. Indeed, those most perfect souls—which live a life in the world of generation <term>generation: the physical world of birth, change, and death</term> without being entangled in the commerce of generated things...

[text continues]

<summary>Proclus defines five ranks of spirits that bridge the gap between the unchanging gods and the material world, explaining how they manage the life cycles and spiritual alignment of human souls.</summary>

they act, just as they have chosen a life consistent with their own god: thus they also live according to a divine daemon <term>daemon: in Neoplatonic philosophy, an intermediary spirit that guides the soul</term>, who previously, while those souls remained among the heavenly beings, had joined them to their own god. For this reason, that Egyptian priest was struck with admiration for Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founder of Neoplatonism; his biographer Porphyry records that an Egyptian priest summoned Plotinus’s guardian spirit, which appeared as a god</note>, as if he were governed by some divine daemon. Therefore, regarding souls who lead their lives here as if they are soon to return to the heights, the daemon here is the same as the one above. But regarding less perfect souls, the daemon who is the essence itself is one thing, and the one who accompanies the life recently brought forth is another.

-> A daemon is spoken of in three ways: by essence, by comparison, or by a certain disposition. <-

If these points are correctly established, no one will now agree with those who say our rational soul is a daemon. For a daemon is something other than a human, as Diotima <note>The wise woman in Plato’s *Symposium* who explains that Love is a "great daemon" bridging the gap between gods and mortals</note> teaches, placing daemons between the gods and humans. This is also what Socrates himself indicates here, when he distinguishes the daemonic cause from the human cause, saying: "Not indeed a human cause, but a daemonic obstacle held me back." <note>original: "dæmonicū obstaculum." Socrates famously claimed to have a *daimonion*, an inner sign or voice that warned him against making mistakes</note>. Moreover, a human is a soul using a body. Therefore, the daemon should not be considered the same as the rational soul. Indeed, this is also manifest where Plato in the *Timaeus*...

<column-break/>

<page-num>401</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>
<meta>catchword: sea</meta>

says that the daemon is the rational principle <note>original: "rationē," referring to the *Logos* or higher reason</note> established within us as living beings. Nevertheless, we should only accept this as a kind of comparison. For one is a daemon by essence, another is a daemon by comparison, and another is a daemon by habit or disposition. Everywhere, they were accustomed to call "daemon" whatever stands immediately above something else in a certain rank of spirits, relating itself to that which is lower. By this usage, Jupiter, in the works of Orpheus, called his father Saturn a daemon. And Plato himself in the *Timaeus* named those gods "daemons" who arrange the process of generation <note>The cycle of birth, growth, and physical existence</note> from a close distance, where he says: "To treat of the other daemons, and to bring their generation to light, is more than human genius can bear." A "daemon by comparison" is of this kind: specifically, one who provides for each thing most closely, even if it is a god, or even if it is inferior to a god. However, a mind is called a daemon by a certain "disposition" <note>original: "habitudine," a state of being or habitual relationship</note> when, through a wonderful resemblance to the race of daemons, it performs actions that exceed human capacity and hinges its entire life upon a daemon. I believe it is for this reason that Socrates, in the *Republic*, called those souls "daemons" who have lived their lives well and have been transferred to a better fate, now inhabiting more secluded places. Finally, he himself...

<summary>Proclus distinguishes between the soul and its guardian daemon, arguing that while the soul is not a daemon by nature, it can be called one through its relationship with higher powers. He classifies daemons into three types: those that are spirits by their very essence, those that are spirits relative to what they govern, and souls that have attained a spirit-like state through virtue.</summary>

# PROCLUS ON

<page-num>402</page-num>

...[se]condly, a daemon by essence is not named a daemon because of its relationship to things that follow it, nor because of any similarity to another thing, but it has obtained this property through itself. It is distinguished in its existence by its own powers and various modes of action. In general, indeed, the rational soul in the *Timaeus* is called an "animal daemon." <note>original: "daemon animalis," referring to Plato's *Timaeus* 90a, where the highest part of the soul is described as a daemon given by God</note> But we are examining the daemon of the human itself, not the daemon of the animal; and we seek the one who guides the rational soul, not merely its instrument. Furthermore, we seek the one who, once the animal [body] is finally dissolved, leads the soul to the judges, just as Socrates asserts in the *Phaedo* <note>Plato’s *Phaedo* 107d describes the "daemon" leading the soul to the underworld for judgment</note>, when the animal no longer exists, the daemon leads each person to the judge. I mean the daemon itself who had been assigned to the person even while they were living. For if the daemon was obtained by that same living person and presents them for judgment after death—namely, the human being—it had not just obtained the animal part alone. Moreover, beginning from above and commanding, it governs our whole constitution.

### Against those who say the daemon is either the active part of the soul, or a part placed over it.

But those are not to be approved of either, who, having dismissed the rational soul, say the
<meta>catchword: soul (ma)</meta>

---

<page-num>403</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>

say the daemon is the very thing that operates in the soul—as if in those living according to reason, the daemon is reason; but in those living by anger, it is the spirited part; and by desire, the concupiscible part <term>concupiscible: the part of the soul driven by physical desires and appetites</term>. Nor should we listen to those who say the daemon is that which, in our very life, presides most closely over whatever is active—for example, that reason presides for the angry, or the power of anger for the lustful. To begin with, to say that daemons are parts of the souls of humans belongs to those who admire human life too much, and who do not approve of Socrates in the *Republic*, who places the heroic and human race after the gods and daemons. He also rebukes the poets because they introduce heroes as being no better than humans, but enslaved to similar passions. Socrates, therefore, rebuking such things, will be very far from this opinion, which would place daemons (who are superior to heroes) among the parts and powers of the soul. For it follows from these things that beings which are better by their very essence would be received within to complete things that are worse. Furthermore, changes in lifestyles would bring about various changes of daemons. For a greedy person is often changed toward an ambitious life; an ambitious person changes into a state of living by right opinion; and this one into a state endowed with knowledge. The daemon...

<summary>Proclus argues that the "daemon" is a distinct, superior entity that guides the human soul both in life and after death, rather than being merely a psychological faculty like reason or desire.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, daemon, Timaeus, Socrates, Phaedo, Republic, rational soul, spirited part, concupiscible part, judgment after death, hierarchy of being</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s discussion on the nature of the "daemon" (guiding spirit). Having previously argued that the daemon is not merely a part of the soul, he now argues that it is a constant companion from birth to death and is distinct from the "intellect" (the higher mind).</meta>

<page-num>404</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

therefore, if it were one way at one time and another way at another, it would be a different daemon. For that very part which acts within the soul exists as one thing at one time, and another at another. If, then, either this acting part itself, or the part presiding nearest to it, were the daemon, then surely the daemons would be changed along with the changes in human life; and thus the same mind would obtain several daemons during the same life—which can in no way happen. For the soul never changes the governance of its daemon in the same course of life, but the same daemon remains from the very birth <note>original: "genitura," referring to the astrological moment of birth</note> until the presence of the judge of life <note>referring to the judgment of the soul after death</note>, governing us just as Socrates asserts in the **Phaedo**.

### *The daemon is not a particular intellect, but a substance differing from the intellect, placed within the range of souls.* <note>Proclus is arguing that our "guardian spirit" is not just our own higher reasoning (intellect), but a separate spiritual entity.</note>

Therefore, those who think that a particular intellect is the same thing as the daemon allotted to us do not seem to me to correctly confuse intellectual property with the daemonic substance. For all daemons are contained within the breadth of souls and exist as posterior to divine souls. But the intellectual order is something other than the soul, and they have not obtained the same essence, power, or action. Moreover, souls indeed enjoy the intellect only when they 
<meta>catchword: have turned toward</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>405</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>

have turned toward it, and have received light from it, and have joined their own action to it. But they possess the governance of a daemon throughout their entire life, their whole way of living, and through everything that proceeds either from fate or from providence. For the daemon itself is that which rules our whole life; it fulfills our choices made before birth, and bestows the gifts of fate and the favors of the leaders and the gods. Furthermore, it suggests and measures out the illuminations from providence. Now indeed, we, as certain souls, depend on the intellect alone; but as souls using a body, we also need a daemon. For this reason, Plato called the intellect the governor of the soul in the **Phaedrus**, but the daemon he everywhere called the watcher and caretaker of men. Yet no one, considering rightly, will find any other single providence closest to us pertaining to all our affairs, except for daemonic providence and care. For the intellect is participated in by the rational soul, as we have said, but by the body not at all. Nature is participated in by the body, but by the intellect not at all. The rational soul indeed rules over anger and desire, but in fortuitous things it does not have [dominion]...

<summary>Proclus argues that the daemon is a constant, distinct spiritual entity that governs a person's entire life from birth to death, distinguishing it from the intellect, which the soul only accesses through contemplation.</summary>

dominion. But the daemon <note>original: "daemon"; in Neoplatonic thought, this is a guardian spirit or intermediary between gods and humans, not an evil spirit</note> alone moves all things, governs everything, and cultivates and arranges all our affairs. Indeed, it perfects reason, tempers disturbances, inspires nature, maintains the body, provides for chance events, fulfills the fated things, and bestows the gifts of providence. This one exists as the ruler of all things that are in us and around us, holding the rudder of our entire life.

->*Three things concerning the daemon of Socrates: first, that it is also a god; second, that it appears through a voice; third, that it calls back, it does not call forth.*<-

Three things must be considered by us regarding the daemon of Socrates. First, that it is called a god, as is clear here and in the *Apology* <note>Plato’s *Apology*, the account of Socrates’s trial</note>. Second, that it gave a sign to Socrates through a voice, which appears in the *Theages* and the *Phaedrus* <note>Two of Plato's dialogues that discuss the "daemonic sign"</note>. Third, that it called Socrates back and did not call him forth <note>Socrates famously noted that his "sign" only told him when *not* to do something</note>, which the *Apology* testifies. In the hierarchy of divine souls that have chosen the intellectual life, there are divine daemons who are the highest in the genus of daemons, but are participants in the gods above all others. For just as there is a "daemonic" quality among the gods, so too is there a "godly" quality among the daemons. But among the gods, the substance is divine while the proportion is daemonic; among these daemons, however, the property is daemonic while the proportion declares a divine essence.

---

<page-num>407</page-num>
<header>SOUL AND DAEMON.</header>
<sig>Bb 2</sig>

essence. For they so excel other daemons that they frequently appear as gods. Rightly, therefore, Socrates calls his daemon a god, as being the highest of the order of daemons. Wherefore Socrates was perfect, being governed by such a daemon and directing himself according to the standard of such a daemon. Furthermore, as far as that voice of the daemon is concerned, pay attention. Socrates indeed first enjoyed the inspiration of the daemon through intelligence and knowledge of separate things <note>referring to the "Forms" or non-material realities that exist apart from the physical world</note>, which diligently called him back to divine love. Second, even in the affairs of life, the daemon directed its providence toward lower things; and according to the action of the daemon itself, Socrates received light proceeding from there—not only through intelligence or thought, nor only by the powers of opinion, but also by the spirit <term>spiritu</term><note>The "pneuma" or subtle body that connects the soul to the physical senses</note> itself, while that daemonic inspiration proceeded suddenly through his whole life and even moved his very senses. For it is established that by the same action hanging over us from above, the reason is perfected in one way, the imagination in another, and the sense in another by the gift given; and each of those parts within us is moved and affected by the daemon in its own specific condition. Therefore, the voice was not passively heard by Socrates from the outside...

<summary>Proclus explains that Socrates's daemon was of a "divine" rank, acting as a total governor of his life and reason. He distinguishes how this daemon communicated—not as a physical external voice, but as a divine inspiration that affected Socrates's intellect, imagination, and senses simultaneously.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s commentary on the "daemonic sign" of Socrates. Having previously established that this sign was a voice, the text now clarifies that this was not a physical sound striking the ears from the outside, but an internal inspiration. It transitions into a discussion of how different daemons serve under different gods, specifically linking Socrates's guardian to the purifying power of Apollo.</meta>

<page-num>408</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

it was striking, but an inspiration from the innermost parts proceeding through the whole soul, and advancing even to the instruments of the senses, finally became a voice, coming into awareness, clearly, not so much by any physical sense, but by a consensus <note>a harmonious agreement between the internal soul and the external senses</note> of the faculties. For such illuminations from good daemons <note>original: "daemonibus"; intermediary spirits between gods and humans</note> and gods are accustomed to happen in this way.

### *Some daemons are ordered under other gods, and hold diverse offices among themselves.*

However, what was said concerning the daemon of Socrates—namely, that it recalls him and does not provoke him to action—is not a benefit common to all our daemons. Therefore, the character <note>original: "ingenium"</note> of Socrates, being naturally full of love toward men and readily prompted to do good of its own accord, did not need daemonic exhortation. Yet the daemon discouraged him as often as he would approach his teaching either at an improper time or toward those unsuited for his discipline: needing, clearly, a bridle rather than spurs. Furthermore, some daemons possess a purifying <term>purgatory|a power that cleanses the soul of lower, material attachments</term> and uncontaminable power, while others possess a generative power, others a perfecting power, others a demiurgic <note>original: "opificem"; a "crafting" or "building" power</note> power, and they are divided entirely according to the properties and powers of the gods under whom they are ordered. Now indeed, according to their own substance, each protects their clients toward a blessed
<meta>catchword: blessed</meta>

---

<page-num>409</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>

blessed life, some indeed moving us toward the care of lesser things <note>referring to the management of physical or worldly needs</note>, others drawing us back from external actions. It is seen, therefore, that the daemon of Socrates obtained a property of this kind—a purifying cause and the cause of an uncontaminated life—but ordered under the power of Apollo, who presides over all purification with a uniform virtue. By this virtue, therefore, the daemon separates Socrates from excessive commerce with the common crowd, and gathers him back to the inner sanctuary of the mind, and to action separated from lesser things. For just as according to Orpheus <note>the legendary poet and prophet</note>, Apollo presides over Bacchus <note>Dionysus</note>, recalling him from his progression into the Titanic multitude <note>In Orphic myth, Dionysus was torn apart by Titans; here it symbolizes the soul becoming scattered or fragmented by the material world</note>, so also does the daemon of Socrates, who is an attendant of Apollo. For our intellect is the offspring of the Apolline divinity.

### *To know the substances of divinities and souls is the work of a perfectly purified intellect; but commonly we know their powers through their actions, and through these we divine their essences.*

Among these things, Socrates promises Alcibiades <note>Socrates's young, ambitious student</note> that not the substance <note>the essential being</note> of the daemon, but its power, will be known to him. For the divine Iamblichus <note>a major Neoplatonic philosopher who lived c. 245–325 AD</note> says that it is very difficult for us to contemplate the substances of daemons and, commonly, of all those beings superior to us, unless we possess an intellect that is perfectly purified. <summary>This section explains that Socrates's daemon acted as an internal purifying force under the authority of Apollo, functioning as a "bridle" to hold him back from unworthy actions. It also notes that while the true essence of such spirits is hidden from most, their power can be understood through their effects on a person's life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Socrates's daemon, moving from the specific instance of his "voice" to a broader metaphysical explanation of why daemons are associated with "Power" and how higher beings possess knowledge of human affairs.</meta>

<page-num>410</page-num>

...and the essence of the soul is not easy for everyone to grasp. Only
Timaeus <note>The narrator of Plato's dialogue of the same name, which discusses the creation of the world and the soul</note> therefore declared its entire essence.
For what its nature is like in its divine totality would require a long 
explanation to declare, as
Socrates says in the *Phaedrus*. However, it is easy to explain the powers 
of the divine beings. For we perceive
those very powers from their actions, 
of which those very strengths are the nearest mothers.
For power is the middle ground between action and essence, 
produced indeed by essence, but 
producing action. Furthermore, power itself
has the greatest familiarity with daemons <note>original: "daemonibus"; intermediary spirits that bridge the gap between gods and mortals</note>.
For everywhere, power—like a daemon—
occupies the middle position: and in matters of the intellect, 
it joins the "father" <note>the source of being</note> and the "intellect."
For power exists simultaneously with the father, while the intellect 
exists from the father. In intellectual matters, 
it couples actions with essences. For action is 
the offspring of power, but essence 
gives birth to powers from itself. Therefore, the spirits <note>original: "numina"</note> that are the middle ground between us and the gods 
are properly designated by the name of "Power," 
possessing the middle position everywhere: for true <term>essence|the fundamental reality or 'being' of a thing</term> 
and true being exist in the possession of the gods. 
But "Power," duly serving the gods, 
has its place in daemons. Finally, "Action" and "Effectuation," 
having proceeded through their 
power, are turned outward within us.

<meta>catchword: Knowledge</meta>

---

<page-num>411</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>

->*Knowledge of future contingencies is certain and necessary among*<-
->*the higher beings.*<-

The divine Iamblichus <note>A highly influential Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD)</note> and that leader of ours <note>Likely referring to Syrianus, Proclus’s teacher</note>
ask and resolve the matter in this way: How 
is it not absurd to determine the 
differences between types of knowledge based on the nature of the things being known, 
rather than the opposite—namely, to divide the things being known 
by the differences of the knowledge or the 
knowers? Thus, 
that which is eternal and divine in its essence 
knows all things eternally and divinely; 
that which is human knows in a human way; 
that which is truly indivisible surely also anticipates 
in an indivisible way those things which are divisible; 
that which is eternal, being above time, knows even temporal things; 
and that which is rational knows even irrational things in a rational way. 
From these points, it is therefore clear that the gods and daemons 
know the indefinite in a definite way, and the unstable 
in a stable and firm way. Do we not see 
that many things unknown to those lower than us are 
nevertheless known to us? For irrational creatures cannot 
grasp the nature of the universal itself, 
but we are able to gather it. Why 
then do we wonder any further that the things we 
estimate with ambiguous and fallible knowledge 
are firmly comprehended by the higher beings? 
For our weakness is not the judge of their power, just as...

<summary>Proclus explains that daemons represent the metaphysical category of "Power," acting as the bridge between divine "Essence" and human "Action." He further argues that gods and daemons possess certain knowledge of uncertain human events because they know things according to their own superior nature rather than the nature of the object.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Timaeus, Socrates, Essence, Power, Action, Iamblichus, Divine Knowledge, Daemons, Metaphysics</keywords>

# 412 PROCLUS ON

...manner, nor does our reason's judgment possess an irrationality inferior to us, even though the irrational elements within us or outside of us may move our intelligence. For why would it not be absurd if a conjectural foreknowledge of the future were found in some places, but a stable, determined, and indubitable knowledge were found nowhere? Indeed, the very nature of things cannot take its starting point from things that are imperfect, impure, or mixed with their opposites. If, however, the conjecture of the future partakes in ignorance and is not certain, where then is the knowledge of the future in its own right? Where is that pure knowledge itself, from which that which comes after it—and which desires it—has obtained its substance, if not among the higher beings <note>original: "superos"; referring to the gods or celestial beings</note>?

->*The higher beings give us good counsel, even if they foresee that we will not obey them.*<-

Pythian Apollo <note>Apollo in his role as the oracle at Delphi</note> answered Laius <note>The King of Thebes and father of Oedipus</note>, advising him not to father a son, for otherwise he would be subject to the violence of daemons. Nevertheless, Phoebus <note>A common title for Apollo, meaning 'Bright' or 'Pure'</note> foresaw in the meantime that Laius would not obey the oracle; yet, being good, he proposed to him the choice of a better life. And indeed, that famous Laius did not err <note>regarding the god's intent</note>, but because of his own ignorance, he was ensnared by future dangers. Truly, all the actions of upright men tend toward their own

<column-break/>

# THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON. 413

innermost parts. Therefore, if a man has acted beneficently and divinely, he already possesses his goal, established clearly in his own action, even if nothing external resulted from it. Similarly, daemons often act regarding us in this way. Consequently, divine things frequently seem to meet and even run ahead of lower things, while in the meantime they do not depart from their own seats. But while they remain within themselves, they seem to approach us because of the aptitude of the recipient. For both in the invocations of the gods and in spontaneous encounters, divinity itself seems to approach us in a certain way, though it is actually we who have set out toward it. It was a custom among the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, known for their strict way of life and interest in the soul</note> to judge those coming to them by certain signs established in the body, to determine whether they were fit for a better life or not. For nature itself, which fashions bodies for souls, supplies them with congruent instruments and displays images of the souls within the bodies; through these signs, those skilled in this art <note>The practice of physiognomy, or judging character from physical appearance</note> can detect the natural dispositions of souls.

Those skilled in <term>dialectics|the formal art of logical argument and reasoning</term> are accustomed to say that even if someone has used ten thousand <term>necessary|propositions that must be true in all cases</term> propositions, but has interspersed one single <term>contingent|propositions that might be true or false depending on circumstances</term>...

...contingent, the conclusion will turn out to be entirely similar to the inferior part. Similarly, if the thing itself that we must know has something of a material and unstable nature mixed within it, this undoubtedly results in our knowledge of it being indefinite and ambiguous. For knowledge of unstable things is not secure from refutation, but such things are barely comprehended through a kind of assimilation <note>original: "per assimilationem"; the idea that the mind must liken itself to the object it perceives</note>. Such is also the nature of natural virtue: because everywhere, that which is imperfect is easily changed, as it is related to matter. Self-sufficiency itself resides first with the gods. For the gods are good, and they are certain "super-essential" goodnesses <note>original: "superessentiales bonitates"; referring to realities that exist beyond the level of mere being or existence</note> and the fullnesses of all beings. Through them, self-sufficiency belongs to all other things: to intellects, divine souls, daemons, and to us. Some things are self-sufficient through a kind of participation, but the gods are self-sufficient by their very substance. Wherefore, common and immutable opinion defines happiness as self-sufficiency. For to those for whom it belongs to fare well, it is also fitting for them to possess things sufficiently.

->*The rational soul comes from the first maker of the world, while the irrational soul comes from the younger gods; therefore, the former is more noble than the latter.*<-

We say that the rational soul within us is more noble than the irrational soul, because
<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<page-num>415</page-num>

the first maker of the world <note>the Demiurge, the primary creator in Platonic philosophy</note> procreated the former, while the younger gods <note>original: "dij iuuenes"; subordinate deities mentioned in Plato's Timaeus who fashion the mortal and irrational parts of the human being</note> produced the latter. Indeed, the nobility of human families is an image of this very nobility of birth, which nevertheless has ignobility mixed in and changes in turn. However, the stable and eternal nobility of souls is derived from the gods, around whom the Fates <note>original: "fatae"; the Moirai who spin the thread of destiny</note> are said to be, and from divine powers under which they were ordered. For those souls are nobler which are followers <note>original: "pedissequæ"; literally those who follow at the heels of a master</note> of the more sublime gods, and which depend on greater powers according to the distribution arranged in the universe. I assert the same thing concerning cities. For from above, according to the divine lots, the difference descends even to souls. And we say that some gods are fiery, others ethereal, others celestial, and finally others are earthly. Again, among souls, some are from the solar sphere, others from the mercurial, and others indeed from the lunar. And there is such a distribution of souls according to these places that one might truly boast of it. Nobility, however, is not according to earthly parents and cities.

<page-num>416</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

the essence of those presiding, and the powers they have been allotted in the world. Likewise, more perfect souls are subject to more powerful <term>daemons</term> <note>original: "daemonibus"; in Neoplatonic philosophy, these are intermediate spirits between gods and humans, acting as guardians</note>, and are preserved by them, and are directed everywhere as is fitting. However, the less perfect souls are subject to lower daemons. For just as souls are allotted among the gods according to their dignity, so too are they rightly ordered under daemons. And just as the father and maker of the world <note>Referring to the Demiurge, the divine craftsman in Platonic thought</note> assigned the gods as leaders, so he is said to have set daemons as caretakers over souls. Therefore Socrates calls the daemon appointed over him his caretaker and god, clearly asserting to Alcibiades that this is the only true caretaker of a human being, who is also the author of happiness on account of his wonderful power; certainly, no human being would be able to achieve this for us.

->*The order of things requires that after the ideal cause there are things always participating in the ideas and entirely stable, then things ordered and always mutable.*<-

**Love** depending on bodily beauty is changeable, because that beauty is also changeable. Truly, it is a violation of divine law <note>original: "nefas"; something contrary to the natural or divine order</note> for an effect to exceed the condition of its causes in any way, and to turn out better than them. For even where causes are ordered, disordered effects are often born; where
<meta>catchword: stable</meta>

<page-num>417</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>

stable causes exist, the results are often mobile; where causes are determinate, they frequently happen to be indefinite. Therefore, much more so from causes lacking stability and order, effects emerge that are devoid of order and stability. The series of the universe descends from above through such steps to the extremes, which finally are of such a nature that they can be changed, and they claim for themselves a natural mutation. Just as concerning the "Just," one thing is the Primary Just—the Just itself <note>The "Form" or "Idea" of Justice</note>—which is not "such" by participation; another thing, however, is the primary participant in that, which is always a participant; and finally, another thing is a participant only at certain times, while at other times it falls away from participation. For it was not right for the Just itself to remain sterile, imparting nothing of its own property to anything at all. Truly, none of the primary things are of such a kind, but sterility itself belongs only to matter, as the lowest of things. Nor again was it right for that which was first to be participated in by others, to be participated in first by those things which participate only sometimes. For from that which remains entirely simple and immutable, that which is composite and mutable is certainly too alien; therefore, it requires a certain middle link for contact with the former. Nor again can that which is participated in by...

# PROCLUS ON

of those things which can always be illuminated from that source, it was necessary to limit its duty only as far as these things, lest this very extreme limit be found in the universe which is always suspended from its first principles. It is necessary, therefore, as we have said, to establish a threefold order, not only regarding the <term>Just</term> <note>referring to the Platonic Ideal of Justice</note>, but also regarding the Beautiful, the Equal, the Similar, and finally all the species of things.

### *On the opportunity through which the communion of goods is everywhere perfected.*

Just as the gods provide cures for diseases—that is, for sins within souls—and provide fruits at the proper times, affecting them with benefits, so also divine men seek the right <term>opportunity</term> <note>original: "opportunitatem"; this refers to the Greek concept of *Kairos*, the "perfect moment" for action or teaching</note> for instruction. But while the gods themselves determine the measures of these opportunities through their intellectual and divine form, humans pursue them through knowledge. There are also those among men who "hunt" for these moments through the inspiration of a <term>daemon</term> <note>In this context, a "daemon" is a guiding spirit or guardian, not an evil entity</note>. For the presence and familiarity of good daemons has a primary power within us for infallibly capturing the right opportunity everywhere, which was the case for Socrates. Now, just as a specific place is assigned to every body by nature itself, so also certain parts of time are suitable for certain actions. And just as there are certain circuits of the whole world that are the causes of fertility or sterility for animals and 

<meta>catchword: plants</meta>

<column-break/>

# THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.

plants, so for individual actions, one time is suitable and another is not for performing this or that specific act. Such a time is the "opportunity" of any action, bringing a kind of good and a goal both to those who act and to the results. For this reason, the <term>Pythagoreans</term> <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, known for their mystical approach to mathematics and timing</note> also named the First Cause—from which good is present for all—as "Opportunity" itself, since it grants to all things that they might be perfected. By such an occasion, Socrates was relied upon by his daemon regarding <term>Alcibiades</term> <note>A prominent and controversial Athenian statesman who was a student of Socrates</note>, approaching him to say certain things exactly when Alcibiades was also thinking of coming to him to ask those same things. For in this way, all things are fitted together with one another, and thus the communion of goods is everywhere perfected: namely, while the "perfecting causes" are most ready to give, the "recipients" are awakened to participate. Those causes, in turn, extend the power of perfection, while the recipients have received a "power of aptitude" from nature. For power is twofold: one belongs to the agent, the other to the patient <note>The "patient" here means the one receiving the action</note>. The former is the mother of the action; the latter is the receptacle of the perfection. Furthermore, it is necessary that what is to produce the effect should be overflowing within itself, and so ready to act that even if no recipient were present, it would still be ready to act.

<summary>Proclus explains the concept of "Opportunity" (Kairos) as a divine timing that allows for the perfect transmission of goodness and knowledge. He uses the relationship between Socrates and Alcibiades to illustrate how a guardian spirit (daemon) synchronizes the teacher's readiness to give with the student's readiness to receive.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Kairos, Opportunity, Socrates, Alcibiades, Neoplatonism, Daemons, Pythagoreans, First Cause, Agency, Receptivity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Proclus's exploration of how divine influence reaches the human world.
