...yet it is perfect in itself and moves of its own accord to give. Then also, that which is to be the recipient yearns for participation and moves itself toward the perfecting power; and even if that power is not present, the recipient is nonetheless awakened to participation through its own supreme aptitude. For since there are two types of causes—some indeed immobile and present everywhere to all things, and others mobile, being present at times to those they perfect, and at other times distant—let us consider them. Certainly, the <term>intelligible</term> <note>original: "intelligibilia"; things grasped by the mind or intellect rather than the physical senses</note> things are present everywhere in the same way, always bestowing their gifts upon all. On the other hand, <term>sensible</term> <note>original: "sensibilia"; things perceived by the physical senses</note> things may be present to those participating in them, and in turn, may be absent—just as the Sun is now present, filling things with light, and then departs. Since, I say, things are so, whenever an aptitude exists in the one who is to participate in one of the immobile causes, then that cause is simultaneously present, and the work of the efficient cause is completed. For in such cases, only the unsuitability of the subjects is accustomed to block the gifts; because those things which are to be bestowed are always bestowed, and they are always everywhere in the same way. Therefore, let that which has become fit suddenly become a participant in the cause. But when something is prepared for one of the mobile causes, even if the subject is ready, that which
<meta>catchword: was to be given</meta>

<column-break/>

was to be given is absent. For it is necessary that this [mobile cause] be recently present, since it requires a specific place for giving. In general, however, as we have said, it is necessary through all the communions of goods that these two things precede: both the perfecting power of the giver and the completed aptitude of the receiver. It is necessary for the perfection of the bestower to concur with the preparation of the receiver, and together with a concurrence of this kind, the efficient cause itself is carried over to the recipient according to its essence. Moreover, a concurrence of this kind is not accidental. Nor is something ordered usually obtained from things that are disordered and not coordinated—such as things which flow together by chance. Rather, it is necessary for such a <term>conspiracy</term> <note>original: "conspirationem"; used here in the Neoplatonic sense of a "breathing together" or harmonious alignment of cosmic forces</note> to occur in some cases by nature, and in others by the intellect. Likewise, in some places it comes from a certain demonic cause, and in others rather from a divine one. Surely, it was under this condition that Socrates and <term>Alcibiades</term> <note>An Athenian statesman and general; Proclus is commenting on the dialogue *Alcibiades I*, where Socrates claims a divine sign told him when to finally speak to the young man</note> opportunely met at the same time. A good <term>daemon</term> <note>a guiding spirit or guardian divinity</note> was the cause of this <term>opportune meeting</term> <note>the Greek concept of *Kairos*, or the "perfect moment"</note>, determining the right moment for Socrates to meet him. For that very opportunity, beginning from above from the divine realms, proceeds down to the furthest extremes. One kind of opportunity is determined by the gods, and another by the daemons...

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s commentary on the nature of the soul and its divine origins. Having just discussed how a soul’s readiness or "aptitude" allows it to receive divine influence, Proclus now moves to the concept of "recollection"—the idea that our earthly desires are echoes of what our souls witnessed in the heavens before birth.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON 
## THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.

<note>original: "monica"; likely a fragment of "monita," meaning "admonitions" or "lessons"</note> Lessons: Some things are fated, while others are truly accidental. For Fortune, as it is said, and Opportunity govern all human affairs.

->*Those things which you had seen more attentively in your celestial homeland, their images you follow here before all others.*<-

Our souls wander here among the images of those things they had seen in their <term>homeland</term> <note>original: "patria"; in Neoplatonism, this refers to the divine or intelligible realm from which the soul descends</note>, and they are more deeply affected by those images whose archetypes they had contemplated more attentively there. For different souls are suited to different spectacles; therefore, some souls dwell upon certain images and others upon different ones. For they love the likenesses of those things they inspected in that other place. Whosoever were followers of great <term>Jove</term> <note>original: "Iouis" (Jupiter); representing the demiurgic or kingly power in the cosmos</note> on high, and circled together with him—he who holds the rank of leader among the twelve princes of the universe—these souls, having set out for this world according to the likeness of their god, surely long for an authoritative kind of life. Yet, they are ignorant of what is truly imperial; instead, they pursue a mere appearance of leadership among earthly images. And if they should obtain wise teachers, such a disposition leads them toward salvation through a certain return to the heavenly realms. But if they linger too long among these mere images, they decline into tyranny. Hence, Socrates says in the *Republic* 

<meta>catchword: Republic</meta>

<column-break/>

# THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.

that souls recently departed from the heavens choose, above all, lives of power and authority. For since they have only recently circled together with the celestial gods and inspected their powers which dominate all things, they themselves also affect a life similar to those gods, seeking to rule over others. And while many indeed abuse such a disposition, a very few use it rightly. Plato ranks the <term>contemplative</term> <note>original: "contemplatiuam"; a life dedicated to philosophical thought and the study of divine truths</note> life as the most excellent, the second as the ambitious life, and the third as the life of pleasure or greed. Those souls coming from heaven that have been educated most rightly choose the contemplative life. Those also coming from heaven, but who were educated poorly, choose the ambitious life; the rest are those coming from the earth.

->*It is a natural sentiment in all people that the gods know the secrets of souls.*<-

All people naturally speak to the gods through their innermost thoughts, passions, and desires, being persuaded by <term>innate anticipations</term> <note>original: "anticipationes"; the Stoic and Neoplatonic concept of "prolepsis" or universal ideas we are born with</note> that divinity itself knows all things, even the hidden movements of our souls. Certainly every bond and communion established by reason requires the presence of God. For what else unites the many, and gathers together those things which were separated, if not divinity?

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic theory of "recollection," where human inclinations toward power or philosophy are seen as reflections of the soul's previous experiences in the celestial realm. It also asserts the inherent human belief that the divine possesses total knowledge of our private thoughts and internal lives.</summary>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s commentary on the soul, transitioning from the soul’s attraction to celestial archetypes to the mechanism of choice and providence as described in Plato’s Republic.</meta>

<page-num>424</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

*God, through His providence, confirms our free will.* <note>original: "Deus prouidentia sua nostrum cōfirmat arbitrium"</note>

The Gods set forth lives for souls, and for those who have chosen, they determine certain lots that are suited to whatever lives were selected; thus, freedom of choice is contained within the middle of <term>providence</term> <note>The Stoic and Neoplatonic idea that a divine intelligence governs the universe while still allowing for human agency</note>. It is moved and determined from that source, revolving in the midst of it. These things are clearly described in the *Republic*, where the <term>Prophet</term> <note>In the Myth of Er at the end of Plato's Republic, the "Prophet" is the spokesperson for the Fate Lachesis who explains the rules of the lottery for new lives</note> proposes lives to the souls, taking the lots from the lap of <term>Lachesis</term> <note>One of the three Fates (Moirai), specifically the one who measures the thread of life</note> and crying out among the souls: *“O ephemeral souls,”* <note>original: "O animę diurnæ"; a famous address highlighting the short-lived nature of mortal existence</note> etc. This presentation is made by the Prophet on behalf of Lachesis. However, the actual choice regarding the lives proposed there belongs to the souls themselves. Yet the Prophet there, anticipating all the choices of the souls within himself, only then casts the lots. Indeed, the very thing that seems unstable and undefined is always grasped beforehand by the higher and more perfect beings according to a single limit, both stably and in an orderly fashion.

### Three Teachings of Plato.

From the words of Plato, we gather three things here. First, that being evil itself is to be judged as entirely worse than not being at all. Second, that the separation of the soul from the body frees us from the evils that dwell in this <term>perishable region</term> <note>The physical world, which is subject to decay and "falling" away from divine perfection</note>, and from the hin—

<meta>Catchword: from the hin—</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>425</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON</header>

—drances which here impede the attainment of a fitting end. Third, that the very desires of souls have the greatest power over the forms of their lives, and that we are not such beings as are formed and moved by external things alone. For we bring forth choices from within ourselves, through which we conduct our lives.

### Higher beings hold certain knowledge of things that are uncertain in the world.

From this, you will receive a wondrous teaching: namely, that superior things, in a uniform way, everywhere anticipate inferior things; but truly, inferior things cannot equal the power of the superior. Likewise, the knowledge of superior beings firmly encompasses within itself the actions of inferior ones. However, the knowledge of inferior beings can never encompass the entire action of the superior. That which is higher receives nothing into itself from the inferior—just as the heavens do not receive any <term>lower influence</term> <note>original: "sublimarium passionem"; referring to the Greek idea that the celestial spheres are immune to the change and decay of the "sublunary" world below the moon</note> from change. Neither does an immovable cause introduce anything worldly from the motion of worldly things; nor do eternal things admit anything into themselves from things that act only in time. Finally, that which is necessary, while it knows all things, does not undergo change, even in the act of knowing that which is <term>contingent</term> <note>Something that may or may not happen; Proclus argues God knows random events with certainty without becoming "random" Himself</note>.

<sig>Cc 3</sig>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s discourse on the soul’s journey, moving from the logic of learning and self-discovery to the metaphysical structure of "the Good" and how the soul returns to its divine source through reasoning.</meta>

<page-num>426</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

->*Argumentation is twofold, through negation and affirmation: the former breaks things down into parts, the latter puts them together, and both harmonize with the soul’s own nature.*<-

If Alcibiades cannot point to a time when he realized he was ignorant of the things he is now considering, then surely he never knew them, nor did he ever learn them from teachers. But if he neither sought them out nor received them from others, he certainly neither learned them nor discovered them himself. If he neither learned nor discovered them, he rightly does not know them. And if he does not know them, he is not a good judge of matters for which he cannot name a time when he realized his own ignorance. You see, indeed, how if a dispute is conducted in this way, we proceed sometimes by <term>composition</term> <note>original: "compositione"; also known as synthesis, the process of building up complex ideas from simpler ones</note> and sometimes by <term>resolution</term> <note>original: "resolutione"; also known as analysis, the process of breaking down complex ideas to find their fundamental principles</note>. We speak by composition when using affirmations, and by resolution when using negations.

Furthermore, this composition and resolution perfectly suit the nature of the soul. The soul slips down from perfection toward lower things through the composition of reasons and the adding on of certain alien "garments" <note>A common Neoplatonic metaphor for the layers of materiality or lower psychic functions that "clothe" the pure soul as it descends into the physical body</note>, until it is locked within this dense and solid bond. Conversely, it rises back to the heights through resolution and the casting out of those things that are at odds with its own nature, until, emerging naked—as the sacred teachings tell us—it
<meta>catchword: forms</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>427</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.</header>

may be joined with <term>forms</term> <note>original: "speciebus"; refers to the Platonic Ideas or Archetypes which exist independently of physical matter</note> that are entirely separated from matter. For this reason, argumentation proceeding through affirmations descends from perfection to the more imperfect. But that which proceeds through negations does the opposite.

->*The Good itself, although it alone stands above intellectual essence, nevertheless reaches down to the lowest things.*<-

The Good itself, as if standing within itself above intellectual nature—if it is lawful to say so—descends even to the very last things, illuminating and preserving everything, adorning them, and meanwhile turning them back toward itself. First, it is present to those things that truly exist; second, to divine souls; third, to the <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "numinibus" or "daemonibus"; intermediary beings in the Neoplatonic hierarchy that watch over humanity</note> presiding over the human race. Fourth, it is present to our souls, and thereafter to animals and plants, and to all bodies; finally, it reaches even to formless matter, the lowest dregs of things. Indeed, the Good itself, secretly and inexpressibly, offers its own gift to all things before any established order. These things participate in it everywhere, each in a different way according to its own rank. And as much as each thing is naturally suited, they receive a benefit from it. These are the things regarding these matters...

<summary>Proclus explains how the soul returns to the divine through "resolution" (stripping away material attachments) and describes the hierarchy of "the Good" as it radiates from the highest existence down to formless matter.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Alcibiades, Neoplatonism, the Good, soul's ascent, hierarchy of being, analysis and synthesis, materiality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's ascent and its relationship with the Divine. Proclus is moving from the logic of human reasoning (composition and resolution) discussed on the previous page to the metaphysical necessity of unity and the role of the god Mercury (Hermes) in the process of gaining knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>428</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON THE SOUL</header>

...more effectively than those: these in a more unified way, those in a more divisible state. Furthermore, these exist eternally, while those are subject to change. For divine things, just as they have an indivisible essence, so too they possess an indivisible and united Good. However, those things which have moved further away from their principles, being placed in a certain distance and division, must acquire their own good. For this reason, such things have a deep need for a gathering that strives toward the same end and a mutual <term>harmony</term> <note>original: "conspiratione"; literally "breathing together," this refers to a metaphysical cooperation or sympathetic resonance between parts of a whole</note>. Since each individual part does not possess the fullness of the Good on its own, it may at least fulfill the deficiency of its nature through mutual communion, thus strengthening through harmony the weakness caused by its division. By this same reasoning, individual men—who are weak on their own—gather together for counsel, thus joining their divided minds to one another, striving to kindle a single light from many sparks. Indeed, a multitude could never provide for its common interest by itself unless it were ordered around a single prevailing principle within it. One may observe this in every order of things: namely, that a multitude is everywhere born from a Unity, and must be called back through that kindred Unity
<meta>catchword: toward</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>429</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON</header>

toward the Absolute Unity itself. The Divine Good has therefore implanted a desire for itself in all things, so that through this desire it might be able to bestow its benefits upon us.

->*Mercury is the author of discovery and learning for us.*<-

Mercury, as the messenger of Jove <note>The Roman name for Jupiter (Greek: Zeus). In this philosophical context, Mercury (Hermes) represents the power that translates divine intelligence into human language and logic</note>, explains the fatherly will to our souls and thus grants us <term>instruction</term> <note>original: "disciplinam"; refers to formal learning or the reception of knowledge from a teacher</note>. Furthermore, as he is the author of inquiry, he provides <term>discovery</term> <note>original: "inuentionem"; the act of finding or "coming upon" truths through the soul's own internal power</note> to his students. Now, when instruction comes into the soul from the higher powers, it is superior to discovery. But when it comes from equals—such as men who merely awaken the cognitive power within us—it is inferior to discovery. Discovery, therefore, is a middle path, suited to the soul's nature which is self-moving. Through this discovery, the power within us that lives and acts by itself is most clearly revealed. Instruction, however, works in two ways: in its better form, it fills the soul from its higher causes; in its lesser form, it awakens the vital power within us through those things outside of us, being moved from elsewhere. That a certain knowledge of things is born in us even from the higher powers is made clear enough by the gods themselves through their manifest presence and explanations, showing the order of the universe to our souls, and running ahead like...

<summary>Proclus explains that because individual humans are limited, they must cooperate to find the "Good," just as all multiplicity must eventually return to a single Source. He identifies the god Mercury as the source of both human discovery and divine instruction, placing the soul's self-driven discovery as a bridge between human learning and divine revelation.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, The Good, Unity, Mercury, Hermes, Jove, Jupiter, discovery, instruction, Neoplatonism, soul's ascent, social cooperation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion on unity and the role of divine guides. It explores why humans often mistakenly believe they possess full knowledge and compares the soul's state of ignorance to the emptiness of matter.</meta>

<page-num>430</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...guides on the very journey to the divine, hanging torches, as it were, like beacon-fires to signal the way.

### Because of the innate notions of things within us, we think we know everything.

Our souls are weighed down by a double ignorance: on the one hand, they believe they know a great many things because of the <term>notions</term> <note>original: "notiones"; these are the "innate ideas" or sparks of knowledge Proclus believes are born within every human soul</note> of things implanted in them from the beginning; on the other hand, they are actually ignorant because of forgetfulness. Therefore, one who is weighed down by this double ignorance searches for nothing further, just as the wise man himself does not search <note>The "double ignorance" is a Platonic concept where one is not only ignorant but also ignorant of their own ignorance, thus feeling no need to learn.</note>. In this very respect, such a person is in a way similar to the wise man—just as matter is similar to God through a certain "dissimilar similarity," as a certain author rightly teaches. For just as matter is formless, so is God; and both are utterly infinite and unknown. Nevertheless, all these qualities apply to God in a superior sense, while they apply to matter in an inferior sense.

### Through acquired knowledge, the soul becomes like the mind; through double ignorance, it becomes like matter.

Through knowledge itself, the soul indeed becomes like the intellect, grasping the object of knowledge through the very act of cognition, just as the intellect grasps the intelligible. But through double ignorance, it becomes like matter. For just as matter appears to possess all things, though it possesses nothing in

<meta>catchword: reality</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>431</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DAEMON.</header>

reality; so also the soul laboring under double ignorance seems to itself to possess what it does not have. Accordingly, there are two types of knowledge for souls. One is indistinct, through simple and bare understanding; the other is articulated, possessing scientific certainty and free from ambiguity. For we seem, as he says, to know all things as if in a dream, and to be ignorant of those same things when awake; for while we possess the <term>essences</term> <note>original: "rationes"; here referring to the "seminal reasons" or formal principles that constitute the soul’s inner structure</note> of these things through our very being, and we breathe forth a kind of knowledge of them from that source, we lack sufficient mastery over them in terms of actualized production. 

Therefore, time does not precede the notion of the <term>Forms</term> <note>original: "specierum"; the eternal archetypes of all things</note> implanted and constant within us through our essence; for we have held this from eternity. But time *does* precede that knowledge which is produced through the active distinction of these principles. Thus, we are able to pinpoint the time when we first received this knowledge. For I know that I learned what a circle itself is at this particular time, or what the species of justice is at another; and similarly for other things of which we possess actual knowledge. Therefore, regarding the thing itself—the object which we remember—we do not recall a specific time [of its origin]. But for the act of <term>recollection</term> <note>original: "reminiscentiae"; the Platonic concept of *anamnesis*, where learning is actually the soul "remembering" eternal truths it knew before birth</note> itself, there is certainly a determined point in time.

<summary>This page discusses the Platonic concept of 'double ignorance,' where the soul's innate but forgotten knowledge leads to a false sense of understanding. Proclus explains that while our essential knowledge is eternal, our conscious realization of that knowledge happens at specific moments in time through recollection.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, double ignorance, innate ideas, matter vs intellect, recollection, anamnesis, Platonic forms, essence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic discussion of how higher beings—such as the stars and guardian spirits—influence and perceive the human soul. It moves from the internal state of the soul to its external relationship with the cosmos and its personal "daemon" or guiding spirit.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

<page-num>432</page-num>

->The seven ages of man, corresponding to the seven planets.<-

The order of the human ages follows the order of the universe. The **first age** follows the power of the **Moon**; for at that time we live according to the nutritive and vegetative faculty <note>In ancient philosophy, the "nutritive soul" is the most basic level of life, shared with plants, focused on growth and feeding.</note>. The **second** follows **Mercury**, when we apply ourselves to the study of letters, the lyre, wrestling, and similar games <note>These represent the classical Greek education of music and gymnastics.</note>. The **third** follows **Venus**, when the limbs swell with seed and we are immediately incited to procreation. The **fourth** follows the **Sun**, for then vigor reigns, the perfection of age hastens, and it is the middle age of life. The **fifth** follows **Mars**, in which we strive after powers and victories. The **sixth** follows **Jupiter**, which desires prudence and an active, civic life. The **seventh** follows **Saturn**, in which it is natural to cease from procreation, to separate oneself from bodily things, and to transfer oneself to another, incorporeal life.

Higher things everywhere embrace the knowledge of inferior things in a unique and more excellent way. For whatever the **senses** perceive in a material condition, the **imagination** perceives more immaterially. Whatever **opinion** considers without knowing the cause, **science** <note>original: "scientia"; here meaning certain, demonstrative knowledge rather than just modern laboratory science</note> grasps through the cause. And so, in succession, more excellent things always contain within themselves, through their superiority, the absolute and universal knowledge that is suited to the lower things.

<meta>catchword: competentem (referring to the "competent/suited" knowledge mentioned at the end of the page)</meta>

---

# THE SOUL AND THE DEMON
<page-num>433</page-num>

...competent. Therefore, nothing of our affairs is hidden from the <term>daemon</term> <note>original: "daemon"; in this context, it refers to a tutelary or guardian spirit assigned to a person at birth, not an evil entity</note> set over us: not the movements of the soul, nor natural power, nor accidental change; instead, he uniquely surveys and governs all things. In this universe, all the "ends" or goals of everything arranged within it lead toward the very goal of the world itself. For so that the world might be perfect, as **Timaeus** says <note>A reference to Plato’s dialogue "Timaeus," which describes the creation of the universe</note>, all living creatures, both divine and mortal, were brought into being. Similarly, in a well-ordered city, the goals of all the parts serve the goal of civic skill. Likewise, just as each thing involved in the process of "becoming" has its being according to a single specific form (and is therefore not infinite), while the "intelligible" itself is all things (since it possesses the causes of all sensible things): so too is any private craftsman mostly ignorant, being skilled in only one thing. But the **statesman** <note>original: "civilis"; the man of civic or political wisdom</note> is absolutely prudent, for he knows both his own goal and the goals of others, and he directs all other arts toward the single life of the city.

->*Through the harmony reigning in heaven, the higher powers temper all other things harmonically.*<-

The **Heaven** is full of concord and the fitness of its motions. Furthermore, the deities superior to us possess the power of that harmony which precedes them from the heavens...

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic discussion of how the soul and the physical world are governed by "harmonic ratios" and higher causes. It transitions from the cosmic order of the planets to the moral and rational order within the individual, drawing heavily on Plato's *Timaeus* and *Sophist*.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

<page-num>434</page-num>

are participants. After these things, a rightly ordered human life receives from there a harmony of morals and a **fittingness** <note>original: "concinnitatem"; referring to a pleasing, well-ordered symmetry or proportion</note> of actions. The gift of these same higher beings also descends even to the brute animals and plants. For these, too, are naturally participants in harmony. For the higher beings contain and perfect all worldly things through harmonic reason, and they accommodate them to one another and to the world. They indeed harmonize the body to the intellect through the **middle soul** <note>In Neoplatonism, the soul acts as a bridge or "mean" between the pure intellect and the physical body</note> by means of harmonic reason. 

Furthermore, they harmonize generation <note>the world of physical change and birth</note> to the **circuit of identity** <note>original: "circuitui identitatis"; the "Circle of the Same" from Plato's Timaeus, representing constant, eternal movement</note> through the constant reason of identity, by means of the **circuit of alterity** <note>original: "circuitum alteritati"; the "Circle of the Other," representing the changing, planetary movements</note> (if I may put it that way) assigned to it. Finally, they link earth to fire through intermediate bonds. Moreover, they cultivate any soul whatever with harmonic reasons, and they couple every body everywhere with similar measures, and they carry out every movement everywhere with musical measures. 

Therefore, the musical **disposition** <note>original: "habitus"; a settled state of character or mind</note> within us is indeed a gift of the Muses, even if it is their last reflection. For the artistic and contemplative dispositions within us have gods as their overseers, and from there they derive their origin.

->*He who is ignorant of the final cause, is ignorant of every cause.*<-

**I**gnorance of the **final cause** <note>The "final cause" is the purpose or "end" for which something is done</note> regarding any matter whatsoever results in the other causes pertaining to the same 
<meta>catchword: pertaining</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>435</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DEMON. 435</header>

being ignored in a similar way: since the **end** <note>original: "finis"</note> is the first, foremost, and most powerful of causes. It is for the sake of this that efficient causes act, and those things that are made are brought to completion. For this reason, Timaeus thinks that among the wise, this kind of cause should be held as the best. 

Plato teaches in the *Sophist* that there is a double **depravity** <note>original: "pravitatem"; a defect or corruption of the soul's natural state</note> of the soul: one of these is indeed ignorance, which falls into the rational part; the other is discord of the *irrational part toward the rational*. Such discord is the disease of the soul, while ignorance is its deformity. For since reason holds the order of the species <note>meaning the "form" or "ideal type"</note> in the universal soul, the vice of reason is deservedly the soul's ugliness, just as the virtue of reason is the soul's beauty. 

For ignorance occurs because of the dominion of the irrational power, while knowledge occurs because of the *rule of the rational*. Moreover, the deformity of the soul happens when reason is overcome—namely, when it produces its own actions as inharmonious and incongruous, ineffective and unfit for the end. It is necessary, in cultivating the soul, to move the soul entirely toward the contemplation of things once impediments have been removed. For the knowledge of truth exists within us; however, we are prevented from noticing it because of the **passions** <note>In this context, "passions" are not just strong feelings, but disturbances of the mind caused by our physical existence</note> arising from our birth.

It emphasizes that the "final cause" (purpose) is the most important for understanding anything and identifies ignorance as a deformity of the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s purification. This page moves into the practical removal of obstacles to that harmony and the distinction between justice and friendship.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

<page-num>436</page-num>

...standing in our way: I mean the Passions—forgetfulness, empty opinion, false imaginations, and immoderate desires. Once these are driven out, the mind must be turned back toward itself; in this way, it suddenly enjoys the truth.

### *The end of war is justice; the end of peace is friendship, which is more desirable than justice.*

**T**he end goal of war is indeed justice. But for peace, there is another, more excellent good: namely, friendship and union. For the goal of all virtue is, as the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, who emphasized the mathematical and harmonic structure of the universe</note> teach and Aristotle confirms, that once all have been made friends, we no longer require justice—specifically when the distinction between "mine" and "not mine" <note>original: "meum & non meum"; a reference to the ideal of communal property and shared identity in perfect friendship</note> has been removed. Yet even if all were just, men would still have need of friendship to gather them together. That injustice is the beginning of wars is shown by war itself, which material forms wage against one another. For the hot fights against the cold, not when they are separated by space, but when one strives to occupy the seat of the other. Since a form lacks its own seat, it rightly resists that which comes from the outside and tries to thrust it from its place. On the other hand, the "reasons" <note>original: "rationes"; the rational principles or blueprints of things</note> implanted in nature run together without any internal strife. For each remains established in itself—

<column-break/>

<page-num>437</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DEMON.</header>

—established. But because material forms cannot exist apart from matter, they rightly struggle with one another over their shared lodging; for matter cannot receive two contrary forms into itself without confusion.

### *How the soul is variously clothed while descending, and is again purified and returns in turn.*

**T**he descent of the soul into the body has indeed separated it from the divine souls, from which it used to be filled with intelligence, power, and purity. Instead, it has joined the soul to generation <note>the realm of physical birth and change</note> and nature and to material things, by which it has been stained with forgetfulness, error, and ignorance. For as the soul descends, many-shaped lives and various "garments" <note>original: "uestes"; a Neoplatonic metaphor for the layers of the subtle body or personality acquired during descent through the planetary spheres</note> from the world grow upon it, dragging it down into a decaying composition and blocking the contemplation of separate, divine things. Therefore, the soul must be led straight from here toward that ever-watchful nature, cutting away the secondary and tertiary powers that arose during its descent—just as the sea-god Glaucus <note>A mythological figure used by Plato in the *Republic* to illustrate the soul; Glaucus became encrusted with seaweed and shells until his true divine form was hidden</note> had to be cleared of seaweed, rocks, and shells. It must restrain those impulses that carry it outward. It must remember again those separate things and the divine essence from which its descent began, and toward which it is fitting for every life to strive.

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic hierarchy of virtues, arguing that friendship is a higher state than justice because it creates total unity. It also describes the soul's descent into the physical world as a process of becoming "clothed" in material distractions that must be stripped away to return to the divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul’s relationship with the physical body. Following the previous discussion on purifying the soul from passions like forgetfulness and false opinions, Proclus now examines how the soul’s essential nature—which is self-moving and active—becomes passive and "moved from without" when entangled with physical matter.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

<page-num>438</page-num>

->The soul is self-moving by its own nature; the body is moved by another:<-
->but because of their mutual interaction, each<-
->takes on the character of the other.<-

**T**he soul, by its very essence, is self-moving; yet, when joined to the body, it becomes in a certain sense "moved by another" <note>original: "mobilis aliunde"; meaning its actions are triggered by external physical stimuli rather than internal will</note>. For just as the soul, by its presence, grants the body a final trace of its own ability to move, so too does the soul—because of its close companionship with the body—take on the mark of the body’s condition, becoming movable from the outside. Therefore, by its power of self-movement, the soul is fertile and inclined toward discovery; it gives birth to "reasons" <note>original: "rationes"; the foundational principles of logic and reality</note> and sciences. But because of its condition of being moved by external things, it frequently requires being "awakened" by others. 

Now, the perfections of the soul are more ready for discovery, while its imperfections require more external assistance. For those perfections are more self-moving and share less in a lower nature. The imperfections, however, move themselves less, suffering more from the weight of the body. Nevertheless, when these imperfections make progress—rising up from the body and collecting themselves out of matter—they immediately become more fertile inventors regarding those things in which they were previously barren and uncertain, due to the sluggishness of a life shaped by 
<meta>catchword: matter</meta>

***

<page-num>439</page-num>

# THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

matter and the filthy lethargy contracted from "generation" <note>term: generation (generatio); the process of being born into a physical, changing body</note>. We, therefore, observing a "middle path" <note>original: "mediocritatem"; the philosophical mean or balanced view</note>, assign the causes of the rational nature and its condition primarily to its habits of imperfection and perfection: and we judge such paths to lead to their perfection. 

Those who assign reasons to these things but fail to keep to this middle path—either rising too high or sinking too low—wander far from the truth of the soul. For we do not approve of those who teach that the soul, having fallen into a "moist body" <note>A reference to the ancient theory that the soul becomes "damp" and dull when it enters the physical world</note> and being hindered by that obstacle, is mindless from the start; and that as the moisture gradually evaporates due to innate heat and becomes more balanced, the prudent power of the mind grows strong. This mode of "perfection" must be judged as merely corporeal and material; it wrongly supposes that the perfection of the soul arises from the physical tempering of the body. 

In truth, the soul possesses its own substance before the elements and the whole of generation; it is a certain kind of life that is mixed neither with the body nor with nature. Nor, on the other hand, do we admit those who think the soul is a literal "part" of the divine essence, and that this part is identical to the whole and always perfect.

<sig>Dd 2</sig>

<summary>Proclus argues that while the soul is naturally self-moving and fertile, its union with the body makes it passive and sluggish. He rejects the materialist view that wisdom comes from physical bodily changes, as well as the view that the human soul remains eternally perfect like the divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the soul’s capacity for change. Having established that the soul is moved by external factors when joined to the body, Proclus now addresses the debate over whether the soul remains eternally perfect or if its very essence can suffer corruption and forgetfulness, citing Plato’s "Timaeus" and "Phaedrus" as evidence for the latter.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

<page-num>440</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

Truly, turmoil and disturbances revolve around that which is the "animal" <note>original: "animal"; referring to the "living being" formed by the union of soul and body</note> within us. For those who say these things imagine the soul to be always perfect and always knowing, never in need of recollection <note>original: "reminiscentia"; the Platonic concept of Anamnesis, where learning is actually remembering eternal truths</note>, always free from suffering, and never corrupted. Yet, in the *Timaeus*, Plato says the essence of our soul is not constituted entirely from the "first principles" <note>original: "primis generibus"; the highest level of cosmic ingredients</note>, as the superior souls are, but is composed of "second and third" degree mixtures. And Socrates, in the *Phaedrus*, says our powers are mixed with their opposite—the good—and are full of mutual discord; for this reason, sometimes the better parts prevail, and sometimes the worse. What more is there to say? Does he not himself say that our "charioteer" <note>original: "aurigam"; the metaphor for Reason/Intellect in the soul’s chariot</note> is also sometimes corrupted? He writes that through the corruption of this very charioteer, many souls become "lame" and many others lose their wings. But indeed, the power of the charioteer in us is quite ancient. This is the power that remembers divine things and uses the second and third-tier powers as its servants, which we are clearly taught in the *Phaedrus*. Truly, as we have said, we must preserve the proper "measures" of the soul, assigning the logic of its perfection and progress without confusing them with either the purely physical or the purely divine; in this way,

<meta>catchword: Platonis</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>441</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DEMON.</header>

let us be interpreters of Plato, and let us not twist the philosopher’s words to fit our own opinions. Therefore, since the soul is at some times imperfect and is perfected once again, and suffers the forgetfulness of divine things and remembers them once more, it is clear that time also contributes to its perfection. For how else could it be moved from ignorance to wisdom, and into virtue from corruption, unless it accomplished these changes through time? For every change is conducted within a span of time. Let these things be said regarding the perfection of souls in general. From this, however, let us accept that he who would know what is just must at some time have crossed over from ignorance into the knowledge of it; he does not always have the very logic of justice ready at hand, as those beings superior to us do. For we are born imperfect from the beginning. Nor, on the other hand, do we obtain the knowledge of justice merely because of the physical changes of the body. For our essence is not corporeal, nor is it constituted of material things. It is necessary for all the soul’s changes to be measured by time. Hence, Aristotle also, in his work *On the Soul* <note>original: "in anima"; referring to Aristotle's "De Anima"</note>, places the change regarding acts and habits (and their opposites) within the realm of time. Therefore, the soul joined to the body...

<sig>Dd 3</sig>

<vocab>Proclus, Plato, Timaeus, Socrates, Phaedrus, Aristotle, soul, demon, change, perfection</vocab>

<summary>Proclus argues that the human soul is not eternally perfect but undergoes changes through time, moving from ignorance to wisdom. He cites Plato’s metaphors of the charioteer and the soul’s "second-tier" composition to prove that we must labor through time to regain our divine knowledge.</summary>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transformation of the soul. The previous page established that the soul possesses different levels of power and can be influenced by its union with the body. We pick up mid-sentence discussing the soul’s transition from ignorance to knowledge.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

<page-num>442</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...to change <note>original: "-ri"; likely the end of "mutari" (to be changed) or "converti" (to be turned)</note>, and in passing from wickedness and ignorance into their opposites, the soul requires an interval of time; of this, there is no doubt. Some, however, believe that the soul itself, remaining separate within itself, does not require time to act, but on the contrary, that time is created from the soul itself. This, I think, requires some explanation. For time is twofold: one kind is born together with natural life and the bodily motion of the universe; the other, however, proceeds through the incorporeal life. This latter time, therefore, measures the circuits of divine souls as well, and in turn perfects the actions of our own souls even when they are separated from the body. The second type of time, which is extended along with natural life, measures our life as it is conducted in union with the body, but it in no way measures the life of the soul living within itself. Yet, there is a certain knowledge within us that exists before all time, just as there is also a knowledge that exists within time. Socrates, therefore, looking at knowledge expressed through action <note>original: "secundum actum"; referring to "actualized" knowledge or knowledge put into practice</note>, inquires what time existed before this knowledge came to be. Alcibiades, however, possessing at least that knowledge placed within his essence <note>original: "in essentia positam"; the innate knowledge of the soul's nature</note>—because of which he thinks he knows even what he does not know—is unable to assign a time when he first received it: for we have held this knowledge from eternity <note>original: "ab æuo"; from the *aevum* or the timeless age</note>. Therefore, if one speaker

---

<page-num>443</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DEMON.</header>

is talking about one kind of knowledge, and the other about another, both are correct: namely, that time preceded knowledge, and yet you cannot readily identify a time that you can claim preceded knowledge. For before knowledge that is not yet fully realized, there is no time; but before knowledge that is perfected in action, time has indeed existed.

->*The many steps through which we ascend from the many to the One*<-
->*by contemplation*<-

Plato and Pythagoras command us to flee from the "multitude" <note>original: "multitudinem"; the state of being many, fragmented, or distracted as opposed to the "One"</note>, so that we may attain the simplest truth. We must flee the multitude of the external populace, which distracts and deceives us in many different ways. But we must flee the multitude of the "inner people" <note>original: "interioris populi"; the conflicting desires and thoughts within the human mind</note> much more, for these distract and deceive us even further. First, therefore, flee the varied multitude of the passions; second, of the senses; third, of the imaginations; and fourth, of the opinions that depend upon them. For every multitude mentioned so far is so diverse within itself that its parts are contrary to one another in every respect. For this reason, it is better to betake oneself to the sciences <note>original: "scientias"; here meaning organized, certain knowledge</note>, in which the multitude no longer possesses any contrariety. For even if passions are contrary to passions, senses to senses, and imaginations to imaginations...

<summary>Proclus distinguishes between two types of time—one cosmic and one spiritual—to explain how the soul can possess eternal knowledge while still learning within time. He concludes by urging the seeker to move away from the chaotic distractions of the "many" (passions and senses) toward the unified truth of the "One."</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Socrates, Alcibiades, Plato, Pythagoras, soul, demon, knowledge, time, multitude, contemplation, passions, senses</keywords>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

<page-num>444</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...opinions [may conflict] with opinions, yet no systematic knowledge <note>original: "scientia"</note> is found to be contrary to another systematic knowledge. In this multitude of propositions and notions, therefore, let us gather them into one, and let us connect the number of sciences by a single bond. For they are so far from being contrary to one another that one notion even serves another, and all the lower sciences minister to the superior ones, having their starting points from them. It is necessary here, moreover, to move from the many sciences that presuppose a single one toward that one itself, which presupposes nothing further, and to refer all others duly to this first science. After the study of this science, it is then expedient to lay aside multiform compositions, divisions, and discursive reasonings <note>original: "discursus"; refers to the step-by-step reasoning of the mind</note>, and to ascend from there to the life of the intellect <note>original: "intellectualem uitam"</note> and its simple intuitions and perceptions. For systematic knowledge is not the highest peak of our cognitions; rather, the intellect stands above it. I speak not only of the intellect as something separated from the soul, but also of that very illumination infused into the soul from that source, of which Aristotle also says: "It is the intellect by which we know the first terms" <note>original: "terminos"; refers to the primary principles or axioms that cannot be proven but are known intuitively</note>. And Timaeus <note>a character in Plato’s dialogues</note> says that this very thing never occurs in anything else except in the soul. As we are ascen-

<column-break/>

<page-num>445</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DEMON.</header>
<sig>Dd 5</sig>

-ding <note>original: "ascendentes"; continuing from the previous column</note> to this intellect, let us together with it contemplate the intelligible essence, specifically through individual and simple perceptions, observing the undivided and simple kinds of beings. But truly, after the venerable intellect, it is finally right to awaken the very highest existence of the soul (if I may call it so), through which we are "one," and under which our internal multitude is united. For just as through our intellect we touch the divine intellect, so also we may reach the first "One"—from which unity is present in all things—by means of that "one" within us, which is like the flower of our essence <note>original: "essentiæ nostræ florem"; a famous Neoplatonic metaphor for the highest, most unified part of the human soul</note>. Through this "one" of ours, we are most closely joined to the divine. For like can everywhere be grasped by like: things that can be known are grasped by knowledge, things that can be understood by the intellect, and the most unified measures of beings are grasped by the unity of the soul. Now indeed, this unity, and the action performed through it, is the summit of all our actions. We are made divine through this when, fleeing all multitude, we take refuge in our own unity, becoming one and acting in a uniform way. Thus far we have advised that the multitude must be fled, proceeding in order through the stages of cognition. Next, we shall proceed similarly through the stages of the objects of knowledge <note>original: "cognobilium"</note> to prove the same thing.

<summary>Proclus describes the soul's ascent from diverse sciences to the singular Intellect, and ultimately to a state of "Unity" or the "One," which he calls the "flower of our essence." This process involves moving away from complex reasoning toward simple, divine intuition.</summary>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DEMON

<page-num>446</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...let us yield. Flee all things perceived by the senses <note>original: "sensibilia"</note>; they are heaped together and divisible, and utterly changeable; for that reason, they cannot be grasped by pure knowledge. From these, therefore, transfer yourself to the incorporeal essence. For everything sensible has a unity that is merely accidental <note>original: "adventitiam"; meaning it is added from the outside and not part of its own nature</note>, and through its own self it is dispersed and full of infinity. Because of this, its good is divisible and accidental, distant and departing from itself, and residing in a foreign seat.

But when you have ascended to that place and have been stationed among the incorporeal things, you will behold that soul-like <note>original: "animalem"; here meaning pertaining to the soul or *anima*, rather than "animal" in the modern sense</note> order above bodies, which moves indeed of itself, acting through itself, possessing its own substance in and from itself. Yet it is multiplied, anticipating within itself a certain appearance of essence that is to be divided among bodies. There you will truly see many relationships of rational principles <note>original: "rationum"</note>, various proportions, and many bonds. Likewise, you will see certain wholes, and parts, and soul-circles, and a multiform variety of powers.

Furthermore, you will see the perfection of souls, which is not eternal, nor consisting of a whole all at once, but unfolded in time and turning itself in discursive movements <note>the step-by-step thinking process of the human mind</note>. For such is the nature of the soul. But after the multitude belonging to souls, bring yourself toward the intellect, and the intel-

<column-break/>

<page-num>447</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DEMON.</header>

-lectual realms, so that you may possess the unity of things. There, indeed, be a contemplator of that nature. Behold the nature remaining in eternity <note>original: "aevo"; refers to *Aion*, the timeless realm of the Intellect, distinct from the passing time of the physical world</note>, a vigorous life, a wakeful intelligence, which lacks nothing of life and does not require the cycles of time for its perfection. 

When you have inspected these things, and seen how much better they are than souls, ask then whether any multitude exists there: and whether the intellect, while being one, might also be universal. Likewise, since it is uniform, whether it is additionally multiform. You will find that it is so. When, therefore, you have perceived that intellectual multitude—though it is entirely indivisible and united—lead yourself back again to another principle. Consider the super-intellectual essences <note>original: "superintellectuales essentias"; likely referring to the Henads, or divine unities, which exist above the level of Being</note>, their unities, and finally the Unity absolute from all things. 

Having proceeded this far, you will have left all multitude behind and will have arrived at the very fountain of the Good. Since it is clear through these steps that the perfection of the soul is acquired by this specific means—that we flee all multitude, both external and internal, and even the multitude of the universe itself—we can also conclude from this that our souls do not merely gather their cognitions from things perceived by the senses,

<summary>Proclus describes the mystical ascent of the soul, moving from the chaotic world of the senses, through the mathematical and temporal realm of the Soul, into the eternal Intellect, and finally reaching the absolute Unity of the Good.</summary>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>448</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

nor is it possible to find the whole and the one from particular and divided things; rather, one must bring forth knowledge from the innermost depths and correct what is imperfect in the things that appear to us. For it is not fitting to think that those things which do not truly exist <note>referring to the physical world, which Neo-Platonists saw as a shadow of reality</note> are the primary causes of the knowledge existing in the soul. Indeed, those things which conflict with one another, and which stand in need of the soul’s reasoning and arguments—and are themselves ambiguous—cannot possibly precede a knowledge that remains always the same. Nor can those things which are changeably transformed produce reasons that remain fixed as one; nor can indeterminate things be the causes of a determinate intelligence. Therefore, it is not right to receive the truth of eternal things from the many, nor the judgment and distinction of universals from sensible things, nor the judgment and opinion of good things from things that lack reason. Instead, it is necessary for the soul, entering into itself, to search there for the true good and the true rational principles <note>original: "rationes"; here meaning the underlying patterns or blueprints of reality</note> of all things. For the very essence of the soul is full of these, but they lie hidden because of the oblivion of becoming <note>original: "obliuione genesica"; the Neo-Platonic idea that the soul forgets its divine knowledge when it is born into the physical world of "generation"</note>. Thus the soul seeks the truth by looking at external things, while all the while possessing that truth in its own essence; and it explores the good outside itself, having deserted itself. From this point, therefore, everyone ought to begin the knowledge of themselves. For if, among a multitude of men—

<meta>catchword: multitu</meta>

---

<page-num>449</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT <note>original: "Anima et Daemone"; a 'daemon' in this context is a guiding spirit or intermediary being, not a malevolent entity</note>.</header>

—multitude of men we distract our gaze, we will never discern their single form <note>original: "speciem"; the ideal "Form" of Humanity</note>, which is clearly obscured by the multitude, the division, the discord, and the manifold change of those who participate in that form. But if we turn our sight toward ourselves, here surely we shall behold both one reason and one nature of men, removed from all disturbance. Rightly, therefore, does Socrates draw the soul far away from the sight of the multitude when it is about to learn what a human being truly is, and he refutes the opinions that act as impediments to this contemplation. For the multitude is an obstacle to us in the soul's conversion into itself and toward the knowledge of the single form. For here, variety overshadows the one, otherness obscures identity, and dissimilarity clouds similarity. For around matter, forms are confused, and the better things are mixed with the worse.

->body, but in a sublime one: therefore all forms here<-
->are sick and wandering <note>original: "delirant"; suggests being out of their true state or "delirious" due to the limitations of physical matter</note>.<-

No one should wonder if, when we say that what is natural is more frequent than that which is against nature, we see that this latter state is contracted into a narrow space, while the natural truly spreads through the entire—

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>450</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...be expanded. However, we admit that the majority of men are ignorant and wicked, while those of the opposite sort are very few indeed. For it is not natural for souls to spend their lives joined with bodies and to be occupied in <term>generation</term> <note>original: "generatione"; in Neo-Platonism, this refers to the physical world of birth, death, and constant change, as opposed to the eternal, unchanging realm</note>; rather, a separate, immaterial, and incorporeal life is more suited to the nature of souls. Therefore, when they are occupied in the world of becoming, they are like people inhabiting a plague-ridden region. But when they live outside of this realm of becoming, as **Plato** says, they are like those who dwell in the meadows <note>A reference to the *Phaedrus*, where Plato describes the "Meadow of Truth" where souls find their nourishment</note>. Just as, therefore, it is not surprising that among those who inhabit pestilent places, very many fall ill while only a very few preserve their natural health, so too you should not wonder that the many souls placed in the realm of becoming are subject to passions and are depraved. On the contrary, it would be a thing to marvel at if souls involved in such bodies, bound by these knots, and surrounded by such great change, were ever found to be sober, pure, and undisturbed. Is it not a thing of wonder for a soul to live immaterially within material things, and to preserve itself unpolluted somewhere amidst things that are falling into decay? And even when it has drunk from such cups, not to be lulled to sleep by these 
<meta>catchword: medications</meta>

---

<page-num>451</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.</header>

medications. For in truth, forgetfulness, error, and ignorance are like a potent drug <note>original: "pharmaco"; meaning both a medicine and a magical potion or poison</note>, turning souls away toward "the place of dissimilarity" <note>A Platonic term for the material world, where the soul loses its likeness to the Divine</note>. Why then do you wonder that many are wolves in their way of life, and many are pigs, while very many others are surrounded by the appearance of some other brute species? For this earthly place is the dwelling-house of **Circe** herself <note>In Homer’s *Odyssey*, the sorceress Circe turned men into beasts; Proclus uses her as a metaphor for the material world's power to degrade the soul</note>, and many souls, because of immoderate desire, are lured by her venomous cups. Rather, wonder at this: if any are found among mortals anywhere who are not charmed by such potions, nor even ensnared; if any, relying on the gift of **Mercury** <note>In the myth, the god Mercury (Hermes) gives Odysseus the herb "moly" to resist Circe’s magic; here it represents Reason or Divine Intellect</note>, betake themselves to reason and knowledge. For just as you should not wonder if you look into **Tartarus** <note>The deepest abyss of the underworld</note> and see souls being tormented—for that is the nature of such a place—so also, if many souls living in the realm of becoming are disturbed, ignorant, and mindless, you should not wonder: for the realm of becoming requires such souls. Just as in heaven all are good, and in Tartarus all are wicked, so in the realm of becoming the majority are depraved, while the fewer are honest. For <term>generation</term> <note>the process of physical birth and existence</note> does not decline toward the better and more divine of extremes, but toward the worse.

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>452</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

->*The evil person agrees neither with others, nor with himself.*<-

The wicked man cannot be in harmony with his own self. For it is necessary that, partly because of his rational nature, he perceives the truth in some way, yet because of his disturbed imagination, he remains profoundly ignorant and is thus at odds with himself. This is demonstrated by the feeling of repentance <note>original: "pœnitentia"</note> that arrives once his emotional disturbances have subsided. The intemperate man, therefore, because of an intellect that is kindred to the divine, occasionally thinks and speaks of divine things; however, because of his lusts and his clouded imagination, he departs far from divine temperance. And since he is always at war with himself, it is no wonder that he cannot agree with others.

->*Whoever knows something about a subject*<-
->*agrees entirely upon it,*<-

It is necessary for those who have knowledge of a thing to be in total agreement concerning it. The first cause of this concord is the determinate nature of the things themselves; the second is that the internal faculties used to judge knowledge are the same in everyone; and the third is that in the realm of first principles, the intellect is united to itself. Therefore, whatever possesses the faculty of understanding also participates in this unity. Knowledge is indeed a kind of illumination depending upon the intellect, but concord is an illumination that proceeds from

<column-break/>

<page-num>453</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

proceeds from unity: for concord is the union of different things. It is necessary, therefore, that those who share in the same knowledge also agree with one another.

->*Each of us is a soul, and this soul contains within itself*<-
->*all forms, both divine and natural.*<-

The entire purpose of **Plato** in this book is to recall us to the knowledge of ourselves, and to demonstrate that our essence consists of "forms" <note>original: "speciebus"; in Platonic philosophy, these are the eternal archetypes or blueprints of all things</note> and rational principles. He aims to show that the soul produces all sciences from within itself, and recognizes within itself all divine and natural forms. A perfect knowledge of ourselves would consist of judging our powers from our essence, and our actions from those powers. However, we usually obtain a more common knowledge of ourselves by working backwards: recognizing our powers from our actions, and the essence of the soul from its powers. These three—essence, power, and action—flow together within the soul. For its essence exists in its very activity, and its activity is essential to its being. The soul becomes "intellectual" <note>meaning characterized by higher reason rather than physical sensation</note> through its actions, while its actions are kindred to its essence, according to the soul's own perfection.

->*The soul leads itself by its own free motion, provides good for itself,*<-
->*purifies itself, and instructs itself, even when it seems*<-
->*to be instructed from elsewhere.*<-

Animals indeed, captured by reason, impelled...

<summary>Proclus explains that moral failing is a result of internal psychological conflict between reason and imagination, whereas true knowledge produces universal agreement. He concludes that all knowledge is already contained within the soul's essence, and learning is actually a process of self-discovery.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Plato, self-knowledge, internal conflict, forms, essence, soul, divine spirit, intellect, repentance</keywords>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>454</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...are governed by external things and are cared for from elsewhere, namely under the condition by which the mover itself moves them—deprived, specifically, of that faculty by which something is able to lead and preserve itself. But the **rational soul**, because of its property of being **self-moving** and **self-operating** by its own power, is able by its own nature to act upon itself, to move itself, and to provide the good for itself. 

Now, in truth, **purification** does not happen to us from external things, but draws its origin from the innermost parts of the soul. For every evil happens from external things and is foreign to the soul, but the good comes from the innermost parts. For the soul is naturally **good-formed** <note>original: "boniformis", meaning shaped by or possessing the form of the Good</note>; and the more perfect it becomes, the more it claims for itself a life that is self-moving. This is because it becomes movable from elsewhere only due to its connection and interaction with the body. 

For this reason, whatever the soul receives from external things remains an opposing force outside of it, such as things perceived by the senses or the **imagination**. Only those things are truly within the soul which are created by the soul within itself and are produced by it. It is therefore **purified by itself**, since reason, beginning from itself, finds its fulfillment in itself. By this reasoning, it is rightly understood what **Socrates** says—specifically among those disputing—that the one who speaks is properly the one who responds. For if this person is refuted, and the one refuted is then purified,

<meta>catchword: certainly</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>455</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

certainly while he is being purified, he purifies himself and frees himself from ignorance, applying the refutation to himself. This is indeed consistent with the nature of the soul moving itself. 

This saying also confirms the idea that **learning is recollection** <note>original: "disciplinas esse reminiscentias", the Platonic doctrine of *anamnesis*</note>. For the fact that those responding say everything from themselves is the greatest sign of recollection: namely, that souls bring forth reasons and arguments from themselves and only need someone to awaken them. They are not like **tablets empty of letters** <note>A reference to the concept of the *tabula rasa* or blank slate</note> which receive shapes from the outside. For they are always inscribed, and the writer himself is within. 

Yet, not everyone can read what is inscribed, or even know that things are inscribed there. This is because, due to the **forgetfulness of birth** <note>original: "genesicam oblivionem", referring to the soul forgetting its divine origins upon entering the physical world</note>, their eye is surrounded by grime, and from that very forgetfulness, they have fallen into passions. It is only necessary, therefore, to remove the opposing obstacle. Knowledge cast in from the outside is not necessary; for souls have within themselves the **doors of truth**, but they are closed off by earthly and material forms. If, therefore, someone demonstrates things with great diligence, the souls—while incited from elsewhere—bring forth known answers from themselves. By this very fact, they declare that Platonic saying to be true: that the soul knows all things and only requires a certain...

<summary>Proclus argues that the rational soul is self-governing and that true knowledge is not learned from outside, but recollected from within. He uses the Socratic method and the metaphor of an "internal writer" to explain how the soul purifies itself of ignorance caused by physical birth.</summary>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>456</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...inciting one to respond from the outside. Furthermore, the fact that when someone is being instructed, he purifies himself, is the work of a daemonic power <note>The word "daemonic" here refers to a *daemon*—a guiding spirit or divine intermediary in Neoplatonic philosophy—rather than an evil "demon."</note>. For these spirits do not act upon us from the outside, but they direct and govern us from within, as if from the ship's stern. Nor do they purify us as if we were mere bodies that had acquired a nature capable of being moved only from elsewhere; instead, they care for us as beings who move by our own power. For in this way, they both provide good things and purge the passions. **Socrates**, therefore, acting in the place of a good spirit, showed that a youth is refuted not so much by another as by himself.

->*Why the wicked and ignorant man is at odds with himself and others, and why the good man is in agreement, and those who know are in harmony with one another.*<-

Frequent discord with themselves rightly happens to most people, because they let some things in from the senses, some again from the **imagination**, some frequently from opinion, some from anger, and many from lust. For such passions are not only moved in men by opinions, as the **Stoics** <note>A school of philosophy that taught that emotions result from errors in judgment.</note> teach, but also, conversely, they often change their opinions because of certain passions and impulses; sometimes often bringing back the worst outcomes from the best beginnings. These people, therefore, from many principles
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>457</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT.</header>

and powers—and receiving multiform motions within themselves from these inferior things—undoubtedly have a soul that is discordant and at odds. Those endowed with knowledge, however, receive all their understanding from a single superior principle; for the **Intellect** <note>original: "Intellectus", referring to the Neoplatonic "Nous," the realm of pure, divine thought.</note> hands down the principles to them. Truly, all true opinions are the daughters of the intellect. Therefore, since they all proceed with simplicity from such a principle, they are all rightly in harmony with one another.

->*Those who think man is composed of soul and body consequently separate the useful from the just; but those who believe man is the soul, join them.*<-

Whether the **just** and the **useful** are the same thing, others indeed disagree, but the **Platonists** think they are the same. For the **Epicureans** <note>Philosophers who prioritized pleasure and the avoidance of pain.</note> and certain of the **Stoics**, placing our ultimate end in "living according to nature" <note>original: "secundum naturam uiuere"</note>; and the **Peripatetics** <note>The followers of Aristotle.</note>, who fulfill our good from necessary things, cannot easily confirm that the just and the useful are the same. Similarly, so too those who say man is an animated body, or composed of body and soul. For someone will flee wounds and slaughter, even if it is unjust to do so, in order to preserve the "animal" <note>The physical, biological self.</note>; since the good of any animal is to live according to nature...
<sig>Ee 3</sig>

<summary>This page discusses how the wise achieve internal harmony through the "Intellect" while the ignorant are torn by conflicting passions. It also argues that only by defining "man" as the soul can one reconcile justice with self-interest (the useful).</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Socrates, Stoics, Peripatetics, Epicureans, Ethics, Intellect, Harmony, Justice, Soul and Body</keywords>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>458</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...to have, and to be preserved in this state. Here, therefore, the useful differs from the just <note>In common morality, people often think what is "just" is different from what is "useful" or "profitable" for themselves.</note>. However, those who place the ultimate goal in the soul itself—not needing the body—and think that the human being is the soul using the body: these certainly judge the just and the useful to be the same. For they place both in the soul, and they understand that the passions of the instruments <note>The "instruments" here refers to the physical body.</note> are separate from those who use them. The argument proving that the just is useful proceeds thus: Everything just is noble <note>original: *pulchrum*</note>, everything noble is good: therefore, everything just is good. And the good and the useful are the same: therefore, everything just is useful.

->*The soul takes on the appearance of the thing to which it clings:*<-
->*also concerning the vehicles of divine souls*<-
->*and our own.*<-

Souls also take on the forms of those things to which they join themselves. For when they liken themselves to the intellect, they soon claim for themselves an immutability in their opinions and life. But when they couple themselves to generation <term>Generation: The physical world of change, birth, and decay, as opposed to the eternal realm of the intellect.</term>, they pursue novelty everywhere; they are carried now into these opinions, now into those, and they do not perceive the very reasons of the soul, which are always stable. Among these things, Plato signifies that the more noble and gifted souls possessing intelligence [are mindful] of the purity

<column-break/>

<page-num>459</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

of the gods, which is inviolable. Bringing this very purity into their own image <note>original: *idolo*; here referring to the soul's lower reflection or its manifestation in the world.</note>, they are also accustomed to be occupied in a certain way with apparent purity. For the essence of divine garments <note>In Neoplatonism, "garments" or "vehicles" are the subtle layers the soul wears as it descends from the divine realm into the physical world.</note> is inviolable, and their purity is immaterial; it is certainly necessary for souls to put these on, purifying their own kindred garments and keeping their robes uncontaminated from the realm of generation, not placing everything in the purity of external clothes.

->*One knowledge, remaining in itself, fills many without being diminished; from this*<-
->*we conclude that the soul is separate from the body.*<-

It is the gift of the same knowledge to persuade both one person and many. In this Platonic sentiment, a kind of hymn and celebration of knowledge is contained. In this way, how great and of what kind each science is becomes apparent from the fact that, by imitation of the intellect itself, the same knowledge fills each individual with understanding, and all people at once. It is indeed indivisible, resting in itself, and meanwhile it perfects all things participating in it, just like the Mind <note>original: *mens*</note>, which hands itself down from itself to all, yet remains apart from each one. These things certainly indicate that our essence is separate from the body and remains in itself; since knowledge, which is our perfection, possesses such a faculty. For corporeal things...
