# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>460</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

When powers deliver themselves over, they diminish themselves. But **Knowledge** <note>original: *Scientia*</note>, remaining one and the same, undiminished and sufficient, offers itself equally to one and to many. And the soul, therefore, is similarly present to the whole body and to all its members, although different members participate in it in different ways.

->*Someone can be prophetic without knowledge; but a prophet*<-
->*certainly knows what he pronounces.*<-

Where Plato says, "you are a soothsayer," he signifies that the person foretells what he announces by opinion indeed, not by knowledge. For those who are divinatory are not endowed with knowledge, but are carried along by opinion. But those who are not only diviners but also true prophets <note>The author distinguishes between the *vaticinator* (a casual soothsayer) and the *vates* (a divinely inspired prophet with understanding).</note> already possess knowledge, and they have a knowledge superior to that of humans. He also signifies that the impulses and movements of noble souls are in some way stirred up by the higher powers, because they act even beyond opinion, often even if they operate without human knowledge.

->*The Good, the Beautiful, and the Just: the super-intelligible, the intelligi-*<-
->*ble, the intellectual; essence, life, and intellect.*<-

Among the principles of things, the **Good** itself stands above **Beauty**, and Beauty above **Justice**. For the Good stands above the intelligibles <term>Intelligibles: Realities that can only be grasped by the mind, existing in a realm beyond physical perception.</term>, situated in an inaccessible place.

<column-break/>

<page-num>461</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

Beauty, however, remains hidden indeed among the first intelligibles, but more explicitly at the end of that order. Finally, Justice remains united in the first order of the intellectual beings, but consequently shines forth at the conclusion of the intellectual procession of the gods. Again, the Good itself reigns among the gods, Beauty in minds, and Justice reigns in souls. For this reason, even the just is beautiful, yet not everything beautiful is just; rather, the very cause of beautiful things precedes justice. Likewise, every beautiful thing is good, but the source of all good things precedes every beautiful thing. Again, the just is indeed good, joined to the good through the mediation of beauty; but the Good is higher than both. 

Thus you will surely find this to be true even when considering the extremes of things. For the material cause <term>Material cause: The "stuff" or matter that makes up the physical world.</term>, although it may be something good <note>original: *bonum aliquid*</note> in a limited sense, is nevertheless foul and deformed. It participates in the "One" indeed, but is devoid of Form <term>Form: The ideal essence or "blueprint" of a thing that gives it shape and identity.</term>. That which is sensible <note>Meaning "perceptible to the senses."</note> possesses the vestiges of beauty, but in no way the vestiges of justice. Hence, in the *Phaedrus*, Socrates says that among fleeting images there is no splendor of justice or temperance; but beauty alone has this privilege, so that it might be most clearly apparent everywhere.

<summary>Proclus explores the distinction between opinion-based divination and true knowledge, while establishing a Neoplatonic hierarchy where the Good, Beauty, and Justice descend from the divine realm to the human soul.</summary>

and would be most desirable. Wherefore where the **Just** is, there is the **Beautiful**; where the Beautiful is, there is the **Good**, whether you wish to look at the primary principles or at the reflections of those principles extended even to the furthest reaches of reality. Indeed, all things participate in the **Good** itself, for it is the principle <note>original: *principium*; the source or origin from which all things flow</note> of all. However, only those things which possess some form <note>original: *speciem*; here meaning a distinct intelligible structure or "look"</note> participate in the **Beautiful**. Finally, only souls participate in **Justice**. Now, in the very center of all things—which is what the soul is—all these are interconnected: the Good, the Just, and the Beautiful. And the "good" of the soul is itself both beautiful and just; the "just" is simultaneously both beautiful and good; the "beautiful" is likewise good and just. 

Nor is this statement true only in a trinity of this kind, but also in many others. Just as among the primary principles **Being** <note>original: *ens*</note> precedes **Life**, and Life precedes **Mind** <note>original: *mentem*; the intellect</note>; so too in their effects, where not everything that possesses being also possesses life, nor is everything that lives immediately endowed with mind. Instead, by turns, all things that possess intellect also live and exist, and things that live also exist by the very fact of their living. But while there is such a distinction among the lowest things through a trinity of this kind, they flow together in the soul, where being, life, and intellect all exist. Indeed, life and intellect are

<meta>catchword: & essence</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>463</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

also essence; and Intellect is similarly both essence and life. For there is one simplicity in the soul, one substance. Neither being, nor living, nor understanding is an external addition <note>original: *aduentitiū*; something added from the outside rather than being part of the thing's nature</note> there; rather, its intellect is essential and living; its life is also intellectual by its very essence; and finally, its essence is intellectual and living. Therefore, everything is everywhere, and from all these things there is a unity in a similar fashion: its "good" prevails in beauty and justice, its "beautiful" is both good and just, and again its "just" is filled with the good and the beautiful. 

To be sure, the subject <note>The "subject" refers to the underlying soul itself.</note> is one, but the "reasons" <note>original: *rationes*; the conceptual definitions or aspects</note> are diverse. Here we neither consider the identity of these three according to their definitions, nor do we think of their difference according to the subject; rather, we preserve their differing definitions among themselves, yet maintain one single subject. For this reason, these qualities always run together through the actions of the soul. For just as the definitions of the **virtues** are different, yet one person is the possessor of them all—nor can it happen that you participate in **Justice** while being empty of **Temperance**, or that you have these two but not the others—so also is this kind of trinity connected to itself. Every "good" is at once a possessor of the beautiful and the just, and similarly for the others. For this reason

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>464</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

Socrates joined the **just** and the **good** together by means of **beauty**. For beauty is the fitting middle ground and the bond between them. **Timaeus** <note>Plato’s character from the dialogue of the same name, which discusses the creation of the universe</note> says that the most beautiful bond <note>original: *nodum*; literally a "knot"</note> is that which makes itself and the things it binds together into a single unity as much as possible. Remember, however, that only **evil** is the contrary to the **good**; the good itself has no contrary. From this, it is clear that good things are in harmony with one another. To an evil thing, however, both the evil and the good are equally opposed.

->*It proves that everything just is, by its very nature, beautiful and good.*<-

We shall confirm that everything just is beautiful through the following arguments. Since the soul is a kind of multiplicity <note>original: *multitudo*; the idea that the soul has different "parts" or functions</note>, and within it there is a first part, a middle part, and a final part, tell me: when do we say that **justice** is created within it? Is it when the middle or the final part tries to rule over those above them? Or is it rather when the first part rules the middle, and the middle rules the final part? But if it were the case that the lower <note>original: *deteriora*; the "worse" or "inferior" elements</note> parts prevailed over the better ones, then those lower parts would no longer be "lower" by nature: for that which deserves to be called "better" is, by nature, more honorable. If, therefore, it is impossible for the same thing to be naturally both better and worse, it is necessary that justice

<meta>catchword: then</meta>

<page-num>465</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

be found in the soul at that time when that which is best within it holds command over the whole of life. The middle part, being subject to the better, then prevails over the last. For then each part of the soul has what is suitable for it: the first part reigning, the second part supporting, and the last part serving the better. Thus, **justice** itself is by nature the distributor of what is appropriate to each part of the soul; it is the cause of each part maintaining its own office and receiving its proper dignity <note>original: *dignitatis*; worth or status</note>, and thus it is the preservation of the whole order. Now, an order and moderation of this kind, which fits its nature, is naturally **beautiful**. Therefore, justice is the cause of beauty, and is itself beautiful. 

There are, moreover, two kinds of the **just**: One exists in contracts <note>original: *contractibus*; legal or business agreements</note>, which maintains **arithmetic equality** <term>arithmetic equality: a simple 1:1 ratio where everyone gets the exact same amount regardless of their status</term>. The other is concerned with distribution, consisting of **geometric equality** <term>geometric equality: a proportional ratio where rewards or responsibilities are given based on a person's merit or worth</term>. In the first case, one maintains arithmetic equality so that we do not defraud a partner in a contract—that is, by receiving more than we ourselves gave. In the second case, one distributes unequal things to unequal people, according to the dignity of each, following a geometric proportion. Just as people differ from one another, so should the things distributed to them differ. Therefore, every

<meta>catchword: just</meta>

<summary>This page explains that justice in the soul is the natural order where reason rules the lower impulses, creating beauty. It also distinguishes between "arithmetic justice" (equal exchange in trade) and "geometric justice" (proportional distribution based on merit).</summary>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>466</page-num>

...just, as we have said, is equal, and everything **equal** is beautiful; for the unequal is disordered <note>original: *incompositum*; lacking structure or arrangement</note> and ugly. Therefore, everything just is beautiful. Furthermore, beauty exists in physical bodies when **form** overcomes **matter**. For matter itself is formless and ugly; when form is overcome by matter, the form is filled with a kind of shapelessness and deformity, and it becomes something virtually without form—made, clearly, similar to the nature of the underlying material. 

If, therefore, that which is **intellectual** within our soul plays the role of form, and the **irrational** part plays the role of matter—for the intellect and reason are counted within the order of the **Limit** <note>original: *termini*; referring to the Pythagorean and Platonic concept of *Peras*, the principle of order and definition</note>, while the irrational is placed in the category of the **Infinite** <note>original: *infiniti*; or *Apeiron*, the principle of unlimited, chaotic potential</note>—for its nature is indeterminate and lacks measure; then it is necessary that beauty shines forth in the soul when **reason** reigns. 

Indeed, those aspects of life that lack reason are made subject to reason. But the **just** itself gives command to the reason and assigns servitude to the irrational. It attributes to each what is fitting for it: to the principal part it designates leadership, and to the inferior part it designates obedience. Since, in the craftsmanship of the world, the irrational is made subject to us, so too is the irrational subjected to the reason residing within us,

<column-break/>

<page-num>467</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE SPIRIT</header>

just like a **vehicle** <note>original: *vehiculum*; referring to the "soul-vehicle" or *ochema*, a Neoplatonic concept of a subtle body that carries the soul through the heavens</note> itself. Therefore, the just is naturally beautiful and is the cause of the soul’s beauty. 

Likewise, the just is something perfect and defined, for the **unjust** is judged to be imperfect and infinite <note>original: *infinitum*; meaning "limitless" or "without boundary"</note>. The infinite wanders and never stops its step, transgressing the boundary of justice. Therefore, the just itself, wherever it is present, imposes measure and limit, and thus makes all things perfect. Since it is such a thing, it rightly makes the soul beautiful. Because it is that which is perfect and measured, beauty naturally follows it, just as deformity accompanies the unmeasured and the indeterminate. 

Therefore, the just is beautiful by nature. Finally, the **World-Maker** <note>original: *mundi faber*; a Latin translation of the Greek *Demiourgos*, the divine architect of the universe</note> himself adorned this whole universe through justice. For he bound the world together with the most beautiful of all bonds <note>original: *nodorum*; literally "knots"</note> and made it indissoluble, because of the power of **proportional analogy** <note>original: *proportio analogica*; the mathematical harmony that links the elements of the universe</note> that contains it, making it friendly to itself in every part. That which is similar to the world—the most beautiful of all visible things—is surely beautiful itself. Therefore, that which is just is necessarily also beautiful.

->*Everything beautiful is naturally good; everything ugly is evil, and vice versa.*<-

That everything beautiful is good, we prove in this way. It is agreed that the beautiful is...

...is lovable by its very nature, especially since even the most external beauty, bearing the image of divine beauty, is lovable and moves souls toward itself at first sight. This is confirmed in the *Phaedrus* <note>One of Plato’s dialogues dealing with the nature of the soul and love.</note>. For in truth, the word **kalon** <note>original: *καλὸν*; the Greek word for "beautiful" or "noble."</note>—that is, the beautiful—is so named either because of **kalein** <note>original: *καλεῖν*; to call or summon.</note>, which is to say it "summons" souls, or because of **kelein** <note>original: *κηλεῖν*; to charm or soothe.</note>, meaning it "soothes" those who look upon it; certainly, by its nature, it is lovable. For this reason, love is said to lead the lover toward the beautiful. 

Now, whatever is lovable is also **desirable** <note>original: *appetibile*; something that stirs the "appetite" or "desire" of the soul.</note>; for love is a kind of robust and vehement desire for something. And whatever loves, desires something that it also lacks. Furthermore, everything desirable is also **good**, whether it is truly good or merely appears to be so. For many things that are not actually good come before us as things to be sought, because they present themselves to those who desire them as if they were good. 

It was demonstrated by Socrates in the *Meno* <note>original: *Memnone*; a common early modern printing variation for Plato's *Meno*. In this dialogue, Socrates argues that no one seeks evil knowing it is evil.</note> that it is clearly impossible for those who recognize the nature of evil to actually desire it. Therefore, every desirable thing is good. And if something is primarily desirable, it will also be primarily good. But if it is only an apparent desirability, it will likewise be an apparent good. In any case, this distinction must be applied to every term: namely, that it is either ap-
<meta>Catchword: parens,</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>469</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

parent or real. For if beauty is apparent, it is certainly both lovable and desirable in that appearance, and "goodness" is a partner to such beauty. But if it is beautiful by nature, it is similarly lovable and good. 

And yet, what else could be desirable? Could it be evil? That is impossible once its nature is known, for no one would seek it. All things desire the good, and no desire is carried toward evil. But neither is desire directed toward anything that is neither good nor evil. For whatever is of that neutral nature is pursued for the sake of something else, and it is not an **end** <note>original: *finis*; the final goal or purpose of an action.</note> in itself; however, every desirable thing is an end. 

If any evil thing is desired, it is because it appears to be good; thus, it is craved *as* a good. And if something not beautiful is loved, it is because it appears beautiful; thus, it is loved *as* a beauty. If, therefore, everything beautiful is lovable, and everything lovable is desirable, and everything desirable is good, it follows that everything **beautiful is good**. 

And conversely, everything good is desirable, and everything desirable is lovable—if indeed to love is to desire vehemently. This is why in the *Symposium* Socrates says that the good is lovable just as the beautiful is. If, I say, everything good is desirable, and the desirable in turn is lovable, and the lovable is indeed beautiful, it follows that the good is o...

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>470</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...[every]thing be entirely beautiful. Nor should anyone now object to us that the Good itself is superior to beauty <note>A reference to a core Platonic debate: in the *Republic*, the Good is "beyond being," while in other contexts, it is closely identified with the highest Beauty.</note>, nor again that what is called "lovable" is of two distinct kinds. For we are not now disputing about the first principles of things, but about those beautiful and good things that exist within us. 

Therefore, that which is good in us is simultaneously desirable and lovable. Through love, then, we acquire and possess it; through love, I mean, and a devoted zeal. And if anyone observes themselves, they will clearly see that this kind of internal good brings us more intense loves, and they will recognize how much more beautiful it is than those things manifest to the senses. 

Therefore, that which is good is also beautiful. For this reason, Diotima <note>The wise woman from Plato’s *Symposium* who teaches Socrates that love is a ladder leading from physical beauty to divine truth.</note> instructs lovers that, after encountering the beauty that meets the senses, they should transfer themselves to the beauty inherent in actions, duties, and sciences. There, they should vigorously exercise the "amorous eye" of the soul. Next, they should move toward the Intellect <note>original: *intellectum*; in Neoplatonism, this refers to the *Nous*, the divine realm of pure thought and Forms.</note>, where they may find the preeminent and divine beauty ruling as if the "Truly Beautiful" itself were flourishing within the power of our soul. For what in us is more beautiful than virtue and knowledge? What, on the other hand, is more shameful than vice and ignorance?

<meta>catchword: Man</meta>

---

<page-num>471</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

> *Man is a soul using the body as an instrument. Therefore, the*
> *evils of the body do not pertain to the soul*
> *and the man, just as the defects of tools*
> *do not pertain to the craftsman and his art.*

Many falsely believe that wounds and death are evils. For which of these is actually an evil for us? Since it does not pass into the soul—which is who we truly are—it cannot be an evil. For the sufferings <note>original: *passiones*; referring to things "acted upon" the body, like injuries or sickness.</note> of the instruments do not change the virtues of those who use them. 

A blacksmith, for example, does not lose the art of cutting simply because his saws are sometimes blunt; nor, if our happiness consisted in the craft of blacksmithing, would we consider the blacksmith unhappy because he lacked a saw. Now indeed, just as the soul has an instrument [the body], so also the body has certain instruments [the limbs/senses] through which it moves external things. A defect in those external tools does not harm the healthy state of the body itself; similarly, the sufferings of the body do not pass over into the soul. 

Therefore, even if death is an "evil" of the body, it is nonetheless not an evil of the soul. If, then, the beauty of an action resides in the soul, while an evil is meanwhile in another thing [the body], it has not yet been shown that the same thing can be both beautiful and evil at the same time. It is also permitted to distinguish by dialectical reason that one thing is something according to itself...

<summary>Proclus explains the Platonic "ladder of love," moving from sensory beauty to the beauty of the soul and intellect. He then argues that because the soul is the true "self" and the body is merely its tool, physical suffering and death are not true evils for the human person.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding the unity of the Good and the Beautiful. Proclus moves from the ethical implications of brave actions to a metaphysical definition of the human person, distinguishing the essential "Self" (the soul) from its "Instrument" (the body). He then employs Aristotelian logic to prove that beauty and goodness are logically interchangeable.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
### THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT

<page-num>472</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...one kind is essential, while another is by accident <note>In philosophy, "by accident" (*per accidens*) refers to a property that is not part of a thing's true nature or essence. For example, a doctor being a musician is accidental to his being a doctor.</note>. For a brave action is beautiful in its own right; but it is called "bad" only by accident, if it ever occurs that it results in something bad—for instance, when it happens that the person acting bravely dies. Even if you wish to argue that death is counted among evils, the brave duty is not bad in the specific respect that it is brave; therefore, the action remains beautiful even if an "evil" happens to it by accident. Thus, one aspect of the event is beautiful, while the other is an unfortunate accident. It has been shown that the reason an act is beautiful is not the same reason it might be considered bad; it is only "bad" by accident, due to the outcome of death.

Furthermore, he is ignorant of the human essence who separates the **good** from the **beautiful**, placing one in one category and the other in another—assigning the "good" to the body and the "beautiful" to the actions of the soul. But in truth, the human being himself is the mind <note>original: *animus*; the rational soul or seat of consciousness.</note>; he is an essence separated from the body. Whatever exists within that essence is both beautiful and good, even if some inconvenience to the body follows from it. For the body is not a part of the human being, but an instrument <note>A famous Platonic analogy: the soul uses the body like a weaver uses a loom or a pilot uses a ship.</note>. The misfortune of the instrument does not belong to the user.

->*How these two differ: namely, "in itself" and "as such."*<-

Phrases of this kind—namely, *in itself* and *as such*—are distinguished even in the works of **Aristotle**. For that which belongs to something 

<column-break/>

<page-num>473</page-num>
<header>THE SOUL AND THE DIVINE SPIRIT.</header>

*in itself* might not belong to it primarily. However, that which fits a thing *as such* <note>original: *qua ipsum*; essentially or by its very nature.</note>, beyond merely fitting it "in itself," also belongs to it primarily and is equal to it. Therefore, if beauty is the cause of proportion <note>original: *commensurationis*; the harmonious balance of parts.</note> in the soul, and for this very reason is called "beautiful," it ensures that the better part of the soul dominates the worse, perfects us, and purges the soul’s deformity. By this very reasoning, it is **good**—not by accident, but by the very fact that it is beautiful. 

For if a thing is proportioned insofar as it is beautiful, and it is good insofar as it is proportioned (since the "Good" is the ultimate measure), then certainly it exists as good insofar as it is beautiful. Similarly, the "ugly" <note>original: *turpe*; can mean both physically ugly and morally shameful.</note> is "bad" insofar as it is ugly. For insofar as it is ugly, it is formless, unlimited, and disorganized—and for this very reason, it is bad. Therefore, whatever is ugly is necessarily bad. Consequently, the same thing is always both beautiful and good, just as its opposite is equally ugly and bad. 

Keep this rule in mind here, which **Aristotle** also used in his *Posterior Analytics* <note>A treatise by Aristotle on logic and scientific demonstration.</note>: if any two terms are interchangeable, their opposites are also interchangeable. Therefore, if "beautiful" and "good" are interchangeable with one another, their opposites—namely "bad" and "ugly"—are likewise interchangeable with each other.

->F I N I S.<-

<summary>This concluding passage argues that the human essence is the soul, while the body is merely its instrument. Using Aristotelian logic, Proclus demonstrates that beauty and goodness are inseparable qualities of the soul, just as ugliness and evil are inseparable qualities of disorder.</summary>

<meta>This page begins a new section of Proclus's work, titled "On Sacrifice and Magic." While the previous page discussed the ethical and metaphysical nature of the soul and its distinction from the body, the text now shifts to the Neoplatonic theory of "sympathy"—the idea that all parts of the universe are interconnected through hidden spiritual bonds. Marsilio Ficino, the translator, was a central figure in the Renaissance who sought to reconcile these ancient mystical teachings with Christian thought.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
# SACRIFICE AND MAGIC.

->TRANSLATED BY MARSILIO FICINO<-
->THE FLORENTINE.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring a putto or infant figure seated amidst floral scrollwork.</image-desc>Just as lovers progress by means of reason from the beauty that appears to the senses toward divine beauty, little by little; so too did the ancient priests, when they considered in natural things a certain kinship and "compassion" <note>original: *compassionem*; here meaning "sympathy" or a shared feeling/reaction between objects in the universe.</note> of some things for others, and of visible things for hidden powers. Finding that "all things are in all things," they established their sacred science. For they recognized the highest things in the lowest, and the lowest in the highest: in the heavens, they found earthly things existing according to their primary cause and in a celestial manner; on the earth, they found heavenly things existing in an earthly manner. For from what else do we think those plants move toward the movement of the sun, which they call <term>heliotropes</term> <note>meaning "sun-turners," such as sunflowers or marigolds.</note>, that is, "sun-followers"? Or why do the <term>selenetropes</term> <note>meaning "moon-turners"; plants believed to move in sync with the moon's phases.</note>, that is, "moon-followers," turn toward the moon? Truly, all things pray and sing hymns to the leaders of their own order. But some do so in an intellectual way, some in a rational way, others in a natural way, and still others in a sensory way. Thus, the "sun-follower" moves toward the sun in whatever

<column-break/>

<page-num>475</page-num>
<header>SACRIFICE AND MAGIC.</header>
<sig>Ff 4</sig>

way it can. If someone were able to hear the vibration made by its circuit through the air, he would surely perceive, through that sound, a certain melody composed for its king—such a song as a plant is capable of producing. For this reason, one may behold the sun and moon upon the earth, though in an earthly quality. In the heavens, however, one may find all plants, stones, and animals according to their celestial nature, possessing an intellectual life. When the ancients contemplated these things, they applied certain earthly things to certain heavenly ones; thus, through a certain similarity, they drew divine powers down into a lower place. Indeed, similarity itself is a sufficient cause for binding individual things to one another. For if someone heats <term>hemp</term> <note>original: *canabim*; also referring to the wick or fiber used in lamps.</note> or <term>papyrus</term>, and then holds it near a lamp—even if he does not touch it—he will see the hemp suddenly catch fire without having touched the flame, and he will see the ignition descend from above to the lower material. Let us, therefore, compare the heated hemp or papyrus to a certain kinship of lower things to higher ones; its approach to the lamp, to the opportune use of things according to time, place, and matter; and the movement of the fire into the hemp, to the presence

<summary>Proclus explains the Neoplatonic theory of universal sympathy, where earthly objects like heliotropic plants "pray" to celestial bodies. This cosmic kinship allows ancient priests to perform magic and ritual by using earthly materials to attract divine influences.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Marsilio Ficino, Neoplatonism, natural magic, sympathy, heliotropism, theurgy, microcosm, macrocosm</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Proclus's discussion on the "sympathy" between the earthly and the divine. The text begins mid-sentence, completing the thought from the previous page regarding the presence of divine light and how material things participate in it.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
<page-num>476</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...the presence of divine light, to that which is able to receive it; finally, a kind of kindling for the deification of mortals and the illumination of material things, which are then carried upward like burning hemp <note>original: *canabis*; here used as a metaphor for a wick or combustible material that catches the divine fire.</note>, due to a certain participation in the divine seed. What truly shall I say of the Lotus? Indeed, the Lotus folds its leaves into itself before the rising of the Sun; but when the sun rises, it gradually unfolds them. And as far as the sun ascends to the mid-region of heaven, so far does it open its leaves; but as it seeks the sunset from the midpoint, it gradually contracts its leaves. It seems to honor the sun through this expansion and contraction of leaves no less than humans do with the gesture of their cheeks and the movement of their lips. Truly, not only in plants, which have a trace of life, but even in stones one may behold an imitation and a certain participation in the higher lights: just as the <term>helitis</term> stone <note>original: *helitis*; likely a variety of sunstone or jasper believed to reflect solar power.</note> imitates the solar rays with its golden rays; and the stone which is called the "eye of heaven," or "eye of the Sun," has a shape similar to the pupil of an eye, and from the middle of the pupil a ray flashes forth. Also the <term>selenitus</term> stone <note>original: *selenitus*; selenite or moonstone.</note>, that is, the lunar stone, similar in shape to the crescent moon, follows the lunar movement through a certain change in itself. Then the stone <term>helioselenus</term> <note>original: *helioselenus*; "sun-moon" stone.</note>, that is, the stone of the sun and

<column-break/>

<page-num>477</page-num>
<header>SACRIFICE AND MAGIC.</header>

moon, imitates in a certain way the meeting of the sun and moon, and represents it in its color. Thus all things are full of divine things: earthly things are full of heavenly ones, and heavenly things of super-celestial ones; and every order of things proceeds even to the last. For those things which are gathered into one above the order of things, these are thereafter expanded in descending, where various souls are arranged under various deities. Then also there are many solar animals, such as lions and roosters, participants in a certain solar deity according to their nature. Whence it is a wonder how much the lower things in the same order yield to the superior ones, although they do not yield in size or power. From this they say the rooster is feared very greatly by the lion, and almost worshipped; the cause of which thing we cannot assign to matter or sense, but only to the contemplation of the higher order: namely because the presence of the solar virtue belongs more to the rooster than to the lion. This is also apparent from the fact that the rooster applauds the rising Sun with certain hymns, as it were, and calls it forth when it is turned toward us from the mid-heaven of the <term>antipodes</term> <note>refers to the opposite side of the earth.</note>. And sometimes some solar angels have appeared, endowed with forms of this kind; and although they themselves were without form in their essence, yet to us...

<summary>Proclus explains the principle of "sympathy," illustrating how plants like the lotus, stones like selenite, and animals like the rooster reflect and participate in the powers of the sun and moon.</summary>
<keywords>cosmic sympathy, solar animals, lotus, astrology, Neoplatonism, Marsilio Ficino, sacred stones, solar angels</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's exploration of "sympathy" (the natural resonance between earthly and divine things). The previous page discussed how certain stones and plants like the Lotus and Sunstone react to the Sun and Moon. This page moves into the hierarchy of beings—from demons to minerals—and explains the practical application of these connections in ancient priestly rites.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON
<page-num>478</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON</header>

...we who are formed <note>The Latin *formati* refers to being shaped or structured to receive divine influence.</note> meet with those things that are likewise formed. Sometimes even solar **demons** <note>In this Neoplatonic context, "demons" (*daemones*) are not evil spirits, but intermediate beings that bridge the gap between gods and humans.</note> have been seen with a **lion-like brow**; however, when a **rooster** was placed before them, they suddenly vanished. This occurs because things lower in the same divine rank always revere those that are higher. In the same way, many people, when looking upon the images of divine men, are accustomed by that very sight to feel a sense of awe that prevents them from committing any shameful act.

To speak briefly: some things revolve in synchronization with the revolutions of the **Sun**, such as the plants we have mentioned. Others imitate the shape of the solar rays in some way, like the **palm** and the **date**. Others mirror the fiery nature of the Sun, such as the **laurel**, while others reflect something else entirely. Indeed, one may see the properties that are gathered together in the Sun distributed throughout the various beings that follow in the "solar chain"—namely, among **angels**, **demons**, **souls**, **animals**, **plants**, and **stones**.

For this reason, the authors of the ancient priesthood discovered the proper worship of the higher powers through these apparent earthly things, sometimes mixing substances and other times performing purifications. They would mix many things together because they saw that while simple, individual items possessed some property of a deity, a single item was not sufficient on its own to summon that deity.

---

<page-num>479</page-num>
<header>SACRIFICE AND MAGIC.</header>

Therefore, by the very act of mixing many things, they attracted celestial influences. By composing **one thing out of many**, they created a likeness of that "One" which exists above the many. They would also construct statues mixed from many different materials. By artistically gathering together various scents into a single composition, they created divine symbols, making a single result that reflects the divine essence, which itself encompasses a vast multitude of powers. When these powers are divided, each is weakened; however, when mixed, they are restored to the power of their original **Ideal Form**.

Sometimes, however, a single herb or a single stone is sufficient for a divine work. For instance, the <term>Cnebifon</term> <note>original: *Cnebifon*; identified here as a type of thistle (*carduus*).</note> (that is, the **thistle**) is sufficient for the sudden appearance of a certain deity. For protection, however, one might use the **laurel**, or the <term>Raccinum</term> <note>original: *Raccinum*; a type of thorny shrub.</note> (a prickly bush), or the **onion**, **squill** <note>A flowering plant in the asparagus family, historically used in medicine and ritual.</note>, **coral**, **diamond**, or **jasper**. For foreknowledge, one uses the heart of a **mole**. For purification, one uses **sulfur** and **seawater**.

Thus, the priests—through the mutual kinship and "sympathy" of things—brought powers together into one, or through natural "repugnance" <note>The opposite of sympathy; the natural way some things repel each other.</note> they drove things away. They purified with sulfur and **asphalt** (that is, bitumen) when necessary, and sprinkled with seawater. For sulfur purifies because of the sharpness of its odor, while seawater purifies because of its **fiery portion**. And animals [are offered] to the gods in the [temples] of the gods...

<summary>Proclus describes the Neoplatonic "chain of being" where solar properties are distributed among angels, plants, and minerals. He explains how ancient priests used "sympathy" to attract divine influences through compound mixtures or specific protective items like laurel and jasper.</summary>
<keywords>theurgy, sympathy, solar demons, purification, sacred plants, ritual magic, Proclus, Cnebifon, sulfur, seawater</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page concludes the short treatise by Proclus on the "hieratic art" (theurgy) and begins a new selection from the philosopher Porphyry. The previous page detailed how ancient priests used "sympathies" between earthly objects—like the rooster or the lotus—and celestial powers to perform rituals. Here, Proclus explains how they moved from using physical objects to direct communion with the gods. The new section by Porphyry shifts the focus to the soul's journey, describing how we must strip away physical attachments to return to our original, divine state.</meta>

# PROCLUS ON SACRIFICE
<page-num>480</page-num>
<header>PROCLUS ON HOLY RITES</header>

...they applied appropriate worship, and other things in a similar fashion. Therefore, by receiving [knowledge] from these and similar things, they first recognized the powers of **demons** <note>In the Neoplatonic tradition of Proclus, "demons" (*daemones*) are not evil spirits, but intermediate beings—messengers and mediators—who occupy the space between the human and the divine.</note>, namely that these powers are closest to natural things and actions. Through these very natural objects, to which the demons are closely linked, the priests summoned them into their presence. Then, from the demons, they proceeded toward the very powers and actions of the **gods** themselves. They achieved this partly by learning what the demons taught them, and partly by using their own diligence to interpret the appropriate **symbols** <note>original: *symbola*. For Neoplatonists, symbols were not just signs, but "tokens" or "signatures" of the divine embedded in the physical world.</note>, thereby ascending toward a true understanding of the gods. Finally, having set aside natural things and actions—and even, for the most part, the mediation of demons—they were received into the fellowship of the gods.

->**THE END OF PROCLUS ON SACRIFICE**<-
->**AND MAGIC.**<-

<column-break/>

<header>FROM PORPHYRY, ON THE DESCENT AND ASCENT OF THE SOUL. BOOK V.</header>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'O' featuring a bird, possibly a swan or pelican, with its neck curved back, surrounded by stylized foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>
**IT BEHOOVES** us (if indeed we are to return to those things which are truly our own) to entirely lay aside these things which we have taken upon ourselves from our mortal nature, together with the inclination that sinks toward them—the very path by which our **descent** occurred. Furthermore, we must remember that blessed and eternal substance. While we strive to ascend toward the **incorporeal** <note>The realm of things that have no physical body, such as thoughts, souls, and divine principles.</note>, we must meditate especially on two things: first, by what reasoning we may lay aside this material and fleeting existence; and second, by what path we may most effectively return. We must go back by a path contrary to the one by which we descended to these earthly things.

For we were, and still are, **intellectual essences** <note>original: *intellectuales essentiæ*. These are beings defined by pure thought or "intellect" (*nous*), rather than by physical matter.</note> pure from all sensation and from living by an irrational nature. However, we have been joined to a **sensible** <note>The "sensible" world refers to everything that can be perceived by the five senses.</note> nature, partly through the weakening of our eternal connection to our **intelligible** <note>The "intelligible" world is the realm of pure ideas and divine truths, accessible only by the mind.</note> home, and partly through a power that leans toward lower things. For all those powers that act in concert with the senses and the body only begin to sprout and take effect when the soul fails to persevere in the intelligible realm itself—much like a field of earth that has grown corrupt; though it has often received good wheat, it finally yields only **darnel** <note>original: *lolium*. A weed that looks like wheat but is actually poisonous, often used in ancient literature as a metaphor for spiritual or moral decay.</note>.

<summary>This page concludes Proclus's work on how priests use earthly symbols to reach the gods and begins Porphyry's discourse on the soul's need to shed its physical attachments to return to its divine origin.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, ascent of the soul, Porphyry, Proclus, demons, intelligible vs sensible, spiritual purification, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the selections from the Neoplatonist philosopher Porphyry. Having established that the soul becomes weighed down by its association with the body, Porphyry now describes the specific process of "unclothing" the soul—stripping away the irrational passions and sensory attachments to return to a state of pure intellect. This reflects the Neoplatonic goal of *henosis*, or mystical union with the divine, achieved through philosophical purification.</meta>

# FROM PORPHYRY

<meta>catchword: with</meta>

Therefore, through a certain corruption of the soul, it happens that it does not indeed lose its essence <note>In Neoplatonism, the soul's core is divine and eternal; it can be buried or "corrupted" by the body, but its fundamental nature remains unchanged.</note> while the irrational sprout grows. Rather, through this very thing, the soul is joined with the mortal, while it is drawn toward what is foreign from its own nature. Wherefore, if at any time we strive with all our might to return to our original state, it is necessary to thoroughly separate the soul from sense-perception and imagination, and from every irrational condition that follows from them, and from passions of this kind—as far as the necessity of nature permits. 

Indeed, we must rightly arrange and articulately compose those things which pertain to the **mind** <note>original: *mentem*. In this context, it refers to the *Nous* or Higher Intellect.</note>, establishing tranquility and peace regarding the irrational part [of ourselves]. In this way, we may not only hear about the **intelligibles** <note>term: *intelligibles* (intellectibilia). These are realities that can only be understood by the mind, such as mathematical truths or divine forms, as opposed to "sensibles" which are perceived by the eyes or ears.</note> from the intellect itself, but also—experiencing how great a power we are—we may enjoy the contemplation of the mind. Thus restored to an incorporeal nature, we may now truly live through the intellect, no longer living falsely alongside these fallen, kindred natures. 

We must therefore loosen many of our **garments** <note>A famous Neoplatonic metaphor: the "garments" are the layers of the body, the senses, and the passions that "clothe" the soul and hide its true form.</note>, both this visible garment of the body and the interior robes still applied to us. For we must not only avoid the effects of vices, but we must also extirpate the causes of those effects by the roots. 

The departure from the body can indeed happen

---

<header>FROM PORPHYRY</header>

with violence, but it can also happen through a certain gentle release. Indeed, it is rightly accomplished through a certain refinement—or as one might say, through the forgetting and death of the passions. This is indeed the best departure, since the mind draws nothing with it from that which it has been torn away. For as we have found in things manifest to the senses, whatever is plucked out by violence usually 

->draws something with it, whether a part,<-
->or a trace of the plucking itself,<-
->and of that from which it is uprooted.<-

->**IN THE MONTH OF AUGUST,**<-
->**IN THE YEAR 1532.**<-
